# The Forger of Verity
*The Long Survey, Book One*
by Sage Lang


# Part One: The Walk


## Chapter 1: A Very Confident Opinion

The hedge moved at a quarter past two in the morning, and it did not want to.

Ilse Marrow felt it refuse before she heard it. A pressure gathered behind her ears, the way it did before thunder, and the ground under her knees went as taut as a drumskin. In the shuttered lantern’s single slot of light, the wet charter lay on her board with its ink still shining. The wren’s-foot flourish of her signature sat over the faint violet underline that wasn’t hers. Nine hours of work. Forty-one stations, two hundred and twelve measured lengths, every angle checked twice by sightvane and once more by spite. It was a very good map of Widow Tull’s orchard strip, accurate to a link except in one particular.

In that particular, the hawthorn hedge on the west boundary stood two rods farther west than it actually did.

“Come on,” Ilse whispered. “You know you want to.”

The hedge did not know any such thing. It had stood where it stood since Haysel, when Lord Saffery’s surveyor had walked this parish with his chainmen and his fine leather case, re-charted the whole of Wick Fallow in nine days, and filed the result with the Concordance Court. The Court had stamped it, and the hedge had moved east in a single night, taking thirty-three feet of the widow’s land and four of her apple trees with it. Corwin Fask’s charter was legal and attested, and it had been filed before anyone else’s. To the ground it was simply the truth, and the ground had obeyed it.

Ilse’s charter was a different truth. It was better made. That was the whole trick.

She laid her palm flat on the sheet, the way old Quince had taught her, and leaned her attention into it. She didn’t push. Pushing was for amateurs and drunks. She *agreed* with the map, the way you agree with a fact you have looked at for so long it has become furniture. The hedge stands here. Of course it does. Where else would it stand? The soil there is better drained. The roots have always wanted the slope. Look at the old ditch line under the grass, look at the stump where the first planting failed, and see how the land itself remembers a boundary two rods west. It isn’t a lie. It’s a very confident opinion about where a hedge ought to be.

The pressure in her ears turned into a ringing, then a single high note, and the hedge gave.

It began as a sound like a long breath drawn through teeth: roots, hundreds of them, sliding through wet earth. Then the thorn branches scraped against each other with a dry rattle, the sound of a crowd shifting its feet. A blackbird burst from the hedge-bottom shrieking. Ilse lifted the lantern shutter a finger’s width and watched forty yards of hawthorn walk.

It didn’t topple or uproot. It *slid*, the whole green wall of it sliding level through the dark, as if the field beneath were a tablecloth and someone very patient were drawing it toward them. Leaves trembled and settled. A sparrow’s nest rode along on a fork, untroubled. Halfway along the run a stone grated and turned over. Two rods is thirty-three feet, and it took the hedge about as long to travel as it took Ilse to say the Ninefold’s Grace under her breath, which she did not believe in and recited anyway. Then it was finished, and the hedge stood still in its new place as though it had always been there. The ground let go of her ears, and a cold sweat broke over her whole body at once.

She sat back on her heels. Her head was splitting. Her right hand, the signing hand, ached from the knuckles to the wrist with the deep, grinding ache she thought of as the bill.

Behind her, at the cottage door, a voice said, “Oh, my word,” and then, more practically, “Is it done, dearie?”

“It’s done, Mrs. Tull.”

Agnes Tull came down the garden path in a nightcap and three shawls, carrying a candle in one hand and a basket in the other. She was seventy-four and walked like a woman who had stopped falling over through sheer resentment. She held the candle up toward the hedge and peered at it, and then at the four apple trees now standing on the right side of it, their leaves silver in the light.

“There’s my Pearmain,” she said. “There’s my old Pearmain back. And my Russet. Oh, you lovely girl.”

“I’m not lovely. I’m expensive.”

“Three marks and the eggs, like we said.” She pressed the basket on Ilse and then, before Ilse could stop her, pressed her dry old cheek against Ilse’s forehead as well. “Your father would have been proud.”

He wouldn’t, Ilse thought. Daun Marrow had believed in the Court and the Atlas and the Ninefold’s Grace until the day those things took his well, and then he hadn’t believed in anything at all, and three years after that he had died. But she said thank you, because Agnes Tull was seventy-four and had her Pearmain back, and that was worth a lie.

She packed in the dark by touch. The chain went into its canvas bag link by link so it wouldn’t jangle, a hundred links, each one seven inches and ninety-two hundredths. Then the sightvane and the folding rule. The levelglass went into its sock, its little bead of quicksilver swimming lazily now that the land was quiet again. Last came the inks: the common black for the visible lines, and the small corked bottle of what Quince had called *the blind*, a pale gall-ink cut with alum and a whisper of quicksilver, which dried to nothing on paper and showed only under a damp lens. With it, under her own flourish, she had traced the mark of a man who had been dead three years.

That was the part even Agnes Tull didn’t know about, and it was the part Ilse was proudest of.

A charter held land because a surveyor had signed it, and a signature had a cost. Every boundary you fixed pulled against you a little for the rest of your life, a thread from your hand to the earth, and if the land strained, so did you. Licensed surveyors carried their burden in their joints. Old ones went grey in the hands, and very old ones went grey all over and stopped. Quince had taught her the one thing the licensed men never learned, because they’d never needed to. You could trace a second mark beneath your own, line for line, in blind ink, and the weight would run down the second thread instead of the first. Quince was in the Hobbet churchyard and well past minding. He’d left her his mark the way another man might leave a watch.

*Don’t carry what you can make someone else carry*, he used to say, *and never let anyone carry you.* He had seen no contradiction in this. Neither, most days, did she.

She slung the bag over her shoulder. The hedge, in its new place, rustled once, settling, like a dog turning round before it lies down.


* * *


By eight the next morning the headache had dulled to the size of a walnut, and Ilse was carrying wet sheets through her mother’s laundry in Hobbet with the eggs in her coat pocket.

“You were out,” said Hesta Marrow, without turning from the copper.

“I was walking.”

“At two in the morning.”

“It’s a free country.” Ilse hung a petticoat across the line and pinned it with three pegs. “Within the boundaries of the Held Reach, as attested by the Ninefold and maintained by Concordance.”

“Don’t be clever at me before breakfast.” Her mother drove the paddle down into the steaming wash and hauled up a bedsheet like a drowned man. Hesta Marrow was forty-nine and built like a bollard, red to the elbows from forty years of other people’s linen, and she disapproved of her daughter with the steadiness other mothers gave to prayer. “Whose was it?”

“Whose was what?”

“Ilse.”

Ilse sighed. “Mrs. Tull’s. The orchard strip.”

The paddle stopped. Her mother looked at her through the steam for a long moment, then went back to the copper. “Well,” she said, “that one had it coming. Fask. Not Agnes. Agnes is a saint. A *nosy* saint.” She thumped the sheet. “Did you get paid?”

“Three marks.”

“Put it in the tin.”

“I always put it in the tin.”

The tin lived behind the loose brick by the chimney: a square one that had once held Lisk tea and still smelled faintly of bergamot. Ilse knew to the copper what was in it. Forty-one marks and six. She added three. Forty-four marks and six. Lord Saffery’s steward had said, two summers ago, with the condescension of a man who expected never to be held to it, that his lordship might consider selling back the north frontage of the Long Field for sixty marks. That meant the strip with the well. Sixty yards of good black soil that had been Marrow land for three generations, until a surveyor named Corwin Fask came through the parish with a new survey and a lord’s money, and the boundary moved sixty yards one autumn night in 1192.

She’d been twelve. She remembered the noise. It hadn’t been gentle like last night’s. Fask didn’t do gentle; he did *thorough*. The whole fence-line had come up out of the ground with a crack like a felled tree, and the well-head had groaned as the stones ground sideways, and her father had stood at the window in his shirt with the lamp in his hand saying *no, no, no* in a voice she’d never heard before and never wanted to hear again.

The Court had upheld it on appeal. Precedence and fidelity: Fask’s charter was newer than the old parish map, but it was better made, and better beat older. That was the law. Her father had spent his savings proving that the law was the law, then gone to work as a hired hand on his own field, and he had got smaller every year until there was nothing left for the fever to take.

Ilse put the lid back on the tin and the brick back in the wall. Not quite sixteen marks to go, if the steward kept his word, and he wouldn’t. But she had taught herself the trade that took the field away, and she was better at it than Fask. When she had a Patent of Charting and a licensed survey, filed and stamped, she would walk the Long Field herself, chain by chain, and make it so true the Court would have to kneel to it.

She just had to stay out of prison until then.


* * *


The market at Hobbet on a Thursday was where the parish did its real business, which was talking. Ilse sold the eggs to the baker’s girl for a penny more than she’d have got from anyone else. She bought a twist of tea and a heel of cheese, and she listened, which was the other half of being a forger. You had to know where the trouble was before the trouble knew where you were.

“—never seen anything like it,” the cheesewife was saying, to anyone. “A College gentleman. Out of the Meridian, all the way out from the Cape, can you imagine. Grey gloves. Kid, I should think. Ever so polite.”

“What did he want?” said the baker’s girl.

“Looking at the parish charters. The Little Wick lane, Jem says. Sat in the vestry with the chest open half the afternoon and then thanked Jem very nicely and went off in a hired gig. Asked the way to the old milestone.”

“Why would the College care about the Little Wick lane?”

“Why does the College care about anything? To make sure we stay put, I suppose.” The cheesewife laughed. “Maybe he heard about your mother’s pastry and thought it would float off.”

Ilse turned the cheese over in her hand and didn’t look up. It might mean nothing. The Meridian sent its people out all the time: to inspect charters and renew the old benchmarks, and to stand around in good coats explaining things to magistrates. It was the College that kept the Held Reach held. Its surveyors walked the oldest boundaries and re-signed them, so that the roads stayed roads and the rivers kept to their beds, and so that nobody woke to find that their house had wandered a furlong in the night. It was a long way from the Cape to the Fallowmarch, though. They didn’t send a man in kid gloves to look at a farm lane for no reason.

There’s been the other business, too, said a voice at the back of her mind that sounded like Quince. The Concordance had sent assessors east since the spring, because of the *loosenings*: a ditch in Marlow that wandered, a gatepost at Fennock that had leaned over in a single night and then kept walking. Talk said the Margin-folk had come in from the west in their painted wagons with their peculiar songs and were unpicking the ground out of spite. Talk said a great many things. But assessors were people who looked closely at boundaries, and Ilse had moved one last night.

She paid for the cheese and walked home the long way, by the Little Wick lane. She told herself it was a nice afternoon for it.


* * *


It was a nice afternoon. That was the thing she kept coming back to, afterward. The barley was in, and the stubble fields lay gold under a high sky that was just beginning to turn green at the edge with evening. Rooks went over in a long ragged line. The Little Wick lane ran between two banks of hawthorn and dog rose, old banks, sunk deep by four hundred years of wheels, so that you walked in a kind of green trench with the fields above your head. At the bottom of the dip the lane forded the Wick brook over a bed of set cobbles. Then it climbed again past the old milestone, a lump of limestone with HOBBET II carved on one side and a benchmark arrow on the other, its notch filled with moss.

Tam Brewin was coming down the far side with his wagon and his big bay mare, a load of hurdles behind him, singing something about a girl in Lisk. Ilse lifted a hand. Tam lifted his whip.

Her ears popped.

She stopped. It was like the moment on a stair when you expect one more step and there isn’t one. The pressure that had been at the back of her head all her life, so constant she only noticed it when it changed, the faint steady hum she had learned at fourteen to call *the level*, simply dropped away. It was like a held note cut off mid-breath. The silence where it had been was so loud she put a hand out to the bank.

The bank moved under her palm.

“Tam!” she shouted.

He’d felt something too; she saw him half-rise on the box. The mare threw her head up. Then the lane began to come apart.

It started at the ford. The set cobbles, three hundred years in their places, began to drift away from each other the way the stitches of a dropped knit run away from the needle, each one loosening its neighbour. Water spread over them. The ruts in the lane were deep enough to turn a cartwheel, and they *swam*, wriggling sideways across the roadway like eels in a bucket, and the banks on either side sagged inward with a sound like a long sigh. Dog roses tore. A root snapped under the bank. The milestone sank with a kind of dignity until it stood in the lane up to its shoulders like a man wading.

The wagon tipped. Its near wheel had been in a rut, and the rut was no longer there; the wheel dropped through soft earth to the hub. The load of hurdles slid with a clatter and the whole wagon heeled over toward the bank, and Tam Brewin went off the box sideways into the mud. The mare screamed and plunged, half-strangled by the traces.

Ilse was already running.

The ground was wrong beneath her. Every step went down farther than it should, or hit sooner, and the lane was shrugging under her like the deck of a boat. She went down on one knee and came up again with her hands full of mud. Tam was on his back with the edge of a hurdle across his chest and his face white. She grabbed the hurdle and heaved, and he scrabbled out from under it.

“The horse,” he gasped. “Get her loose, she’ll break her neck—”

The mare was down on her knees, thrashing, the shaft rearing up over her as the wagon settled. Ilse got her knife out. She had cut traces before; you don’t grow up in the Fallowmarch without having cut something loose from something else in a hurry. She sawed through the near trace and then the belly-band, ducking a hoof that would have taken her jaw off, and Tam got the mare’s head. Together they hauled her up the shifting slope of the bank. She went up it in great panicked surges, and they all fell together into the stubble at the top: horse, man and forger, in a heap, under the green evening sky.

Below them, the Little Wick lane went on unravelling for another minute or so. Then, by degrees, it stopped. It didn’t go back; it just stopped. The cobbles lay scattered over the bed of the brook like spilled dice. The ruts had settled in new and senseless curves. The milestone stood up to its shoulders in the earth, HOBBET II looking out at a road that no longer went to Hobbet or anywhere else.

“What,” said Tam Brewin. He was lying on his back in the stubble and breathing like a bellows. “What. What in the name of the Atlas was that.”

Ilse didn’t answer. She was sitting up with her arms around her knees, looking down at the ruin of the lane, and the level was coming back into her ears, slowly, like hearing after a blow. She had felt land move many times in her life. She had *moved* land, most recently last night. And she knew how it felt when one charter pushed against another, the strain and the give and the settling. That was a kind of argument. The ground took a side.

This hadn’t been an argument. Nothing had pushed. The lane had simply been let go, as though someone had reached into the parish chest and taken out its charter and burned it, and the land, with nothing left to tell it where it was, had forgotten.

But that wasn’t possible. You couldn’t simply un-write a road. Not one this old, deep in the Reach, held by four hundred years of the Fallowmarch walking up and down it. Not without—

*A College gentleman. Grey gloves. Ever so polite. Looking at the Little Wick lane.*

“I need to go home,” she said aloud.

“You saved my life,” said Tam.

“You’d have got out.”

“I’d have got out *flat*.” He rolled over and looked at his mare, who stood trembling in the stubble with her head down and a long graze along her flank. “Look at her. Look at my wagon. Who’s going to pay for my wagon?” His voice cracked. “Ilse, what *was* it?”

“I don’t know,” she said, and it was the truest thing she’d said all day.


* * *


She should have gone home. She knew it the way you know you shouldn’t pick a scab. But there was a forger’s itch at the back of her neck, the one that had kept her out of the magistrate’s ledger for nine years, and it said that a strange day was a day to check your work. So she went the long way round in the last of the light. She came up through Fenner’s copse and over the stile behind Widow Tull’s, to look at her hedge.

That was how she found him.

There was a dark lantern set in the grass beside the hedge, shuttered to a thin gold blade of light, and in the light a man was crouching. He was young, perhaps her own age or a little less, in a long coat of the particular grey-blue that the Concordance gave its assessors, with brass buttons that caught the light. He had a notebook open on one knee and a folding rule in his hand, and he was measuring the raw edge of the soil along the hedge-bottom: the fresh earth where the roots had dragged through last night, which no rain had yet softened. He measured it, wrote something down, and measured again.

Ilse stood very still in the shadow of the stile and thought, with great clarity, *bloody hell.*

She had a choice. She could walk away; he hadn’t seen her. But if he was what she thought he was, he would be at the parish office in the morning and at Agnes Tull’s door by noon. Agnes Tull was a saint, a nosy one and a terrible liar. Better to know him now, in the dark, on her own ground.

She stepped out over the stile, letting her boot scuff the step so he would hear her coming. He looked up. The light caught his face from below: a fine, serious, well-made face, the kind that had been told it was handsome and had decided that was beside the point, with dark-blond hair combed back with water and eyes she couldn’t see the colour of. He didn’t startle. He looked at her the way she looked at a boundary stone, as if he was checking it against a record.

“Good evening,” he said.

“Evening.” She let a little of the Fallowmarch into her voice, a little farm-girl curiosity. “You’re out late, sir. Lost?”

“No.” He closed the notebook on one finger to keep his place, and with his other hand he tapped the folding rule against the raw earth at the hedge-bottom: once, twice. When he spoke again it was pleasantly and without hurry, in the voice of someone who expected to be believed.

“This hedge,” he said, “has moved.”


## Chapter 2: The Assessor’s Notebook

“Has it?” said Ilse.

She came down off the stile and stood at the edge of his light, where the grass was wet and the hawthorn smelled green and bruised. She let herself look at the hedge the way a farm girl would look at it, which was to say without much interest, as a thing that kept sheep in.

“It has.” He stood, unfolding himself carefully, like a man who had been told as a boy not to slouch. “Forgive me. I should have introduced myself before accusing your hedges. Kasimir Reyne. Junior assessor, attached to the Concordance inspection of the Fallowmarch voids, under Assessor Pollard, who is at the Bell in Hobbet and has gone to bed.”

“The what voids?”

“The loosenings. The ditch at Marlow, the gatepost at Fennock.” He paused. “The lane at Little Wick, this evening. I imagine you heard.”

“I was in it.”

That stopped him, which she enjoyed more than she should have. He looked at the mud dried to her knees, and his face rearranged itself from courtesy into attention.

“You were in the lane when it went?”

“Me and Tam Brewin and his mare. We got out.” She shrugged. “Tam’s wagon didn’t.”

He opened the notebook again, a small thing in black calf, ruled by hand. His writing was beautiful even at speed, upright and narrow, the descenders all one length, and she read it upside down out of pure professional reflex. *Little Wick lane — witness — ford first, then ruts — time?*

“What time?” he said.

“Half past six. Near enough. The rooks were going over.”

He wrote *rooks*, and then, after a moment’s thought, crossed it out. “And did you feel anything before it began? Some people describe a pressure.”

There it was: a little door, held politely open. Anyone could measure, but only the levelled felt a lane let go. If she said yes, he would write that down too, in a notebook already full of things she didn’t like.

“I felt the ground go soft under my feet,” she said. “Same as Tam. Same as the horse.”

“Of course.” He didn’t write anything, which was worse. His eyes were grey or blue, the colour of slate in rain. “And this hedge. Did you see it move?”

“Hedges don’t move.”

“That is the general view.” He crouched again and laid the folding rule flat against the raw soil at the hedge-bottom. “It is also, in this case, wrong. Look here, if you would.”

She looked; it would have been stranger not to. In the gold blade of his lantern the earth along the east side of the hawthorn lay open in a strip a little under a yard wide. It was dark and crumbed and stippled with the white ends of broken rootlets, like a bed someone had just got out of.

“Forty-one yards, nine inches, end to end,” he said. “The rootlets are broken, not cut, and the breaks are still wet. Last night’s dew lies on top of the turned soil, not under it. This hedge walked within the last day, west, two rods almost exactly.” He looked up at her. “Two rods. Thirty-three feet. That is not a number the weather chooses.”

“Maybe it was homesick.”

“Maybe it was.” He did not smile, but something near his mouth considered it. “It was re-charted in Haysel by a licensed surveyor acting for Lord Saffery, and moved east. A hedge cannot move two rods in a season against a Patent survey unless somebody has filed a later charter of better fidelity. Nobody in this parish holds a Patent.” He tilted his head. “Unless you know of someone.”

“The vicar’s uncle had one,” said Ilse. “He’s dead.”

“Then I think we may exclude him.”

He stood and brushed his knees, and she saw his boots, and they nearly undid her. Tessary boots, lasted to his own foot by someone who had done nothing else for forty years, with a welt like a line of handwriting. He had walked them to the ankle through a wet orchard and ruined them, and either he hadn’t noticed or he had decided it was a fair price for a hedge. She couldn’t decide which was worse.

He was studying her too, openly, the way he’d studied the soil. She knew what he saw, because she had arranged it: a small parish girl in a man’s old coat, hair hacked short, come over a stile to stare at a stranger. She watched him decide she was what she looked like, and watched a smaller part of him decline to decide anything at all.

Then he breathed in, and frowned. “You smell of oak-gall. Iron-gall ink, or a near cousin of it.” He said it almost apologetically, as a man might mention that your collar was turned under. “My mother’s clerks reek of it.”

The blind had a sour green edge that never washed out of the skin entirely, however much you scrubbed with lye. She had scrubbed. It was the alum, Quince used to say. Alum got into you like guilt.

“My mother’s a laundress,” she said. “We dye the mourning blacks. Oak-apples and copperas, three days in the vat. You can smell it on the whole street when someone’s died.” This was true. It had always been true. That was the beauty of it. “There’s a funeral Saturday. Old Pettifer.”

“I’m sorry.”

“Don’t be. He was a terrible man. He cheated at skittles.”

Now the smile did come, very briefly, and was put away again like a coin in a purse. “Then I am sorry for the skittles,” he said. “May I ask your name?”

She considered lying. It was a parish of thirty-one families and a mill, and any of them would tell him her name for the pleasure of telling. A lie would cost her nothing tonight and everything tomorrow, when he found it and asked himself why.

“Ilse Marrow.”

He wrote it down. *Ilse Marrow*, in that tall clean hand. “Miss Marrow. And do you know whose hedge this is?”

“Mrs. Tull’s. She’s seventy-four and she’s asleep.” Ilse let her voice go sharp. “If you knock her up at this hour about her hedge, sir, you’ll get a chamber pot thrown at you, and I’ll say you deserved it.”

“I am not going to knock anyone up,” he said, as though she had suggested he pick his teeth with the folding rule. “I am going to look at the parish chest in the morning, with the assessor, as is proper.” He closed the notebook. “Good night, Miss Marrow. Mind the lane, if you go home that way. It no longer goes where it went.”

She said good night and went back over the stile, not quickly. All along the field edge she felt his lantern lying flat across her back. He wasn’t watching her, she knew. He would be measuring the hedge again. That was worse.


* * *


The vestry linen went back on Fridays.

The surplices of the Ninefold chapel at Wick had to be starched until they stood up without a priest in them, and Ilse had carried them up the church hill every Friday since she was eight. She had never once been glad of it until that morning, when she saw the vestry door propped open with a hassock and heard, from inside, a voice she knew. She set the basket on the side step and took her time over the folding.

The parish chest stood open on the table: oak, iron-bound, three locks. Jem Rudd the clerk stood beside it with the keys and the stricken look of a man whose biggest event of the year had been a dispute about a pew. A stout man in the same grey-blue coat as Kasimir Reyne’s, with the buttons straining a little, sat in the vicar’s chair with his hat on his knee and a slice of seed cake on the hat.

“I’m sure it is all in order, Mr. Rudd.” Pollard ate some cake. “Mr. Reyne is very thorough. Thoroughness is what he is attached to us to learn.” He looked at his watch. “We dine at Saffery Hall at four.”

Kasimir Reyne stood at the window end of the table, where the light was best, with a charter flattened under his two hands.

She knew it by the way it lay, cockled at one corner where Agnes had held it with a damp thumb. Agnes had brought it to Jem yesterday morning: an amendment filed by the landholder, which was every landholder’s right, attested by a local surveyor, which was every hedge-man’s trade. Ilse had done everything properly. It was the improper part she had done better.

“Two charters for one boundary,” Kasimir said. “Corwin Fask’s, under Patent, filed Haysel the ninth. And this amendment, filed yesterday for Mrs. Agnes Tull. Forty-one stations. The lines are very good.” He said it the way a man might report that a bridge was well built, shortly before it fell down. “The surveyor’s mark is not registered with the Court.”

“Hedge-mark,” said Pollard, through cake. “Half the Fallowmarch is charted by hedge-men. Wheelwrights. Old women with a feel for drainage. It’s a scandal, not a crime. A freeholder may survey her own holding. Statute of Small Holdings, 1061.”

“Yes, sir. It is a crime when the survey binds the land to a state contrary to a Patent charter already in force. That is contrary charting.” Kasimir turned the sheet toward the light. Through the gap of the door Ilse saw her own wren’s foot, three lines and a spur, dark and confident in the lower corner. It looked very small from here. “To supersede a competent Patent survey, this would have to be of greater fidelity. I find that improbable for a sheet drawn by a hedge-man, at night, on a farm.”

“Improbable,” said Pollard, “is not evidence.”

“No, sir. Which is why I should like to request a drawing of the mark.”

The words went into Ilse like cold water.

She had seen it done once, at sixteen, to a Fennock wheelwright named Hobb who forged ditches. The Court’s examiner, a dry little woman with a case of lenses, had laid his charters out in the tithe barn, wetted each one and set a damp lens over the signature. A mark was a thread from the hand to the land, and an examiner could follow it home. Under a damp lens, blind ink showed. So did a second mark ghosted beneath the first. And the thread of a ghosted mark ran to whoever carried the burden: in this case to a disgraced hedge-surveyor in the Hobbet churchyard, whose mark was still on the Court’s rolls, and whose apprentice for six years had been a girl named Marrow.

They had cut a line through every one of Hobb’s marks with a silver knife. Every ditch he had ever moved had moved back in one night, and he had gone to the Tessary gaol for nine years. His wife had taken in washing. Hesta had given her the overflow.

“A drawing,” said Pollard. “Over a hedge.”

“Over a contrary charter, sir. If it is one. And there is the other matter. Mr. Rudd, you said a gentleman came to see the Little Wick lane charter on Tuesday.”

“That’s right, sir. A College gentleman. Very civil.”

“May I see it?”

Jem found it near the bottom, folded in four, the ink of the lane’s two banks faded to the colour of weak tea. Kasimir read it twice, and held it to the window and looked through it at the light.

“It is in order,” he said at last.

“It is in order,” Kasimir said again, “and the lane is not.”

There was a silence in the vestry. Pollard put down his cake.

“Old charters,” he said. “Old ground. It tires. Write it up with the others, Reyne. The Tessary office has a theory about the Margin wagons.”

“The nearest Margin wagon is a very long way from here, sir.”

“They are a mobile people,” said Pollard, with dignity. “That is rather the point of them.”

Kasimir let it go. She watched it cost him something. “And the drawing of the mark on the Tull amendment?”

Pollard sighed like a man watching his dinner recede. “The sheet would go to Tessary under seal. Correspondence, an examiner’s calendar. Three weeks at the least, Reyne, and you’ll be gone by then.”

“I will write the request myself, sir. It needs only your signature.”

“Of course it does.” Pollard held out his hand for the pen. “Seal the chest, then. Mr. Rudd, nobody opens it until the Court says so. Nobody. Not the vicar. Not the Ninefold, should they drop by.”

So that door closed too. She had been thinking, in the cool back room of her mind where Quince had taught her to think, of getting into the vestry at night and burning the amendment, or releasing it, unsigning it with her own hand. Agnes would lose her Pearmain, but there would be nothing left to draw. Either way she needed the sheet. She watched Kasimir drip a coin of grey-blue wax across the hasp and press the Concordance seal into it, neatly, and then do it twice more, once on each of the other locks.

Then he looked up and through the door and directly at her.

“Good morning, Miss Marrow,” he said.

Ilse picked up the basket of surplices and held it in front of her like a shield.

“The vicar’s linen,” she said. “I’ll leave it on the step.”

“Thank you,” said Kasimir Reyne, as if it had been his.


* * *


“You could run,” said Hesta.

It was past eleven. The copper was out and the kitchen smelled of wet ash and cooling starch. Her mother sat at the table with her red hands flat on the scrubbed wood, the way she sat when she meant to stay sitting until a thing was decided. The candle between them had burned down to a stub. Neither of them had moved to light another.

“Where?” said Ilse.

“Lisk. You’ve a cousin in Lisk.”

“Cousin Barnaby is a canal-warden. He’d have me in a cell by Tuesday. And the Court sits everywhere. That’s the point of a Court.” She heard the edge in her voice and put it away, and went to the window.

She did what Quince had taught her to do with any trouble big enough to be frightening. She surveyed it.

Station one: Hobbet. Three weeks, a drawing of the mark, and Agnes Tull saying *I didn’t know, dearie*, which would be true and wouldn’t help. Then the Tessary gaol, and every boundary she had ever moved snapping back overnight: the Gethins’ ditch, the chapel glebe, Widow Ames’s goat-run. Every small truth she had made, unmade with a silver knife.

Station two: any town in the Reach. The Court’s rolls went everywhere the Great Meridian Road went, and the Road went everywhere.

Station three: the Margin. The Tenting wouldn’t have her, and the Margin was no place to hide. It was a place where things got lost, which was different.

The Drift wasn’t a station at all. It was the edge of the board.

So she went back and looked again, the way you looked at a traverse that wouldn’t close, and found the error. It was a thing Quince had said once in the Hare and Lantern, with gin on his breath, explaining why the College men walked around the Reach as though they owned it.

*Because they’re not ours, girl. Members of the Meridian answer to the Meridian. Charting offences, any of them, it’s their own Board tries them. Not the Court. The Court can’t touch so much as a link-boy. Privilege. Four hundred years of it. It makes Lady Muck in Tessary spit teeth.*

And how did a smallholder’s daughter become a member of the Meridian? Patent examinations, Patent fees. Or—

*Any fool with the level can try the Walk. Turn up at their gate on the equinox and walk out into the Margin and back. A good few don’t come back. I thought about it once.* He’d laughed into his gin. *Then I thought about it twice.*

“The autumn equinox,” Ilse said aloud.

Her mother looked up.

“It’s the twenty-second. Today’s the sixteenth.” Ilse sat. She found she was counting on her fingers, and stopped. “Five hundred and sixty miles to Cape Verity. The express mail goes through the Bell at dawn tomorrow, day and night, changes at every stage. Four days. I’d be at the Lantern Gate the night before the equinox. Once I’m a member, the Court can’t have me. The College tries me itself. Which takes longer. Which might take—” She stopped.

“How much,” said Hesta.

“What?”

“The coach. Don’t look at me like I’m simple. I’ve been listening to you add sums under your breath for nine years. How much.”

“Thirty-eight marks. Inside.”

Neither of them looked at the chimney.

The tin was there anyway, behind the loose brick, the way a grave is there whether you look at it or not. Forty-four marks and six. Not quite sixteen short of a strip of black soil with a well in it. She could see the frontage in Haysel with the barley standing, and the well-head with its stones ground crooked.

She had meant to buy it back. That had been the whole of the plan since she was fourteen: buy it back, then walk it with a Patent and chain it so true the Court would kneel. She had meant to do it for him. It came to her, with the candle dying, that she had never once asked whether he would have wanted it done this way, and that she wasn’t going to ask now.

“It’s a field,” said Hesta.

“It’s *our* field.”

“It was your father’s field. And he’s dead, and it didn’t save him, and it won’t save you.” Her mother’s voice didn’t rise. It went lower and harder, like a paddle going down into the copper. “I’ll not stand at the back of a courtroom in Tessary watching them take a knife to your name so that you can buy back sixty yards of dirt. I did that once. I did it for a year. You remember what it got us.”

Ilse remembered.

She took out the brick and counted the money onto the table, though she knew to the copper what was there, because counting was a thing to do with her hands. She put six marks and six back in the tin and the rest in her purse. The purse made a small, final sound against the table, like a latch dropping.

“Six and six,” she said. “For the rent.”

“Keep it.”

“It’s for the rent, Mam.”

Hesta looked at her a long time. Then she lit another candle from the stub and went to the press by the stairs and took out the coat.

It was Daun Marrow’s good coat, dark brown broadcloth gone soft at the elbows, the one he had been married in. The parish lent a coat for burying, and Hesta had not let them have this one. Her mother laid it on the table and turned back the lining.

“If you’ll be taking your bits,” Hesta said, not looking at her, “they’ll want to be somewhere a nosy body won’t find them.”

So they sat up until two by a single candle, as they had sat up over other people’s shirts a thousand times. Ilse unpicked the lining along the hem. In went the blind in its corked bottle, rolled in flannel; the damp-lens in its leather; a twist of alum; the fine steel pen Quince had ground for her the year before he died. Her mother stitched it closed again with laundress’s stitches, eleven to the inch, invisible. Neither of them said what it was for.

At four, Hesta wrapped half a loaf and the heel of cheese in a cloth and put it in the coat pocket where the eggs had been. Ilse didn’t find the six marks and six tucked in beside the cheese until Tessary, and by then there was nothing to do about it but swear, fondly, at a woman two hundred and fifty miles behind her.

“Write,” Hesta said, at the door. “You wretch.”


* * *


The Bell’s yard at dawn was all steam and harness and swearing.

The express mail stood under the arch with its lamps still lit, maroon and black, a crowned horn on the door and four greys stamping. The guard looked at Ilse’s thirty-eight marks as though she had made them in a back room. She had not, as it happened. Not these. She paid for an inside seat and then climbed up onto the roof among the mailbags, where the wind would be. She wanted to see.

Across the yard, on the first floor of the Bell, a window stood open.

He was in it, coat off, sleeves rolled, packing a leather case on the bed and folding each shirt into thirds. She watched him lay the black notebook on top and square it to the corners. A post-chaise waited at the side door with a trunk roped on, and the trunk had a label too far away to read.

Then he straightened and looked out of the window and saw her.

He saw her on the roof of the westbound mail with her father’s coat buttoned to the throat and her surveyor’s bag between her knees. She saw him see the bag, and the board on the yard wall chalked TESSARY — VERITY CROSS. She thought he might lift a hand. He didn’t. He only stood at the window with a shirt folded in thirds over his arm, the way he had stood at the hedge, checking her against a record.

The guard blew the horn. The greys leaned into the collars, and the mail lurched, and swung, and went out under the arch into the Hobbet road.

She did not look back at the window. She looked at everything else instead.

The sun came up behind them in a long red bar, and the light ran out ahead of the coach across the stubble. Every field she had ever walked went past at eight miles an hour: the Gethins’ forty acres, with the ditch she had moved four feet south in the spring; Widow Ames’s goat-run; the hedge of Saffery’s Home Farm, black and dead straight, which she had never touched. She didn’t look for the Long Field, and saw it anyway, the dark square of the well-head on the wrong side of a line.

Every hedge was a line on a sheet. She knew which ones. She knew whose. She knew Fask’s straight black work and the old parish lines in brown ink gone faint as tea, and her own, the few, the small. Every one of them was a sentence written by someone with a pen and a reason, and the land had believed them all. The hedgerows ran away from her in the low red light, dark green, unbroken, knitting the whole Fallowmarch together from horizon to horizon, and holding it still.


## Chapter 3: Cape Verity

The Great Meridian Road was the straightest thing Ilse had ever seen, and it had been made that way on purpose.

It began at the Zero Stone, and she joined it there, at Tessary, after two days and two nights of Eastern Post lanes that went round every field as though apologizing. The Road did not apologize. It ran west out of the capital like a ruled line, paved in grey setts worn into two shallow gutters by a thousand years of wheels, with a milestone every mile and a benchmark arrow cut into every milestone. The mail took it at a gallop.

That was how the journey stayed with her afterward: a handful of sheets out of a folio, out of order.

The second passenger on the roof, from Hobbet onward, was a hedge-preacher named Brother Amos, who wore a rope belt and swore on the Atlas the way other men cleared their throats. “Fine weather, swear on the Atlas,” he said, and “Terrible pie, swear on the Atlas,” and, when the mail hit a pothole at Coldharbour and nearly threw him into a hayrick, “Swear on the Atlas and all the Ninefold standing, I will see that coachman *damned*.” He offered to pray over her bag. She told him it had been prayed over already, which was true in the sense that Quince had cursed it often.

There was the night stage, the coach-lamps swinging, and Ilse wedged between two mailbags listening to the level. In the Fallowmarch she had never thought about it, the way you never think about the floor. But as the mail ran west toward Tessary it deepened. Every mile nearer the centre of the Reach, the pressure behind her ears grew steadier and lower, until by midnight it was a note so deep she felt it in her back teeth, like the bottom string of a viol under a bow that never reached its end. The ground here had been held for twelve hundred years without a break. It knew to the inch where it was. It was almost smug.

There was Tessary at dawn on the third day. She saw it in pieces between warehouses: copper roofs gone green, hundreds of them, stepping down in terraces, and smoke going straight up from every chimney as if even the air stayed where it was put. Then a gap in the buildings, and through the gap, for the space of a long breath, a broad paved plaza and in the middle of it a grey obelisk. It was not tall. She had expected tall. It was perhaps the height of three men, square and plain and very slightly darker than the stone around it, as if it were standing in its own shadow. People walked round it without looking at it, carrying bread.

“The Zero Stone,” said Brother Amos, and took off his hat. “Where they first set the chain down. Every boundary in the Reach is measured back to that, swear on—”

“I know,” said Ilse.

She did know. She had worked out once, with Quince, how many chains it was from the Zero Stone to the bottom of the Gethins’ ditch, and had felt for a moment a long thread running through the earth from her hand to that stone. She looked at it until the buildings closed again.

There was the afternoon west of Tessary when the land began to thin.

That was the only word for it. Nothing looked different: sheep country, then long downs with chalk showing through like knuckles. But the level grew lighter, as though someone were taking weight off the bow, until it had a reedy, uncertain edge she didn’t like. The levelglass in her bag felt it too. When the mail stopped to change horses at Garrow she took it out on the inn wall and watched the little bead of quicksilver wander off true and drift back, like a cow that has seen something interesting in the next field.

And on the last day, the Road came to the Margin.

The Road ran along its edge for nearly forty miles, high on the downs, and the Margin lay on their left hand all the way to a smudged horizon: heath, brown and purple, broken by glints of standing water. Nothing on it looked wrong. That was what unsettled her. But the level dropped and steadied and dropped again as the coach went along, and she found her right hand closing on the strap of her bag without being told to.

Then she noticed the milestones.

Every one of them, along that forty miles, had a new benchmark arrow. The old arrows were there too, weathered soft and furred with lichen, but beside or beneath each one somebody had cut a fresh one. The notches were sharp and pale, the stone in them bright as a new cut in cheese. At one milestone the new arrow was so recent that the chips still lay in the grass at its foot.

“The College men,” said Brother Amos, following her look. “They come along every few weeks now. Never used to. Re-signing the Road.” He spat over the side, politely. “Afraid it’ll wander off.”

“Would it?”

“Swear on the Atlas,” said Brother Amos, “I hope not, my girl. I’ve a sister in Verity Cross.”


* * *


They came over the last of the downs at dusk on the twenty-first, and there was the sea.

She had never seen it. She had expected to be moved, the way people in songs were, and was mostly surprised by how *flat* it was. It lay out to the north and west like a sheet of beaten pewter, darkening, with a long scarf of cloud lying on it far out and lit underneath by the last of the sun. The land ran down toward it in a long tongue of granite: Cape Verity, black against the water, with the lights of the town strung along the near end of it.

And at the far end, on the headland, was the Meridian.

She saw the Held Side first, because it was meant to be seen first. It was honey-coloured stone that held the last light as a hearthstone holds heat, quadrangles and towers and long slate roofs, and in the middle of its western face stood a single tall arch with a light burning on top of it. The Lantern Gate. Even from two miles off, the light did not flicker.

Beyond the Gate, the College went on, and changed.

It ran out from the arch along the spine of the headland and then down off it, onto the dark heath beyond, in a long, loose, rambling line of buildings. Halls and glass domes, stair-turrets and covered bridges, joined end to end as if added one at a time over centuries by people who didn’t speak to each other. It went on for half a mile or more, getting lower and stranger as it went, and it curved, very gently, the way a sleeping animal curves when it lies down in long grass with its tail out behind it. Windows were lit all along its length, hundreds of them, small and gold. The Drift Wing.

She was looking at one window in particular, low down near the far end, a single gold square with a dark shape moving in it, when it moved.

It slid. It went a few feet along the dark, smoothly and without hurry, like a lamp carried along a corridor. But nobody was carrying the window. The whole of the building around it, the stretch of wall she could just make out by the light of its neighbours, went with it. Then it stopped, and was still, and the window beside it was a little closer than it had been.

The guard blew the horn for Verity Cross. Ilse had the level in her ears like a held breath and her right hand was aching at the knuckles, though she hadn’t signed anything for a week.


* * *


The Chain & Anchor stood at the top of Verity Cross market square, its sign a surveyor’s chain coiled round a ship’s anchor, which seemed to Ilse a joke at somebody’s expense. A room cost two marks. She paid it, thought about the four marks and six left in her purse, and stopped thinking about it on purpose.

The taproom was full of students.

She knew them by their rings, plain iron on the right hand, and by their voices, which were mostly Tessary and loud. She took a bowl of mutton stew to a corner settle by the fire and kept her head down over it and listened.

“—Brask’s taking it again. Nineteenth year running. Nobody else will.”

“Nobody else is let. Steyne would rather she was out on the heath on the equinox than in the Mess making faces at him.”

“What are the odds this year?”

“On the Walk? One in three come back with a passing sheet. One in three come back with a bad one. One in three—” A low whistle, a hand flat on the table, sliding away.

“You’re foul, Debenham.”

“I am *accurate*.” Laughter. “I’ll tell you what, though, the chains had a shocker this morning. Did you hear? Hollis’s lot. Went out at First Bell and their stair had turned clean round in the night, and they surveyed it the wrong way up and signed it. Their landing’s been facing the sea all day. Arnaud fined them a mark apiece.”

“Lost your stair, chains!” said someone, in a falsetto, and the whole table roared and banged their mugs.

A girl near the fire, with her hair in a long Tessary plait, was saying to her friend in a lower voice, “—went out to the heath end of the Second Range this afternoon and the door’s opening on gorse again. Just gorse. Moy says it’ll come back by morning if the Gannet Stair lot get their sheet in—”

Ilse finished her stew and went up to bed.

She slept badly. The bed was good and the sheets were clean, which made it worse. Every time she slid toward sleep she saw the window going along the dark, smooth as a carried lamp. She saw the cobbles at the Wick ford drifting apart, and the wren’s foot under a seal of grey-blue wax. Somewhere toward three she gave up and sat on the windowsill in her shift with the casement open, and watched the light on the Lantern Gate burning two miles off, steady, small, unblinking, until the sky behind it started to go grey.


* * *


There were nineteen of them under the Gate before dawn.

The arch was bigger close to. It rose sixty feet or more, plain honey stone with no ornament but a single line of letters cut across the span, each letter as tall as a child: WE RECORD WHAT IS. The lantern sat on the keystone in a cage of black iron, burning yellow and steady. The fog had come in off the sea in the night. It lay over everything beyond the arch in a soft grey wall, and the lantern-light went into it a little way and stopped.

A sour clerk took their names. He wrote *Wick Fallow* as though it were a disease.

They stood about in the grey half-light, nineteen people not looking at each other. One boy of about eighteen had brought a chain so new it still had the chandler’s tag on it, and he kept counting the links under his breath. A gaunt, silent man of thirty or so in a quarryman’s jacket stood off by himself against the wall of the arch. His name, when the clerk called it, was Oskar Fell. He didn’t speak to anyone, then or later.

A girl about Ilse’s height but twice as wide in the shoulder stepped up beside her, grinning.

“Yara,” she said, and stuck out a hand. “Yara Penhallow. Dairy farm, out past Holm, other side of Tessary. You’re a farmer too. I can tell by your hands.”

“Laundress.”

“Same hands.” Yara beamed. She had a round red face and fair eyebrows that went up when she talked, which was often. “My da says the Walk is for people who can’t afford the Patent fees or the sense to stay home. I said that’s me, Da, both halves. Our old bull, Hector, he’s held like a church, you could survey off him—”

Something very large trod on Ilse’s foot.

“Oh, I’m sorry, I’m so sorry, I didn’t—that was your foot. That was definitely a foot. I’m so sorry.”

He was the biggest young man she had ever seen, well over six feet and broad all the way down, round-faced, with curly auburn hair and a beard that was trying hard and not quite managing. He was sweating, though the morning was cold enough to show breath. Held against his chest with both arms like a baby was a pie in a cloth, at least a foot across.

“Teo. Teodor Anselm. Lisk. I’ve brought a pie.” He looked down at it as though it had followed him there. “It’s eel. It’s my mother’s. It’s for the road, or for my wake, she said, whichever comes first, and I don’t think she was entirely joking, though it’s hard to tell with her. Would you—later, I mean. If we’re both—it’s a big pie.”

“I can see that,” said Ilse.

“Road and rain,” said someone behind her. “Paper-walls bring *pies* now?”

The girl leaning against the far pillar of the arch was brown-skinned and freckled, wiry as a whip, with black hair in dozens of long braids. Each braid was tied off with knotted cords in faded colours: red, ochre, a blue gone almost white. She wore a leather jerkin, a skinning knife at her belt and a sling looped round one wrist. She was tossing a smooth grey sling-stone up and catching it, up and catching it, without looking at it.

“It’s a very good pie,” said Teo, with some dignity.

“Smells good. Margin’ll want it too. Hey-ho.” She nodded at Ilse. “Cass. Tenter wagon. You lot ever been out there? Past your nice paper walls?”

“No,” said Ilse.

“No,” said Teo, with feeling.

“Hey-ho.” Cass caught the stone and grinned, all teeth. “Going to be a long day for somebody.”

Through the arch behind them, out of the fog, came a sound of chain.

Four students passed through the lantern-light in a bunch, walking fast, collars up, one with a coiled chain over her shoulder and one with a levelglass held out in front of him like a man carrying a full cup. One was reciting under his breath, “—three chains, twelve, three chains, thirteen—” and none of them looked at the Walkers at all. Behind them came another party, and then another, out of the dark of the Old Court and through the Gate and on into the fog toward the Drift Wing, chains clinking, lanterns bobbing, the low voices counting. Ilse heard a bell ringing somewhere behind them in the Held Side, a single heavy note, then another.

“Morning Survey,” said Yara, in a reverent whisper. “They do it every day. Every single day. Can you imagine?”

Ilse could. She watched the lanterns go out along the causeway one by one into the grey, and thought of the window sliding along the dark, and of what it would take to get up every morning of your life before dawn and walk a building back into its place.

“Walkers,” said a voice. “Here.”


* * *


Field-Surveyor Odile Brask came out of the fog on the Drift side of the arch, which was the wrong side to come from, and did not look as though she cared.

She was tall and rawboned, with white hair cropped close and a face the weather had been working on for sixty years. A black leather patch covered her right eye. Her left hand hung at her side with the fingers half-curled, and the skin of it was grey: not pale, not bloodless, but grey right through, the colour of slate, from the fingertips to somewhere under her cuff. She wore no glove on it. In her right hand she carried a brass-shod staff marked down its length in links, and leaned on it as if it had been made for her.

She looked at them. The one eye went along the line of nineteen and came back, unhurried, the way Ilse would look along a line of pins to see which one was out of true.

“This is the Long Lie,” she said, and lifted the staff an inch. “It is one rod exactly, and it is the only honest thing in the building, and it lies, because it’s held. I mention it so you’ll know where it is if I hit you with it.” She leaned on it again. “You’ve come to Walk. Fine. Here is the Walk. Go to the cairn. Come back. Bring a true survey. Don’t die. It’s paperwork.”

Somebody laughed nervously. Brask let the laugh go out into the fog and die there by itself.

“The cairn is three miles southwest, as the crow flies, which you aren’t. It is a heap of stones with a College benchmark on top and a crow, usually, who is not your friend. Survey your route there and back by chain and sight, draft it and attest it under this arch. You may carry one chain, one levelglass, one sightvane, one plane-table board and one water flask. Anything else you carry is your own affair. Food is not an instrument. Eat what you like.” Her eye rested briefly on Teo’s pie. “Within reason. No assistance from anyone who is not a Walker. No horses. No maps but your own. You have until dusk tomorrow. A sheet that comes in after the lantern’s lit is late, and a late sheet is a dead sheet, and I don’t care how pretty it is.”

She shifted her weight on the staff.

“Some of you aren’t levelled. You’ll find out when you attest. Nothing will happen, and you’ll have had a nice walk. The Margin is not trying to kill you. It is not trying anything. Ground out there is about as sure of itself as a drunk on a stair, and it will lie to your chain and your eyes and, if you let it, your feet. If you get lost, sit down. Don’t follow lights. Don’t follow voices. Don’t follow anyone who says they’ve found a shortcut. There are no shortcuts. If there were, I’d have found them.”

“And if we see a hollowing?” said the boy with the new chain, in a high voice.

Brask looked at him for a long moment.

“Then you’ll have something to tell your grandchildren, Walker,” she said, “if you’re careful. Any other questions? No. Good.”

Someone had come out of the Held Side behind them while she spoke: an old man in a master’s gown of dark green standing just inside the arch, smiling at them all as if they were his own grandchildren and had come a very long way to visit.

He was bald, with a short white beard trimmed neat, and half-moon spectacles that caught the lantern-light. His face was round and lined in all the places where people line from smiling. He wore grey kid gloves, fine as a second skin; Ilse, who looked at how things were made before she looked at anything else, noticed that the stitching at the fingers was too small to count. Grey gloves. For half a breath the Hobbet cheesewife was in her ear, *ever so polite*, and then she let it go; half the gentlemen in the Reach wore grey kid, and this one had a face like a Lisk baker’s.

“Field-Surveyor,” he said to Brask, pleasantly.

“Master Hallam.” Brask did not move. “Come to see them off?”

“I always do, Odile. You know I always do.” He came forward among them. He touched the shoulder of the boy with the new chain and said a word to him that made him nod hard, twice. He came to Yara and asked her where she was from and said, “Holm! Good dairy country. Held like a church, Holm,” so that Yara went pink with delight. He came to Teo and looked at the pie and his eyes crinkled behind the spectacles. “Is that Lisk eel? Then you will certainly come back to us, my boy. The Margin could never digest it.”

Teo laughed, surprised.

Then he came to Ilse. He looked at her face for a moment, kindly and with interest, and at her bag, and at the ink stain on her right forefinger that no lye had ever shifted.

“You’re a long way from home, I think, my dear.”

“Fallowmarch, sir.”

“Ah. Good straight hedges, the Fallowmarch.” He smiled. “She’s a wide old country, the Reach, and there’s not a field of her that isn’t loved by someone. Remember that, out there. The Ninefold said it at the Holding: *we do not ask the ground to love us, only to know us.*” He laid a gloved hand on her shoulder, light, warm through the wool. “Walk true, and come home.”

“Thank you, sir,” said Ilse, and found to her surprise that she meant it.

He moved on. She looked up, without quite knowing why, at the gallery.

A stone gallery was cut into the inner face of the arch, and it was full of students. Patent first-years, she thought; they had the scrubbed, anxious, pleased look of people at the start of something. They leaned on the balustrade and looked down at the Walkers like punters at a fair sizing up the runners.

He was at the end of the row, a little apart. His hair was combed. His coat was not grey-blue anymore but black, a student’s plain black with a white stock, and on his right hand was an iron ring. He was looking down at her already. He had perhaps been looking down at her since before she looked up.

She saw him understand. She saw it go across his face, small and exact, like a line ruled in ink. *Of course. Privilege. The Walk.* His mouth went tight at one corner. Then he did a thing she didn’t expect. He inclined his head to her, very slightly, as one duellist acknowledges another across a field before they begin.

She didn’t return it. She looked away, and found that Brask’s one eye was on her.

It had gone from Ilse to the gallery and back again. It had seen the whole thing. Brask didn’t say anything for a moment. Then she said, “Friend of yours, Walker?”

“No.”

“Good,” said Brask. “Friends are heavy.” She turned away and lifted the Long Lie and brought it down once on the flagstones of the arch, a flat hard crack that went out into the fog and didn’t come back. “Walkers. Go.”

They went, nineteen in a ragged line, out from under the arch and down the worn steps to the path off the headland. Ilse went with them. Teo was on her right, clutching his pie. Cass was on her left, the sling-stone going up and down, up and down. Ahead of them the fog stood waiting, soft and grey and absolutely blank, like a sheet before the first line.

She walked out of the lantern’s light, and into it.


## Chapter 4: The Walk

The first thing the Margin did was move her pin.

It was an iron chaining-arrow, one of the ten on a ring at the end of her chain, and she had pushed it into the turf at station two not twenty minutes before. Now she stood at station three, sixty-four links on, with her eye to the sightvane. The rag she had tied to the arrow was there in the hairline, fluttering, in the wrong place. The bearing she had taken forward from station two and the back-sight disagreed by a degree and a half.

Ilse straightened and looked back. Nothing had happened. The heath lay as it had lain, heather and tussock grass silvered with fog, a few gorse bushes hunched like men in cloaks. Far behind her, faint, the bell of the Meridian rang the hour.

She walked back and re-chained the leg. Sixty links, not sixty-four.

“Bloody hell,” she said to the heath, which didn’t care.

The fog had lifted an hour after the Gate into a white, sourceless brightness, so that the Margin lay under a lid of light like the inside of a shell. Nineteen Walkers had come down off the headland together and spread like a handful of seed. Now Ilse could see only two: Oskar Fell, walking fast and straight and alone, already small, and the giant from Lisk, labouring along a ridge to the south with his pie.

She had been doing it properly. That was the galling part. She had begun at the benchmark post at the foot of the headland steps, held as hard as a church door, oriented her board by back-sight to the lantern on the Gate, and chained the way Quince had taught her. Peg the follower end, walk out the hundred links, pin the leader, walk back, pull the follower. Sight. Plot. Every leg checked twice. It was work that held like iron in the Fallowmarch.

It was also slow, and the Margin did not hold still to be measured. It moved while she was walking back for the other end of her own chain.

She made herself think instead of swearing. The ground had not jumped. It had *crept*, in the time it took her to measure it. So the answer was not to measure better, but faster than it crept. Short legs, then. Thirty links, forty at most. Take the bearing forward and the back-sight immediately, within a minute of each other, before the ground had time to change its mind. Close a little triangle on some third mark at every second station so that she could see her error and how big it was. And write down the time. If the error grew with time, that was the ground creeping. If it didn’t, that was her.

It was hideous work, half running, the chain snaking in the heather. But at the next station the back-sight was out by a quarter of a degree, and at the one after that by less, and she plotted the stations in a tight little zigzag with the minute beside each one.

At station eleven she noticed that the ground was breathing.

She stood still and paid attention. The heath rose. It rose perhaps an inch, all of it, heather and stones and the little pool of rainwater beside her, whose water crept up one bank and down the other. Then it sank. The pause between was longer than her own breath. Rise, and hold, and sink. The level in her ears went with it, thinning and thickening, a sound she had never heard it make.

“Ground’s leaning,” said a voice. “Can’t you feel it?”

It was the Tenting girl, Cass, coming along the heath at a lope with her chain coiled on one shoulder, looking at nothing in particular, the way a dog looks at nothing when it is listening.

“I can feel it breathing,” said Ilse.

“That’s nothing. That’s just the Margin sleeping.” Cass squinted at Ilse’s board and whistled through her teeth. “Pretty. You lot draw like your mothers were looking. But you’re fighting it. It’s leaning *that* way.” She pointed southwest without looking. “Everything here’s sliding off toward the Drift, slow, like a plate on a tilted table. You chain across the lean, it fights you. Chain with it, it don’t notice you.”

“That’s not how a survey works.”

“That’s how *ground* works, paper-wall. Ground don’t care what you wrote on it.” Cass went on, stepping her distances, crouching now and then to lay a palm on the turf, and always going a little left or right of where Ilse would have gone, and always, Ilse saw with intense irritation, onto firmer ground.

She closed her eyes and leaned her attention into the ground the way she leaned it into a charter. And there, under the breathing, so faint she might have imagined it, was a lean: a slope in the level itself, the way a floor in an old house runs gently downhill toward one corner. It ran southwest.

She opened her eyes and laid out her next leg along it. The back-sight came in out by nothing at all.

“Bloody hell,” said Ilse again, in a different voice entirely, and picked up her chain.


* * *


The giant from Lisk was sitting on the bank of a creek with his head in his hands.

She came on him a little before noon, where a salt creek crossed her line between banks of grey mud fringed with samphire. It was perhaps thirty links across. The pie sat beside him on a tussock in its cloth, like a guest.

“Don’t look at it,” he said, as she came up. “I mean—sorry. Hello. You can look at it. I can’t. It gets wider.”

“What does?”

“The creek.” His face was grey under the sunburn. “When I got here it was twelve links. I chained it. Then I looked up to sight the far bank and it was eighteen. Then I looked down at my board and back up and it was twenty-two, and the far bank keeps going *away*, and the ground is going up and down, and I don’t do well with up and down. Or away. I’m sorry. I think I’d better sit down for a while.”

As she watched, the far bank went back a little further, the mud shining as the water spread to fill the gap.

The sensible thing was to leave him. She was thinking that quite clearly, in Quince’s voice, when she heard herself say, “How fast?”

Teo blinked at her. “What?”

“How fast is it widening? You chained it at twelve. When?”

“At—” He looked at the sky, then at nothing, and his face changed, like a drowning man’s who has found the bottom with his toe. “At eleven and a quarter, by my watch. It was eighteen links at eleven-twenty. Twenty-two at about eleven twenty-six. So that’s six links in five minutes and then four in six, which isn’t linear, it’s slowing, unless it isn’t, unless it comes in—oh.” He sat up straight. “It comes in breaths. Doesn’t it? It goes with the up and down. It widens on the rise and then it comes back a little on the fall, only not as much as it went, so the whole thing creeps out. Like a pastry rising. Like—sorry. Like a pastry.”

“Count it,” said Ilse. “Count me a breath.”

He counted, staring at his own knees. The ground rose under them, and held, and sank. “Forty-one seconds,” he said. “Forty-one and a bit. And the creek closes up, it closes about three links on the fall, I think, give or take.”

“Then we cross on the fall.” She was uncoiling her chain. “I take the leader end and wade. You hold the follower and count. When I’m over, I pin my end and you come across along the chain. Don’t look at the bank. Look at the links and read them off, and that’s the crossing measured by both of us, and nobody can say we didn’t.”

“I’m not sure that’s allowed,” said Teo. “No assistance from non-Walkers—”

“We’re all Walkers,” said Ilse. “A chain’s got two ends for a reason.”

He looked at her. Then he got up, hands shaking, and took the follower end of her chain in both fists.

“Road and rain,” said Cass, from behind them. “Two paper-walls drowning in a ditch.”

She was sitting on a tussock twenty yards up the bank, eating an apple. Ilse had no idea how long she had been there.

“If you’ve something useful to say,” said Ilse, “say it.”

Cass chewed, and seemed to consider whether she wanted to say it to them. Then she pointed upstream with the apple.

“You’re at the wide bit,” she said. “Course you are. Paper-walls always go where it’s *narrow*, never where it’s *narrowing*. Up there, forty yards, the ground’s leaning in from both sides. Banks are pulling together, not apart. Go cross there. You’ll get your feet wet. Won’t get your feet wet up to your neck.”

It was true. At the bend the creek was eleven links across, and on the fall of the ground’s slow breath the far bank came *toward* her, a hand’s breadth, then another.

She waded on the fall with the leader end, the water cold to her thighs, and the far bank stayed where it was. She pinned the chain and shouted, and Teo came across, eyes fixed on the links, reading them off in a high cracked voice, “—four, five, six, seven—” all the way, and when he stepped up onto the far bank he sat straight down again in the samphire and put both hands flat on the ground and laughed.

“Eleven links and nine,” he said. “Eleven and nine. I’ll never forget it. I’m going to have it carved on something.”

Cass came over last, not wading at all. She stepped from a stone to a hummock to a stone, three steps, and landed light beside them.

“Hey-ho,” she said. “Now you owe me.”


* * *


They didn’t discuss it. They simply went on together, the three of them.

Teo took the leader end because his legs were longest, and Ilse took the follower and read, and Cass ranged ahead with her nose in the wind and called back the lean. All three sighted and plotted at every station, each on their own board, in three very different hands. Ilse’s was small and exact. Teo’s numbers were beautiful and his lines a little wobbly. Cass’s board looked as though a crow had walked across it after a fight, and was, when Ilse glanced at it, broadly right.

The fog came back in the middle of the afternoon.

It came up off the salt-pools, low and thick, until they were walking in a white room twenty yards across that moved with them. Their voices came back flat. The clink of the chain seemed to come from the left.

Then Teo stopped dead, with the chain going taut in Ilse’s hand.

“Do you hear that?”

“Hear what?”

“I don’t *hear* it.” He had his head on one side. “Sorry, that’s a stupid thing to say. I feel it. In my feet. Like standing on the bridge at Lisk when a barge goes under. But lower.”

She stood still, and then she felt it too.

It came up through her boots into the long bones of her legs: a sound too low to be a sound, a vast slow thrumming that climbed and sank. It had a tune, almost. Her teeth hummed with it. The pool beside them shivered in rings.

Cass had gone very still, ahead of them in the fog. She turned her head toward the south.

“Oh,” she said, very softly. “Oh, there you are.”

And the fog parted all at once, like a curtain pulled. Two hundred yards off there was a hill.

It was an ordinary hill: a long whaleback of heather and grey stone with a stand of thorn trees near the crest. She had sighted on it before the fog came down.

The hill began to rise.

It swelled. The whole long back of it lifted, as the sea lifts under a moored boat, and the heather on its flanks rippled and parted. Then the turf began to slide. It slid off in sheets and slabs, peeling away like the skin off hot milk, and stones went rolling and bounding down in little avalanches. Underneath was grey. It was a grey like wet slate, enormous and smooth and gleaming, scarred with old pale weals and crusted along the ridge with flints and roots and the white shells of creatures she didn’t know. The thorn trees rode on top of it. They leaned, their roots tearing out in long pale threads, and toppled, and slid away down the grey slope like toys off a tilted tray.

It came up, and up, longer than the whole of Hobbet market square. Earth gone liquid sheeted off its flanks in brown falls that steamed in the cold air. It rose in a slow arc and then, just as unhurried, began to roll. As it rolled, a seam opened in the grey hide near what must have been its front.

It was an eye.

The eye was the size of a cartwheel. It was dark and wet and very deep, dark the way a well is dark, with a ring of pale like frost around it. It turned in its socket, slow as a millstone. It turned and found the three of them standing on the heath with their chain between them, and it looked at them. She had been looked at by Kasimir Reyne over a hedge and by Brask under the Lantern Gate, and she knew what being looked at was. This was that, from so far down that she felt it in the soles of her feet.

Then the eye closed, the seam sealed, and the great grey back went on rolling over and down. The hill closed over it. It closed like water over a diver, heather and stone and turf running together, folding in, smoothing over, and a long ripple went out from it across the heath in rings, one after another. The rings came toward them. The ground under Ilse’s feet rose a foot, gently, and held, and set her down again, and the next ring did the same, and the next, smaller each time, until they stopped.

The hill was a hill again. It was a little lower than it had been. The thorn trees were gone.

The song went on for a while under their feet, going away south. Then that was gone too.

Ilse realized she couldn’t hear the level.

It wasn’t like the Little Wick lane. That had been a cut, a note stopped dead. This was a silence of a different kind, the way birds go silent when a hawk goes over. The level was still there, she thought. It had simply stopped speaking, as though nothing in the world were fixed enough to register beside what had just gone by.

Cass had knelt down in the heather. She put both palms flat on the ground and bent her head and said a few words in a language Ilse didn’t know, a soft run of words with the shape of a blessing. Then, in plain Reach speech, very quietly: “Go easy, old one. Road and rain go with you.”

Teo was crying. He wasn’t making any noise about it. He stood with the chain hanging from one big hand and tears running down into his poor beard, looking at the place where the hill had opened, and he didn’t wipe them away.

“A sounder,” he said at last. “My grandfather saw one. He said it was the size of a church, and nobody believed him.” He sniffed enormously. “It was bigger than a church.”

Ilse found that her right hand was gripping the edge of her board so hard her knuckles had whitened. She made it let go. She looked down at her neat zigzag of stations, and at the place where she had plotted that hill an hour ago, carefully, in brown ink. It had been an animal all along. She had drawn it as though it would stay.

The level came back like feeling into a foot you’ve sat on, stinging.


* * *


Yara called to them half an hour later.

They had re-chained the stretch where the ripples went through, because every station had moved, and were working along the edge of a grey mere when a voice came out of the fog to their right, bright and close and laughing.

“Halloo! Is that you lot? It’s Yara! There’s a dry way round the mere, it’s firm as a floor, I’ve found a shortcut—come on, it saves a mile at least—”

“Yara!” Ilse turned toward the voice. “Wait. Wait there. Brask said—”

Nothing.

“Yara?”

The fog moved a little, and showed nothing. The water lapped at the mud. Cass had put a hand on Ilse’s arm, hard.

“Don’t,” Cass said.

“She was right there.”

“She was. Now she ain’t.” Cass’s face had gone closed and still. “You heard what the one-eyed woman said. Don’t follow voices.”

“It was *her* voice.”

“Yes,” said Cass. “That’s what makes it a voice.”

They called until their throats were raw, Teo’s great voice booming over the mere and coming back flat. Nobody answered. When at last they went on, Ilse looked back twice, and the second time she thought she saw a shape in the fog, round-shouldered and cheerful, walking away from them along a path that wasn’t there. Then it wasn’t anything.


* * *


The cairn stood on a knoll above the heath, and they reached it as the light was going.

It was a rough cone of grey stones taller than a man, with an iron post rising from it and a brass plate cut with a benchmark arrow and the words MERIDIAN — WALK CAIRN — HELD. On top sat a grey-necked crow as big as a hen, building a cairn of its own out of pebbles. It placed each one, considered it, adjusted it, and looked at the three of them with a bright, unimpressed eye.

“Evening,” said Teo to the crow, politely.

The crow put down another pebble.

Ilse closed her traverse on the brass arrow by the last of the light. A hundred and seventy-one stations, through a Margin that crept and breathed and had once stood up and looked at her, and her closing error was nine links in three miles. She checked it three times and felt something warm and fierce come up under her breastbone, and said nothing about it to anyone.

They camped in a hollow on the lee of the knoll. Other Walkers’ fires showed here and there around the cairn, small and orange. Teo built theirs with tremendous care, and then unwrapped the pie.

It had survived, battered on one side, its crust glazed brown and crimped all round like rope. He cut it with Cass’s skinning knife into exact sixths, and then cut the sixths into twelfths, so there would be more.

It was eel, and onion, and something green, and pepper, and a jelly that had set in the cold. It was the best thing Ilse had ever eaten.

“My mother’s,” said Teo, with his mouth full. “She makes forty on a market day. Forty-one, if it’s a good day. I wanted it to be a good day.”

“It was,” said Cass. She lay on her back looking up. The fog had gone, and there were more stars than Ilse had ever seen over the Fallowmarch. After a while Cass began to sing, low, more talked than sung.

*The road to Corrow goes by the high stone*
*when the moon is new,*
*and the low ford when it’s old,*
*and the white thorn when it’s neither,*
*and you’ll know the white thorn*
*by the crow that sits on it*
*and the crow by the thorn.*

It went on like that for some time, verse after verse, a road that went one way in one season and another way in the next. Teo lay back and listened with his hands folded on his belly. Ilse sat with her arms round her knees.
