A map that moved. A route written down not as where a thing was, but as what it did. She turned the idea over once, as she would a stone the plough had brought up, and put it away. Roads didn’t move. Held roads didn’t, anyway. That was what a road *was*.

“What’s that for?” she said, when Cass stopped.

“For? For getting to Corrow.” Cass turned her head and looked at her. “Why’d you come, Fallowmarch?”

“Why did you?”

“Nan told me to. My grandmother. She said, go and learn what the paper-walls know, and then come home and tell us.” Cass grinned at the stars. “I think she wanted to know if they knew anything at all.”

“I failed the Patent,” said Teo, to the fire. “Twice. I know the whole Mensuration syllabus backward, but they put you in a hall with two hundred desks and a very loud clock, and my hand—” He held it up and looked at it. “So I thought, well. There aren’t any desks on the Walk. My mother said I was the stubbornest fool in Lisk. I think she was pleased.”

They both looked at Ilse.

“I wanted to be a surveyor,” she said.

Cass laughed. It was a short, warm, delighted laugh, and it went up into the dark like a spark.

“Liar,” she said. “Nice one, though.”

Ilse didn’t answer. After a while Teo began to snore, softly, like a kettle a long way off. Cass turned on her side with her braids across her face. The fire burned down. The crow on the cairn above them had gone to sleep, or was pretending to.

Ilse lay awake on the hard ground with her father’s coat over her and her board under her head. She listened to the Margin. Under her back, very slowly, it rose, and held, and sank. It rose again. It was not trying to do anything, Brask had said. It was only breathing, as a sleeper breathes. Ilse lay and listened to it and did not sleep for a long time, and she could not have said, if anyone had asked her, whether what she felt was fear.


## Chapter 5: A True Survey

In the morning the way home was somewhere else.

Ilse sat up under her father’s coat in the grey first light with dew on her face and looked down from the knoll the way they had come, and the land was wrong. There was the heath, and the long pale gleam of the mere, and the low whaleback hill where the sounder had gone down. But the mere lay further west than it had. The hill was on the wrong side of it. And the salt creek, which should have been a brown thread winding in from the left, wasn’t anywhere.

“Hey-ho,” said Cass, sitting up beside her with her braids full of heather. She looked at the view and yawned enormously. “Ground’s been walking.”

“All of it?”

“All of it that wanted to.” Cass stretched. “Told you. It leans. It leaned all night.”

Teo woke last and saw it and lay back down again and put his arm over his eyes.

On the cairn the crow stood on its finished tower of pebbles, looking pleased with itself. The cairn had not moved at all. When Ilse laid her levelglass on its base the bead of quicksilver sat dead centre, as steady as a seed pearl. Held. The College had put its mark on this one heap of stone out in the half-world and the Margin had flowed round it all night like water round a pier.

So there was that, at least. Her traverse closed on something that stayed put.

They ate the last of the pie for breakfast, three twelfths, cold. Then they sat in a row on the cairn’s lee side with their boards on their knees and looked at what they had.

“The first mile’s all right,” said Teo, comparing their boards with his big careful finger. “Near the Held ground it hardly moves. We can check it and annotate the creep. But from the creek on—” He made a small helpless gesture at the landscape. “That’s all gone somewhere. We’ll have to do the whole back half again. Coming home.”

“A hundred and ten stations,” said Ilse.

“A hundred and eleven. And we’ve until dusk. And I don’t want to be a nuisance,” said Teo, “but I would point out that we’ve also got no pie.”


* * *


The way back was worse than the way out.

It was the same heath, the same slow lean toward the southwest that Ilse could feel now without trying, like a draught along the floor. But every leg they chained was a leg they had chained the day before, lying in a slightly different place, so that the morning had the maddening quality of copying out a letter you had already written, from memory, with the words rearranged.

Short legs, quick sights, triangles closed every second station. They found the salt creek again an hour before noon, nearly a furlong east of where it had been, wide and lazy and only knee-deep now, as if it had exhausted itself. They found the mere where Yara had called out of the fog. Nobody said anything about that. Ilse looked for footprints in the mud along its edge and found plenty, and none of them meant anything.

The bell of the Meridian rang noon, faint across the heath, and then one o’clock, and two.

At a quarter past two they came to a stream.

It was nothing much: a peaty brook a yard wide between cut banks of black earth, with a stone in the middle you could step on. She had chained across it the day before without a second thought. Today it was a few rods west of where it had been, like everything else, and they chained across it again, and Ilse plotted, and closed the triangle.

The triangle didn’t close.

She re-took the sight. She made Teo re-chain the leg. She made Cass stand on the stream stone with the ranging-rod while she sighted from both sides. The numbers came back the same each time, and wrong. Between the station on this bank and the station on that, the stream bank was fourteen links out from where every other measurement said it should be. Not four links, which she could have called creep. Fourteen. Nine feet and a quarter, as though the ground there simply hadn’t agreed with itself about where it was.

“Huh,” said Cass. She crouched by the water with her palm on the earth, frowning. “It’s—huh. It’s leaning both ways here. That’s queer.”

“It can’t lean both ways.”

“Well, it is.” Cass stood and wiped her hand on her jerkin. “Ground does what ground does.”

Teo was writing in his field-book, steadily, in his round clear numbers. He wrote the fourteen links down. He drew a little box round it. Beside it he wrote *misclose — stream — cause unknown — ground leaning (Cass) — re-chained x3*, and then a careful line underneath, and he looked up at Ilse and said, “That’s all right. We’ll just record it. It’ll be a bit ugly, but—”

“Yes,” said Ilse.

She looked at her board.

It was beautiful. She could admit that, on her heels on the wet bank with her eyes gritty from two days of squinting through a sightvane. Two hundred and thirty stations plotted in fine brown ink at ten chains to the inch, the route crossing the Margin in one long confident line that bent where the ground had made it bend. It was the best survey she had ever drawn, of the strangest country she had ever seen.

And there, across the middle of it, sat the stream, with its banks fourteen links apart from themselves.

She knew what it would look like drawn. Two lines that didn’t meet, a stutter in the long clean run, and a note saying, in effect, *I don’t know.* She had seen surveys like that in the Hobbet parish chest, made by honest men with shaking hands, with notes that said *uncertain* and *as near as may be*. They were the weakest charters in the chest. The ground hardly bothered to believe them.

The bank was *there*. She could see it was there. Any fool could see it ran straight, on from the bank above and the bank below, a little curve of black peat that ought to sit, by every station on either side of it, just so. The fourteen links weren’t the bank. They were the ground having a bad moment, the way the Long Field’s well-head had ground its stones crooked in the night. They were an error. And an error, unrecorded, was only a gap; but an error *corrected* was a truth told better.

*A good lie is ninety-nine parts truth*, Quince said, in the back room of her head. *The hundredth part’s just tidying.*

She took out her little knife and scraped the ink of the bank-line gently off the paper, along an inch of its length, so lightly the surface didn’t roughen. She blew the shavings off. She dipped her pen. She drew the bank where it ought to be, in a single unbroken stroke, and she drew the stream between its banks running clean and narrow, and she joined the stations on either side of it to the new line without a seam. She did it in less than a minute. Her hand didn’t shake once. When she sat back the board was whole and lovely and the route ran across the Margin without a stutter, and the stream lay in it like a vein in a leaf.

It felt like the right thing. It felt like making the sheet tell the truth.

“There,” she said, and stood up, and rolled her shoulders. “Let’s go. We’ve two hours.”

Teo glanced at her board as she shouldered it. He looked at the stream, and then at his own sheet with the fourteen links in its careful box, and opened his mouth.

“I’ve drawn it as near as may be,” said Ilse.

“Oh,” said Teo. “Yes. Right.” He closed his book.

Cass, already ranging ahead, didn’t look back at all.


* * *


They came up the steps from the heath at four o’clock in the afternoon, with an hour and a half of daylight to spare, and the Lantern Gate was full of people.

She had pictured coming in to a clerk and a ledger. Instead the arch was crowded with students, and someone had brought out an urn of soup, and when the three of them came up over the top of the steps, filthy to the waist, a ragged cheer went up from the gallery. It was the kind of cheer people give for anyone who comes in, because they have been waiting and counting. But it went into Ilse like the first mouthful of hot tea, and she found she had to stop on the top step for a moment and look at her boots.

“Oh, *well done*. Well done, all three of you.”

Master Hallam came through the crowd toward them with both hands out. He took Teo’s hand first, in both his gloved ones, and shook it, and then Cass’s, though Cass looked at her hand afterward as if he might have put something in it. Then he took Ilse’s, and held it a moment, warm and light.

“Home again,” he said, “and the Margin no worse for you, and you no worse for her. Get some tea into you. Field-Surveyor Brask is in the Hall with the sheets that have come in; you’ll attest here first.”

“How many,” said Ilse. “Sir. How many have come in?”

“You make nine, my dear. Nine of nineteen, and the day not done. That’s a good year.” He smiled, and the smile was not quite as easy as before. “We’ll hope for a better one by dusk.”

Teo was staring at Hallam’s hands. He had been since the handshake, Ilse realized, with the open, worried look he gave to anything he thought might be broken. He turned pink when Hallam noticed.

“I’m sorry, sir. It’s just—your gloves, sir. I wondered. Are your hands cold? Only my grandmother has the joint-ache and she says kid’s no good for it, she says you want lambswool—”

Hallam laughed. He looked down at his own hands, the fine grey kid, and flexed the fingers once, as a man does when he is reminded of an old ache.

“Old Sallow frostbite, my boy,” he said. “Twenty years ago, and more, and they still complain when the wind’s off the sea. Your grandmother is quite right about lambswool. I shall tell my glover.” He patted Teo’s arm. “Go on. Attest.”

They attested under the arch, one at a time, at a stone lectern set in the middle of the passage where the light from the lantern fell. Teo went first and came away shaking. Cass went second and signed her sheet with a quick fierce scrawl like a bird’s track in mud and came away looking at nothing.

Then Ilse laid her board on the lectern.

She took up the College pen they gave her. And there, with the nib over the corner of the sheet, she stopped.

Her hand knew what it wanted to do. It wanted to make the wren’s foot, three lines and a spur, as it had on thirty-one charters in three years. It wanted to reach for the blind afterward and lay the dead man’s mark underneath, line for line, and let him carry it. The bottle was sewn into the hem of the coat she was wearing. She could feel its little weight against the back of her knee.

She signed with neither. She wrote *I. Marrow* in plain capitals, the way a child signs a slate, and set her intent on it, and leaned.

The thread went out.

She felt it go from her hand down through the lectern and the flags of the arch and out across the heath, a long fine line running southwest the way they had come, through a hundred and seventy-one stations to a cairn with a crow on top. It was not like the Tull hedge. There was no strain and no give, no hedge to move. The Margin did not fight her. It merely let her lie on it, lightly. Only in one place, two-thirds of the way along, the thread caught, like a stitch on a splinter. It caught, and pulled, and settled.

The ache came up into her knuckles, faint, and stayed.


* * *


The Hall of Instruments was long and high and full of brass.

Sightvanes as long as oars hung on the walls. Glass cases held chains that had belonged to the Ninefold, their links worn thin as wire. The floor was laid in tiles each painted with a fragment of map, so that you crossed a jumbled Reach of coastlines and rivers to reach the long table under the windows where Brask sat with the sheets.

She had a lens screwed into her one good eye like a jeweller, and she was bent so low over the sheet in front of her that her cropped white head nearly touched it. The Long Lie leaned against the table beside her. Her grey hand lay curled on the corner of the sheet, holding it flat.

Hallam stood a little behind her with his hands folded. The other Walkers sat on benches along the wall in various states of collapse; the boy with the new chain had fallen asleep sitting up.

They put the three sheets down in front of her side by side: Teo’s, Cass’s, Ilse’s.

Brask looked at Teo’s first. She went down it with the lens, station by station, and made small marks with a pencil on a slip of paper beside it. She stopped at the box Teo had drawn round the fourteen links and read his note. Her mouth moved slightly.

“Ground leaning,” she said. “Cass. Who’s Cass?”

“Me,” said Cass.

Brask grunted. She went on. At the end she sat back and took the lens out and rubbed her eye with the back of her good hand.

“Pass,” she said. “Narrowly. Your lines wander, Lisk. Your numbers don’t. Next time make them agree.”

Teo made a sound like a kettle coming off the boil, and sat down very suddenly on the end of the bench.

Cass’s sheet was ugly; there was no getting away from it. The stations were stabbed into the paper rather than drawn, and in one place she had run out of ink and finished in pencil. But Brask went down it with the lens and her pencil and at the end she grunted again, a different grunt.

“True,” she said. “Hideous. True. Pass.”

Cass grinned all over her face and didn’t say anything at all.

Then Brask drew Ilse’s sheet in front of her and screwed the lens back in.

She went down it as she had the others, station by station. Ilse stood with her hands behind her back and watched the cropped white head move along the line. Out from the headland. The first creek. The long stretch past the mere, the sounder’s hill. The cairn, and the turn. Back. The lens moved steadily, the pencil made its small marks. Then it reached the stream, and stopped.

It stayed stopped.

Brask did not move. She did not make a mark. She sat bent over the sheet with the lens to her eye and her grey hand flat beside it, and the Hall of Instruments was very quiet. The boy on the bench snored once. A clock on the wall ticked. Outside the long windows the light was going amber over the heath. Brask reached out with her good hand, without looking, and drew Teo’s sheet across beside Ilse’s, and laid them edge to edge, so that the two streams sat side by side. One had its banks fourteen links apart from themselves, boxed and annotated. The other was whole and clean and drawn in a single unbroken line.

She looked from one to the other. She looked for a long time.

Ilse kept her hands behind her back. She kept her face still. In the back room of her head she was running, as she had run all her life, through every answer to every question that might come next. None came. Hallam, behind Brask, glanced at Ilse over his spectacles and smiled at her, encouragingly, as if to say *she’s always like this*.

At last Brask took the lens out of her eye. She did not look up.

“True enough,” she said.

She made a mark on the slip, and pushed the sheet across to the clerk, and drew the next sheet toward her. That was all.


* * *


The last Walkers came in with the dusk.

Two more passed: a quiet girl from the Salt Steps and a man of about thirty from the Ammonite Hills with a broken nose. Several failed. One had a survey so short it was clear he had turned back at the first creek and walked in circles. Another had a lovely sheet that, when Brask held it up to the window, simply wasn’t attested. There was no hold in it at all, and the boy who had drawn it looked at it as though it had died in his hands.

At half past six the clerk lit the lamps in the Hall, and out at the Gate someone tended the great lantern on the keystone, though it had never gone out. The sheet that came in after that would be late, and a late sheet was a dead sheet.

Eleven passed. Six failed.

Two had not come back. The clerk read the names off the ledger in a flat voice. Oskar Fell. Yara Penhallow.

Brask stood up.

She did it without hurry, using the table and the Long Lie, and she took her coat from the back of the chair and put it on, one-handed. Hallam made a small movement toward her.

“Odile. The wardens can go out at first light. It’s full dark in an hour. You’re—” He stopped, and changed what he had been going to say. “You’ve done this day already.”

“I’ve done this day nineteen times,” said Brask. “It doesn’t get shorter.” She took a lantern from the clerk’s table. “Fell walked straight. He’ll have gone straight on past the cairn, the fool, he’ll be in the Rushes by now. Penhallow—” She stopped. Her face did nothing at all. “Penhallow, I’ll look.”

She went out. Ilse went to the long windows and watched her go.

The heath was blue-black under a sky going green at the edges. Brask came out of the Gate below with the lantern in her good hand and the Long Lie in the crook of her stilled arm. She went down the steps to the heath and out across it, alone, the small yellow light swinging, getting smaller. She didn’t look back. After a while the light went over a rise and was gone, and there was nothing out there but the dark and the Margin breathing.


* * *


The steward found her in the Old Court an hour later.

He was a thin old man in a black gown, and he put the ring on her right hand himself. It was iron, plain and heavy and a little too big, with a single fine line inlaid across its face in brass: the meridian, Tessary to the Cape. He said, “Ilse Marrow, Member of the Meridian, Walk of twelve hundred and three,” in the voice of a man who had said it many hundreds of times, and wrote her name in a book, and went away.

She stood in the Old Court with the ring on her hand.

The quadrangle was lit by lamps on brackets, the honey-coloured stone gone gold in their light. The brass studs of the meridian line ran across the flags at her feet, catching the light, one every yard, from the far gate to the Lantern Gate and on into the dark. She looked down at the line in the stone and the line on the ring.

Member. They couldn’t touch her now. Not Pollard, not the Concordance, not the examiner with her lenses. The Court could seal every chest in the Fallowmarch and it wouldn’t matter. She was the College’s. The relief came up through her slowly, from the feet, warm, like sitting down at last. She put her other hand over the ring and held it.

“Congratulations, Miss Marrow.”

He had come across the court from the direction of the Library. He wore the student’s black coat and white stock, and his hair was combed, and on his right hand was the same iron ring with the same brass line. He stopped a careful two yards from her, the way he had stopped at the edge of his lantern-light at the hedge.

“Mr. Reyne,” said Ilse.

“That was a very fine survey. I saw it on the table.” He said it plainly, as a fact. “Brask does not say *true enough* to many people.”

“She said it to eleven.”

“Yes.” He inclined his head. “I wanted to tell you myself, rather than have you hear it from the Provost’s office. I thought it was only fair.” His grey-blue eyes were steady on her face. “This afternoon I wrote to the parish of Wick. I have asked that the Court’s inquiry into the Tull amendment be withdrawn from Tessary and forwarded instead to the Board of Errata here, as the proper authority over a Member of the College. The parish clerk will need to be told where to send the sheet.”

Ilse said nothing.

“The Board sits quarterly,” Kasimir said. “It is very thorough. I understand it is also very slow.” He paused. “Good night, Miss Marrow. And congratulations. I mean it.”

He went away across the court, between the lamps, over the brass line without stepping on it.

Ilse stood where she was. She looked down at the ring on her hand, iron and brass, too big for her finger. She had spent the Long Field on it. She had walked out into the Margin and come back and lied on the best sheet she had ever drawn. And it had bought her exactly what she had paid for, no more: not a pardon, not a door out, only a different courtroom and a longer road to it. A slower trial. She turned the ring once on her finger, and then again, and found that it was already warm.


## Chapter 6: Benchmarking

The Mess had stars on the ceiling and nobody looked at them.

Some long-dead artist had laid out the whole night sky across its blue vault in gold leaf, every constellation labelled, and two hundred students ate under it twice a day without raising their eyes. On the second morning after the Walk, the business was parties.

“It’s like a cattle fair,” said Teo unhappily. “My uncle sells heifers at Ambry Market and this is exactly how they look at them.”

He was right. The Patent first-years had spent their week sorting themselves, and sat in fours along the upper tables, heads together. Now and again one of them looked down the hall to where the eleven Walkers sat, and looked away.

“We need a fourth,” said Cass. She had her feet on the bench opposite and was eating porridge out of the bowl with her fingers, which was causing comment. “Proctor says. Party of four or they split you up and give you out like spare boots.”

“Then we’ll be given out,” said Ilse.

“I don’t want to be given out,” said Teo. “I want to be in this one. I’ve got used to this one.” He went pink. “If that’s—sorry. If that’s all right.”

In three days he had trodden on her, fed her, cried in front of her and walked across a widening creek on her chain, and Ilse found she didn’t want to be given out either. She didn’t say so. She looked at her porridge.

A tray came down on the table beside her with a crash that made the bowls jump.

“Right,” said the girl who had put it there, and sat. She was smaller even than Ilse, freckled all over, with fair hair scraped back so tight it looked painful. “Henrike Solberg. Hen. Salt Steps. I’m Patent but I’m scholarship, so they don’t count me, so I’ve been sitting up there for a week being not-counted, and frankly, the tide’s out on that one.” She pointed at Ilse with a spoon. “I watched you come in off the Walk. You looked like you knew what you were doing. And the others—” She jerked her head toward the upper tables. “The others looked like they wanted to marry each other. I’d rather be with you.”

“Hey-ho,” said Cass, delighted.

“I’m very good at Mensuration,” Hen added. “Ask anyone. Don’t ask anyone, they’ll only be rude. But I am.”

Then she laughed, a tremendous laugh much bigger than she was, the laugh of a salt-pan foreman’s daughter who had to be heard across a mile of flats in a gale. Heads turned up and down the Mess. Teo stared at her as though she had pulled a sounder out of her pocket.

“Teo,” he said. “Anselm. I’m very good at Mensuration too.”

“I know. You did the mean in your head at the Gate when the clerk got the Walk times wrong. I heard you.” Hen beamed at him. He went the colour of a brick.

The senior proctor, a tall fifth-year called Arnaud, read their rooms off a board without looking up. “Marrow, Anselm, Tenter, Solberg. Second Range, Wren Stair, top landing. Morning Survey stretch, the Wren Stair and the Long Gallery, three chains forty-one links. First Bell is an hour before dawn. If you lose your stair you pay the fine, and you will lose your stair.”

From the next table, where four Patent boys were sitting, there was a snort.

“The *Wren Stair*,” said one of them. “Oh, bad luck.”

“Why bad luck?” said Teo.

“Because it hops, link. Why do you think they call it that? It turned clean round twice last year.”

Ilse said nothing. She was thinking of three lines and a spur in the corner of a sheet in a sealed parish chest five hundred and sixty miles away. It was only a name.

The rooms were four small bedrooms off a sitting room at the top of a tall grey building that leaned, with a hearth, four mismatched chairs, a window over the heath and a wardrobe of black oak that had clearly been there since the Lost Wing. That night Ilse lay and listened to the building. It creaked. Once, very faintly, it groaned, like a ship’s timbers when the tide turns under them.

In the morning the wardrobe was a foot from the wall.

It stood with a clean rectangle of unfaded boards behind it, looking innocent. Teo stood in his nightshirt and considered it.

“Well,” he said. “That’s not ideal.”


* * *


The First Bell rang in the dark like someone dropping a cauldron down a well.

They had been up an hour already, because Hen insisted, with the instruments laid out by lamplight beside the charter of record for their stretch. It showed the stair coming down to the Gallery’s east door in a tight right-hand spiral of forty-one steps, and the Long Gallery running a chain and sixty links west to the double doors at the end.

“So,” said Hen, bright-eyed. “We measure it as it is. Then we draw it as it should be, and we sign, and if our numbers are good enough the building agrees with us and goes back.”

Ilse looked at the sheet. She looked at it for longer than she meant to.

“That’s—” she said.

“That’s what you lot call *holding*,” said Cass, pulling on her boots.

In Wick Fallow they had another word for it. Ilse kept it to herself.

They went down the stair with a lantern, counting, and at the bottom they stopped.

“It turns left,” said Teo.

It did, as neat and plain as if it always had. The stones of the newel were worn smooth on the wrong side.

“Oh, *no*,” said Teo. “It’s gone round in the night. My stomach knew. My stomach’s been saying so all the way down and I didn’t listen.”

“It’s still forty-one steps,” said Hen, who had counted. “That’s something.”

“It’s forty-one steps in the wrong *direction*—”

“Gallery,” said Ilse. “Gallery first. Stair after. The stair’s a nightmare; the gallery’s just long.”

The Long Gallery had windows on one side over the black heath and portraits on the other: dead Masters holding dividers, all looking faintly disappointed. Teo walked out the leader end while Ilse held the follower, and Hen held the lantern and the watch, and Cass trailed one hand along the wall.

“A chain,” called Teo from the dark. “And sixty. And—sixty-three. A chain and sixty-three links.”

“It should be sixty.”

“I know it should be sixty, but it’s sixty-three. It’s grown two feet in the night. Like bread.”

They did it again and got the same. Then the four of them stood in the middle of the Long Gallery at a quarter to six in the morning and argued.

Teo wanted to chain everything twice more and take the mean. Hen wanted to triangulate off the windows. Ilse wanted to start drafting before the building changed its mind again. Cass leaned against a dead Master and said nothing for a while. Then she said, “Ground’s leaning.”

“The ground’s *always* leaning,” said Ilse.

“Yeah. And right now it’s leaning west, toward the end doors. Whole thing’s sliding off toward the heath like a drawer coming out, and the stair’s got twisted coming with it.” Cass pushed off the wall. “Fix the gallery, the stair’ll come round by itself.”

They all looked at her.

“Twelve minutes to the Dawn Bell,” said Hen, looking at her watch, “and the Antechamber’s eight minutes away if we run, which we will.”

So Ilse drafted, standing, on the board against the wall, with Teo reading her the bearings and Hen the minutes. It was the work she knew best in the world: to measure a thing that was wrong and draw it the way it ought to be, so well that the world gave in. She drew from the numbers and from the lean under her boots, the gallery at a chain and sixty, the stair turning right. Teo checked her sums in his head and said “yes” and “yes,” and Hen said “four minutes,” and then they ran: along the causeway in the grey beginning of the light, through the Lantern Gate, across the Old Court over the brass meridian studs, and down a flight of stone steps into the cape itself.

The Register Antechamber was a long, low vault cut into the rock, lit by brass lamps on chains and smelling of old paper and ink. Folios bound in faded calf, thick as paving stones, ran back into the shadows. At a high desk by the door sat a thin, anxious man with ink stains up both cuffs to the wrist. “Merrin Coyle,” Hen breathed. “Registrar-Adjunct.” He had the Second Range folio open in front of him.

“Wren Stair,” he said, before they could speak. “Last in. You’ve—” he glanced at the clock above the door “—three minutes. Links sign with initials until Benchmarking. Here, here and here.” He turned the great book round and pointed with an inky finger. “Here, not in the Ranges. Master Hallam likes every sheet signed here. He’s very particular.”

“Why?” said Ilse.

The clerk blinked at her. “It’s the Register,” he said, as if that answered everything, and turned the book a little further toward her.

They signed, four sets of initials one under another. As Ilse set her intent on hers, she felt the faint familiar pull in her knuckles, and then, very far away, at the other end of the long causeway, something settle. It was like a door closing in another house.

The Dawn Bell rang.

Coyle closed the folio. “On time,” he said, and for the first time the beginnings of a smile crossed his anxious face. “Well. Nearly.”

They walked back in the first real light, with the sun coming up red behind the Held Side. Not everyone had been so lucky. A party of Lisk boys from the Third Range stood in a miserable huddle at the foot of a stair that went up eight feet and stopped at a blank wall. Further along, a door that should have opened onto a corridor stood wide on nothing but bright yellow gorse and a grazing wayhorn, which lifted its great branched head and looked at them in mild surprise.

The Wren Stair turned to the right. The Long Gallery was a chain and sixty links; Hen chained it again to be sure. Teo sat down on the bottom step and laughed until he had to wipe his eyes.


* * *


The Ossel Fair was held at the Verity Cross town gate on market days, and Hen took them, because nobody’s first week was complete without it.

The Tenting wagons stood in a line along the town wall, tall-wheeled, painted from axle to roof-ridge with route-diagrams in red and ochre and white that wound and branched and doubled back. Some had been repainted in a different direction, the old route showing faint beneath the new. The stalls sold glasswrack, coiled and ringing faintly in the wind like a wet finger round a glass, and wayhorn velvet, and pale curved plates of sounder-ivory that Teo stood before a long time without speaking.

And there were the ossels.

The sellers held them up to passing students and described them, loudly and confidently, and as they described them, they *changed*. “A fox!” cried a woman with a pipe, holding up a squirming handful. “A little red fox with a white tip to his tail!” And it was a little red fox, all at once, with a white tip to its tail. “A rabbit!” shouted a boy at the next stall, and his ossel went long-eared and brown in his hands. A Patent girl was bargaining for a marten while her friend said “No, a stoat, a white stoat,” and the poor thing blurred between the two like a reflection in disturbed water.

“That’s called flicker,” said Cass, watching it with a closed face. “Two of you saying two things. It hurts them.”

“Does it?” said Ilse.

“Course it does. How’d you like it? Ossels got no hold to speak of, so they go to whatever’s said about them loudest. They’ve their own shape, left alone. Nobody leaves them alone. You lot—” she said it without heat, as a fact “—you lot describe things into whatever you want, and then you call it buying.”

Ilse was looking back over her shoulder at the flickering marten-stoat when she stepped on something.

It was small and soft and it squeaked. She leapt back. “Sorry—*sorry*—”

At her feet, on the cobbles, was a creature.

That was all she could call it. It was the size of a teapot, grey-brown and indeterminate, mostly ears, like a hare drawn by someone who had heard of hares but never seen one. The rest was a vague furred shape that hadn’t decided on legs. And two eyes, enormous, the clear pale amber of honey held to the light, looking up at her.

“Oh,” said Ilse. “What *are* you?”

The eyes looked at her.

“It’s a—” She found herself crouching. She found herself, absurdly, answering her own question, out loud, in the voice she used for telling a hedge where it lived. “It’s a—a cat? A small grey cat. With ridiculous ears.”

And it was.

It happened without fuss, the way a drop of ink takes in water. The blur drew itself together: four white-tipped paws, a thin tail, a narrow face and a pink nose, and on top of its head two ears far too large for it, which turned toward her like a pair of sails. A small grey cat sat on the cobbles. Its eyes were still that clear amber.

It blinked at her, slowly.

“Oh,” said Hen. “Oh, *look* at it.”

“That’s one of Mab’s,” said Cass. “Got out of the basket. Hey, Mab!”

A Tenting girl of about twelve came running from a wagon and stopped dead. She looked at Ilse, and at the cat, and her face went from dismay to calculation.

“A penny,” she said.

“I don’t want—”

“It wants you.” The girl pointed. The cat had stood up, and walked to Ilse’s boot, and sat down again on it, firmly, the way a cat sits on a letter you are trying to write. “See? A penny. Can’t sell it to no one else now. It’s been said.”

Ilse paid a penny. She hadn’t meant to.

It followed her two steps behind her boots all the way back to the College, its ears swivelling at every sound. At the foot of the Wren Stair, Teo, who had been looking at it with growing fondness, crouched and held out a finger and said warmly, “Look at him. He’s just like a little ferret, isn’t he? A grey ferret.”

The cat flickered.

It went long and low and sinuous, and then it was a cat again, and then a ferret, and then neither, a smear of grey between the two. It made a thin small sound, a sound of real distress, and pressed itself flat against Ilse’s boot.

“Teo,” said Cass, sharply.

“I’m sorry! It’s a cat. You’re a cat, little one, a cat.” Ilse said it at the same time, firmly, “A small grey cat,” and Hen said “Cat!” as though calling a dog. Between them it settled, shuddering, into the cat again, and looked up at Teo reproachfully.

“Cat,” said Teo, humbly. “Agreed. Cat. I’ll never say the other word again.”

They tried to name it that evening by the hearth, with the wardrobe watching. Hen suggested Pebble. Teo suggested Pie. Cass suggested a Tenting word she wouldn’t translate. The cat washed one enormous ear and ignored them all. Then Ilse, getting up to put the kettle on, stepped too near its tail, and said “Sorry,” without thinking, and the cat looked up at once, and trotted over, and wound itself round her ankles.

“Oh, *no*,” said Hen, and began to laugh. “Oh, that’s it. That’s its name. That’s all it answers to.”

Ilse tried it three more times, to be sure. Pebble, nothing. Cat, nothing. *Sorry*, and the ears swung round at once.

So it was Sorry.


* * *


Benchmarking was on the thirtieth, in the Old Court, under a high white sky.

The Old Wall was the north wall of the court, the oldest stone in the College: honey stone two hundred feet long, and every inch of its lower half cut with witness-marks in rows under brass benchmark arrows. There were thousands of them. Crosses, circles, single bars; knots, keys, little flowers. Some were sharp and fresh and some so worn by rain they were faint as a signature on blotting paper, older and older as the rows went down toward the ground, each with a name and a year. In the lowest row, near the corner, worn nearly away, a little mark like a bird in flight had *4 H.* beside it.

Master Halloway presided, a thin, twitchy man with three watches on three chains across his waistcoat, who stammered so badly that Ilse winced for him.

“W-welcome. To. Um. To Benchmarking. Which is. Ah. An old—a very old—” He gave up and gestured at the wall, helplessly. Then a link at the front asked how deep to cut, and his whole face cleared like a sky.

“An eighth of an inch. Exactly. At sixty degrees to the face. Shallower and the weather will have it off you in two hundred years. Deeper, and it spalls in the first frost. The mallet is eleven ounces. Strike from the wrist.” He beamed. Then he lost the thread again and pointed to the clerk with the great Register of Marks. “Y-your names. There. And then. Um. Cut.”

They went up one by one, showed the clerk their mark, and cut it under the newest arrow while Halloway watched the angle. Some of the Patent links had been planning theirs for years: family devices, crowns. Hen cut a gull’s wing over a line, very neat, very fast. Teo cut a looped T like a pothook, for Teodor and for the hook his mother hung the eel-pot on, and apologized to the stone for the loop. Cass looked at the wall for a long while and then cut a quick Tenting zigzag, like a road going down a hill, and stood back from it, and said “Hey-ho,” quietly, to the stone.

Then it was Ilse.

She stood with the chisel in her hand. The clerk waited with his pen.

She had thought about it all week. She could choose anything: a clean new mark, a circle and a bar, with no history at all. Nobody would ever look at it twice. That was the sensible thing.

She thought of the wren’s foot.

She had drawn it thirty-one times in three years. She had drawn it on Agnes Tull’s amendment, and Kasimir Reyne had turned that sheet to the light in the Wick vestry and sealed it in a chest. And every time, underneath it, in the blind, she had drawn Quince’s, line for line, so the weight would go to him and not to her. The wren’s foot had never carried anything. A dead man had been carrying it for three years in the Hobbet churchyard, and she had thought it clever.

And yet it was the only mark that had ever felt like hers. She had made it at sixteen, watching a wren hop along the Gethins’ hedge: a small brown bird that was nobody’s and went where it liked.

“Miss?” said the clerk.

“A wren’s foot,” said Ilse. She drew it for him on the margin of the Register. Three lines and a spur. “Like that.”

She set the chisel at sixty degrees and cut it, an eighth of an inch deep, from the wrist. The stone was soft and sweet under the steel, and the dust ran down in a fine honey-coloured stream. Halloway, watching, said, “G-good. *Good* angle,” and moved on.

When she stepped back and looked up, Kasimir Reyne was standing three yards along the wall.

His own mark was fresh in the stone beside him: a capital R with a level line struck through it, precise as a proof. He was looking at her wren’s foot. He looked at it a moment longer than a man looks at a thing that means nothing to him, and his lips moved once, as if he were counting strokes. Then he looked at her, and his face did nothing at all, and he turned away to his party.

Ilse found that she didn’t care. She was surprised by how little.

“Oh, that’s lovely.”

Master Hallam had come along the line behind them, gloved hands folded behind his back, and stood looking at the wren’s foot with his head on one side.

“A clean mark,” he said. “Not fussy. Half the Patent links want to cut their whole family tree.” He smiled at her over his spectacles. “You have a draftswoman’s hand, my dear. I noticed it on your Walk sheet. A beautiful line. We don’t see enough of them.”

“Thank you, sir.”

“Keep it,” he said, kindly, as if it were a thing that could be lost, and went on along the wall.

The last part of Benchmarking was the stone.

Each link drew a paving stone of the Old Court by lot, to survey, chart and sign with their new mark, and keep in place for a year through frost and feet and the slow lean of the cape. Ilse’s was a grey sandstone flag near the southeast corner, two feet by three, cracked at one corner, worn hollow in the middle by centuries of boots.

She measured it on her knees with Quince’s folding rule: four sides, two diagonals, and its offset from the nearest brass stud of the meridian, eleven links and four-tenths. She measured the hollow in the middle, which she didn’t have to do. All around her the other links knelt over their own stones like people at prayer. It was the smallest charter she had ever made, and the plainest. It held a single stone where it already was.

Then she signed it.

She set the wren’s foot in the lower corner, three lines and a spur, in plain black ink. She set her intent on it, and leaned.

And there was nothing under it.

She waited, out of habit, for the second thread: the cool sideways slide of the burden running off down the ghosted line toward a churchyard in Hobbet. It didn’t come. There was only her mark and the stone and a single thread between them, short and clean, and the stone took it as a dry field takes rain.

The ache came into her knuckles, small and exact. It was hers.

She knelt there with the sheet on her knee, and the thing she would remember was how light it was. She had always thought that carrying your own weight would be heavy. It wasn’t. It was a flagstone, two feet by three, a little cracked at one corner. She could have carried it for a year with one hand.

Across the court, on the warm stones by the Mess door, a small grey cat with ridiculous ears sat and washed one paw and watched her with eyes the colour of honey.


## Chapter 7: Fieldwork

The Fieldwork shed had been built, as far as Ilse could tell, out of whatever the rest of the College had thrown away, with the roof added last and grudgingly.

It stood at the far end of the First Range, where the causeway gave out and the heath began. Its back wall was the College; its front wall was mostly door. Under a roof of tarred planks that had shrunk apart in four places sat fifteen first-years on two benches, and under each leak someone had set a bucket: one copper, one a coal scuttle, and one a chamber pot that nobody wanted to look at closely. The rain came through in four thin strings and struck four different notes. Teo had been tapping his knee in time with them for ten minutes.

“It’s nearly a scale,” he whispered. “If the coal scuttle were a bit fuller it’d be a scale.”

“Don’t fill the coal scuttle.”

“I wasn’t going to. I was only saying.”

Odile Brask came in out of the weather without hurrying. Rain ran off her oilskin and off the brass shoe of the Long Lie, and she stood in the doorway with her back to the heath, looking at them with her one eye as if she had been sent a parcel of the wrong size. Her left hand hung at her side in its grey half-curl, bare to the wet. It was hard not to look at, like a stopped clock.

“Fieldwork,” Brask said. “Compulsory. You’ll hate it. Good. Come to the door. Don’t push, Grice, you’ll go through the wall and then we’ll have to survey it.”

They crowded the doorway. Outside, the Margin lay low and brown under the rain: heather, bracken gone the colour of rust, a gleam of standing water, and gorse in dark humped clumps like sheep lying down to wait out the weather. The nearest clump was a big dense bush with a few late yellow flowers on it, very bright against the grey.

“That gorse,” said Brask. “How far?”

“Two chains,” said Absalom Grice, who was the size of a small barn and seemed to regard this as an accomplishment.

“How might you be wrong?”

Grice looked at the bush, and then at Brask, like a man who suspects a trap but cannot see its shape. “I’m not, ma’am.”

“Then you haven’t measured anything. Next.”

A thin Patent boy said two and a half chains, or perhaps three, and Brask told him that was being wrong in two directions at once and hoping one took. Piety Mercer said three chains with great firmness. Cass didn’t look at the bush at all, only at the ground in front of the door, and said, “Bit over three. Ground’s leaning off toward that pool,” which got a long look from Brask and no comment. Hen closed one eye, held up her thumb and said, “Three chains, eighteen links, give or take a chain, because I’ve only ever done this with boats.”

Then the eye came to Ilse and stopped.

“Marrow.”

She had been working at it since Grice’s two chains. She hadn’t wanted to, and then she couldn’t stop; it was like being handed a knot. She had stepped the distance out in doorframes and caught herself reading the bush closer because it was large.

“Three chains, ten links,” she said. “Give or take six.”

“How might you be wrong?”

“Short, most likely. There’s a fold about a chain and a half out, where the heather goes dark, and I can’t see the bottom of it, and measuring over a dip you can’t see into always reads short. The bush is big, which makes it look nearer; I corrected for that, but I might have corrected too much. The light’s flat, so there’s no shadow to show the ground, and flat light makes things look farther, so that might cancel some of it, or it might not.” She hesitated. “And it’s the Margin. It’s possible the bush isn’t where it was when I started talking.”

Somebody laughed, not unkindly. Brask didn’t.

“Anselm. Solberg. Take the chain to that bush.”

Teo and Hen went out into the rain calling the tallies to each other across the heather. Ilse watched them go down into the fold she had seen, waist-deep, and felt a small hard satisfaction she was careful not to show. Hen planted the arrow at the gorse and shouted back through cupped hands.

“Three chains, twenty-two links!”

“You said give or take six,” said Brask. “You were out by twelve. Wrong by twice what you said you might be. That’s the worst sort of wrong there is, Marrow, because it’s the sort that thinks it knows.” The class went back to looking at the rain. “The rest of you were wronger, but at least Grice is honest about being an idiot. Sit down.”

She talked for an hour without sitting, the Long Lie upright in her good hand: light and fold and ground-lean, and what heather did with water under it. Ilse wrote down every word and was angrier with each one. She had been so pleased with her six links.

At the end Brask said, “Go away. Not you, Marrow.”

Ilse turned in the doorway.

“The chains in the box by the wall are rusting. There’s tallow and a rag. Every link. Then the levelglasses, and don’t drop them, they cost more than you do. Tomorrow you’ll carry the instruments out for the Second Range class, and the day after for the Third, and you’ll do it until I say otherwise.”

“Is this a punishment?”

“It’s not paid, and it’s not an honour. It’s chains, and chains rust.” Brask was working one-handed at the toggles of her oilskin, and Ilse saw for the first time how tired she was: grey under the brown of her face, mud dried and cracked on her boots to the knee, as if she had walked a long way in it and then sat a long while without taking them off. “You were wrong by twelve links. But you knew there was a fold. None of the others saw the fold. Solberg saw boats.”

“I heard they found Oskar Fell,” Ilse said.

“I found Oskar Fell. Three days out, sitting in a salt-pool up to his chest, counting his fingers. He’d got to nine and was having trouble with the tenth.” Brask took her clay pipe out of her coat, looked at it, and put it back unlit. “Orle says he’ll mend, mostly.”

“And Yara?”

The rain played its four notes into the buckets.

“The tallow’s by the door,” said Brask. “Every link, Marrow.”


* * *


Charting was taught in a high, cold room halfway along the First Range, with benches stepped up in tiers above a long table, so that the students sat looking down on their work like gulls on a harbour. The Master of the Register did not teach first-years in their first term. They had his adjunct instead, Master Wystan Pell, a narrow, pink-handed man who handled every sheet as though it might bruise, and who began each class by reminding them that Master Hallam would see their work at the end of term and had very high standards and a very kind heart, and they should try not to disappoint either.

Today they were charting cobbles. Each of them had a tray of fine sand and a cobble the size of a hen’s egg set loose on it. A cobble on sand had a latitude of a few inches, Pell explained, so it could not go far wrong and could not do much harm when it did. They were to measure it, draw it, and attest, and then Pell would come along the table with a wooden mallet and tap each tray.

“The s-simplest charter you will ever make,” he said. “And in it is the whole of the art.”

Ilse’s cobble was a dull brown thing with a white vein of quartz across one end. She measured it from the left and near edges and from the far corner as a check, and she drew the white vein in full, because it was the cobble’s one distinguishing feature and the sand would have trouble arguing with it. When she set her wren’s foot to the quarter-sheet she felt the small tightening in her ears that meant the charter had taken, like a string drawn up to pitch.

Pell came down the table tapping. Each cobble jumped and settled. Grice’s rolled a full inch and lay on its side looking guilty. Piety Mercer’s slid a quarter-inch, which she said was the sand’s fault. Ilse’s moved a hair and a half; she saw it blur and steady.

Pell tapped Kasimir Reyne’s tray. The sand jumped. The cobble did not.

He struck it harder, so hard that a little fountain of sand went up and pattered down over the table, and the cobble sat in the middle of it as if it had been cast in iron and bolted through the tray into the bench.

Pell held the quarter-sheet up for them all to see. It had the beauty of a well-cut key, the beauty of nothing extra. Every line had been put down once and had stayed.

“That,” said Pell, “is what fidelity looks like.”

“It’s a stone,” said Orla Devane from the bench above, leaning back on her elbows, pleased with the world. “He’s been drawing stones since he could hold a pen. His nurse gave him gravel instead of sweets.”

“Thank you, Miss Devane.”

“He cried when they took it away.”

“*Thank* you, Miss Devane.”

Kasimir didn’t look round. He wiped his pen.

“Some of us were taught to draw before we came,” said Piety Mercer, not quite quietly. “I suppose on the Walk they give you a stick and point.”

“They give you a chain and a cairn three miles off,” said Ilse, without turning, “and the ground moves while you work. I suppose in Tessary they give you sand that holds still.”

Piety’s face closed like a purse. Orla laughed out loud. Pell said “Ladies” in a hurt voice, as though they had disappointed Master Hallam personally.

She knew how she could have made her cobble hold like his. She had known it the moment she lifted the pen. You leaned the drawing a hair toward where the sand wanted the stone, so the land had less to argue with, and signed it as the plain truth. Quince would have done it in his sleep. It wouldn’t even have been much of a lie.

She hadn’t, and her cobble had moved a hair and a half. She found she minded less than she would have expected. She minded a little.


* * *


The Library of Record had a hammerbeam roof dark with age, long tables under green-shaded lamps, and a tiled floor that made a map of the Reach, so that to reach the legal commentaries you had to walk across Tessary. Ilse had found what she wanted in the region of the Salt Steps: *Concordance Procedure, Practice of the Board*, bound in calf and smelling of mice. The Board of Errata sat four times a year, took evidence on oath, and could expel, fine, or put a Member to such use as the College saw fit. She had read that clause twice. It didn’t say what use.

She felt him come down the bay before she saw him. A quiet room went quieter.

“Miss Marrow.”

“Mr. Reyne.”

He sat down across from her without asking and laid his notebook on the table, squared to the edge. Small, black, with a clasp: *errors observed*. She had watched him write in it at Benchmarking, straight after he had looked at her mark on the Old Wall.

“I have had a reply from Hobbet,” he said.

“That was quick.”

“The express mail. Four days there, four days back, and the clerk is a prompt man.”

“Jem likes letters. He doesn’t get many.” She was doing the sum without wanting to: Reaping 23, when he wrote; eight days on the road; a day for Jem to write back in his best hand with his tongue between his teeth. It came out at today. He had read it this morning, gone and drawn his iron cobble, and come here afterwards to tell her. Courtesy or cruelty; with him they might be the same thing.

“Mr. Rudd informs me that the Tull amendment is, in his words, in order,” Kasimir said. “He also reminds me that it lies under the Court’s seal, which I put there myself, and that he will not break it on the word of a first-year student, however politely asked. He did not enclose a copy.” He paused. “I had half hoped he would break it anyway and send me a tracing of the mark. It would have saved us both a good deal of time. He is an honest man.”

She kept her eyes on his face and her right hand flat on the book. Whether he had looked at a flourish an inch high at the foot of a parish amendment closely enough to know it again on the Old Wall, she didn’t know. She suspected he looked at everything that closely.

“I’m sorry he disappointed you,” she said.

“He followed procedure. I knew he would, and I wrote anyway.” He wrote a single line in the notebook. She would have given a mark to read it. “The inquiry must be refiled with the Board directly. It sits in Frost. If it accepts, it applies to the Court to lift the seal, the Court writes to the parish, and in due course the charter comes here and someone draws the mark. That may take until spring.”

“So you have no proof.”

“I have no proof.”

“Then why tell me?”

He looked at her properly then, and she saw again that his eyes were the colour of slate in rain, and wished she had stopped noticing.

“Because I would not like you to think I had given up. I will be patient, Miss Marrow. I am very good at being patient. I have had a great deal of practice.”

“What a thing to be good at.”

“It is a useful thing to be good at, in law. My mother is the Chancellor of the Concordance Court.” He said it the way you might mention that your mother kept bees. “I have lived in the law since I was born. It is like a house. Its walls keep out the weather.”

“Its walls belong to whoever built the house.”

“They belong to everyone who lives inside it.”

“Have you ever been outside it?” She heard her voice rise and pulled it down; an old man was asleep two bays off with his spectacles on his forehead. “Have you ever watched a man with a licence and a lord’s money draw a line through a field, and then gone to the Court and been told the line is true because it was drawn better? The law doesn’t protect the weak from the powerful. The law is what the powerful call it when they win.”

“And what do the weak call it when they win?”

She opened her mouth and shut it again.

“Justice,” he said. “Or forgery. It depends, I suppose, on whether they are caught.” He said it gently, which was the worst of it. “The law is not a good wall. It has holes in it, and the rich know where all of them are. But if everyone who disliked where it ran were free to move it in the night, there would be no wall at all. Who do you suppose suffers most then?”

She thought of the Long Field: sixty yards, the well, an appeal that cost every penny they had, her father at the window with the lamp. It was right there. It would have been easy to lay it on the table between them like a card and watch his clean face take it in.

She didn’t. It was the only thing she owned outright, and she was not going to spend it on him.

“The people outside the wall,” she said. “Same as always.”

He turned the notebook a quarter-turn on the table and turned it back.

“I do not dislike you, Miss Marrow,” he said at last. “I want you to understand that. I dislike that you are good at something you ought not to be able to do.”

“Which thing?”

“I think you know which thing.”

He stood, inclined his head with perfect, maddening courtesy, and went away across the tiled Salt Steps. Ilse read the clause about such use as the College saw fit a third time and still didn’t know what it meant.


* * *


“The trick,” said Teo, “is you don’t let it know you’re frightened.”

He was crouched over the spirit-stove on the hearthstone, the only part of the room certain to be stone. The stove was a squat brass thing the size of a fist with a blue flame that smelled like a chemist’s, forbidden in the Ranges on pain of a two-mark fine. Teo had three. He had brought them from Lisk in a hatbox along with a jar of dripping, a string of onions, a hard sausage and a tin he called *emergency mustard* and would not open for anyone.

“Don’t let what know?” said Hen.

“The pan. If you hesitate, it sticks. My grandmother says that about pans and horses and the tax-assessor.”

Hen laughed. It came all the way up from her boots and seemed to surprise her as much as anyone, and she put her hand over her mouth and then took it away again as though she’d remembered she was allowed.

Their rooms on the Wren Stair were four narrow bedrooms round a sitting room: a hearth, a table, four chairs that didn’t match, a window over the Margin, and a wardrobe that would not stay put. On the first night it had taken a step out from the wall, as though to hear better, and it had been wandering in and out ever since. Every morning Hen measured the gap and pencilled it on the inside of the wardrobe door. Eleven inches now. Cass said it was getting brave.

Cass had her boots off and her feet on the table, which Teo said was worse than boots, and her braids hung over the chair-back with two route-cords come loose and trailing on the floor. She was telling them about a man in Verity Cross selling charms against hollowings: a knot of string and a holed pebble.

“I said, hey-ho, where’d you get it? He said, Tenting make ’em. I said, I’m Tenting. He said, then you know how good they are. Sold three to a pair of furlongs while I stood there.”

“Are they any good?” said Hen.

“It’s string and a pebble.”

“But are they any good?”

Cass considered. “Pebble’s good. You can throw it.”

Teo dished out slices of the hard sausage fried with onion in a great deal of dripping, on four chipped plates borrowed from the Mess that he fully intended to return, and they ate with their fingers. It was the best thing Ilse had eaten since Hobbet. When she said so he went red up to his curls and said the sausage did all the work, really; he only encouraged it.

“Where’s Sorry?” said Hen.

It had been asleep on the end of Ilse’s bed when they came in, a tight grey knot with its ears folded down over its face like a pair of hands. It was not there now. It was on Teo’s desk, sitting very upright beside the inkstand with its ears pricked and its amber eyes half shut, and its whole small grey face from the whiskers to the chin was black.

“Oh no,” said Teo.

The ink bottle lay on its side, empty. Not a drop had been spilled. Sorry regarded them with mild, contented inquiry and licked its lips with a small tongue that was also black, and a blue-black bead ran down one whisker and dropped off.

“That was my good ink,” said Teo. “Lisk ink, my uncle sent it, it’s oak gall from the Ambry woods, it doesn’t fade, you could write a will with it—”

“You could write a cat with it, apparently,” said Hen, and set herself off, and Cass laughed until she had to hold on to the chair-back, and Ilse found she was laughing too, helplessly, with her greasy hands held up away from her coat. Sorry watched them with its ears turning one way and then the other. Then it walked across the desk, lay down on Teo’s Mensuration notes, and began to wash its black face with a black paw.

“It’s going to be sick,” said Teo.

“Ossels eat ink,” said Cass, wiping her eyes. “Ink, flies, seeds, letters from your mother. It’ll be smug, but it’ll be fine.”

“It’s already smug,” said Teo in despair.

They stayed up later than they should have. Hen told them how the brine in the salt-pans went pink at high summer, so the pans looked like a field of roses laid flat, and how her father could taste a pan on one fingertip and tell you to the day when it would crust. Teo told them about Anselm’s pie shop, which had been Anselm’s for a hundred and nine years, and his grandmother, who had once thrown a tax-assessor into the canal. Cass didn’t tell them anything about the Tenting. She sang instead, very low, a tune without words that went up and down like a road over hills, and when Hen asked what it was Cass said, “Song,” and wouldn’t say more.

Ilse didn’t tell them anything either. Nobody seemed to mind. She sat against the wall with her knees drawn up and listened to the three of them, and to the Wren Stair settling round them for the night, a joist creaking, the window-frame easing a hair in its stone. She had not sat in a room like this, warm and late and full of other people’s voices, since she was a child. She didn’t know what to do with it. She held it carefully, as you hold an egg you’ve found in the grass.

It was nearly midnight when Hen said, “There’s something under the door.”

It was a square of paper pushed through from the landing, though nobody had heard a step on the stair: a page torn from a field-book, the kind with a faint blue grid, and on it, in a cramped, square hand that pressed so hard it had dented through the grid, eight words.

*Walk with me tomorrow, Marrow. Before the bell.*

There was no signature. There didn’t need to be.

“Who’s it from?” said Hen.

Ilse folded it along its crease and put it in the pocket of her father’s coat, where it lay against her ribs.

“My employer,” she said.


# Part Two: The Ranges


## Chapter 8: The Quiet

Brask was waiting at the door of the Fieldwork shed at four in the morning with a shuttered lantern in her good hand and the Long Lie crooked in the elbow of the other. She looked Ilse up and down once, the coat, the boots, the scarf Hesta had knitted in a fit of temper two winters ago, and said, “You’ll do,” and walked off into the dark.

Ilse followed. There was no path. There was the causeway’s end, and a gap in the bracken, and then the Margin, black and breathing.

They walked for half an hour without a word. The lantern made a small gold room around them that moved when they moved, and outside it the heath went on in every direction, felt rather than seen: the wet slap of bracken against Ilse’s knees, the give of peat, a smell of salt and crushed thyme. Once something large went away from them through the heather, unhurried, and Brask didn’t even turn her head. The level in Ilse’s ears had dropped as soon as they left the causeway. Out here it wasn’t a steady hum but a low, uneven note, like a hum from someone who has forgotten the tune.

At last Brask stopped on a slight rise and set the lantern down and opened its shutter wide.

“Look,” she said. “Don’t talk. Look.”

Ilse looked. Gorse, a scatter of it, black-green and glittering with wet. Heather. A slab of grey stone half-buried. Beyond the light, darkness, and very faintly in the east the first thinning of it over the Reach.

“The gorse,” said Brask, after a while. “Which way’s it leaning?”

It wasn’t leaning. Ilse said so. Brask said nothing, and Ilse looked again, longer, and saw that it was. Not the bushes, which grew any way they liked, but the tips of them, the young green spines at the end of each shoot. On every bush within the light they turned very slightly the same way, north and east, like a congregation turned toward the pulpit.

“Toward the Cape,” she said.

“Toward the hold. Gorse is a coward. It grows toward whatever’s firmest. In the Reach it doesn’t bother, because everything’s firm. Out here it’s got opinions.” Brask crouched, awkwardly, her stilled hand held out from her body, and touched a fingertip to the stone slab. “Now the dew.”

The slab was furred all over with dew, fine grey beads close as the hairs on a moth. Beside it, on a patch of bare peat no more than a foot away, the dew lay differently: fewer beads, larger, and when Ilse bent close she saw they were trembling. Not much: as water trembles in a cup when a cart goes by in the street.

“Held ground keeps its dew still,” said Brask. “The stone’s held. Someone charted it once, and it hasn’t forgotten. The peat isn’t. It doesn’t know quite where it is, so its water doesn’t either. On a still morning you can walk a line across the Margin by the dew and never put a foot wrong. You won’t find that in a book. Most of the people who write books have never been up at four.”

She stood, and drove the Long Lie into the peat with a soft chock, and then into the edge of the stone, where it rang. Two notes: one dead, one bright.

“Now shut your eyes,” she said, “and tell me what your ears are doing.”

Ilse shut her eyes. It was hard, at first, to separate the level from the wind and the sea. Then she had it: the note was higher when she faced the stone and lower when she turned from it, and when she turned slowly round in a full circle it rose and fell like a voice saying a long word.

“It changes,” she said. “With direction.”

“That’s the grain. You’re hearing which way is more held. Cass Tenter walks by that the way you walk by your feet; she doesn’t even know she’s doing it. You can learn it, slower. You’ll never be as good as her, and you’ll always be better at saying what you heard. Between the two of you, you might make one surveyor.”

They went on. Brask taught as she walked, in short hard sentences with long gaps between them. She showed Ilse how a stream on the Margin cut its bank deeper on the side toward the Reach. She showed her a wayhorn’s scrape on a birch trunk, and the shed tine lying under it, its points all curving the same way, like a hand pointing home. She made Ilse walk fifty paces with her eyes shut, following the level, and Ilse came out eleven paces off line and furious, and Brask said, “Eleven. Good. Last week you’d have been in the sea.”

It was when Brask bent to pick up the antler that Ilse saw the hand properly. Brask had hooked the Long Lie through the curl of her grey fingers to free her good hand, as you might hang a coat on a peg, and the fingers didn’t close round it or open to let it go. They stayed as they were. The staff rested in them as in a bracket bolted to a wall. When Brask straightened and took the staff back, the hand stayed in its curl around nothing, and the skin of it in the lantern light had the dull sheen of old pewter.

Ilse looked away, but not quickly enough.

“Don’t,” said Brask.

“I didn’t say anything.”

“You were going to.”

They walked another furlong. The sky had gone from black to the colour of a bruise, and the gorse was coming up out of the dark in grey shapes, each one leaning its tips toward the College.

“Why’d you come, Marrow?” Brask said.

“To be a surveyor.”

“Liar.”

Cass had said that, at the cairn. Ilse was beginning to think it was a thing people said to her the way they said good morning. She walked a few paces, and then, because the dark made it easier and because Brask hadn’t once used her first name, she said, “My family had a field. Eleven acres, in Wick Fallow. When I was twelve a lord’s surveyor re-charted the parish and the boundary moved sixty yards, with the well in it. It was all legal. The Court upheld it.” She kept her voice flat. “I want a Patent. Then I can chart it myself. Properly.”

It was true, every word. It just wasn’t all of it. It was the bones without the joints, and she waited for Brask to see the gaps.

Brask said nothing at all. She walked, the Long Lie swinging, the lantern swinging, and after a while Ilse understood that nothing was what she was going to say. It had been heard, and weighed, and put somewhere. Ilse didn’t know whether to be relieved.

They stopped at the last rise before the causeway. From here she could see the Drift Wing strung out along the dark, its windows beginning to light as the College woke, and out beyond it, west, the Margin going on and on into a grey that wasn’t sky and wasn’t land.

“Yara Penhallow,” said Ilse. “Will you go out again?”

“I’ve been out four times.” Brask looked west. The patch on her right eye made her face, in profile, a face with only one side. “The Margin keeps what it keeps. Sometimes it gives things back. Fell it gave back. Penhallow it hasn’t yet.” She knocked the Long Lie twice against the causeway stones, ringing, and turned for the College. “That’s the bell in ten minutes. Run.”


* * *


It was eleven before she was done in the Fieldwork shed, and then Brask sent her off with a canvas roll of instruments under one arm and orders to take them to the infirmary.

“Orle’s calipers,” Brask said. “And her fine chain. She lent them for Fell. Tell her the chain had a kink in the forty-third link and I took it out, and if she wants to argue she knows where I live.”

The infirmary was at the north end of the Old Court, where the stone was oldest, in a long range of whitewashed wards. It smelled of carbolic and boiled linen, which made Ilse think of her mother’s copper with a homesick little jolt. A porter pointed her down a corridor. It was quiet, with tall windows on one side giving onto a garden of herbs, and benches against the other wall, and on one of the benches a young man was sitting with his hands in his lap, looking at the wall.

She knew him slightly; everyone knew everyone slightly by now. Wil Ashdown, a second-year, a chain. She had seen him in the Mess in her first week, she thought, or someone like him. He was a tall young man with a long, pleasant, bony face and brown hair that wanted cutting, and he was sitting quite straight, with his feet together, as though someone had arranged him there and gone away.

“Good morning,” Ilse said.

He turned his head and looked at her. It took him some time. It wasn’t slowness, quite. It was that he seemed to have to decide, at each part of the movement, that he would go on with it.

“Good morning,” he said.

His eyes were brown, and there was nothing wrong with them, and they were the worst thing she had ever seen. They were flat. That was the only word she could find. They took her in the way a window takes in light, without any interest in it, and when she smiled at him they did nothing at all.

Then she looked at his hands.

The left hand was an ordinary young man’s hand, chapped from the cold. The right lay palm-down on his knee, and from the knuckles to the tips the fingers were grey. It wasn’t dirt and it wasn’t bruising. They were grey the way slate is grey, all through, with a faint dull sheen, and the forefinger where a pen would rest was darkest of all.

Ilse knew that grey. Quince’s hands had gone that colour in his last two years, the right worse than the left, until he could hardly hold a pen and had to tie it to his fingers with thread. *That’s the bill coming in*, he’d said, and laughed, and she had laughed too. He had been signing land for fifty years.

Wil Ashdown was twenty, perhaps twenty-one.

“Are you waiting for someone?” she asked.

“Yes.” He considered. “No. I am waiting.”

A door opened down the corridor and a woman came out of it fast, drying her hands on a towel: middle-sized, middle-aged, in a grey gown with the sleeves rolled to the elbow, with a frown that looked as though it had been there since breakfast and expected to stay until supper.

“Who are you?”

“Ilse Marrow. Field-Surveyor Brask sent your calipers back. And your chain. She says it had a kink in the forty-third link and she’s taken it out.”

“Of course she has.” Dr. Sabeth Orle took the canvas roll and tucked it under her arm without looking at it. Her eyes went past Ilse to the young man on the bench, and something in her face shut and locked. “Wil, you’re to go back to the ward. Dinner’s at twelve.”

“Yes,” said Wil. He didn’t move.

Orle went and put her hand under his elbow, and he stood when she lifted, and walked when she walked. Ilse stood aside to let them pass. Through the ward door as it swung she saw a long whitewashed room and a row of beds. In the third a girl lay propped on pillows, a furlong by the look of her, with her hair in a neat plait over one shoulder and her hands folded outside the blanket. Her face was turned to the window. She wasn’t looking out of it.

Orle came back out alone. She seemed surprised to find Ilse still there.

“Is it catching?” Ilse said.

Orle looked at her properly for the first time. “What do you think it is, Miss Marrow?”

“I don’t know. I heard it called Drift-fatigue.”

“Did you.” Orle snapped the towel straight and folded it into precise thirds. “Lectures and fieldwork and early rising, and grown young men and women out of the best families in the Reach go flat as pond water and grey in the hand. It isn’t fatigue. Whatever the Provost likes to call it.” She seemed to hear herself, and stopped. “Thank Brask for the chain.”

She went back into the ward. The door swung shut behind her and went on swinging, smaller and smaller, until it didn’t.


* * *


The Provost spoke at supper.

The Mess was full, as it always was at the evening meal: three long tables of students down the length of the hall, and the high table across the end under the painted ceiling, where the College’s stars wheeled in gilt across a sky of blue that had faded to the colour of a thrush’s egg. Provost Steyne rose between the soup and the mutton. He was a tall, silver-haired man with a beautiful voice, and he used it the way some men use a good walking stick, not to lean on but to be seen with.

“A word before we eat,” he said, “and I shall keep it brief, because Cook has views on mutton going cold. Some of you will have heard that a few of our students are resting in the infirmary. I want to put your minds at ease. We are seeing a little seasonal Drift-fatigue, as we do most autumns when the weather turns and the Wing is restless. It is not serious. It is not catching. Dr. Orle has the matter well in hand. The cure is rest and broth, and I am assured by Cook that the College has a great deal of broth.”

There was a ripple of laughter. Someone at the furlongs’ table called out, “Is that the cure or the cause?” and the laughter went up a notch, and the Provost smiled in a way that let them know he had enjoyed the joke and would now like it to stop.

“Rest,” he said, “and broth. And a little less of the Chain & Anchor, perhaps, for some of you. Thank you.” He sat down.

“Broth,” said Cass, into her soup. “Road and rain.”

“It’s quite nice broth,” said Teo, doubtfully.

“That isn’t the point, Teo.”

“I know it isn’t the point. I was only saying.”

“My gran had a cure for fatigue,” said Hen. “She’d stand you in the brine-pan up to your knees till you felt better. Nobody was ever tired twice.” She put her hand over her mouth. “Tide’s out on broth, I think. We should send them a gran.”

She turned to laugh at something Orla Devane was saying further down the table. Ilse wasn’t listening to any of them. Across the hall, near the door, a proctor had come in, and Wil Ashdown was with her. Ilse didn’t know why. Perhaps someone had thought supper in the Mess would do him good, or perhaps he had simply walked here, because he had been told to walk somewhere and had not been told to stop. He stood beside the proctor looking at the long tables with his flat eyes, and the chains near the door had gone quiet, and then a second proctor came, and between them they turned him round, gently, and led him out again, his grey right hand hanging at his side.

At the high table, Master Hallam had watched it too. He sat a little way down from the Provost, small and round-shouldered in his dark gown, his bald head shining under the lamps and his white beard neat on his chest. His grey-gloved hands lay on the cloth on either side of his plate. He had put down his spoon. He had eaten, Ilse thought, almost nothing. As the door closed behind Wil Ashdown he took off his spectacles and pressed finger and thumb to the bridge of his nose and sat like that for a moment with his eyes shut, an old man tired by a sorrow that wasn’t his.

Then he put the spectacles back on and murmured to the Provost, and the Provost laughed, and the mutton came.


* * *


She couldn’t sleep.

It wasn’t the room, though the room was restless tonight. The wardrobe creaked. The floor made small sounds like someone turning over in bed. Through the wall she could hear Teo snoring, gently and with great regularity, like a man sawing a very soft plank, and through the other wall Hen’s breathing, and nothing at all from Cass, who slept like the dead or like a fox, it was hard to tell which. Sorry lay on Ilse’s feet, a warm grey weight no bigger than a muff, and when she moved it complained in its sleep without waking.

She got up in the end, and lit the candle, and sat at the table with her father’s coat around her shoulders.

There was scrap paper in the drawer: old Mensuration sheets of Hen’s, written over on one side in her quick round figures. Ilse turned one over and took up the pen and drew her mark.

Three strokes and a spur. The first stroke down and a little left. The second straight down, the longest. The third down and a little right. Then, lifting the pen not quite off the paper, the spur, a short hook back from the foot of the middle stroke. A wren’s foot, as a wren leaves it in frost on a windowsill.

She drew it again. And again, a row of them across the page like a small bird walking.

For three years she had drawn this mark over the top of another one. Quince’s mark had been a sort of knot: a crooked, old-fashioned loop with a hook caught through it, like a fish on a line. She had traced it under her own so many times in blind ink that her hand could make it in the dark. She had never liked drawing it. It had always felt, under the pen, like walking in someone else’s boots: they went where you wanted, but they rubbed, and they were shaped by another man’s feet. She had thought that was just the blind ink. She had thought that was just what it was like.

It wasn’t. She could feel the difference now in her wrist and the heel of her hand. The wren’s foot came off the pen as naturally as breath. It didn’t rub anywhere. It was hers, and on the Old Wall it was cut in stone under a brass arrow with her name beside it, and every time she set it on a sheet the sheet tightened a hair in her ears and let go again, and there was no one underneath it. Nobody carrying her. Only her.
