She turned the pen over in her fingers, and looked at them. Her right forefinger was stained black along the side, as it had been since she was fourteen. Under the ink the skin was pink.

She thought of Wil Ashdown’s forefinger, darker than the rest, where a pen would rest.

She thought of Quince laughing, *that’s the bill coming in.* Fifty years of signing land. And a chain at twenty, in a College that kept its students on rest and broth.

She didn’t know what that meant. She didn’t know enough yet to know what it meant, and she was too tired to pretend she did. She folded the page of wren’s feet and slipped it into the lining of her father’s coat, where she had sewn a pocket the week she arrived and kept the things she didn’t want found. Then she pinched out the candle.

Sorry had moved in her absence to the warm place she had left. When she got back into bed it grumbled and shifted down again to her feet, and turned round twice, and lay down with its too-big ears flat, and she lay in the dark listening to Teo’s soft saw and the creak of the wandering wardrobe, with her right hand closed loosely on the blanket, until the window began to grey.


## Chapter 9: Rain on the Register

It had rained for two days, and on the morning of the tenth of Gleaning it was still raining, and the Long Gallery was raining too.

The Gallery ran along the top of the Second Range under a roof of leaded glass, a hundred and sixty-odd links of bare boards with tall windows down one side and a row of doors down the other. On a fine morning it was the best room in the College, full of grey light and the smell of the sea. This morning water came through the leading in a hundred places and stood on the boards in shining puddles, and the party went down it at First Bell in the dark with the lantern hissing and the chain slithering wet through their hands.

“Tally,” called Hen from the far end, invisible.

“Forty,” said Ilse, feeling the four-pointed brass tag go past her fingers. “Forty-one. Hold there.”

“Holding. I’m standing in a lake. Did you know I was standing in a lake?”

“We all know you’re standing in a lake,” said Cass, who had the levelglass and was crouched in the middle of the Gallery over a puddle, watching the bead of quicksilver. “Whole Gallery’s a lake. Bead’s swimming. Ground’s leaning toward the stair this morning. Gallery’s long.”

“How long?”

“Long.”

“Cass.”

“Couple of links. Ask the chain, it’s the one with numbers.”

The chain said two links and a half. The Gallery had been one chain and sixty links yesterday, as the record had it, and today it was sixty-two and a half, and the Wren Stair had a new step in it, about a third of the way down, that Teo had found with his foot in the dark and nearly fallen over.

“Forty-two steps,” he said, at the bottom, his voice coming up the stairwell with a little echo to it. “It was forty-one yesterday. I counted. I always count. There’s a new one and it’s shorter than the others by, I think, an inch and a bit, and it’s wet, and I don’t like it.”

“Nobody likes it, Teo.”

“I know. I just want it written down that I especially don’t.”

They drew it on the board under the dripping lantern, all four of them hunched over it to keep the rain off the paper: the Stair with its new tread, the landing, the Gallery two and a half links longer, the doors in their places. It was ugly work and they did it fast, the way you bail a boat. Three chains and forty-one links had become three chains and forty-four, and they had to say so, honestly and precisely, before the Dawn Bell rang and the stretch was signed, or by tomorrow it might be three chains and sixty and the Wren Stair might open on the gorse.

Sorry rode through all of it inside Ilse’s coat. It had not meant to. She had left it asleep on the bed, but when she reached the landing it had begun to make a noise, a high thin wail like a kettle left on the hob, and she had gone back and stuffed it inside her coat before it woke the Stair. Now it sat with its head out between the second and third buttons, ears flattened against the wet, watching the lantern.

“You know they’re not allowed in the Register,” said Hen.

“It’s not in the Register. It’s in my coat.”

“And your coat’s going in the Register.”

“My coat,” said Ilse, “is a Member of the College, and has privileges.”


* * *


The Register Antechamber lay at the bottom of a long stair under the Old Court, through a door of black oak bound with iron. It was the mouth of the Register and the only part of it most students would ever see: a long vaulted room, dry and warm after the Gallery, lit by brass lamps that hung on chains from the groins of the vault. Slanted desks stood along both walls, and at each desk a party bent over its folio, copying fair from its rough board. The air smelled of ink and lamp-oil and wet wool. Past the desks, at the far end, a second door stood shut. It led down to the levels where the parish charters were kept, and to Master Hallam’s study, and no student went through it.

At a high counter by the entrance sat Merrin Coyle, the Registrar-Adjunct, handing out folios. He was a thin, damp-looking man of about thirty-five, with spectacles he kept pushing up his nose with one inky knuckle and shirt-cuffs so stained with ink that they might have been dyed. He kept a cup of tea at his elbow, milky and very sweet, and drank it in quick nervous sips between parties, like a bird at a puddle.

“Wren Stair,” he said, before Ilse had spoken. He found their four folios in a rack without looking. “You’re wet. Don’t drip on them. Master Hallam likes them signed here, mind, not upstairs—”

“—not in the Ranges,” said Hen, Cass and Teo together, and Coyle looked hurt.

“It’s a good rule,” he said. “It keeps the sheets together.”

The folios were heavy linen-rag sheets, a little larger than a quarter, each printed with a faint grey frame, the College seal and the title of the stretch in copperplate. The *Wren Stair, Second Range*; the *Landing*; the *Long Gallery, East*; the *Long Gallery, West*. Each wanted two marks at least and would take four. They took the desk nearest the counter because it was under a lamp, and Teo, who had the best hand for numbers, read off the board while Ilse drew the Stair and Hen the landing and Cass, swearing softly, the doors of the Gallery.

“I’ll sign all four,” said Hen, when Cass had finished. “If you like. I’m quickest.”

“You signed all four yesterday,” said Teo.

“I’m quickest every day.” She dipped her pen. “It’s the Salt Steps. We’re brought up to sign for things. Barrels, nets, brothers.”

Ilse took the Gallery East. She drew the last line, the end wall, and checked the length against the board—one chain, sixty-two links and a half, Atlas help them—and dipped her pen and set her wren’s foot in the box at the bottom right: three strokes and a spur, fresh and black and glistening under the lamp. She felt it take. A small, clean tightening in her ears, and then the familiar lightness, nothing pulling, nothing behind it. One chain and sixty-two links and a half of the Long Gallery, held by her.

Sorry chose that moment to see the tea.

She felt it gather itself inside her coat, a small hard coil of interest. She said, “No—” and grabbed, and caught a handful of grey fur, and the rest of Sorry went out between the buttons like water out of a cracked jug, up onto the desk, across Teo’s board, and onto the counter, where Merrin Coyle’s tea stood steaming at his elbow.

Coyle saw it come. He made a sound like a hinge. Sorry put its face into the cup, and Coyle snatched at the cup, and the cup went over.

Milky tea went in a broad brown wave across the counter and over the edge of it, and down onto the desk below, and across all four of the Wren Stair’s folios, ink still wet.

“Oh, *no*,” said Coyle. “Oh no no no—the blotters—the *blotters*—”

He came round the counter faster than Ilse would have thought him capable of moving, with a sheaf of blotting paper in each hand, and began to press them down on the folios with little moaning sounds. Teo was apologizing in a continuous stream, not to anyone in particular. Cass had Sorry by the scruff and was holding it at arm’s length, where it hung blinking its amber eyes with milk on its whiskers, unrepentant. At the other desks heads had come up all along the room.

Ilse lifted the Gallery East by its dry corner before Coyle could reach it.

Her drawing had survived. The tea had caught only the bottom edge, a pale brown stain running in across the corner where her mark was. The ink of the wren’s foot had bloomed a little in the wet, the way gall ink does, its sharp edges gone soft, the black going faintly purple at the rims.

And under it there was a second line.

It was very faint. It ran from beneath the spur of her mark, where the tea had soaked the paper through, out toward the edge of the sheet in a fine straight thread, and it was violet. Not the faint purple bloom of her own wet ink. A paler colour, clean and cold, the colour of a crocus in frost. She knew that colour. She had seen it a hundred times by shuttered lantern, under the damp edge of her own forgeries, where Quince’s knot lay hidden in the paper and showed only when the paper was wet.

She stood quite still with the sheet in her hand.

Coyle took it from her. “Let me, let me, it must be blotted at once or it’ll cockle—” He laid it flat and pressed a blotter across the corner and held it down with the flat of both hands, and when he lifted the blotter the stain had gone pale and dull and the paper was only damp, and under her mark there was nothing at all. Only linen-rag and a fading tea-mark, and her wren’s foot gone a little fat at the edges.

“There,” said Coyle, breathing hard. “There. No harm. No harm at all. It’s still a good sheet. The mark’s taken, I can see it’s taken. Oh, Miss Marrow, your *cat*.”

“I’m very sorry,” said Ilse.

“I know you are. Everyone always is.” He gathered the four folios together, damp and stained, and squared them on the counter and looked at them with the misery of a man whose day has been spoiled before breakfast. “Master Hallam won’t like it. He likes them clean.”

“It’s an ossel,” said Cass. “It’s not a cat. It’s just being a cat for her.”

“I don’t care what it is,” said Coyle, with surprising force. “Get it out of the Register.”

Out on the stair Ilse stood in the cold with her back to the wet stone and Sorry inside her coat again, its small damp head under her chin, and thought it through. It was what she did with any strangeness: walked round it, measured it, and wrote it down.

She had smudged it. That was what had happened. The tea had wet her mark and she had dragged the sheet, lifting it, and drawn a thread of her own ink out across the wet. Gall ink went violet when it was wet; everyone’s did. College ink was gall ink. The line had been straight because she had pulled the sheet straight. It had been a different violet because it was thinned with tea. And she had been looking for blind ink for nine years, every time paper got damp, because that was the habit of the trade, and she had seen what she was used to seeing. That was all. That was all it was.

It was a good explanation. It closed. She had made a hundred charters close on worse.

“Marrow?” said Teo, behind her. He had come up the stair with the board under his arm and rain still in his curls. “You all right? You look as if you’ve seen—I don’t know. A long division.”

“I’m fine. I’m hungry.”

“I’m always hungry,” said Teo, relieved. “Come on. There’s porridge. Hen says there might be honey, but Hen says that every day.”


* * *


Master Hallam’s lecture on the Principles of Hold was given in the Antechamber itself, at ten, when the Morning Survey sheets had been filed away and the slanted desks cleared. The first-years sat on benches brought in for the purpose, in rows, with the lamps lit overhead even in daytime because no daylight reached that far underground. Ilse had been dreading going back. In fact the room looked entirely ordinary. Coyle was at his counter with a fresh cup of tea, which he kept a hand around at all times. He did not look at her.

Hallam came in by the far door with a flat leather case under his arm. He was smaller than she had remembered from the Lantern Gate, and older; he walked carefully, the way a man walks in a house full of things he doesn’t want to break. But when he turned to face them and smiled, the room warmed by a degree.

“Good morning, my dears,” he said. “I hear the Long Gallery has been raining. I hope none of you drowned. It would make a great deal of paperwork for Merrin, and Merrin has had a trying morning already.”

Coyle went pink. The benches laughed.

Hallam set the case down on the lectern and unbuckled it. His hands were gloved in grey kid, close-fitting and soft, buttoned at the wrist with a single pearl button, and he moved them with the delicacy of long practice. From the case he drew a single sheet, old and brown and soft as cloth, and held it up.

“This,” he said, “is a charter of the Ammonite Hills. The northern quarries, the Delve and Corbel Edge, in the hand of a surveyor called Ottilie Vance, attested in the spring of 803. Four hundred years. It is not a copy. It is the original, from Level Four, and if any of you sneeze on it I shall have to ask you to leave the College.” He smiled over his half-moon spectacles. “I am going to pass it round. Hold it by the edges and feel it. I don’t mean the paper. I mean what the paper is doing.”

It came along the rows from hand to hand, each student holding it a moment longer than they meant to. Ilse watched their faces change as it reached them. Grice’s went blank with surprise. Piety Mercer closed her eyes. Kasimir, at the end of the row in front, held it perfectly level, and when he passed it on she saw his fingers stay curved for a moment, as though the shape of it were still in them.

Then it was in her hands.

The level in her ears dropped and deepened all at once, the way the sound of the sea deepens when you put your head under. It wasn’t loud. It was low, lower than any note she had ever felt, the sound of a bell heard through a hill. The sheet weighed nothing and it was heavy. It was heavy as a held breath is heavy. She looked down at it and saw the fine brown lines of the quarry faces, the hachures of the slopes, the little numbered benches of the old workings, and all round the margin, crowded in, dozens and dozens of small dark marks, re-attestations, the witness-marks of four centuries of surveyors who had walked those hills and agreed with Ottilie Vance and set their names to it.

Four hundred years of people saying *yes, this is true; yes, still.* That was what she was holding. That was what hummed in her ears: not the drawing, but the agreement.

She passed it on to Hen with great care. Her hands, after, felt strange and light and empty, like hands that have just put down a child.

“What you felt,” said Hallam, when the sheet had come back to him, “is the oldest truth in our trade. A charter is not strong because it is old. It is strong because it has been *kept*. Every surveyor who walks a boundary and finds it true and signs again adds to its hold. Every farmer who ploughs to the line and no farther, every child who walks the lane to school by the way the map says, agrees with it a little. The Ammonite Hills have been agreed with for four hundred years. That is why the Delve has never slipped.” He laid the charter down in its case as gently as if it might bruise. “Every map is an act of love, my dears. We hold her still so that the children of the Reach can find their way home.”

Ilse had come to the College ready to despise everything in it. She found that she could not despise that.


* * *


At the end of the lecture, as the benches emptied, he said, “Miss Marrow, would you stay a moment?” and she stayed, with Cass making faces at her from the door until Teo pulled her away by the sleeve.

Hallam was buckling the case. He took his time over it, the gloved fingers patient with the old straps.

“I am sorry about the tea,” Ilse said.

“The tea?” He looked up, puzzled, and then his face cleared. “Oh, the folios. Merrin told me. My dear, if I expelled every student whose ossel had got into the Register I should have no students and a great many ossels. Is it a nice one?”

“It’s—” She considered. “It eats ink.”

“They all eat ink. I had one myself, at your age. It ate most of a treatise on precedence and was never quite the same afterwards. Neither was I.” He finished the last strap and laid his hands flat on the case. “No. I wanted to ask you something else. I watched you at Benchmarking. I said then that you had a draftswoman’s hand, and I have been looking at your Charting work since, which Master Pell sends me, and I was right. Your cobble moved a hair and a half,” he added, with a twinkle, “which is very respectable, and the quartz vein was beautifully observed. Pell was quite moved.”

“Thank you.”

“I need a copyist in the Register. Two evenings a week, after supper. Fair copies of the older parish charters, for the working files, so the originals are handled less. It is dull work and close work and it wants a steady hand and a good eye, and I think you have both.” He paused. “It is paid, of course. Half a mark an evening. And you would have the run of Levels One to Three, which no first-year has had in some years. I think you would find them interesting.”

Half a mark an evening. A mark a week. Ilse had a little over four marks to her name, after the coach and the Chain & Anchor, and nothing at all coming in. She did the sum before she could stop herself: by Thaw, near twenty marks. It wasn’t the Long Field. It was a long way from the Long Field. But it was something going into the tin instead of out of it, for the first time since she had left Hobbet.

And it wasn’t only the money. That was the part she didn’t want to look at directly. He had watched her at Benchmarking. He had read her Charting work. He thought she had a good eye. No one had said that to her since Quince, and Quince had said it only once, drunk, and then denied it in the morning.

“I’d like that,” she said. “Very much.”

“Good. Good.” He looked genuinely pleased, like a man whose small plan has come right. “The week after next, then. Merrin will show you where everything is.” He took up the case and turned to go, and then turned back, and when he spoke again it was more quietly. “I gather you have been helping Field-Surveyor Brask.”

“Carrying instruments. Cleaning chains.”

“She is a remarkable surveyor. I want you to know that I think so. We were on the Sallow together, a long time ago.” He was silent a moment, and his face, in the lamplight, looked older. “She is a brilliant woman, my dear, broken by grief. It is not her fault. But grief makes people hard to stand next to, sometimes. Don’t let her break you.”

He touched her shoulder lightly with one grey-gloved hand, as an uncle might, and went away through the far door toward his study, and the door closed behind him without a sound.


* * *


Brask was in the Fieldwork shed, sitting on an upturned bucket by the open door with a chain across her knees, working tallow into the links with her good hand and holding the chain down with the heel of the grey one. Outside, the rain had thinned at last to a drifting mist, and the heath had gone silver with it, every spike of gorse strung with drops.

Ilse took the other end of the chain without being asked, and sat down on the step, and worked.

They did twenty links in silence. The tallow smelled of mutton and smoke. Brask’s pipe was in her teeth, unlit, and she moved it from one side of her mouth to the other now and then without seeming to notice she was doing it.

“Master Hallam’s asked me to copy in the Register,” said Ilse. “Two evenings a week. Paid.”

Brask’s hand went on working the tallow into the link. Forty. Forty-one.

She did not look up. The rag went round the link, and through it, and round again, and the mist moved over the heath outside like breath on a glass, and a long way off a cairn-crow said something short and harsh and was answered. Ilse waited. She found that she was holding her end of the chain very tightly, and made herself loosen her hand.

Forty-six. Forty-seven. The fifty-link tally, round and brass, went through Brask’s fingers and she rubbed it clean with her thumb.

She took the pipe out of her mouth, and looked at it, and put it back.

“Mind the stairs,” she said.


## Chapter 10: The Six Laws

“The Law of Witness,” said Master Hallam, and wrote it on the slate in a round, clear, old-fashioned hand. “A record binds only what its maker has personally measured.”

The chalk squeaked. His grey glove left a faint pale smear along the bottom of the slate where his wrist brushed it, and he didn’t notice, or didn’t mind.

“This is why we walk,” he said. “Not because the Board is cruel. Because the land knows the difference between a surveyor who has stood on it and a clerk who has copied what she said. You may copy the finest charter in the Register a thousand times, my dears, and every copy will be a picture. Only the walker binds.”

It was the fourteenth of Gleaning, and the Antechamber was cold. They had begun the Laws at ten and it was now nearly noon, and the slate had filled slowly down its length like a field being ploughed. Witness. Fidelity: a record holds in proportion to its truth. Precedence: where records conflict, the truer wins, and at equal truth the older. Attestation: a record binds only when its maker signs it with intent. At each one Hallam stopped and told them a story, a lost case or an old disaster, a road in the Salt Steps that had held for two hundred years on the strength of one careful woman’s chain, and the room listened as children listen. Ilse wrote it all down. She knew most of it already, in the way you know the bones of your own hand. She had never heard it said so well.

“The Law of Burden.” Hallam wrote it, and turned, and for a moment didn’t speak. “An attesting surveyor carries the strain of every record she has signed. When the land pulls against the record, she feels it. It is the price of the trade, and I will not pretend to you that it is small. Some of the masters you see at high table carry more than you will carry in your whole lives. We carry it gladly. It is what holds her.” He smiled. “And it is why I shall be grateful, when you are all very great surveyors, if you would draw your charters carefully, so that the land has nothing to complain of. A good charter is light. A careless one is a stone in your shoe for fifty years.”

The sixth he wrote at the bottom of the slate, and underlined. *The Law of Latitude.* A record can fix land only within the range of states it might plausibly take. Held land had a latitude of inches. The Margin had rods and furlongs. The deep Drift had more than anyone had ever measured.

“You cannot chart a mountain into a lake,” said Hallam, “however beautiful your drawing. You can chart a hedge a little to the west, if the ground would bear a hedge there. That is the whole of Concordance in a sentence, and Magistra Calder will spend a year teaching you the rest of it.” He laid down the chalk and dusted his gloves together. “Six Laws. They are the grammar of the world. Everything that holds, holds by them.”

Ilse waited.

She waited with her pen above the page, the way you wait at the top of a stair for the last step. He had stopped at six. He was turning to the lectern. He was opening his notes. He was going on, in that kind, careful voice, to talk about the Ninefold and how they had come to the Laws, by trial and long error, in the years of the Wandering.

There were seven.

Every hedge-forger in the Fallowmarch knew there were seven. Quince had taught her the seventh before he taught her to hold a chain properly. *The Law of Transfer*, he’d called it, sitting in his kitchen with his grey hands round a cup of something that wasn’t tea. *A mark can be ghosted. Trace a man’s mark under your own in blind ink, and the weight goes down his line instead of yours. They don’t teach it at the Big College, girl, and do you know why? Because the Big College would sooner nobody knew it could be done.* He had laughed, and coughed, and she had thought it was the drink.

She had thought for nine years that the College didn’t teach it because the College didn’t know. Gentlemen with Patents didn’t trace dead men’s marks in the dark. They had no need. Now she sat in the Register Antechamber with the six Laws in chalk on the slate before her, and the seventh missing from the bottom like a stair taken out, and found she could not tell which was true: that they didn’t know, or that they didn’t say.

“Master Hallam.”

It was Kasimir. He had raised his hand, not high, the way a man does who expects to be seen.

“Mr. Reyne.”

“The Law of Precedence speaks of conflicting records,” Kasimir said. “Two honest surveyors who disagree. But what of contrary records? A record made untrue on purpose, within latitude, by someone who knows how to make the land believe it. Does such a record hold by the same Laws? Or is there some other principle at work?”

He did not look round at Ilse. He did not need to. She felt the question go through the room and come to rest against her like a hand laid flat on a door.

Hallam took off his spectacles and polished them on a fold of his gown. He seemed to consider it seriously, which was worse than if he hadn’t.

“A very good question,” he said. “And the answer, I am afraid, is a very dull one. A contrary record holds by Fidelity, like any other. It holds exactly as well as it is made, and no better, and the moment a truer record is set against it, it fails. There is no special principle, Mr. Reyne.” He put the spectacles back on and smiled at the room. “Forgery is not a law of nature. It is a failure of character.”

They laughed. All of them. Hen laughed, and Teo, who laughed at everything Hallam said because he liked him, and Piety Mercer laughed with a little sideways glance at Cass as if the joke had been meant for the Margin. Even Kasimir’s mouth moved, though he wrote in his notebook while it did.

Ilse didn’t laugh. She sat with her pen above the page and felt the heat climb up her neck and into her face the way water climbs a wick, and she kept her eyes on the slate and her face perfectly still. *A failure of character.* Agnes Tull’s Pearmain. The Hedleys’ ditch, old Brack’s gatepost, twenty other small corrections across three parishes, every one of them restoring a line that a better-paid surveyor had moved. And under every one of them a dead man’s mark, carrying it. She thought of Quince’s grey hands round the cup.

She wrote, very small, at the bottom of her notes, *7. Transfer.* Then she inked it out until it was a black bar, and turned the page.


* * *


Master Pim Halloway wore three watches. One was on his left wrist, one on his right, and one on a chain across his waistcoat, and he consulted all three at the start of every lecture and frowned at the differences, which he wrote on the board in the top left corner each morning under the heading *Today’s Disagreement.* This morning it was eleven seconds.

“Eleven,” he said. “Eleven s-seconds between the three of them. Which is right?”

Nobody answered. Nobody ever answered. He seemed to like it that way.

“None of them,” said Halloway. “All of them. That is the l-lesson. Sit, sit.”

He was a slight, twitchy man of forty with a high forehead and hair that stood up at the back where he ran his hands through it, and when he was talking about anything but numbers his words came out in a stammer, catching and restarting like a cart on a rutted road. When he came to the numbers the ruts smoothed out and he went along like a canal barge. Ilse had liked him at Benchmarking. She liked him now. That was the trouble.

“Error theory,” he said, and the stammer went away. “Every measurement you will ever make is wrong. The chain stretches. The ground is not flat. The man at the far end is cold and does not pull straight. You will never know the true length of anything. What you can know is how *probably* wrong you are, and in which direction, and by how much. That is not a weakness of the trade. That is the trade.” He chalked five figures on the board, one under another. “A line in the Old Court. Measured five times, by five of last year’s links. Four chains twelve links. Four fifteen. Four eleven. Four fourteen. Four thirteen. What is the length of the line? Miss Marrow.”

She stood. The figures looked up at her from the board. She knew this; she had done this all her life. You chained a line, and chained it back, and if the two disagreed you chained it again, and you took the one that felt right.

“Four chains thirteen,” she said. “Near enough.”

“Good. How did you get it?”

“The fifteen’s wrong. It stands out. Someone let the chain sag. Throw that away, and the others are round about thirteen.”

There was a short silence. Halloway’s face went kind and pained, as if she had shown him a drawing of a horse with five legs.

“Why is the fifteen wrong?”

“Because—” She looked at it. “It’s the farthest out.”

“The eleven is as far out the other way. Why not throw that away?”

She had no answer. She had thrown away the fifteen because it looked wrong, the way the stream bank on the Walk had looked wrong, and her hand had moved to smooth it before her head had been asked.

“You cannot discard a measurement because you dislike its face, Miss Marrow,” said Halloway, not unkindly. “If you do that, you are not measuring the line. You are measuring your opinion of the line. Come up to the board, please. Show me the residuals.”

She came up to the board. She took the chalk he gave her. She looked at the five figures.

She did not know what a residual was.

She knew how to chain a field in a rainstorm with a drunk man on the other end. She knew how to read a hedge-line through four hundred years of ploughing. She could draw a boundary so true that the ground went to it like a dog to its bed. And she stood at the board in front of fifty-odd first-years with a stick of chalk in her hand and could not have told them what a residual was if they had offered her the Long Field for it.

“The d-difference,” said Halloway gently, “between each measurement and the mean.”

She did that. She could do subtraction. Minus one, plus two, minus two, plus one, nothing. She wrote them in a column. Behind her somebody shifted on a bench.

“Now square them, and sum the squares.”

“Why square them?”

“Because we must,” said Halloway, “for reasons I will explain next week. For now, trust me.”

One, four, four, one, nothing. Ten. She wrote ten.

“Divide by one less than the number of measurements. Take the square root. Multiply by nought point six seven four five.”

She stood with the chalk against the board. Ten divided by four was two and a half. She knew that. The square root of two and a half was—something. One and a half, and a bit. She didn’t know how to find the bit. Quince had never needed to know the bit, and she had never needed to know anything Quince didn’t. She tried, under her breath, and lost the thread of it, and started again, and the room behind her went from quiet to a particular kind of quiet that she remembered from the village school in Wick Fallow, the quiet of thirty children enjoying someone else’s trouble.

From the third bench Piety Mercer said, not loudly, “One point five eight one.”

Halloway didn’t look at Piety. He didn’t take the chalk away from Ilse. He came and stood beside her at the board, a little stooped, his three watches ticking out of time with one another, and when he spoke it was only to her.

“One point five eight one. Times the factor, one point oh seven. That is the probable error of a single measurement, in links. Divide by the root of five for the probable error of the mean, and you have four chains thirteen links, plus or minus nought point four eight.” He wrote it under her column, neatly. “You were right about thirteen, Miss Marrow. You were right for the wrong reason. Your eye is very good. It is better than the arithmetic of most people in this room. But an eye cannot tell you how much to trust it. Only this can.” He tapped the board. “Sit down. You did well to try.”

She sat down. Her face was burning for the second time in a week.

At the end of the lecture, as they filed out, he caught her eye and beckoned, and she went to him, braced.

“Miss Marrow,” he said. “You w-were—you were embarrassed.”

“I didn’t know what a residual was.”

“No. Now you do.” He was winding the watch on his left wrist, carefully, not looking at her. “Error is not s-sin, Miss Marrow. Not knowing is not sin. Throwing away the fifteen because you did not like it—that is the beginning of sin. *Unrecorded* error. That is the only kind that kills anyone.” He snapped the watch-case shut. “Next lecture. Squares. Come early, if you like. I am always here early. The watches insist.”


* * *


The Errata Society met in the Mess kitchen after the last bell, when Cook had gone to bed and the great ranges had been banked down to a red glow, and it was, as far as Ilse could tell, neither a society nor particularly concerned with errata. There were about a dozen of them that night, chains and furlongs and a few links, sitting on the scrubbed tables and the flour bins in the warm dark under hanging copper pans. The chair was a large, mild furlong called Dunstan Moss, who presided with a ladle and banged it on a pan when people talked over one another, which was always.

“Tonight’s motion,” said Moss, “is that a map is a theft. Somebody please argue it, or we’ll just eat.”

“We’ll just eat,” said several people at once.

Teo was already at the end range with one of his spirit-stoves going on the iron top, because he didn’t trust the range, and a slab of pastry on a floured board. He had been told about the motion and had decided it was beyond him. He had decided instead to explain probable error to Ilse, and he had decided to do it with pie.

“Look,” he said. He had the rolling pin in one hand. “You roll the crust. You want it a quarter of an inch, all over, because a quarter of an inch is right, and my grandmother will hit you with a spoon if it isn’t. Now. It’s never a quarter all over. It’s a bit thick here—” he pressed the pastry with his thumb, “—and a bit thin there. Yes? Those are your residuals. How far each bit is from right.”

“I know what a residual is now,” said Ilse.

“I know. I’m only saying. So. Why square them?” He looked at her earnestly over the rolling pin. “Because a crust that’s a bit too thick in two places isn’t half as bad as a crust that’s *very* too thick in one. It’s worse. The very-too-thick bit is raw in the middle. Raw is much worse than a bit chewy. A mistake twice as big isn’t twice as bad, it’s four times as bad, because—well, because it’s raw. So you square it. It makes the big mistakes count for more. It’s fair to the crust.”

Ilse looked at the pastry. She could find nothing wrong with it.

“And one less than the number,” she said. “Why one less?”

“Ah.” Teo set down the rolling pin. “That’s the oven. You’ve made five crusts, yes? And you used one of them to find out how hot the oven is. You burned it a bit, finding out. So you’ve only really got four left that can tell you anything new. You spent one finding the middle.” He considered. “That’s not quite right. But it’s nearly right. Halloway would say it’s within probable error.”

“Teo,” said Ilse, “you’re a genius.”

He went red from his collar to his curls. “I’m not. It’s just crust. In Lisk everyone knows about crust. In Lisk we don’t measure bread, we *understand* it.”

“That’s the most Lisk thing anyone’s ever said,” said Hen, who had come over to see what was on the stove. She leaned over his shoulder to look into the pan, where onions were doing something golden, and her blond head came very close to his ear, and Teo stood absolutely still with the rolling pin in his hand like a man who has been told not to startle a bird.

“What’s that going to be?” said Hen.

“It’s—” Teo swallowed. “It’s going to be a pie. Mutton and onion. With a crust of probable error.”

Hen laughed, her big laugh, right by his ear, and Teo turned the colour of a boiled crab and dropped the rolling pin on his foot.

The kitchen door banged open, and Cass came in backwards out of the dark with a stone jar under each arm and a third hanging from her fingers by its loop, and said, “Hey-ho. Who’s arguing for theft? Because I am.”

“Cass,” said Moss, with feeling, “is that cider?”

“It’s rain,” said Cass. “Very old rain. From an apple. Don’t ask me where from.” She set the jars on the table. A great many people became friendly to her at once. She pulled the cork from one with her teeth, poured it into whatever was nearest, a pudding basin, and passed it along, and swung herself up onto a flour bin with her heels drumming on the side.

“Here’s my argument,” she said. “You lot come out to the Margin with your chains. You walk a bit of heath that’s been nobody’s, that’s been the wayhorns’ and the gorse’s and our wagon’s on the way to winter, and you draw it on a bit of paper and put your name at the bottom, and then it’s *yours*. It’s held. It can’t move. It can’t go anywhere it used to go. Ground didn’t ask to be held. Wayhorns didn’t ask. *We* didn’t ask.” She drank from the basin and passed it on. “That’s theft. You’re taking the moving out of it. You just do it with very nice handwriting.”

“But it’s the moving that kills people,” said a chain from the Salt Steps. “The Lost Wing. The Wandering. If we didn’t hold the ground, it’d—”

“It’d *go*. Yes. It goes for us too. We go with it. That’s what feet are for.” Cass grinned. “I’m not saying don’t hold Tessary. Hold Tessary. Hold the whole Reach, I don’t care, it’s yours. But you keep coming *out*.”

They argued it for an hour, while the cider went round and Teo’s pie came out of the iron oven of the range with its crust risen and golden and very slightly uneven, which he apologized for and everyone ate anyway. Dunstan Moss banged his ladle at intervals. Hen sat next to Teo on the end of the table and ate her slice off his plate as well as her own. And at some point, warm with cider, Ilse found herself saying, “It’s only theft the first time.”

They turned to look at her.

“The first map,” she said. “The one that says *this is mine* when it wasn’t anybody’s. That’s the theft. After that, every map is just a receipt. It says what was taken, and who has it, and how much.” She looked at the cider in the basin. “And if the receipt’s wrong, you can write a better one.”

Cass looked at her for a long moment from the flour bin.

“Huh,” she said.

Moss banged his ladle. “I’m calling it a draw,” he said, “on account of the pie.”


* * *


On the twentieth of Gleaning she sat alone at the table in the Wren Stair sitting room, with the others asleep and Sorry asleep in the coal scuttle and a candle burning down beside her, and did thirty problems from the back of Halloway’s textbook.

She did them slowly. She did them on Hen’s scrap paper, writing every step, every residual, every square, even the ones she could see in her head, because she had learned that seeing it in her head was where it went wrong. She found square roots the way Teo had shown her over the cold end of the pie, guessing and halving the difference and guessing again, a little closer every time, until the figure stood still. She did not throw anything away. When a number looked wrong she wrote it down anyway and worked it through, and twice it turned out to be wrong, and once it turned out to be the only right one.

At two in the morning she checked her answers against the back of the book.

Twenty-six.

She sat and looked at the page for a while. Four wrong. She went back to each of the four and found where she had gone off, a slipped figure in one, a square root she had stopped too soon in another, and she wrote beside each one in small square letters what the mistake had been and where. It took twenty minutes. It was the most satisfying twenty minutes she had spent since she came to the College.

Then she pinched out the candle and went to bed, and lay in the dark with her hands behind her head, listening to the wardrobe ease itself another hair out from the wall, and thought, without quite meaning to, *nought point four eight*, and then, *plus or minus*, and fell asleep before she could get any further.


## Chapter 11: The Ladder

The Ladder was a tower at the far end of the Third Range, square and windowless and older than the rooms around it, and it was called the Ladder because the only way up it was one. An iron ladder ran up a shaft in its middle, bolted to the stone, and off the shaft at every landing opened a single door. Behind each door was a room. There were six of them, one above another, and they were called the rungs. They were the least held rooms in the College that anyone could find.

“Rung Five,” said Lisbet Arnaud, reading from her list at the foot of the shaft. The senior proctor had a voice like a well-oiled lock. “Marrow, Anselm, Tenter, Solberg. Turn out your pockets.”

She took Teo’s tinderbox, which he had forgotten, and a stub of candle from Cass that Cass claimed was for luck, and ran her hands down their coats. She did not touch Cass’s hair, where among the black braids a dozen knotted route-cords were tied off in their usual places, red and brown and undyed, and Ilse, who had asked Cass the night before to wear every cord she owned, was careful not to look at them.

That morning, like every link in the College, Ilse had gone down to the Crypt of Benchmarks and put a penny into the grey palm of a surveyor who had stilled two centuries ago. She had felt foolish doing it, and done it anyway.

Master Halloway stood at the foot of the ladder with all three watches out in his hands at once, like a man dealing cards.

“The rules,” he said, and stammered on none of them. “You will have one chain, one folding rule, one plumb-line, one sounding-mallet, a board with paper pinned, a box of pins, pen, ink and chalk. You will have two hours exactly, from the closing of the door. You will produce an attested survey of the room and its contents. You will have no light. None. The room will not hold still for you. It has a latitude of something above a rod, and it uses it.” He looked up from the watches. “You will f-find it very dark.”

Kasimir’s party had already gone up to Rung Two.

The door of Rung Five was oak, lined on the inside with black felt. The room beyond it, in the light from the shaft, was bare stone: a floor, four walls, a low ceiling, and a squat stone pillar standing a little off the middle like a man who has forgotten why he came in. Then the door swung shut behind them and the bar dropped on the outside with a sound like a coffin lid, and there was nothing at all.


* * *


She had thought she knew dark from moonless nights in the Fallowmarch. This was dark the way deep water is wet. It pressed on her eyes. After a few seconds her eyes, offended, began making their own light: slow green blooms, a drifting purple wheel, pricks like sparks from a struck flint. She shut her eyes against it, and with them shut could at least stop trying.

The level was louder in the dark. It hummed in her ears like a hive, low and uneven, and under her feet she could feel the floor not quite still: a faint slow uncertainty, as though she stood on the deck of a ship moored in a harbour.

“Hen,” she said. “Count.”

“One,” said Hen, at once, and then went on under her breath. Ilse had asked her two days ago whether she could count seconds for two hours without losing her place, and Hen had looked at her as though she had asked whether she could breathe. *I counted the brine-pans for my da from the age of six. You lose your count on a pan, it crusts, you lose the pan.* Her voice in the dark was a small steady murmur, like a stream heard through a wall.

“Right,” said Ilse. “Teo. Walls. Cass, floor. I want corners first.”

They had planned it in the Wren Stair over three nights and a great deal of sausage, and it went at first as planned, which frightened her. Teo found the wall by walking into it, apologized to it, and began working along it with the sounding-mallet, knocking at every pace and listening. Ilse heard the knock change: dull, dull, dull, and then a hollow note like a knuckle on a cask.

“There’s a niche,” said Teo. “Here. About a yard wide, two feet deep. A hearth, maybe, bricked at the back. And further on, here—” knock, knock, “—that’s a doorway that’s been filled in. Different stone. It rings higher. Thinner.” He went on. “Corner.”

“Mark it. Chalk. A.”

They chalked a cross at the foot of each corner, by feel, and Teo called them as he found them, A at the corner nearest the door, then B, C, D round the room. Cass had gone down on her hands and knees and was crawling across the floor with her palms flat on the stone, like someone feeling for a dropped ring.

“Ground’s leaning,” she said. “Toward D. Not much. It’s—” She was silent a moment. “It’s uneasy. Like a horse before weather. Don’t like it.”

“Count?”

“Four hundred and eighty,” said Hen.

“Pillar,” said Cass, a little later, and then, “Ow. Found the pillar.”

They chained it. It was the oldest kind of survey there was, and the only kind possible in the dark. You couldn’t sight an angle without light. But you didn’t need angles. You needed lengths. Three sides fixed a triangle; a triangle could only be one shape. Chain every side of the room, and both of its diagonals as well, and the room had nowhere to hide.

So Ilse held the end of the chain in the crack of corner A and Teo walked it out along the wall to B, and when he had it taut he called, and Ilse ran her fingers along the links from her end, counting the brass tallies by touch: one point at ten links, two at twenty, three at thirty, four at forty, the round tag at fifty. Then two links past it, and the handle.

“Fifty-two,” she said. “AB, fifty-two links.”

“Count fifteen hundred and twelve,” said Hen.

Hen called the count every time a length was taken, and Ilse said the length and the count back to her, and Hen kept both. That had been Hen’s idea. *If you’re going to forget something,* she had said, *forget it with me.* AB fifty-two. BC thirty-eight. CD fifty-two. DA thirty-eight. Then the long diagonal, B to D across the room, with Teo stumbling round the pillar in the dark: sixty-six links. Then the pillar tied from A and from B. Each number went into Hen and stayed there.

Ilse knelt at the board with the cords.

Cass had untied them from her braids with bad grace the night before, one at a time. *This one’s the road to Corrow, and you don’t cut it, paper-wall. You don’t even look at it rude.* Ilse didn’t cut it. She tied knots in it. With the folding rule, which had a brass stud set into its edge at every inch so that you could read it with a thumbnail, she measured off cords to scale, one inch to five links. AB, ten and two-fifths inches. AD, seven and three-fifths. BD, thirteen and a fifth. Each length she marked with a knot pulled tight, and checked against the studs, and checked again.

Then she pinned A and B to the board and took the AD cord from A and the BD cord from B and drew them both out taut across the paper until the two knots met. Where they met was D. It could be nowhere else. She pushed a pin through both knots into the board, and felt the triangle under her fingers, three cords drawn tight between three pins, a shape that could not lie.

“That’s pretty,” said Cass, who had crawled over to feel it. Her fingers moved lightly over the cords. “That’s nearly a song.”

“It’s a triangle.”

“Song’s just a triangle that goes somewhere.”

C from B and D, the same way. Then the check line: A to C, the second diagonal, which the figure on the board said ought to be sixty-two links and three-quarters if the room was what they had measured. Teo and Ilse chained it.

“Sixty-three,” said Ilse.

“Count two thousand six hundred and ten.”

It closed, within a quarter link. Ilse sat back on her heels and felt the tight bright pleasure of it go through her: a room she could not see, laid out under her hands in pins and cords, every side agreeing with every other. Forty-three minutes gone. They could tie in the niche and the old doorway at leisure and sign with half an hour to spare.

The level dropped out of her ears like a stone down a well.


* * *


She felt it a moment before it happened. Then the room groaned.

It was a long, low grinding that came from everywhere and from the floor most of all, and the floor under her knees slid a few inches sideways, smoothly, the way a rug slides when someone treads on its edge. Teo shouted. There was a clatter: the sounding-mallet, dropped. The plumb-line Cass had hung from a pin at corner A began to swing; Ilse heard the bob tap the wall and tap it again.

Then it stopped. The level came back, thinner than before, a high uneasy whine.

“Everyone,” said Ilse. “Say something.”

“Here,” said Hen, and then, without a break, “three thousand and forty-one.”

“Here,” said Teo. “I dropped the mallet. I’m sorry.”

“Grain’s turned,” said Cass, sharply. Ilse heard her palms slap the floor. “It was leaning to D. Now it’s leaning to C. Whole room’s gone round, paper-wall.”

“Chain BD,” said Ilse. “Now.”

They chained it. Her fingers ran along the links: four points, the round tag, then four points again, which from that end meant sixty, and on past it, counting.

“Sixty-nine,” she said. Her voice came out level. That surprised her. “It was sixty-six.”

“AB?”

They chained AB. Fifty-two. BC: thirty-eight. The sides were the same, to the link, and the long diagonal had grown three links.

Ilse knelt in the dark with the chain in her hands and understood. The room hadn’t moved. It had racked: leaned, the way a bookcase leans when you push its top, every shelf still the same length, but the whole thing slewed out of square into a lozenge. AB stood where it had stood. The far wall had slid along it. Their figure on the board was a room that no longer existed.

“Chain it again,” she said.

They waited. Hen counted. Ilse could hear Teo breathing, quick and shallow, the breath of a man trying hard not to think about the ground. At three thousand four hundred they chained BD again. Sixty-nine. At four thousand, again.

“Seventy.”

“It’s still going,” said Teo. His voice cracked. “Marrow. It’s still going.”

“I know.”

“We have to measure it all again, fast, all of it, before it—”

“It’ll move while we do. It’ll move while we pin it. It’ll move while we sign.” Ilse heard her own voice saying it and was almost calm. “Every number we take is already wrong by the time we write it down.”

Nobody said anything. Hen counted.

She knew what she could do. She had known it from the moment the floor slid. Take the figure on the board, the clean one, the rectangle that closed, and ink it, and sign it, and lean on it. Lean the room back into square. The room had been square, nearly, when they came in; that was well within its latitude. A good confident charter, beautifully drawn, and a hard push behind the pen, and the room would grind back to the shape she said it was, and hold. It would hold beautifully. It would be a lie, and it would hold, and nobody would ever know but her.

It would be the stream bank again. She had made the bank on her Walk survey agree with itself because it would not agree with the ground, and it had felt so right, so clean, that her hand had done it before she had decided. She felt that same rightness now, waiting at the end of her fingers.

*You cannot discard a measurement because you dislike its face, Miss Marrow.*

Seventy at four thousand. Sixty-nine at three thousand four hundred. Sixty-nine at three thousand and forty-one, just after the groan.

She knelt where she was in the dark.

“Hen,” she said. “When we chain BD again, what count will it be?”

“Four thousand six hundred, if you keep to it.”

“Teo. If it’s seventy-one at four thousand six hundred—”

“Then it’s a link every ten minutes,” said Teo. And then, slowly, as though he were hearing it as he said it: “Oh. Oh, Marrow.”

At four thousand six hundred they chained BD.

“Seventy-one.”

“A link every six hundred counts,” said Teo. The tremble had gone out of his voice. It had the far-off sound it took on when he was doing long division to steady himself. “Steady as a clock. It’s not lurching any more, it’s creeping. If it’s creeping steady—”

“Then we don’t need to beat it,” said Ilse. “We don’t need to measure it all again. The sides don’t change. Only the lean changes, and we know how fast. We pick a count. The count we sign at. We work out what the room will be at that count, and we draw *that*. And then we write on the sheet how fast it’s going.”

“That’s not allowed,” said Hen, and then, “Is that allowed? Four thousand six hundred and thirty.”

“Nobody said it wasn’t.”

“Ground’ll like it,” said Cass, out of the dark. She sounded surprised. “You’re not telling it to stop. You’re telling it what it’s doing. Ground likes being told what it’s doing.”

There was a thought in that, one that brushed past Ilse’s mind like a moth in a dark room and was gone before she could see its shape. There wasn’t time to go after it.

“Teo. If it’s racking, the other diagonal shortens. Can you say how much?”

“The squares of the two diagonals,” said Teo, “add up to twice the squares of the sides. Always. In a lozenge. It’s in Euclid, or it’s in a pie, I forget which.” A pause. “Twice fifty-two squared and thirty-eight squared is eight thousand two hundred and ninety-six. So at seventy-one, AC wants to be fifty-seven and a bit. By four thousand eight hundred it’ll have crept. Fifty-six and a half, about.”

They chained AC at four thousand eight hundred. Fifty-six links and a half, near as the tallies would tell.

“That’s within probable error,” said Teo, and laughed, a little wildly, and then stopped.


* * *


It went fast after that, because it had to. They chose to sign at the count of six thousand nine hundred, five minutes before the bar would lift. Teo worked it out in his head with his eyes shut, though there was no need to shut them: at six thousand nine hundred BD would be seventy-four links and eight-tenths, and AC fifty-two, near enough. They tied the pillar again from A and B, twice, and Hen gave the count each time, and Teo carried the lean forward to the signing count. Ilse retied the cords and pinned the figure afresh. She dropped a pin and found it again by touch in the crack between two flags. Her hands did not shake; she noticed that from a long way off.

When the new figure was pinned she laid a sheet of paper over the board, smoothed it with the flat of her hand, and pressed down until she felt the pinheads through the paper like the knuckles of a hand under a sheet. Then she took a fresh pin and pricked through the paper beside each one, a hole at every corner, every tie, both edges of the niche, both sides of the filled door. It was how Quince had taught her to copy a map she could not take away: prick it through, and join the holes. She took the pins out. She found each pricked hole with her left fingertip, laid the edge of the folding rule against it and the next, and drew the line between them with her right, slow, the pen held light so it would not catch.

Then the words. She pinned a cord taut across the bottom of the sheet to keep her letters level, and wrote, large and square and slow, one letter at a time:

*At count of attestation. BD 74 links 8. AC 52. The figure racks about AB. BD lengthens one link in each ten minutes. Recorded.*

“Six thousand six hundred,” said Hen.

“Last check. BD.”

They chained it. Ilse ran her fingers along the links. Seventy-four, and a third of the next, near as she could feel.

“It’s on time,” said Teo softly. “It’s on time.”

They knelt round the board in the dark, the four of them, close enough that Ilse could feel the warmth of Teo’s shoulder on one side and Cass’s knee against hers on the other, and Hen counted down from six thousand eight hundred and ninety in a voice that never once quickened. At *six thousand nine hundred* they signed: Hen’s mark, Teo’s, Cass’s, and Ilse last, her wren’s foot found by touch on the bottom corner, three strokes and a spur.

The charter took. She felt it go into the room like a key into a lock, and the faint creep under her knees, which she had stopped noticing, stopped. The level steadied to a single held note. Nothing groaned. Nothing slid.

“Seven thousand,” said Hen, after a while. And then, “Can I stop counting now?”


* * *


When the bar lifted and the door swung in, the light from the shaft was so bright that they all cried out and covered their faces. Then Halloway was in the doorway with a lamp, and behind him Lisbet Arnaud with another, and Ilse saw Rung Five for the first time since they had come in.

It was not a square room any longer. It leaned. The far wall stood slewed along the near one, so that every corner was a little wrong, two sharp and two blunt, and the pillar stood off-centre in a way that made the eye ache, as if the whole room had been drawn on a sheet of paper and someone had pushed the top of the sheet sideways. In the middle of the floor lay the board with its pins and cords, a cat’s cradle of knotted red and brown string stretched between brass heads. Beside it lay the charter, the lines a little wavering but every one of them joined, and the black words straggling across the bottom.

Halloway lifted the sheet and read it by the lamp. He read it twice. He chained BD himself, and then AC, and wrote the figures on his cuff.

“*Recorded*,” he said.

Nobody spoke.

“You w-wrote,” said Halloway, “you wrote down how fast you were wrong.” His face had gone pink. He looked at the four of them, and at the route-cords on the board, and back at the sheet. “In eleven years of setting the Ladder no one has ever written down the *rate*. They fight it. They chain like madmen and sign in a panic and half the time the charter tears. You asked it how f-fast it was going. And then you believed the answer.” He held the sheet as Hallam had held the charter of the Ammonite Hills. “This is error theory. This is the whole of error theory. Oh, *well* done.”


* * *


The results went up on the board in the Hall of Instruments at six. Rung Two, Reyne, Devane, Mercer, Grice: held to a fifth of a link, first. Rung Five, Marrow, Anselm, Tenter, Solberg: held to two-fifths, second, *with commendation for the rate annotation*. Under that, in Halloway’s hand: *Best result by a Walker party since 1189.*

Kasimir was standing in front of the board when she came in. He read it, and then he turned and saw her. They were alone for a moment in the crowd, the way two people sometimes are.

“Your room racked,” he said.

“Yours didn’t?”

“Mine racked. I chained the whole of it in forty minutes, and again in the last twenty, and signed the second set.” He looked at the board. “I fought it. You recorded it.” He seemed about to say more, and then didn’t. “Interesting,” he said, and went away.


* * *


The Chain & Anchor in Verity Cross was full to the doors that night, as it always was the night of the Ladder, with first-years who had passed and first-years who had failed and furlongs who had come to watch both. There was a fiddler, and a man with a squeezebox who knew three tunes and played them with great conviction, and the lanterns along the beams swung a little in the heat and smoke so that the light went to and fro across the faces like light on water.

Teo danced with Hen. He could not dance, and Hen didn’t care. She took both his hands and pulled him out among the tables, and he went red and stepped on her twice and said sorry both times, and then forgot to be sorry and swung her round so that her feet left the floor and her big laugh rang out over the fiddle. Cass danced with a Lisk furlong, then with Orla Devane, then with a fisherman from the harbour whom nobody knew and who turned out to dance better than anyone in the room, and came back to the table flushed and grinning with her braids half-undone and three of her route-cords missing, which she said she would go back for in the morning.

Ilse didn’t dance. She sat at the end of the bench with her back to the wall and a cup of cider she wasn’t drinking, and watched Teo’s face when Hen laughed. Her fingertips were still sore from the pins.

She was happy. She noticed it the way you notice you have walked out onto ice. She had not meant to be. She had come to the College for a shield and a Patent, and she had a party now, and a room that moved and a cat that wasn’t a cat, and three people who would kneel round a board with her in the dark and trust her count. She had something to lose here. She had not had anything to lose since she was twelve.

Hen came back to the table, out of breath, and dropped onto the bench beside her.

“You’re not dancing.”

“I’m watching.”

“Tide’s out on watching,” said Hen, and took her by both wrists, and pulled.


## Chapter 12: Watermarks

The Register hummed.

A vault cut seven levels down into a granite headland ought by rights to be as quiet as the inside of a stone. But every box on every shelf was a place, and every place was held, and every held place pressed very faintly against the back of Ilse’s skull. Walking the aisles of Level Two was like walking through a choir whose members were all humming different notes under their breath and pretending not to.

The charters were shelved by parish, in boxes of grey board with brass corners. The Ammonite boxes had a low, thick note, like a cellar door swinging shut. The Lisk boxes buzzed, which seemed fair. The Fallowmarch boxes stood along the far wall and sounded thinner and higher, a reed note. She had found Wick Fallow on her first evening and stood in front of it longer than she’d meant to, not touching it.

She had been coming down two evenings a week since the week before the Ladder, to copy. It was the dullest work in the College, and she loved it with a passion she kept to herself. Old gall-ink browned and bit into the paper, and the reading-room clerks wanted fair copies a magistrate could read without a glass. A copy held nothing. Under the Law of Witness you could copy the entire Reach and not move a pebble, because you had not walked it. So the Register let a first-year link with a good hand sit at a long oak table for four marks a month and reproduce the oldest promises in the world with a steel nib.

Tonight it was Bellwether, a hill parish in the Ammonites, chartered in 811. She set the proportional dividers to one-to-one, walked them from the original under its glass to the fresh folio and back, and pricked the corners of each field so lightly that the holes would vanish under the ink. The hedges went in with a hard pencil first, and only when everything sat where it should did she ink. Quince had made her copy his maps forty times before he let her forge one. *You can’t lie well,* he’d said, *until you can tell the truth exactly.*

“Still at it, my dear?”

She hadn’t heard him on the stair. Master Hallam came down the aisle between Crane Abbas and Cullingford with a tray in his gloved hands: a brown teapot, two cups and a saucer of ginger biscuits. He set it down without a single rattle of china.

“I thought you might be cold,” he said. “She keeps a chill down here.” He leaned over her copy, and his spectacles slid down his nose, and he pushed them back with one grey-gloved knuckle. “Bellwether. Oswy Tarrant’s hand. He drank, poor man. You can see it in the long strokes. Look how the hedges lean on the right side of the sheet; that’s Friday.”

Ilse looked. They did lean. She had copied the lean without thinking.

“You’ve kept it,” Hallam said, pleased. “Most copyists straighten him. They think they’re doing him a kindness.” He poured her tea and set the cup by her right hand, well clear of the folio. “You hold your pen like someone who was taught by a craftsman, Miss Marrow, not by a schoolroom.”

Her nib stopped. She made it start again.

“My father was a farmer,” she said.

“Then a farmer taught you well.” He didn’t press. He never did; it was the thing she liked best about him. He took a biscuit and ate half of it. “Don’t stay past the ninth bell. Bellwether has been there since 811. She’ll keep another night.”

He went up the stair slowly, his gloved hand on the rail, like a man who has once fallen and never quite forgiven the stairs. Ilse sat with both hands around the cup, warming the ache out of her knuckles and feeling, absurdly, looked after.


* * *


Merrin Coyle came down an hour later with an armful of paper that reached to his chin.

“Miss Marrow, I’m so sorry, would you mind awfully—” He lowered the stack onto the table, apologized to the table, and straightened his cuffs, which did no good; they had been ink-stained so long that the stain had become the cuff. “It’s the year-store. The Master likes every Morning Survey folio kept, every one, and Level One is full to the ceiling, and they have to be bundled by year and boxed, and you have such neat hands.”

He said it as if neat hands were a medical condition. Ilse took pity on him.

The bundles were Morning Survey sheets, kept for no reason she could see except that the Register had never in its life thrown anything away: each party’s stretch for each morning, drawn in a hurry by cold students in the dark. They smelled of damp wool and lamp oil. Ilse squared them, tied them with linen tape, and wrote the year on each label in Coyle’s copperplate, which was not hard to imitate. It was mindless work, until her hands did the thing they always did with paper and lifted a sheet to the lamp.

Quince had taught her that too. *Know your paper before your paper knows you.* So, without thinking, she held a sheet from Candlemonth 1200 up to the flame.

The light came through the linen-rag in a warm, uneven glow. The chain lines ran down it an inch and a quarter apart, and in the lower corner stood a wheat-sheaf with a ribbon round its middle and the letters D & S beneath: Dunmore and Sons of Tessary, who had made the College’s paper for as long as the College had made maps.

She picked up the next bundle, Thaw 1201, and held a sheet to the flame out of nothing more than the rhythm of the thing.

The wheat-sheaf was gone. In its place a bridge stood in the corner of the sheet, a little bridge of three arches over a wavy line of water, with no letters at all. The chain lines were closer, an inch apart or a hair under. The sheet was heavier in the hand and harder-sized, so that it clicked when she flexed it instead of sighing.

Seedtime 1201: bridge. Highsun 1202: bridge. The last week of Candlemonth 1200: wheat-sheaf. The year had turned, and the paper had turned with it.

“Master Coyle,” she said, “the paper changed.”

Coyle looked up as if she had told him the stair was on fire, then saw the sheet and the lamp, and his shoulders came down half an inch.

“Oh—yes. The Master changed the supplier.” He blotted a figure. “Cheaper. From Lisk. The Bridge Mill, on the Ambry. Dunmore’s had got very dear, and the Survey goes through two hundred sheets a morning.” He laughed apologetically, as if the economy had been his own idea. “You’d think the Register could afford Dunmore’s. But he’s very careful with the College’s money.”

She tied the bundle and put it in its box. It was a detail, the kind a clerk changes on a Tuesday to save four marks a quarter. She put it in the long untidy cupboard at the back of her mind where Quince had taught her to keep everything she couldn’t yet use, and left a little before the ninth bell, because the Master had asked her to.


* * *


The way home ran out through the Old Court, under the Lantern Gate with its single flame burning on the keystone, and down the covered causeway into the Drift Wing, whose corridors stretched a little every night, like a cat in its sleep. Ilse counted her paces home out of habit. From the foot of the Second Range stair to the Wren Stair door was eighty-eight paces by daylight. Tonight it was ninety-one, which was about normal for a damp evening in Fogmonth.

At pace sixty she stopped, because there was a door in the wall.

It stood between the third lamp and the fourth, where for six weeks there had been nothing but whitewashed plaster and a damp stain shaped very like the city of Lisk: oak gone black with age, iron studs, a ring handle the size of a saucer, and at eye height a brass plate, green at the edges:

FAR RANGE—CLOSED BY ORDER 1105

Under that, in a much later hand, someone had scratched with the point of a knife: *and stay closed.*

Sorry was sitting in front of it.

It must have come looking for her, as it sometimes did when the room got too quiet. It sat bolt upright on the flagstones with its too-big ears pricked forward and its tail wrapped round its feet, perfectly still, the way it watched moths. Its amber eyes didn’t move when she came up beside it.

The door hummed wrong. Everything in the College had a note: the deep, settled note of the Old Court, the nervous, wavering note of the Wing. This door had no note at all. It *swayed*. Standing in front of it was like standing on a jetty and feeling the planks lift on a swell you couldn’t see. She put her hand out to the iron ring.

“Oh, don’t—oh, please don’t, not with your bare hand.”

She snatched her hand back. A tiny old man was hurrying down the corridor, so bent that he seemed to be arriving mostly by willpower. He wore a scholar’s gown that had once been black, and a pair of round lenses on hinged brass arms was clamped to his head like the antennae of some studious insect. One lens was swung down over his left eye, which made that eye enormous. He smelled powerfully of vinegar.

“If you open it and it decides to go somewhere else while you’re halfway through, there’s no telling which half of you goes with it,” he said, arriving. “Archivist Damask. Palimpsest. You’re the Walker who copies for Aurek.” He beamed at the door as if it were a grandchild come to visit. “Isn’t it wonderful? It hasn’t been in the Second Range for eleven years.”

“What is it?”

“The Unheld Stacks. The Far Range library. The oldest books the College owns that aren’t in the Register, and a great many it doesn’t know it owns at all.” He swung the lens up and down, peering. “In 1105 the Provost and the Master of Works quarrelled about the connecting gallery. They pulled the old one down and argued for six years about the new one, and by the time they’d finished, nobody had re-surveyed the way in. Nobody has since. Ninety-eight years. It comes and goes like a cat.” He chuckled. “It’s been in the kitchens. Once—this is my favourite—it was behind the mirror in the Provost’s own dressing room for a week, and the poor man had to shave by feel.”

Sorry still hadn’t moved. Its ears were so far forward that they almost met.

“Why hasn’t anyone surveyed it?” Ilse said.

“Because it isn’t *here*, my dear, not in the way you mean. Survey it tonight and you’ve charted a door to a place that tomorrow is under the kitchens. You’d be attesting a falsehood by breakfast.” He tilted his huge eye at her. “What did you notice? Before I shouted. You put your hand out like someone who’d noticed something.”

She thought about lying, and couldn’t think of a reason to.

“It doesn’t sit,” she said. “Everything else sits. Even the Wing sits, a bit, until morning. This sways.”

The little man made a noise like a kettle starting.

“Oh! Look, *look*,” he said, to the corridor. “She feels the *grain* of it.” He clutched her sleeve in a hand that was all knuckle. “Do you know how many of my seniors can feel that? Two, and one of them only on Thursdays. Links don’t start Palimpsest until the winter term, I know, but come on a Thursday to my seniors and sit at the back and look at things. The fourth bell. Bring nothing. I have far too much of everything.”

“I—yes. All right.”

When she looked back, the door was gone. There was only plaster and the damp stain, and Sorry sniffing along the skirting board with its ears flat and offended, like someone who has been promised a pudding and handed a plate.


* * *


The letter came on the fourth of Fogmonth, by the noon post, in her mother’s square, laundry-ticket hand.

Ilse took it up to the Wren Stair to read. The party’s rooms were empty for once. Teo was at Soundings, Cass was somewhere being rude to someone, and Hen had gone to the Mess to argue about a sum. The wardrobe had drifted a foot and a half from the wall overnight and stood in the middle of the floor like a guest who doesn’t know where to sit. Ilse took the window seat.

*Ilse,* her mother had written, *Your letter came. You did not say if you are eating. I expect you are not.*

*Tam Brewin’s mare is mended. The lane is still a bog and the parish has petitioned the Hundred and the Hundred has petitioned somebody else. Agnes Tull asks after you every Thursday at market and has sent a jar of her damson, which I have kept, as it would not survive the coach and neither would you if you ate it.*

*Now. Corwin Fask has been round the parish asking about the Tull hedge. He came to the laundry. He was very civil, which I did not care for. Jem at the vestry says an inquiry is coming from the Big College, he says, whatever that is when it is at home. He says the young gentleman in the good boots wrote for the papers to be sent on. I said I knew nothing about anything, which is true, as nobody ever tells me.*

*Your Long Field money. I hope it bought you something.*

*Write, you wretch.*

*Your mother, H. Marrow*

Ilse read it through once, quickly, the way she’d read a magistrate’s notice. Then she read *I hope it bought you something* a second time and a third, and folded the letter in half and in half again and sat with it pressed flat between her palms.

Thirty-eight marks for a seat on the roof of the express mail coach, and six marks and six left in her pocket at Verity Cross. That was the field: the well, the sixty yards of good black frontage, her father at the window with the lamp. She had paid for her own neck with it, and she would do it again; she was not a fool. But her mother had not written *I hope it was worth it*. Her mother had written *I hope it bought you something*, the way you’d say it about a dress, and Ilse found she could not breathe properly around the difference.

And Kasimir Reyne had written for the papers. When they came, the Board would hold the Tull charter to a damp lens, and under the wren’s foot now cut into the Old Wall in her own hand, they would find the mark of a man three years dead.

The door banged. Hen came in with her cheeks pink from the wind and her blond hair coming out of its pins, saying, “He was *wrong*, he was wrong to the third place and he knew it, and he had the face to say rounding—” and then she saw Ilse and stopped.

Ilse waited to be asked. Hen didn’t ask. She came across the room, kicked off her boots, climbed onto the window seat beside Ilse and pushed her stockinged feet, which were very cold, under Ilse’s leg.

“Mothers,” Hen said.

“How do you know it’s my mother?”

“Because you’re holding it like a summons and you’d rather it was one.” Hen tipped her head back against the window. “Mine writes to tell me the salt’s fine, and then three pages about what the salt’s doing. The salt is very white this year, Henrike, whiter than the Pellows’ salt, which your father says is grey as a curate.” She wriggled her toes. “Tell me what yours says and I’ll tell you if the tide’s in or out on it.”

“She says I’m not eating.”

“Tide’s in. You’re not.”

“She sent damson jam and kept it.”

“That’s love, that is. On the Steps that’s practically a proposal.”

Ilse laughed. She hadn’t meant to. And Hen laughed too, her proper laugh, which went up all at once like a gull off a pan-wall, loud and sudden and entirely without dignity, so that two first-years passing on the stair outside stopped to see who was being murdered. Sorry came out from under the wardrobe at the noise and climbed onto the window seat, where, before either of them could stop it, it put its nose to the folded letter and licked the word *wretch* clean off the paper.

“Oh, you *animal*,” Hen said, delighted. “That was the best bit.”

“It wasn’t,” Ilse said, and unfolded the letter, and looked at the pale smear where her mother’s word had been, and at the line above it, which Sorry had left alone. She folded it again and put it inside her shirt.


* * *


Brask held the Fieldwork class that week at night.

“Wayhorns,” she said, at the door of the shed, with the Long Lie in her right hand and her grey left hand hooked into her belt. “The herds are coming down the Margin. You’ll lie in the gorse and you’ll keep your mouths shut and you’ll take a bearing off three animals. Anyone who frightens them walks home in front of them.”

There were fourteen of them that night, Piety Mercer among them, looking as if she’d been told this was a lecture. They went down onto the heath in a ragged line, each with a sightvane, a shuttered lantern and a plane-table board slung across the back. The moon was a day off full, and the fog had gone back out to sea and left the whole of the Margin silvered and dripping.

Brask put them down in a fold of gorse above a shallow valley and lay down herself, full length, the Long Lie beside her like a rifle. Ilse lay next to Hen. Teo lay on Ilse’s other side and immediately began to whisper.

“I’m sorry, I just—are they big? I know they’re big, my uncle sells the velvet for gloves, but are they the kind of big where if one stood on you—”

“Anselm,” said Brask, without turning her head.

“Ma’am.”

“Count something.”

Teo got to four hundred and twelve under his breath, and then the herd came over the shoulder of the valley, and he stopped.

There must have been eighty of them. They came down out of the dark at a slow walk, heads low, grey-brown, deer-shaped but heavier in the shoulder, and their antlers caught the moon. Every antler was the same pale silver and every antler branched the same way. The tines didn’t spread evenly like a stag’s; they all leaned toward the north and the east, so that the herd moved under a forest of silver hooks bent toward the same invisible thing, like iron filings walking. When a young buck turned his head to crop at the heather, his antlers stayed leaning the same way, and Ilse could see the stiff effort in his neck to keep them there.

“High hold,” Brask breathed. “They grow toward it. Every season, a tine at a time. Best compass on the Margin. Three animals. Bearings. Go.”

Ilse put the sightvane to her eye and laid its edge along the leading tine of the big doe at the front of the herd. She read the bearing off the plane-table rose by the slot of the lantern: north sixty-one east. Then a young buck halfway down the herd: north fifty-four east. Then an old animal at the back with one antler broken off at the burr: north forty-eight east. She pencilled three lines across her board, each from the place she had paced the animal to be standing. They met in a small, untidy triangle about eleven chains off to the northeast.

“Mine cross in a triangle you could cover with a farthing,” Hen whispered. “Look at *that*.”

“You paced them. I guessed mine.”

“You never guess.” Hen made a noise of pure satisfaction into the heather.

Cass hadn’t touched her sightvane. She lay with her chin on her forearms, watching the herd with what looked very like tenderness.
