“They’re pointing at the old stone in the hollow,” Cass murmured. “Past that gorse. Could have told you that from the shed.”

“Then tell me with numbers, Tenter,” said Brask, “or it’s gossip.”

Cass sighed through her teeth and picked up the sightvane.

When the herd had gone on down the valley, with the sound of eighty animals walking through wet heather, which is the sound of a very large, very soft rain, Brask made them chain it out: eleven chains and forty links through gorse up to the waist, Teo apologizing every time he snagged the chain. At the end of it, on a flat grey rock among the heather, they found a cairn no taller than Ilse’s hand: eleven small stones piled with fussy precision, each one set on the one below so that it neither rocked nor wobbled. A white feather stuck out of the top.

“Cairn-crows,” said Brask. “They build where the ground’s surest, and they’ve never been wrong. Note the lodestone.”

Ilse held the compass flat on her palm. It pointed with great confidence twelve degrees east of where the wayhorns had said, and then wandered off, as if it had thought better of the whole business.

Teo, standing on a rock in the middle of the Margin at midnight, raised his hand as if they were in a lecture hall.

“I’m sorry, ma’am, I just—the Drift. Out there. Past the Margin.” He pointed west into the dark, where the heath ran down to nothing and the horizon couldn’t quite decide where to sit. “Is it trying to kill us? As a—as a general policy?”

Piety Mercer snorted. Brask didn’t. She looked west with her one eye for long enough that Ilse looked too: dark country, a long low line that might have been hills, and above it the stars, the only things in that direction that kept still.

“It’s not trying to kill you,” Brask said. “It isn’t trying anything. That’s the frightening part.” She hefted the Long Lie. “And it’s also a lie, but you’re not ready for that one.”

She set off back toward the College without waiting to see if they followed. They followed. Behind them the cairn sat on its rock with its white feather lifting in the wind. Far down the valley, the herd had stopped to graze, and as Ilse looked back, every silver head in it lifted at once, every tine leaning the same way, toward the little cairn and the lit windows of the Meridian beyond it, as if they had heard someone there call their name.


## Chapter 13: Brine End

“No copying for you this week,” Hen said, sliding onto the bench with a bowl of porridge and a notice she had plainly torn off a door. “Coyle’s posted it on the Antechamber. *The Master of the Register is absent on inspection in the Salt Steps until Fogmonth 14. Copyists will not attend.*” She salted her porridge with the solemnity of a priest. “He’ll be up on the Steps eating our herring and calling it *bracing*. They all say bracing. It’s what Reach people say when they mean cold and they’re too polite to say cold.”

“It is cold,” said Teo, who had his hands wrapped round his tea like a man warming them at a hearth.

“It’s *bracing*, Teodor.”

Ilse was about to say that she would miss the Register, when a shadow fell across the table. It belonged to Lisbet Arnaud.

“Marrow. Field-Surveyor Brask wants you at the Lantern Gate with your boots on and a week’s worth of chain.” She considered Ilse’s porridge. “Now, I think she meant.”

Cass, opposite, raised both eyebrows. “Week’s worth of chain. Somebody’s lost something.”


* * *


What somebody had lost was a village.

Brask told her that much at the Gate, and nothing else. There was a College dog-cart waiting on the causeway with a fat dun pony in the shafts, a bag of instruments in the back, and a wicker crow-basket that swore at them in a low continuous mutter as Ilse climbed up.

“Nettle’s,” said Brask, as if that explained the language, and took the reins in her right hand.

Brine End lay six miles south, on the salt-marsh edge of the Margin, where the heath ran down into reeds and the reeds into mud and the mud, eventually and without much conviction, into the sea. The road there was a Margin road, which meant it was mostly an agreement between previous travellers. Brask drove it without looking at it. The pony seemed to know the way; or rather, Ilse thought, it seemed to know which way the way was leaning this morning, and leaned with it.

Brask didn’t speak. She had her clay pipe clamped in her teeth, and Ilse waited for her to strike a light, because out of doors she always did, filling the Fieldwork mornings with a smell like burnt hay. She didn’t strike it. The pipe stayed cold. After the first mile Ilse stopped waiting and watched the country instead.

The level changed as they went south. Near the College it was a steady pressure, the Cape’s bedrock humming under the heath. Two miles out it began to waver, and by the fourth mile Ilse could feel the land breathing under the cart wheels, a slow, uneven rise and fall that made her stomach wish it hadn’t had porridge. Then, at about the fifth mile, the level dropped.

It wasn’t silence, not quite. It was a hole in the sound, off to the left and ahead, the way a missing tooth is a hole in a smile. She had felt it once before, in a green lane at home with Tam Brewin’s mare screaming, and she had hoped never to feel it again.

“You feel it,” said Brask. It was the first thing she’d said in five miles.

“Yes.”

“Where?”

Ilse pointed, without thinking, south-southeast. Brask looked at her finger and then at the reeds, and then she nodded once and turned the pony a little left, and for the last mile neither of them said anything at all.


* * *


Brine End had seven cottages, a chapel the size of a cart shed, a row of eel traps drying on poles and, until four nights ago, a well on the green.

The well was still there. It had simply gone somewhere else. It stood forty yards off its old place, in the middle of somebody’s onion bed, a ring of grey stones with its windlass and bucket intact and its rope still swinging a little in the wind, as though someone had just let go of the handle. A path of trodden shingle ran from the cottage doors across the green to where the well had been and stopped at a round patch of dry, pale ground, like the mark a cup leaves on a table. The onions around the new well-head had been shoved outward in a neat ring, roots and all, and lay on their sides in the furrows like spilled skittles.

Two cottages at the seaward end had slid. They hadn’t fallen; they had slid, a yard or two each, and the ground between them and their gardens had torn open in long brown seams. The garden wall of the nearer one was still coming apart. Ilse stood and watched it. A stone in the third course eased out of its place as if it were being drawn by a patient hand. It tipped, rolled down the face of the wall and lay in the grass. A minute later the stone beside it followed. The whole wall was unknitting from one end, course by course, the way a sock goes when you pull the right thread.

The hens were the funniest, and Ilse was ashamed of finding them funny. The coop had moved a few feet in the night; the hens had not. Twelve brown hens were walking in a slow, puzzled circle round the place where their coop had stood, pecking at the bare ground and making the small, appalled noises of creatures who suspect that someone is having a joke at their expense.

At the doorstep of the second slid cottage a woman was on her knees, crying.

She was forty or so, with her sleeves rolled and her arms red from the brine pans. The doorstep in front of her was a single slab of old sandstone, worn hollow in the middle by generations of boots. It had turned round. It lay in its place under the door, but backwards, the worn hollow facing into the house and the sharp unworn edge out into the weather. The woman had her palms flat on it.

“My mother scrubbed that step every day for forty years,” she said to nobody. “Every day. You could have eaten off it.”

Brask stood looking at her for a moment. Then she turned to Ilse.

“Chain the well,” she said. “Old site to new. Then the slides. Then I want the levelglass on every corner you can find. I’m going to see the parish copy.”

Ilse chained the well, glad of something to do with her hands. She set an arrow in the dry ring and paid out the chain across the green into the onion bed, a boy from the nearest cottage holding the far handle with an air of tremendous responsibility. One chain. Eighty-two links to the stone kerb of the new well-head. A hundred and twenty feet and a little over. Forty yards, near enough, as the reeve had said. She sighted the bearing: south seventy east, toward the sea. She set the levelglass on the kerb and watched the bead of quicksilver swim in lazy circles and refuse to settle, like a fish in a bowl.

“Is it coming back?” said the boy.

“I don’t know.”

“Mam says the Tenting did it.”

Ilse wrote down *1 ch. 82 l., S. 70 E.* and didn’t answer.

The reeve, a lean worried man called Tobin Sallis, kept the parish copy of the charter in a tin box under his bed. Brask unrolled it on the cart-tail with a stone on each corner. It was a neat clerk’s copy of a charter made in 1006, ink a little faded, every cottage and the well and the green and the chapel in their proper places, with *A True Copy* written at the foot and a College seal.

“Fine,” said Brask. “It’s perfectly fine.”

“Then why—”

“Because it’s a copy, Marrow. A copy is a picture of a promise. It doesn’t hold a blade of grass.” She rolled it up. “The master’s in the Register. The master’s what holds this place. If the ground’s let go, either the master’s been overruled, or it’s been damaged, or it isn’t there.”

She wrote a note on a page torn from her field-book, folded it small, and took a crow out of the wicker basket. It was a big grey-necked bird with a beak like a bent nail, and it said something to her that Ilse was glad she couldn’t translate. Brask tied the note to its leg and threw it up, and it went off north in a straight grey line for the crow-loft on the Cape, the one fixed point it trusted.

They worked the rest of the day. Ilse chained the slides and set the levelglass on every corner-stone and doorpost, and the bead swam everywhere. By late afternoon she had a sketch of Brine End as it now stood, and it looked like a drawing of a place made by someone who had been told about it once, in a pub. Brask walked round with her, prodding things with the Long Lie and saying very little.

The crow came back at nightfall. It dropped out of a sky the colour of slate onto the cart-tail, and Brask untied the reply and read it by the reeve’s lantern. Then she handed it to Ilse.

It was in Merrin Coyle’s copperplate, which wobbled.

*Field-Surveyor: The Brine End master is not in its place. The parish box is present. The sheet is not. I have looked twice, and on the shelves either side, and in the year-store. I cannot account for it. M. Coyle, Registrar-Adjunct.*

“Misfiled,” said Ilse.

“Coyle doesn’t misfile,” said Brask. “He’s too frightened to.”

Ilse looked across the dark green to the well in the onion bed. She thought of the Little Wick lane, the milestone sinking to its shoulders. She had thought then, sitting in the stubble with Tam Brewin, that it had felt as though someone had reached into the parish chest and burned the charter. She had told herself it wasn’t possible.


* * *


In the morning the village had decided who to blame.

There was a Tenting wagon camped on the dunes half a mile north, two wagons really, painted red and ochre with route-diagrams in white along their sides, and a smoke going up from a cookfire. By the time Brask and Ilse came out of the reeve’s loft, where they had slept on sacks, five men were standing on the green with a hay-fork, two cudgels and a great deal of opinion.

“Came the week before,” one was saying, “and then the ground goes. You tell me.”

“Wainwrights,” said another. “They’re all Wainwrights. They were at Little Sallow when the ditch went, my cousin says.”

“Nobody’s going anywhere,” said Brask.

She didn’t raise her voice. She was a head taller than any of them, and the eye-patch did half her arguing for her. The man with the hay-fork looked at the Long Lie and then at her stilled grey hand, and lowered the fork about six inches.

“With respect, ma’am—”

“With respect, Tobin’s boy could chart better than you can think. Stay here.” She turned to Ilse. “You. With me.”

They walked out across the dunes. The marram grass hissed against Ilse’s boots. When they were halfway, someone at the wagons began to whistle through their teeth, a long falling note that Ilse had heard Cass make, and an old woman came down the wagon steps and stood waiting for them with her arms folded. She was small and brown and very upright, with grey braids tied up in route-cords like Cass’s but three times as many, so that her head looked like a ball of knotted string.

Brask stopped six feet from her and did a thing Ilse had never seen her do. She bowed. It was short and stiff, but it was a bow, the kind you would give to someone in their own house.

“Hey-ho, mother,” Brask said. “Road and rain to your wagon.”

The old woman considered her. “And rain to yours, paper-wall,” she said, “though you’ve not got one. Thessaly Ford, are you? You’ve got the Ford in your mouth.”

“I was.”

“Tansy Wainwright.” The old woman nodded at the village. “They want to burn us, then.”

“They want somebody to blame,” said Brask. “It’s not the same, but it ends the same if nobody’s careful. Did you feel it go?”

“Course we felt it go. Four nights back. Woke the babbies. Felt like a knot slipping.” Tansy Wainwright spat neatly to one side. “We sang the wagons down off the soft and come up here on the sure. We didn’t touch their bloody well. What would we want with a well that walks? We’ve got legs.”

“I know,” said Brask.

The old woman looked at her a while, and then at Ilse, and then back toward the reeds and the grey sea beyond the village, and her face did something complicated.

“Ground’s been letting go up and down the Margin since Thaw,” she said. “Little Sallow. The Hollin camp at Dunny Water. Here. Your people say it’s us. Our people say it’s rent.” She unfolded her arms. “Rent’s coming due, paper-wall. Everybody on the road knows it. Only you lot in the Big House think you can write your way out of paying.”

Brask went very still.

It was not the stillness of her grey hand. It was a living stillness, the kind a hare has in the grass when the hawk’s shadow crosses it, and it lasted as long as it took the marram grass to hiss once in the wind. Then Brask nodded.

“Stay up here on the sure,” she said. “I’ll speak to them.”

“You do that,” said Tansy Wainwright, and went back up her wagon steps.

Brask took the five men into the chapel and shut the door. They came out a quarter of an hour later looking like boys caught in an orchard and let off, and the hay-fork went back into the barn.

Then Brask made Ilse chart the village.

“Not as it was,” she said, when Ilse began to lay out the old 1006 lines. “As it is. The well where it stands. The cottages where they’ve gone. You’re holding what’s here so it stops sliding. It’s a stitch, not a repair.”

“The well’s in an onion bed. They want it back on the green.”

“Can you put it back?”

Ilse looked at the bead in the levelglass. The ground had moved, and it was still moving; its latitude was wide open. She could feel the give in it, the way you feel a loose tooth with your tongue. She could chart that well back forty yards and the ground would very likely let her.

“Yes,” she said.

“Are you sure where it was? To the link? Are you sure the water’s still under the old ring, and not under the new? Put it back and you’re guessing with their water.” Brask jabbed the Long Lie at the onion bed. “Hold it where it is and they’ll trample a new path by Candlemonth. Paths are cheap.”

They ran a closed traverse round the village core: nine stations, the well, the green, the seven cottages and the chapel. Brask held the far end of the chain and read the levelglass, and Ilse set the stations and drew, and they did it fast, the way Brask taught it in Fieldwork, so the ground couldn’t shift between one station and the next. The whole loop ran to fourteen chains and six links. When Ilse closed it on her board, the last line fell three links short of the first station.

She looked at the gap. Three links. A little under two feet. On the Walk she had smoothed fourteen links out of a stream bank without a second thought. Her pencil hovered over the misclosure, and she could see how she’d lose it: a hair added to each of the long legs, nothing anybody would ever find.

She wrote, in the margin, *Misclosure 3 l., distributed. Ground moving at time of survey.* Then she inked.

Brask watched her do it and said nothing.

“You sign it,” said Ilse, holding out the pen. “You’re the Field-Surveyor.”

“I measured it. So did you. I’ve signed enough of the world, Walker.” Brask didn’t take the pen. “You sign.”

So Ilse put her wren’s foot at the foot of the sheet, with intent, and felt Brine End take hold. It was not like the hedge. It was not like anything she had done before. The whole village, all fourteen chains of it, gripped her right hand like a frightened child grabbing a sleeve, and then the grip eased, and the garden wall at the seaward end stopped unknitting with a stone halfway out of its course, and the bead in the levelglass on the well-kerb gave one last swim and lay down.

The ache came after, a long dull pull from her wrist to her elbow.

Afterwards she borrowed a crowbar from the reeve and spent an hour with the woman’s son turning the doorstep round by hand. It took the two of them and a great deal of swearing on the son’s part. When it was done, the worn hollow faced out into the weather again, and the woman got down on her knees and ran her palm over it and said nothing at all, which was better than thanks.


* * *


The hollowing came at dusk.

Ilse was on the green packing the board, with the charter rolled in oilcloth on the cart-tail. Brask was at the pony’s head. The sun was going down red behind the dunes, and the light came level across the marsh, so that every reed threw a shadow ten feet long. Ilse noticed the gorse first. There was a clump of it at the edge of the green, forty yards off, bright yellow in the last light, and as she looked at it, it went soft.

It didn’t move. It went soft, as things go soft when your eyes are tired, the edges of it blurring into the heath behind. She blinked. The blur stayed. It was about the size of a haycock, and it was not anything. It was a place where the evening didn’t come into focus, like a thumbprint on a lens. And it was coming toward the green.

Where it passed, the ground slumped. Ilse watched a line of turf sag as the blur slid over it, the way sand sags when the tide goes out from under it. A loose stone from the seaward wall, lying in the grass, sank an inch into the earth and lay there looking surprised. The blur made no sound. It slid on toward the middle of the green, toward the well and the fourteen chains and six links of fresh charter she had signed at noon, and Ilse’s level went out like a candle.

It was the silence from the lane, the silence on the road yesterday, but closer, and *hungry*. She had never thought of an absence as hungry before.

“Marrow,” said Brask, quite quietly. “Step back from it.”

The charter. It was on the cart-tail, and the cart was beside the well, and the blur was ten yards from both. Every forger’s nerve in Ilse’s body said *the sheet*, and she was moving before she had decided to. She got her hand on the oilcloth roll. The edge of the blur was a yard from her, then a foot, and she reached past it to pull the charter clear, and her fingertips went in.

It was cold. That was all she had time to think: cold, like putting her hand into a stream in winter.

And then she was sitting on the grass with her back against the well-kerb, and she didn’t know how she had got there.

The sun had gone. A minute ago its rim had been on the dunes; now there was only a red line where it had been. Her right hand was numb to the wrist. The oilcloth roll was in her lap, held so hard that her fingers had crushed it. Someone was singing.

It was Brask. She was standing between Ilse and the blur with the Long Lie planted in front of her, and she was singing, low, in a voice Ilse had never heard her use and would not have believed she owned: a deep, steady, unhurried voice like a woman singing a child down, or a cart going over a long road. The words were in Tenting, or near it. Ilse caught the shape of them and not the sense. It was a road-song. She knew it from the rise and fall, the way Cass’s song at the cairn had risen and fallen, a road going by the high stone and the low ford. The tune went down and down, and came round, and went down again.

The blur flinched.

There was no other word for it. It drew back from the song like a hand from a stove, and its edges crawled, and the gorse behind it shuddered into focus and out again. Brask sang on, not louder, only steadier. The blur slid back across the green the way it had come. The turf it had slumped stayed slumped. At the edge of the heath it seemed to thin, and then the gorse was only gorse, dark in the dusk, and the level came back into Ilse’s ears with a rush that made her retch onto the grass.

Brask stopped singing. She stood for a moment with her head down, leaning on the staff. Then she came and crouched by Ilse, stiffly, on one knee.

“What’s my name?”

“Brask. Odile Brask.” Ilse’s tongue felt thick. “What happened?”

“You put your hand in a hollowing. You’ve been sat there five minutes asking me what happened. That’s the fourth time.” Brask took Ilse’s right hand in her own good one and turned it over and looked at the fingertips, which were white and wrinkled as if they had been too long in a bath, and then let it go. “It’ll come back. The feeling. The five minutes won’t.”

Ilse looked down at the oilcloth roll in her lap. The charter was safe. The well stood in the onion bed with its rope hanging still, and the hens had gone to roost somewhere, and the cottages were lit, and nobody in Brine End seemed to have seen a thing.

“It came for the sheet,” she said. “Didn’t it? For what I signed.”

“They come for anything fresh.” Brask stood up, using the Long Lie, and her knee cracked like a stick. “Get in the cart.”

“That song—”

“Get in the cart, Marrow.” Brask turned to the pony and began to check the traces, her back to Ilse, her grey hand hooked in her belt. When she spoke again her voice was her own again, dry and flat, as though the other one had never been. “And not a word of that song to the Provost.”


## Chapter 14: What Brask Knew

The note was under the Wren Stair door when Ilse came back from Mensuration, written in Brask’s square capitals on the back of an old chain-tally.

MY HOUSE. NETMAKERS’ ROW, VERITY CROSS. BLUE DOOR. AFTER THE SIXTH BELL. COME ALONE AND DON’T BRING THAT ANIMAL.

“That animal,” said Cass, reading it over her shoulder, “is sitting on Teo’s pillow eating a pencil.”

“It’s eating the paint off the pencil,” said Teo. “It doesn’t like the wood. I’ve been watching. It’s very particular.” He looked at Ilse’s face and stopped smiling. “Is it the hollowing? Are you all right? You still can’t feel your fingers properly, you said, and you’ve been holding your pen like it’s a stranger all day, and I don’t think—”

“I’m all right.”

“You said that yesterday,” said Hen, from the window seat. “Four times. In a row. With the same face.”

That was the thing about the five minutes. She didn’t remember them, and other people did. She had come back up the causeway in the dog-cart last night with Brask silent beside her, and the party had been waiting on the stair, and Hen had told her afterwards, quite kindly, that she had asked Teo what day it was and then asked again before he’d finished answering. The tips of the first two fingers on her right hand were still numb, white and puckered like the skin of a cold custard. Everything else had come back. The five minutes hadn’t, and Brask had said they wouldn’t.

She put on her father’s coat. It was too long in the sleeve and too broad in the shoulder and had been his good coat, his chapel coat, and in the lining at the left hem, under a seam she had unpicked and re-sewn herself, was the small flat oilcloth packet that held everything Quince had left her. She felt it knock against her knee as she went down the stair. She always felt it. It was the only thing in the College that nobody knew she had.


* * *


Verity Cross was two miles inland from the Cape, along a lane between stone walls with the wind coming off the sea behind. Netmakers’ Row was at the bottom of the town, near the old harbour, a crooked terrace of cottages with their backs to the weather. The blue door had a cairn-crow sitting on the lintel above it. It looked at Ilse with one pale eye and said nothing, which, for a cairn-crow, was practically a welcome.

Brask opened the door before she knocked.

“You’re late,” she said, and stood back.

The cottage was one room downstairs and a ladder to a loft. It was full of books. They were not on shelves, because there were no shelves; they stood in stacks on the floor, waist-high, leaning against each other, so that walking from the door to the table was like walking a path between cairns. There were crow feathers everywhere, grey and white, stuck in jam jars on the windowsill and pushed between the pages of the books as markers and tucked behind a cracked mirror. The hearth was cold. There was ash in it that might have been a week old. The table was an old plane-table board on trestles, its brass compass-rose still set into one corner, and on it lay, flattened under two stones and a candlestick, Ilse’s survey from the Walk.

She knew it at once. She would have known it across a field. Her own hand, her own linework, the route from the Lantern Gate out to the cairn and back, the stations numbered in her neat small figures. And the stream bank.

“Sit,” said Brask. “I’m making tea.”

She made it the way a person makes tea who has read about it once and disagreed with the method. She put a fistful of black leaf into a pan of water on a spirit-lamp and boiled the two together until the water was the colour of a peat bog. Then she poured it, leaves and all, into a chipped enamel mug for Ilse and a jam jar for herself, and sat down opposite, and put her grey left hand on the table, where it lay curled like a thing she had brought in from the garden.

Ilse drank the tea. It tasted like a boot. She drank it anyway. Her hands wanted a job.

“Fingers?” said Brask.

“The first two are still numb.”

“And the five minutes?”

“No.”

“Didn’t think so.” Brask took a lens from her pocket, a plain brass reading-glass, and set it on the survey over the stream bank. “Look.”

Ilse didn’t need to look. She looked anyway.

It was station nineteen to station twenty-three, the return leg, the morning after the cairn. She remembered every link of it: the cold, the shaking in her legs, the stream that had moved in the night, the bank that wouldn’t close. Her chainings had come out fourteen links long. Fourteen links was nine feet and a bit, and the bank simply hadn’t been where her figures said it should be. And she had been so tired, and the drawing had been so nearly beautiful, and there had been a way to make it beautiful that nobody would ever see. So she had taken the fourteen links out. She had shaved them from the long legs, a link here, two links there, and redrawn the bank half a rod west, where it agreed with everything else, and she had gone over the new line twice to give it weight.

Under the lens, the line had gone over twice. She could see it now. The ink lay thicker along the bank than anywhere else on the sheet, a darker, more confident brown, the line of someone who wants very much to be believed.

“Nobody closes a Margin traverse to nothing,” said Brask. “Nobody honest. The ground moves under you while you work; that’s the Margin. There’s always a gap. On every sheet that came in that day there was a gap, two links, nine links, Tenter’s was out by most of a chain and she wrote it down in letters a foot high. Yours closed like a box lid.” She tapped the bank with a fingernail. “And you went over that twice. People go over a line twice when they’re not sure of it, or when they want you to be sure of it. You weren’t unsure.”

Ilse said nothing. The tea was cooling in her hands.

“You corrected it,” said Brask. “Forgers correct. Surveyors record.”

There it was.

Ilse sat with the mug in both hands and thought, with great calm, about the coach fare home. She had a little over six marks, with the copying money. The express was thirty-eight. The slow coach was eleven, and took nine days, and would set her down in Hobbet just in time for Corwin Fask and the parish magistrate to meet her in the yard. She thought about her mother’s letter inside her shirt. *I hope it bought you something.* She thought about the iron ring on her finger, the meridian line on it, and how it would feel to take it off and put it on the table beside the jam jar. She found she wanted very badly not to cry in front of this woman, and that she wasn’t going to, and that this was the only thing she was sure of.

“Yes,” she said.


* * *


“Good,” said Brask.

Ilse looked up.

Brask was leaning back in her chair with the jam jar in her good hand, and her one eye was on Ilse, and there was a look in her face that Ilse couldn’t place, because she had never seen it there before. It took her a moment to recognize it as relief.

“So was I, once,” Brask said. “Every surveyor who ever chose what to leave out.”

“I don’t—”

“You thought I’d brought you here to put you on a coach.” Brask drank her terrible tea. “If I wanted you gone I’d have said so in the Hall of Instruments with the survey in my hand and Aurek Hallam at my elbow, and you’d have been in Verity Cross by nightfall looking for a cart east. I passed you. I wrote *true enough* on it. I meant it. It was true enough. It was the best Walk survey I’ve seen in eleven years, and the only lie on it was a lie about nine feet of stream bank, and the stream bank moved again two days later anyway, so you were wrong and it didn’t matter.” She put the jar down. “But I wanted to see what you’d do if I put it in front of you. You could have said the ground moved after you chained it. You could have said you smudged it. You’re a good liar. I’ve watched you tell half the Fieldwork class you’d done the reading, and they believed you.”

“Cass didn’t.”

“Tenter doesn’t believe anybody. It’s her best quality.” Brask looked at her. “You didn’t lie to me just now. That’s what I wanted to know.”

Ilse put the mug down because her hands had started to shake.

“I’ve done it before,” she said. “Not on sheets like that. On charters. At home. Small things, hedges and ditches, for people who—”

“Stop.” Brask held up her good hand. “I don’t want the parishes. I don’t want the names. What I don’t know I can’t be made to say, and someone, one day, is going to try very hard to make me say it.” She paused. “I know the shape of it. That’s enough. I knew the shape of it the moment I put the lens on that bank. I know what a forger’s hand looks like.”

“How?”

Brask was quiet for a while. Outside, the wind had got up, and the crow on the lintel muttered in disgust and shifted its feet. The candle on the table leaned and recovered.

“Because I’ve left things off sheets,” Brask said. “Not hedges, and not for money. But I’ve stood on ground and measured it and gone home and drawn it, and chosen what went on the paper and what didn’t, and signed it, and the land held to what I drew and not to what was there. Every surveyor does it. Every map is a choice about what matters. Most of them don’t know they’re choosing, so they call it accuracy.” She turned the jam jar on the table. “The ones who know they’re choosing are either very honest or very dangerous. Usually both.”

“The Sallow,” said Ilse, before she could stop herself.

Brask’s eye came up. For a second Ilse thought she had gone too far. Then the older woman laughed, a short dry bark with no humour in it.

“Everybody knows that much,” she said. “Even Walkers. All right. You’ve earned a bit of it, putting your hand in a hollowing for a sheet of paper. That was the stupidest thing I’ve seen a link do in nineteen years, and I’ve seen a Patent boy try to chain a sounder.”

She told it plainly, without looking at Ilse, as a woman might read out an inventory.

“Eleven eighty-three. Seven of us. Me in charge, because I was the best field surveyor in the College and I knew it and so did everybody else. Aurek Hallam as my second, because he was the most careful man I’d ever met and somebody had to stop me walking off cliffs. Ansel Corrow, Mira Tey, Jory Paske, Benedek Vass, and a girl called Wenna Rook who was nineteen and could read grain like Tenter. Thirty leagues southwest, into the deep Drift, further than anyone had chained in a hundred years. We were going to find the sounders’ singing-grounds and chart a road to them.”

“Did you?”

“We found them.” Brask’s voice didn’t change. “A valley where the ground was all sounders, Marrow, hundreds of them, rising and going down like porpoises in a bay. The whole valley singing, so low you heard it in your teeth. You’ve seen one breach. Imagine a sea of them.” She stopped. “And we found something else. I’ll not describe it. I can’t, and every time I tried, back here, the Board of Errata wrote down *hallucination under Drift-sickness* in a very neat hand. It was there. It was there, and it was trying to talk to us. The ground moved when it spoke. I don’t know what it was saying. I know it wasn’t saying *go away*. That’s all you’ll get.”

Ilse didn’t move.

“Four of them didn’t come back. Corrow and Tey in a fold that closed. Paske and Vass in the night; we never found them. Rook and Hallam and I came out. I lost this”—she touched the leather patch over her right eye—“to a hollowing on the way, three days from the Margin. Bigger than the one at Brine End. Much bigger. I don’t remember the next day and a half at all, which is a longer five minutes than yours. When I came back to myself I was on Aurek’s back.” For the first time something moved under the flat voice. “He carried me twenty leagues. Over ground that wouldn’t keep still for him. I was not a small woman, then or now. He never once said I was heavy.”

Ilse thought of the kind old man coming down the aisle on Level Two with tea and ginger biscuits, his gloved hand careful on the stair rail.

“The gloves,” she said. “He told Teo it was frostbite. From the Sallow.”

“It was cold enough out there.” Brask looked down at her own grey hand on the table. “We all brought something back. I brought this, and an eye short. The Board brought a verdict, and that was the end of me as a field surveyor. They gave me Fieldwork and a shed that leaks.” She shrugged with one shoulder. “I don’t sign maps now. I signed enough of the world out there to last me. It can hold itself or it can’t.”

“And the song? At Brine End?”

“Thessaly Ford had Tenting neighbours. A Wainwright woman wintered at our gate every year when I was a girl and taught my mother songs for the soft ground. They work. Hollowings hate a song and they hate ground that moves. Nobody at the College will tell you that, because the College doesn’t hold with things that work for reasons it can’t sign.” She looked at Ilse. “The Provost would like that song a great deal less than he’d like you.”

She leaned forward, and her voice changed again, became the Fieldwork voice, the one that bit at the end of every sentence.

“The map is a promise, Marrow. Not a cage. Every time you sign one, you’re promising the ground something. That it’s here. That it’ll stay here. That this is what’s true about it. Sometimes that’s a good promise, and the ground is glad to keep it. Sometimes it’s a cage, and the ground strains at it, and the strain comes back up your arm for the rest of your life.” She tapped the table beside the stream bank. “Know which one you’re making. That’s all the craft there is. The rest is arithmetic.”


* * *


It had grown dark outside. Brask got up and lit a second candle from the first, and the books in their stacks threw long leaning shadows up the walls.

“So,” she said, sitting down again. “Here’s the bargain.”

Ilse waited.

“I know what you are. I’ll keep it. I won’t lie for you; if the Board asks me straight I’ll tell them straight. But nobody’s asked, and I don’t volunteer.” Brask held up one long finger. “One condition. No more lies on any College sheet, not by a link or a hair, not on Morning Survey, not on the Ladder, not on a practice sheet you throw in the stove. Every gap you find, you write it down. Every misclosure. Every bloody stream bank. You did it yesterday at Brine End, three links, *ground moving at time of survey*. I watched you think about losing it, and I watched you not. That’s the surveyor I took on. You keep being her, inside this College, and what you did outside it is between you and the parishes.”

“Yes,” said Ilse.

She said it at once, and she found she meant it, and the meaning of it went through her like a long drink of cold water. She thought of the oilcloth packet in the lining of her coat, the blind ink and the damp-lens and Quince’s mark folded small, and she thought: *I won’t need it. Not here.* It was a lighter thought than she’d expected.

“Yes,” she said again. “I promise.”

“Don’t promise. Promising’s what you do on paper. Just do it.” Brask drained her jam jar and made a face at the dregs. “And one more thing. Your young man from the Court.”

“He isn’t my—”

“Reyne. He came to the shed a week ago. Very polite. Hat in his hand, which is more than most of the Patent boys manage. Asked whether there’d been anything irregular in your Walk survey. Asked whether I’d looked at it closely.” Brask’s mouth twitched. “I told him I look at everything closely. I told him I’d passed it true enough, which is what I wrote. He wrote down what I said in a little black book, word for word. I watched him do it.”

Ilse felt cold all the way down. “What else did he ask?”

“Whether I knew where you’d learned to draft. I said Hobbet, I supposed, since that’s where you’re from. He wrote that down too.” Brask looked at her for a long moment. “He’s not a fool, that boy. And he’s got the look of someone who finishes things. I don’t know what he thinks he knows. I don’t want to know. But I’d not leave anything lying about, if I were you.”

“There’s nothing to leave lying about.”

“Good,” said Brask, in a tone that didn’t believe her in the least and didn’t mind. She got up. The interview was over. At the door, with her hand on the latch, she said, without turning round, “That was a decent charter at Brine End. The well held. Sallis sent a boy this morning to say so.”

Then she opened the door, and the wind came in and set every feather in every jam jar trembling, and Ilse went out.


* * *


She walked back the long way, because she wanted to.

The lane up from Netmakers’ Row ran past the Chain & Anchor, which was full and loud and smelled of fried fish, and up through the market square, where the stalls were shuttered for the night and a lone Tenting girl was packing away a cage of something grey and indeterminate that peered at Ilse with large amber eyes as she passed. Then the town ran out, and there were only the stone walls and the wind and the two miles of dark lane to the Cape.

She had been trusted. That was what had happened in the blue-door cottage, under the books and the crow feathers. She turned it over as she walked, the way she would have turned over a coin she didn’t recognize, looking for the mint mark.

Quince had trusted her, in his fashion. He had trusted her with his hands and his tricks and the mark of a dead man, and she had loved him, but he had always kept one eye on the till. Her mother trusted her the way you trust weather, which is to say she expected the worst and laid in candles. Teo trusted everybody, and Hen trusted anyone who laughed at her jokes, and Cass trusted nobody and made exceptions with her whole heart and a great deal of noise. Master Hallam was kind to her, and kindness was a warm thing, and she liked it more than she let herself show. But kindness was not the same as this.

Brask knew what she was. Brask had put the worst thing Ilse had done in Ilse’s own handwriting on the table between them, under a lens, and looked at it, and then looked at her, and said *good*. And then she had given Ilse a rule and her silence, and asked nothing back except that Ilse keep her word.

Ilse had never in her life been given something she couldn’t hide. Everything she owned that mattered had a lining to go into. The tin behind the brick. The packet in the coat. The mark under the mark. This had no lining. She would have to carry it out where anyone could see.

She found she didn’t know where to put it. So she carried it in her hands, as it were, up the dark lane, and it was not heavy.

At the top of the rise the wind dropped, and the College came into view on its headland. The Old Court sat squared and honey-dark against the sea, and the lantern burned on the keystone of the Gate, a single steady point. Beyond it the Drift Wing trailed out over the heath, its hundred windows lit. As she watched, one of them in the Second Range slid a foot or two along the dark and stopped, as though somebody inside had shifted in their sleep.

The fingers of her right hand had begun to tingle. She held them up in front of her face in the starlight and flexed them. It was pins and needles, sharp and hot and alive, running from her knuckles to the tips, the feeling coming back into the first two fingers a little at a time. She stood in the lane and let it come. Then she put her hand in her father’s pocket, where it was warm, and walked on toward the light.


## Chapter 15: Blind Ink

For a week she kept her promise without even noticing she was keeping it, which was the best kind of promise to keep.

Morning Survey on the eighteenth of Fogmonth came up out of a fog so thick that Teo, at the far end of the Long Gallery with the chain’s handle, was a voice and nothing else. “A chain and sixty-one,” said the voice. “Sixty-one and a—sorry—sixty-one links and a hair, Marrow, I can’t see the arrow, I can see my own hand if I hold it very close to my face and that’s all, it’s like being inside a dumpling.”

“Write the hair,” said Ilse.

“How big a hair?”

“Your best guess, and that it’s a guess.”

She heard Hen, somewhere behind her in the white, laugh and write it down. It was the old drill now, and they were good at it. The Wren Stair and its landing ran to a chain and eighty-one links from the top step to the Gallery door, if the stair hadn’t turned in the night, which this morning it had, by about a hand’s breadth, so that its bottom step now pointed fractionally more toward the sea. The Long Gallery ran a chain and sixty from the stair-foot to its double doors, and this morning it ran a chain and sixty-one and a bit. Three chains forty-one, the stretch was meant to be. Three chains forty-two and Teo’s hair, it was. Cass walked the length of it with her hand on the wall, as she always did, and said the grain was leaning southwest and sulky with it, and Ilse drew it all in, the hand’s breadth and the link and the hair and the sulk, on four sheets of good hard Lisk paper, and wrote *est.* beside Teo’s guess.

Then they went to the Antechamber to sign.

The long vault under the Old Court smelled, as it did every morning, of wet coats and ink and lamp-oil. Eight sloping desks stood along its walls, each with a chained brass inkwell and a rack of pens, and by the door Merrin Coyle sat behind his high counter with the roll, ticking off parties as they came in out of the dark. Master Hallam liked every sheet signed here, under the Register’s roof; Coyle had said so on their first morning, and on most mornings since.

Hen pulled the four sheets toward herself at the desk.

“I’ll take the first round,” she said. “I’m quickest.”

She was. She signed the way she counted, quick and certain, her neat little gull’s wing over a line, and she had signed all four before Cass had uncapped her pen. Every sheet wanted two marks at least and would take four, and Hen always signed all four. It had been a joke in Gleaning, and then a habit, and then a thing nobody noticed. Ilse signed two, the *Landing* and the *Long Gallery, West*, the wren’s foot set down cleanly in the printed box at the foot of each sheet.

She looked at the box afterwards. She couldn’t have said why. It was a plain printed rule, a rectangle a little larger than a playing card, with *Attested* in small type above it and three faint ticks along its foot that divided it into four places, one to a mark, the same on every Morning Survey folio she had ever signed. She looked at it for long enough that Coyle cleared his throat from the counter.

“Is something the matter, Miss Marrow?”

“No,” said Ilse. “Nothing.”

On the way to breakfast she found she was thinking about rain.

It had been nagging at her for days, under everything else: under Brine End and the blue-door cottage and the promise. On the tenth of Gleaning, in the driving rain, Sorry had knocked Coyle’s tea across the folios, and on the wet sheet, under her fresh wren’s foot, she had seen a line. Pale and violet, for no more than a breath, and then Coyle’s blotter had taken the wet and the line with it. She had told herself then that she had smudged her own mark. She had been not three weeks in the College and frightened of everything and very glad of the excuse.

But she had not smudged it. She knew how her own pen moved. And she knew only one ink in the world that showed violet when it was wet and dried to nothing on the page, because she had a bottle of it sewn into the hem of her father’s coat.

The Brine End master was not in its place. Coyle had looked twice. A sheet had gone out of the Register that should never have gone anywhere, and a village had come loose. And somewhere in her chest, in the place where she kept the things she did not let herself think, a small cold voice that sounded like Quince said, *You know what blind ink is for, girl. You’ve only ever used it for one thing.*


* * *


The Wren Stair was empty that evening. Teo was at the Sounders’ Choir, practising notes so low that the Old Court windows hummed with them. Cass had gone to Verity Cross with Orla Devane for what she described as *none of your business, paper-wall, and also cider*. Hen had gone to bed straight after supper, which was unlike her. She had said she was tired, and she had looked it, and Ilse had thought nothing of it.

She sat on the floor with her back against the wardrobe, which had drifted nearly to the middle of the room again, and took her father’s coat across her knees.

The seam at the left hem had been her mother’s work the night before the coach, eleven stitches to the inch. It was her own now, unpicked and resewn more than once in brown thread, small even stitches that a laundress’s daughter could do in her sleep. She cut four of them now with the point of her knife and drew out the oilcloth packet. It was the size of a prayer book and about as thick. She unrolled it on the floorboards in the lamplight.

Everything was there. The corked bottle of blind ink in its roll of flannel, the size of her thumb, its pale contents clinging to the glass. The fine steel pen Quince had ground for her the year before he died. A square of chamois. A little paper twist of alum crystals. The damp-lens in its leather: a disc of watch-glass two inches across, ground thin at the edges, in a ring of brass with a short handle, which Quince had made himself out of a broken pocket-watch and a curtain ring. And folded small in the middle of it all, on a scrap of old vellum, the mark of Abelard Quince in his own hand: a crooked Q, a loop doubled back on itself with its tail hooked through the ring like a fish on a line.

She didn’t unfold the vellum. She didn’t need to. She had drawn that mark so many times she could have done it blind in the dark, and had, more than once.

Sorry came out from under Teo’s bed and sat at the edge of the oilcloth, watching. It reached out a paw toward the alum twist, sniffed it, and sneezed so hard it fell over.

“Serves you right,” said Ilse.

She made the lens the way Quince had taught her. She ground a few crystals of alum to powder in her palm with the back of the knife, tipped the powder into an eggcup, and added warm water from the kettle on Teo’s spirit-stove, a thimbleful at a time, stirring with the nib-end of a pen until the crystals had gone and the water was faintly cloudy. Then she dipped a corner of the chamois, wiped the watch-glass with it so that a film of alum-water lay across the whole disc, thin and even, and breathed on it twice to keep it damp. Blind ink was gall and alum, and dried to nothing. Wet it and the gall came back up violet: that was what the tea had done in Gleaning, and what a Court examiner did when she wetted a sheet in a tithe barn. A forger couldn’t afford to cockle the paper. A damp film laid flat over the line woke the gall just enough to show, and left no trace behind. Quince had called it *asking the paper politely*.

To be sure, she tested it. She dipped a nib in the blind ink and drew a short line across a scrap of paper. The line went down pale and shining and dried in a few seconds to nothing at all. She laid the damp glass flat over it and looked.

There it was: a hair-fine line, violet as a bruise, lying on the paper as plain as day.

Ilse sat back on her heels. Her heart was going quickly. She told herself that she had made a promise to Brask, and that the promise was *no lies on any College sheet*, and that this was not a lie; it was looking. Looking was what surveyors did. Brask herself had said it: if you can’t say how you might be wrong, you haven’t measured anything. She was only trying to find out whether she had been wrong.

It was a good argument. She noticed that it was a very good argument, and that she had built it very quickly, and that she had been building good arguments very quickly since she was fourteen. She wrapped the lens in the chamois and put it in her pocket anyway.


* * *


She had a copyist’s key. Hallam was back from the Salt Steps, and the copying evenings had begun again, and on the nineteenth she spent two hours on Level Two finishing Bellwether while the Register hummed around her and her mind was entirely elsewhere. The Master did not come down with tea. A note from him lay on her table instead, in a hand so neat and careful it was almost a drawing: *My dear—kept upstairs with the Provost all evening, alas. Bellwether looks splendid. Don’t stay past the bell. A.H.* At the ninth bell she capped her ink and went up the stair as if to leave.

The Antechamber was dark and empty. Coyle had gone. The lamps were out except for one turned low over the clerk’s counter, and the eight sloping desks stood in the gloom like a row of empty pews. The filing presses ran along the back wall, tall oak cabinets with doors of brass mesh, and behind the mesh lay the Morning Survey folios for the current year, bundled by week and by party, waiting for Coyle to carry them down to the year-store.

Ilse turned the lamp up a quarter-inch and took down the bundle for that morning.

She found her own party’s sheets first, because she knew them by heart. The *Long Gallery, West* in her hand, with Teo’s guess marked *est.*, and Hen’s gull-wing at the foot and, beside it, her own wren’s foot, a little heavier on the spur than she liked. She laid the sheet flat on the counter. She took the lens out of the chamois and breathed on it twice. Then she laid it over her mark and looked.

The line was there.

It lay beneath her wren’s foot, violet in the damp glass, finer than a hair. It didn’t touch her mark so much as run through it. It came in from the left along the printed rule of the attestation box, lying so true beneath the rule that the visible black line and the hidden violet one were a single line until the lens pulled them apart. At the place where her mark sat, the violet line turned and made a small, intricate figure, a knot of curves no bigger than a fingernail, and her wren’s foot had been set down squarely over it, the way you set a seal over wax. Then the line went on along the rule, knotting again at each of the box’s other three places, left the box by its right-hand side, ran along the foot of the sheet to the binding edge, and stopped there at the margin, as if it continued on some other sheet that wasn’t there.

She moved the lens to Hen’s mark. The same line. The same small knot under the wave. The same run to the edge.

She took the next sheet, and the next. Teo’s looped T, hung like a pothook. Cass’s zigzag. Hen’s gull-wing on all four. Her own on two. Every one of them sat squarely on a violet knot, and every knot ran out along the rule to the margin and stopped.

She put her own party’s sheets down and took up another party’s at random. Then a third. Then she went along the press and pulled bundles from the back, last week, the week before, Reaping, Haysel. Then Kasimir Reyne’s party: Orla Devane’s flourish, Absalom Grice’s laborious cross, Piety Mercer’s spiky little steeple, and Kasimir’s own mark, the square with its single diagonal, every stroke as clean as an engraving, sitting on its violet knot like a jewel on a pin.

Every mark on every sheet.

Her hands had gone cold, but very steady. It was the old steadiness, the one that came over her at two in the morning in a stranger’s orchard with the lantern shuttered and the ink wet. She stopped being frightened and began to look the way Quince had taught her to look: at how the thing was made.

It was not drawn by hand. That was the first thing. She knew hands. Every hand wavered, every hand pressed harder at the start of a stroke than at its end, every hand made each knot a hair different from the last. These knots were identical. Sheet after sheet, the same curve, the same thickness, the same tiny hook at the same place. No hand on earth drew hundreds of identical figures a morning. These had been printed, from a plate, in blind ink, before the paper ever came to the Antechamber, and laid under the attestation box so that any student who set a mark in it, as they were told to, set it squarely over a knot.

And that was the second thing, the thing that turned her stomach over. She knew what the knot was for.

It was what she did. It was what Quince had taught her to do in his back kitchen in Hobbet, with the shutters closed. You took the mark that would carry the weight and you put it under the mark that would be seen, line for line, in blind ink, and you signed over it with intent, and the charter took. The burden ran down the hidden line and away to whoever it belonged to. She had done it on the Tull charter. She had done it on every charter for three years. A ghost was a channel. It had two ends, and what ran along it was weight.

Here, one end was the student’s mark. The other end was off the edge of the sheet.

She looked at the knot under Hen’s gull-wing and thought of Wil Ashdown on the bench outside the infirmary in Gleaning, staring at nothing. She thought of his right hand lying on his knee, slate-grey from the knuckles to the tips. The signing hand.

She looked down at her own right hand on the counter. The first two fingers were pink again. The pins and needles had gone two days ago. It looked like a hand.

*Someone is ghosting the whole College.*

She made herself go on. A surveyor finds the edge of a thing. She pulled the year-store bundles that Coyle hadn’t yet carried down, and when those ran out she found the key to the stair and went down to Level Two in the dark and brought up the boxes she had taped and labelled herself a fortnight ago. Highsun 1202: violet knots. Seedtime 1201: violet knots. The first week of Thaw 1201: violet knots, every one. The last week of Candlemonth 1200, the old wheat-sheaf paper, and she held the damp glass over a dozen marks, then twenty, then every mark in the bundle, and there was nothing under any of them but paper.

Clean. Clean until the year turned, and then every mark on every sheet, every morning, for nearly three years.

Something about Thaw 1201 snagged in the long cupboard at the back of her mind, and she reached for it.

Then it came to her, all at once, what it would mean to tell.

She would go to someone, Brask or the Provost or Master Hallam. She would say: *Every Morning Survey sheet since 1201 has a ghost-line under every student’s mark. Someone is passing burden onto the students. That’s why they’re going Quiet.* And they would look at her, kindly or not, and say: *How do you know, Miss Marrow?* And she would have to hold up the watch-glass in its curtain ring and say: *With this. It’s a forger’s lens. I know because I’ve done it. I know what a ghost looks like because I’ve drawn hundreds.* And the Board of Errata, which would in a few weeks be holding the Tull charter up to a damp lens of its own, would not need to wait for the papers from Hobbet at all.

Only a forger would see this. Only a forger could say so. And the moment she said so, she would be finished.

She stood at the counter with the lens in her hand and the violet line under Hen’s mark, and at the far end of the Antechamber a page turned.


* * *


She closed her fist round the lens. The damp film soaked cold into her palm. In one movement she slid it up her left sleeve, where her cuff held it against her wrist, and turned.

There was a light among the far presses, behind the last row of cabinets, where the room ran back into an alcove she had always assumed was a store. It was a dark lantern, half-shuttered. It moved. And then Kasimir Reyne stepped out from between the cabinets with the lantern in one hand and a ledger with a red spine under his arm, and stopped, and looked at her.

For a moment neither of them said anything. Ilse was acutely aware of the counter behind her: the open bundles, the sheets fanned out, the year-store box labelled *1200* in her own copy of Coyle’s hand, standing open on the floor at her feet with its tapes undone.

“Miss Marrow,” said Kasimir.

“Mr. Reyne.”

He came forward a few steps into the light of the counter lamp. He was in his shirtsleeves and waistcoat, without his gown, and his hair, for once, was not quite perfect; a strand of it had come loose over his forehead. He moved the ledger from under his arm to behind his back, not quickly, but not slowly either. His eyes went past her to the counter and the sheets and the open box, and she watched him read the scene the way she would have read it, item by item.

“It is past the ninth bell,” he said.

“I’m a copyist. I have a key.” She kept her voice level. “What’s your excuse?”

“I do not believe I owe you one.”

“You’re in the Register after hours with a lantern you’ve been keeping shuttered, and you hid when you heard me come in. You’ve been standing back there in the dark listening to me for—” she had no idea how long; she thought of the whole silent hour at the counter and went cold again—“for some time. I think you owe somebody an excuse.”

“I was reading.” He tilted his head at the counter. “You were not copying. Those are Morning Survey folios. They are not on your list; Coyle does not let copyists touch the current presses. And that”—he nodded at the box at her feet—“is from the year-store, which is on Level Two, which is locked at the ninth bell.” It was the voice of a man reading out a charge. “You have the sheets of the whole College spread on a counter in the dark. What are you doing to them?”

“Nothing.”

“That is not an answer.”

“It’s the true one.” She lifted her chin. “What are *you* doing here? With your mother’s Court so very interested in the Register, and you the son of the Chancellor, reading in the dark behind the presses with a book you don’t want me to see. If I went to Master Hallam tomorrow and said I’d found Lady Reyne’s boy in the Register after hours with a lantern, what do you think he’d say?”

A look crossed his face, quickly, and was put away.

“I think,” he said, “that he would ask what *you* were doing here. And I think you would not want him to.”

They stood and looked at each other across ten feet of lamplit floor. Ilse could feel the lens against her wrist, the damp spreading into her cuff. She kept her hands still at her sides by an effort that made her shoulders ache.

Kasimir looked at her for a long time. He was not looking at the sheets now. He was looking at her face, the way he had looked at her in the dark by Widow Tull’s hedge, as though she were a boundary stone he was checking against a record.

“You are shaking, Miss Marrow,” he said quietly.

“It’s cold.”

“It is not especially cold.” He set the lantern on the nearest desk. “I am told you sat in the dark at the Ladder with the room turning under you and drew it by touch, and were not frightened. I have seen you come back from Brine End with your hand dead to the wrist, and you were not frightened. You are very frightened now.” His grey-blue eyes went once more to the sheets fanned across the counter, and back to her. “For someone who is merely copying.”

She said nothing. There was nothing she could say that would not be a lie he could check, or a truth that would hang her.

He waited. When she didn’t speak, he nodded once, as if she had answered after all and he had written the answer down. He picked up the lantern. He did not open its shutter. With the red-spined ledger held close against his side, away from her, he walked past her down the length of the Antechamber, between the empty desks, and did not look at the counter again.

At the foot of the stair he stopped, with his back to her.

“I would put those back exactly as you found them,” he said. “Coyle counts.”

Then he went up. She heard his footsteps on the stone, unhurried and even, up the long stair toward the Old Court, until the door at the top opened on a breath of cold air and closed again, and there was nothing but the Register humming, and the violet line under Hen’s mark, and her own heart going like a chain paid out too fast.


## Chapter 16: Hen

Teo noticed first.

It was the twenty-fifth of Fogmonth, a grey morning, the Mess full of steam and the smell of burnt porridge, and Teo was explaining to the table why the bread of Lisk was the finest in the Reach and the bread of every other place was a kind of upholstery.

“In Lisk,” he said, “we don’t say a loaf’s gone stale. We say it’s *matured*. We say it’s *developing character*. My grandmother sold the same loaf for three days at three different prices and called it a vintage.”

Cass snorted into her tea. Ilse, who had been half listening, smiled. And Hen, who had heard the story before and had laughed at it each time, a laugh like a gull going up off a pan-wall, looked up from her bowl and said, “Yes.”

Just that. *Yes.* Then she went back to her porridge.

Teo looked at Hen, and then across the table at Ilse, the way he looked at a sum that hadn’t come out. Ilse looked away. Everyone was tired in Fogmonth, she told herself. The fogs came in off the sea and sat on the Wing like a wet fleece, and half the links in the College had colds.

“She’s not laughing, Marrow,” Teo said on the stair afterwards, low. “Have you noticed? At anything. Since about Thursday. I told her the one about the eel and the bishop and she just nodded, like I’d told her the time.”

“She’s tired.”

“She was tired at the Ladder and she laughed so hard she had to sit on the floor.” “I don’t like it.”

Ilse said she was sure it was nothing. She said it well; she had been saying things well for nine years.

Since the night in the Antechamber, she had signed one sheet each morning. One was the least Coyle’s roll would tick. She had found reasons: a cramp in her hand, a pen that wouldn’t draw, the Gallery sheet she wanted to check before she put her mark to it. And Hen, who always took the first round, who was quickest, had gone on signing all four without a word, because Hen always did. Ilse had noticed this. She had put it in the cupboard at the back of her mind with everything else, and shut the door, and leaned on it.

She had written a note to Dr. Orle on the twenty-first, unsigned, in a disguised hand. *Look at the Morning Survey sheets under a damp alum glass.* She had read it over twice and seen at once that there was only one kind of person in the College who would know to write it, and she had burned it in Teo’s spirit-stove and watched the corner curl.

One more week, she had told herself. One more week to think of a way to say it that wouldn’t hang her.

On the twenty-seventh, Hen didn’t come down to the window seat after supper. She sat at the table in the common room instead, with her hands flat on either side of a sheet of error problems she wasn’t doing, and when Sorry climbed into her lap she didn’t stroke it. It turned round twice and settled, and looked up at her with its amber eyes, waiting to be told what it was.

Hen used to describe it every night. *Look at you, you ridiculous article, ears like two mainsails.* Sorry liked it; its ears grew a little every time, until it looked like a cat assembled from spare parts by an enthusiast.

Hen looked down at it.

“It’s a cat,” she said.

She said it with perfect confidence, because it was true, and Sorry obeyed. Its ears drew in and settled, until they were a small grey cat’s ears, neat and ordinary, the ears of a cat in a picture-book. It looked up at Hen with its amber eyes in its ordinary face, and Hen looked back, and neither of them found anything to say.

Ilse got up and went over and picked it up.

“A small grey cat,” she said, “with ridiculous ears.” And the ears came back, too big again, and Sorry pushed its head hard into her chin, and Ilse stood holding it in the middle of the room with her eyes stinging, while Hen looked at the error problems and didn’t do them.


* * *


Cass tried on the twenty-eighth.

She came in with the look of someone who has decided to solve a thing with violence, sat down across from Hen, put her boots up on the table, and began.

“Salt Steps,” she said. “What d’you do up there all day? Rake salt? Watch it dry? Hey-ho, salt’s dry, let’s have a sing-song about it. Paper-walls on a beach.” Hen didn’t look up. “Your sums are wrong, Solberg. That one. And that one. Halloway’ll have you for it. Little Henrike, best at Mensuration, can’t carry a seven.”

“All right,” said Hen.

Cass reached across and took the pen out of her hand.

It was Hen’s good pen, the one her father had sent from the Steps in a box with a ribbon on it, the one she had once chased Teo the whole length of the Long Gallery to get back. Cass held it up between two fingers, like a dead mouse.

“Mine now,” Cass said. “Come and get it.”

Hen looked at the pen. Then she looked at Cass, quite calmly, with her flat pale eyes.

“You can have it,” she said.

Cass stood up so fast that her chair went over. She threw the pen on the table, where it rolled off and clattered on the floorboards, and went out, and Ilse heard the outer door bang. Hen bent, slowly, and picked the pen up, and put it on the table beside the problems, and sat looking at it.

Ilse found Cass on the stair outside, sitting on the bottom step in the cold with her knees pulled up and her braids hanging forward over her face. She was crying, and furious with herself for crying, and wiping her nose on her wrist.

“She’d have hit me,” Cass said. “A week ago she’d have hit me with the inkwell. Two weeks ago she’d have hit me with the *table*. Road and rain, Ilse, I called her salt-scraper and she said *all right*.” She dragged in a breath. “What is it? What’s the matter with her? You know things. You always know things. Tell me what’s wrong with her.”

Ilse sat down beside her on the cold stone and did not tell her.


* * *


On the first morning of Frost the Long Gallery windows were furred white on the inside, and every breath went up in a cloud, and the chain was so cold it burned to hold.

They did the stretch. Teo held the far end. Cass walked the wall with her palm flat against it. Ilse drew. And Hen counted, because Hen always counted, in a clear, steady voice that carried the length of the gallery.

“Sixty-three,” said Hen. “Sixty-three. A chain and sixty-three links.”

It was right. She had always counted perfectly. But she used to count the way other people sang, with a lift on the round numbers and a little crow of triumph when a length came out even. This morning every number came out the same, the same weight and the same pitch, like pegs driven one after another into the same hard ground.

The stair had turned a hand’s breadth again. The gallery was three links long. Ilse drew it all in, four sheets, and they went down to the Antechamber with the frost still in their hair.

Coyle was at the counter with the roll, his nose red, and a dozen parties were ahead of them at the sloping desks, signing and blowing on their fingers. Ilse laid the four sheets on their desk, and before she had straightened, Hen reached past her and drew them toward herself.

“I’ll take the first round,” Hen said. “I’m quickest.”

She dipped her pen. Her right hand came down on the *Wren Stair* sheet, steady, the pen held between thumb and two fingers, and Ilse, standing at her shoulder, saw the fingers.

The tips of the first two were grey.

It was the grey of pencil-lead rubbed with a thumb. It began at the very tips, under the nails, and ran down past the first joint and faded, the way a stain fades into cloth. In the lamplight it had a faint sheen to it, like stone that has been handled for a long time. Hen set down her wave over a line in its printed box, neat and quick and confident, and her grey fingertips lay on the paper beside it, and they didn’t look like a hand at all. They looked like a hand that had been drawn by someone who had only ever been told about hands.

Ilse stopped breathing.

She saw it then, the whole of it, as clear as a traverse closing. Hen signed four sheets every morning, every sheet, all four, since the last week of Reaping. Teo signed two. Cass signed two. And Ilse, these last two weeks, had signed one. Sixty mornings, four marks a morning: two hundred and forty knots, two hundred and forty violet lines running off the edge of the paper to wherever they went, and something coming back down every one of them into the first two fingers of Hen Solberg’s right hand. She was the best of them. She was the quickest and the most careful and the most willing. She was the most diligent signer in the party, maybe in the whole College, and she had been carrying it all.

Whatever came down a sheet came down to the hands that had signed it, shared between them, and Ilse had taken her hand off three sheets in four. Hen had been carrying Ilse’s share, too.

*Don’t carry what you can make someone else carry.* Quince’s voice, dry, in the back kitchen in Hobbet. Ilse had never once in her life thought she would hear it as an accusation.

Hen had moved on to the *Landing*. Ilse put her hand out and closed it over Hen’s right hand, over the pen.

It was cold. It was not the cold of a hand on a frosty morning, which warms when you hold it. It was the cold of a stone taken out of a stream: a cold that went on being cold, that sat in her palm and gave nothing back.

“Let me,” Ilse said.

“I’m quickest.”

“I know. Let me.”

Hen let go of the pen. She didn’t argue. She didn’t ask why. She stood up from the desk and stood beside it with her hands at her sides, the grey fingertips just showing under her cuff, and waited, like a passenger for a coach.

Ilse sat down and signed the other three sheets herself, one after another, wren’s foot over violet knot, three lines and a spur, knowing exactly what she was doing and doing it anyway. Her hand did not shake. She thought that it ought to. Across the desk Teo was watching her, and Cass was watching Hen, and from behind the counter Merrin Coyle looked up from his roll, and looked at Hen’s hands, and went on looking for a moment longer than a clerk needs to look at anything, and then looked down.


* * *


Hen sat down at breakfast and didn’t eat.

She sat with her porridge in front of her and her hands in her lap, and after a while she said, in the same voice she had counted in, “I’m cold.” Then she said nothing else. Teo put his own coat round her shoulders, which made her look like a child in a tent. He was very white.

“Infirmary,” he said. “Now. Marrow. Now.”

They took her between them. She could walk, but only as though each step had to be decided on. Ilse went ahead and opened the doors, and people in the Old Court turned to look.

Dr. Sabeth Orle met them at the door, a square, brisk woman with her sleeves rolled, and she took one look at Hen’s face and then at Hen’s right hand, and her mouth went thin.

“Bed by the window,” she said to an orderly. “The end one. Get her warm.” She turned Hen’s hand over in her own, gently, and looked at the fingertips under the window light, and pressed one of them with her thumb, and watched it not go pink. “How long has she been like this?”

“A week,” said Teo. “Since Thursday. The laughing stopped on Thursday.”

Orle wrote that down in a ledger she took from her apron pocket. Ilse watched her write it. Her pen moved fast and hard, with a kind of anger in it.

“What is it?” said Cass. “What’s wrong with her?”

Orle closed the ledger. “Drift-fatigue,” she said, and the words came out of her mouth like a thing she had been made to swallow and was bringing back up. “That is what I am to call it. Rest. Warmth. Broth.” She looked at the three of them, at their frost-wet hair and their white faces. “I’m sorry. She’s the fourth. I don’t know what it is. I know what it isn’t. Go and get warm. Come back after dinner. One at a time.”

There were five beds on that side of the long ward. Wil Ashdown lay in the second, on his back, looking at the ceiling. His hands lay on the blanket. Both of them were grey now, to the knuckles. The furlong girl with the plait, whom Ilse had glimpsed through the ward door in Gleaning, lay in the third, propped on her pillows as before. In the fourth a girl Ilse didn’t know lay asleep or not asleep, with her face to the wall.

Teo didn’t go and get warm. He sat on a bench outside the infirmary door, a bench made for one ordinary person, his huge frame folded onto it with his knees nearly at his chin. When Ilse came back after dinner he was still there, with a pie wrapped in a cloth on his knees that he had gone to the kitchens and made, and then not taken in.

“It’s eel,” he said, not looking at her. “Her favourite. She says it’s the only good thing that ever came out of Lisk except me. Then I thought she might say *thank you* in that voice, and I couldn’t.” He held it out. “You take it.”

She took it.

The ward was quiet. The windows looked west over the Margin, and the afternoon light came in long and pale across the beds. Hen was sitting up against the pillows with a blanket round her, quite still, her hands on the coverlet. Somebody had brushed her hair. The grey fingertips lay on the white linen like a child’s that had been drawing with charcoal.

Ilse sat on the stool by the bed and put the pie on the blanket.

“Teo made it,” she said. “Eel.”

Hen looked at it. “Thank you,” she said, in that voice.

Down the row, Wil Ashdown breathed in and out. Ilse wanted, very badly, to make Hen laugh. She had never been the one who made people laugh; she was the one who said the dry thing in the corner. She tried anyway.

“Teo says the pie’s a vintage,” she said. “He says it’s developing character.” Hen looked at her. “Tide’s out on that one, I suppose.”

Hen considered this.

“The tide goes out twice a day,” she said.

Ilse looked down at her own hands. After a while, Hen spoke again, without being asked. Her voice was quite level. It was the voice she had used to count the gallery, to say *I’m cold*, to say *you can have it*.

“I can’t remember why things were funny,” Hen said.

Ilse looked up.

Hen was looking out of the window at the Margin. Her face was calm. It was not sad. That was the worst of it. It had nothing in it at all to be sad with.

“I remember that they were,” Hen said. “I remember laughing. I remember the eel and the bishop. Teo told it in the Chain & Anchor and I laughed so much I spilled the cider on Cass. I can remember all of it. The words. Where we were sitting.” She turned her grey fingertips over on the blanket and looked at them, not with fear, only with attention, the way she would have looked at a sum. “I just can’t remember why.”

Ilse sat on the stool and did not move. She found she could not.

Nearly two weeks since the night in the Antechamber with the violet line running under Hen’s mark. She had known. She had seen it as plainly as she had ever seen anything in her life, and she had gone back to the Wren Stair and slept, and got up the next morning and done the Survey and signed one sheet, and let Hen sign the rest. She had written a note and burned it. She had told herself one more week. And she had done it all, every day of it, for the same reason she had done everything since she was fourteen: to keep herself out of the ledger. To keep the wren’s foot clean. To protect the one thing she had that was hers.

Hen had carried it. Hen had carried Ilse’s silence down two hundred and forty violet lines into the tips of her fingers, and had not known she was carrying anything at all.

“I’m sorry,” Ilse said. It came out very small.

Hen looked at her, mildly.

“What for?” she said. It was a real question. She wanted to know.


* * *


That night Ilse walked the Drift Wing.

She walked it because she couldn’t lie still, and because the Wing at night was the only place in the College that seemed to be telling the truth. It was never quite where it had been at dinner. She counted her paces without meaning to: ninety-three from the stair to the turn tonight, the frost making the hold go thin. On the Long Gallery the moonlight came in through the frosted windows and lay on the floor in long white panes, and the gallery was a few links longer than it had been that morning, and by First Bell it would be longer still, and four people would come with a chain and a levelglass to tell it where it was. And it would believe them, because they would sign. Hundreds of signatures every morning, holding the whole sprawling Wing in place over the moving heath. And every one of them sitting on a violet knot.

She stopped at the end window and put her palm flat against the wall. The stone was cold and faintly trembling, like the flank of a horse. She went over it the way she would have gone over a survey, station by station.

She could go to Brask. Brask would believe her. Brask would also have to ask how she knew, and Ilse would have to tell her about the lens, and the lens was the one thing she had kept back in the blue-door cottage, the mark under the mark. *No more lies on any College sheet.* She had kept that promise. She had kept it so carefully that she had let a girl go grey.

She could go to Master Hallam. He was the Master of the Register; these were his sheets. He would be kind.
