He would pour her tea and listen with his gloved hands folded, and he would be appalled, and then he would ask, gently, the way he asked everything: *But how did you see it, my dear?* And she would not be able to answer him either.

She could say nothing. She could go on signing her one sheet a morning and hope that someone else found the knots, someone who didn’t have to explain how they knew. Kasimir Reyne, perhaps, with his red-spined ledger. She could let the Board of Errata hold her tribunal, and keep her secret all the way to the end of it, and walk out with a clean wren’s foot, and come back to the infirmary in Thaw to sit by Hen’s bed and find it still and grey.

*Never let anyone carry you.* That had been the other half of Quince’s saying, the half she had always been proudest of. She had come five hundred and sixty miles on the Long Field money so that nobody would ever carry her. And for three years, on every charter, she had let a dead man do it, and for two weeks she had let a nineteen-year-old girl from the Salt Steps do it, and she had called the first one clever and had not called the second one anything at all.

She took her hand off the wall.

The truth was not complicated. It never was, at the bottom. She had always known that; it was what made her good at lying. The ghost under the Morning Survey sheets could only be seen by a forger, and only told by a forger, and the moment she told it she would be one, out loud, in daylight, with her name on it. She would lose the Patent she didn’t have yet. She would very likely lose the College. She would lose the field, for good this time; there would be no buying back the Long Field for a woman struck off by the Board of Errata. She looked at the whole cost, every link of it, and wrote it down in her head with no gap smoothed over.

Then she went back along the gallery and down the stair, ninety-three paces, to the Wren Stair.

The Bell of Hours had struck one some time ago; it was the third of Frost now, strictly speaking. There was a line of lamplight under the common-room door. Of course they were awake. Teo would be sitting at the table with his hands round a cup he wasn’t drinking. Cass would be at the window with her boots on, because she always put her boots on when she was miserable, as if she might need to walk somewhere in a hurry.

Ilse stood outside her own door. She had never knocked on it. Nobody knocked on their own door.

She knocked.

There was a silence inside, and the scrape of a chair, and then Teo opened it, enormous in the doorway with the lamp behind him and his eyes red. Past him she could see Cass at the window, in her boots, turning round. Sorry was on the table among the cold cups, its too-big ears pricked forward, watching her.

“Marrow?” said Teo. “Why did you knock?”

Ilse looked at the two of them, at the lamp and the cups and the empty window seat where Hen used to push her cold feet under someone’s leg, and she made herself stand in the doorway, in the light, where they could both see her face.

“I have to tell you something about myself,” she said, “and then something about this College, and you’ll like neither.”


# Part Three: Errata


## Chapter 17: Confession in the Mess Kitchen

The Mess kitchen after midnight smelled of cold mutton fat and lye soap and the ghost of a thousand loaves, and it was the only room in the College that Ilse trusted not to listen.

The Wren Stair listened; voices went through its lath walls the way water goes through a sock. Besides, Hen’s bed was up there, stripped to the ticking since Dr. Orle’s porters had carried her down, and Ilse could not have said any of this with that bed at the edge of her eye.

So they had come down the back stair with one candle, the three of them in their coats over their nightshirts, and Teo had lit his spirit-stove at the end of the long scrubbed table where the Errata Society argued on Thursdays. He did it the way other men crossed themselves: eleven pumps of the brass reservoir, a quarter turn of the valve, a match, and the blue ring of flame coming up with a noise like a cat sneezing. Nobody had asked for tea. Cass sat on the bread-board with her boots on the bench and her arms folded and watched Ilse the way she watched weather coming in off the heath.

Ilse took off her father’s coat, turned it inside out on the table and slit the lining along the hem with her knife.

“Marrow,” said Teo. “That’s your good coat.”

“It’s been unpicked and sewn up more times than you’ve had pie.” She drew the things out one at a time and laid them in a row on the boards, as she would have laid out instruments for Brask. A corked bottle the size of her thumb. A disc of watch-glass in a brass ring, a square of chamois and a twist of alum. The fine steel pen Quince had ground for her the year before he died. A scrap of old vellum, folded small, which she opened flat and turned to the candle so that they could see what was inked on it: a crooked, old-fashioned Q, its tail doubled back and hooked through its own ring.

“That,” Ilse said, “is the witness-mark of Abelard Quince, hedge-surveyor, of Hobbet in the Fallowmarch. He died three years ago in Seedtime. I’ve been signing it under my own ever since the week we buried him.”

Teo’s hand stopped on the kettle handle.

She told it in order, because order was the only way she knew how to tell anything. She told them about Quince, who had taken her on at fourteen because she had walked into his shop with a measured drawing of his own back garden and pointed out that his privy stood four links over his neighbour’s line. She told them about blind ink, gall and alum and a whisper of quicksilver, which dried to nothing and came up violet under a damp lens. She told them about the Law of Transfer, which nobody in the College taught and every hedge-forger in the Fallowmarch knew: a mark traced in blind ink under another mark would take the burden down into itself, as a drain takes rain.

“So the weight goes to him,” said Teo. “To—to the dead man.”

“The dead don’t feel it. Quince thought it was the neatest thing in the trade. Use the dead as a ditch.”

She told them how many. Thirty-four charters in nine years: three as Quince’s hands under Quince’s mark while he lived, and thirty-one since, under her own wren’s foot. Hedges, ditches, a mill-race at Fennock. Every one inside its latitude, and every one, she believed, put back where it ought to have been. She told them about Agnes Tull’s hedge, and the young man in the grey-blue coat crouched in the grass measuring the root-tear, and how she had spent the money she was saving for something else on a coach seat, and walked into the Margin because the Court could not try a Member of the College.

“That’s why you came,” said Cass. “Not to be a surveyor.”

“I wanted to be a surveyor as well. I just wanted not to be in prison more.”

“And the something else?” said Teo.

“A field.” Ilse looked at the vellum. “A man with a licence moved my father’s fence when I was twelve, and the Court said he could. That’s a long story and I’ll tell it you another night.”

The kettle began to tick and then to whine. Teo took it off the flame and didn’t pour it. He stood holding it in both hands like a man holding a cat that might go for him, and his round face had gone a dull and blotchy red.

“You let me tell you about the Patent exam,” he said. “Twice. About being sick in the examination-hall bucket in front of the Dean of Lisk. And you sat there the whole time with—with a dead man in your coat lining, and said *that’s rotten, Teo*, and I thought —” He stopped. “I’m sorry. I thought we were the sort of people who told each other things.”

“We are,” said Ilse. “I’m telling you.”

“Seventy-one days late.”

“Yes.”

He put the kettle down very carefully, as though it were the thing that had wronged him. Ilse had braced for a great many reactions on the back stair, and not for this one: the plain hurt of a man who would have lied for her if she had asked, and had never been asked.

Then Teo said, “Right,” and blew out through his nose, and poured the water over the leaves. “Right. You lied about yourself. All right. You’re not lying about this.” He looked at her. “Are you? About Hen?”

“No.”

“Then I don’t care about the hedges. I’ll care about them later, properly, with a list.” He pushed a cup at her. “Drink that.”

From the bread-board Cass made a noise. Ilse thought at first that she was choking. Then she saw that Cass was laughing, silently, with her braids shaking and her knuckles pressed against her mouth.

“Road and rain,” Cass said, when she could. “Oh, Road and rain. I knew it. Didn’t I say? Day of the Walk, I said you was a liar.”

“You said it was a nice one.”

“It was a nice one.” She wiped her eyes. “Knew you was Tenting at heart. Paper-wall with a hedge-thief’s hands. Thirty-four hedges, and you sit in Calder’s class prim as a chapel cat.” She swung her boots down off the bench. “Now tell the other part. The part about this Big House that we won’t like.”


* * *


She told them that part with Quince’s watch-glass in her hand.

She told them about the Antechamber at night, and the copyist’s key, and the thirtieth sheet, when she had stopped hoping the next one would be clean. On every sheet filed since 1201 there was a knot of blind ink printed on the rule of every signing box, fine as a hair and laid in before anybody ever touched pen to paper, so that every mark in the College came down on top of it in perfect registration. When you signed on the rule, as you were taught, you rode it, and you never knew.

“Every mark,” said Teo. “Every morning.”

“Two hundred marks a day. The lines run off toward the binding, going somewhere. I don’t know where.” She set the glass down. “It’s the same trick as Quince’s ditch, turned the other way. I put a dead man under my mark so I wouldn’t carry. Somebody has tied all our marks in under theirs. We’re the ditch.”

“Hen,” said Cass. The laughter had gone out of her altogether.

“Hen signs the most. She always has. She signs for the party when we’re slow, and she signs extra when Coyle’s short, because she’s quickest.” Ilse made herself say the rest. “I saw the lines on Fogmonth 19. I’ve known for two weeks. I didn’t say anything because I couldn’t say how I knew without saying all this, and I was frightened, and I told myself there’d be a better time. Hen went grey in the meantime. That’s mine. I’d like it recorded.”

“Recorded,” said Teo, automatically, and then looked stricken, as if the College had spoken through him.

“Who?” said Cass. “Who’s doing it?”

“Someone with a key to the Antechamber and a forger’s hand, or a forger’s money.” Ilse spread her hands. “Coyle prints the folios. The proctors carry them. Any Master could walk into that room. And on the night I found it, Kasimir Reyne was in the far stacks with a lamp, where he’d no business being, and he very much didn’t want to tell me why.”

“The Chancellor’s boy.” Cass’s eyes narrowed. “Court’s wanted the Big House on its knees a hundred years.”

“I don’t *know* it’s him.”

“You don’t know it ain’t.”

“There’s evidence it’s happening,” said Teo. “Not who. Those are different columns.” He sat down heavily. “So we go to the Provost. We wake him up if we have to.”

“And tell him how I know?”

Teo opened his mouth and shut it.

“The minute I say *blind ink* to Steyne,” Ilse said, “somebody asks how a Walker from Hobbet knows how to read it, and the Board already has a letter from Mr. Reyne about the Tull charter. They’d have me out of the Gate inside a month. Maybe that’s fair. But whoever is doing this would go on doing it, and they’d know someone had seen.” She turned the cup in her hands. “Brask knows what I am. She’s known since the Walk. She made me promise, three weeks ago, that I’d never put another lie on a College sheet. That was the price of her keeping quiet.”

“Then we stop signing,” said Teo. “All three of us. We just—don’t.”

“And the stair goes, and Lisbet Arnaud stands at the bottom of it asking very loudly why the Wren Stair party has stopped attesting. Whoever laid those lines reads the proctors’ book, I promise you. A party that suddenly won’t sign is a party that’s seen something. We have to sign every morning and look like idiots who don’t know anything.”

“That,” said Cass, “I can do.”

“So we sign,” said Teo, “and we carry.”

“No.” Ilse picked up the steel pen and turned it over in her fingers. “We sign crooked.”


* * *


Quince had called it *signing off the rule*. A ghost-line, he used to say, was a lodger who had copied your key, and it would only let itself in if the key went all the way home in the lock. You laid the blind mark down; the true mark came on top of it stroke for stroke, and the burden ran down from one to the other through every place they touched. But if the mark on top was set a hair aside, or turned the smallest part of a degree, the strokes crossed the ghost instead of riding it. They touched at a point or two, and a point or two carried next to nothing.

“It’s still a witness-mark,” Ilse said. She had taken a sheet of the Mess accounts paper from the drawer under the bread-board and was ruling a signing box on it with the edge of a ladle. “You mean it. You attest with intent, so the Law of Attestation is satisfied and the sheet holds. But it isn’t quite your mark. It’s your mark telling a small lie about where it is.”

“That’s the bit Brask won’t like,” said Cass.

“That’s the bit Brask would take my head off for.” Ilse didn’t look up. Three weeks ago she had sat in Brask’s cottage with a cup of terrible tea and promised, and meant it, and the meaning of it had lasted twenty-two days. She drew the box. “And it costs. A mark that’s off the rule holds a little weaker than a true one. The building will believe us a little less. If the stretch has a bad night, we might lose a few links.”

“How many is a few?” said Teo.

“I don’t know. I never signed the Drift Wing before.”

She showed them: a sixteenth of an inch high of the rule, turned a little sunwise, as if the hand had been jogged. She did her own wren’s foot ten times skewed and once true, and the difference was so small that Teo had to bring the candle over to see it.

Cass got it in four tries. Her mark had always looked as though it had been made in a moving cart; a slightly different cart cost her nothing.

Teo could not do it.

He tried nine times. Each time his big hand set the pen down a sixteenth high, and each time, as the stroke went on, the pen crept back onto the rule by itself, until the mark sat dead true in its box, as if a plumb-line had drawn it.

“I can’t,” he said. He was sweating. “I’m sorry. It’s like trying to sing flat on purpose. My hand knows where it goes.”

“Honest all the way down to the fingers,” said Cass. “Terrible affliction.”

“Start your mark from the wrong end,” said Ilse. “You can’t lie with your hand. Fine. Don’t try to. Lie with the order.” She took his pen hand and turned it on the paper, gently, the way Quince had once turned hers. “Begin where you usually finish. Your hand won’t know where it is, so it won’t know where to go back to. It’ll be a hair out all on its own.”

He did it. The mark came out stiff and very slightly wrong, like a word spelled correctly in someone else’s handwriting.

“That’s horrible,” he said.

“It’s perfect.”

“That’s what’s horrible about it.”


* * *


Sorry had come down inside Ilse’s coat and slept through the confession in the sleeve. It came out now and sat among the instruments with its too-big ears turned toward the inkwell, a small grey cat because she had said so at the Ossel Fair, give or take an ear.

“Go on, then,” said Ilse, and tipped a little of the common black into a saucer from the accounts inkwell.

Sorry went at it as it went at everything it was allowed to eat, with its whole body. The saucer was clean in under a minute. It looked up for more with black whiskers.

Ilse uncorked the little bottle from her coat lining and held it out at the length of her arm.

The ossel recoiled. Its fur went up all along its spine, its ears went flat, and it backed across the table, scattering the alum, until its hindquarters met the teapot.

“Quicksilver,” said Cass softly. “They hate it. Every Tenting kid knows. You crack a levelglass in camp, you won’t see an ossel for a week.”

“Blind ink’s got a whisper of it.” Ilse corked the bottle and set it down at the far end of the table, and Sorry’s fur settled a little. Then she reached into the coat lining again and drew out the last thing, a sheet she had taken from the spoiled-folio basket in the Antechamber the night she found the lines: a blank Morning Survey form, never signed, its signing boxes empty and ruled in common black. She laid it flat beside the saucer.

Sorry wouldn’t go near it. It wouldn’t even look at it. It turned its back on the folio and sat facing the wall with its tail wrapped tight round its feet, like a dowager who has been seated next to the wrong person at dinner.

“It’s blank,” said Teo. “It ate my Mensuration notes.”

“There’s ink on it,” said Ilse. “We just can’t see it.”

The sheet lay on the scrubbed boards, cream-coloured with its neat empty boxes, as innocent a thing as Ilse had ever seen, and all three of them leaned a little away from it.

Teo stood up to fetch more cups from the rack. He was a large man in a small kitchen at two in the morning, and his elbow caught the hanging copper pans, and the biggest of them came off its hook and hit the flagstones with a noise like the Bell of Hours falling down a well.

Sorry went up.

It didn’t jump. It *rose*, and while it rose it changed, so quickly that Ilse’s eye couldn’t follow the joins. The cat went long and then longer. When it came to rest it was standing on the table among the instruments on two thin dark legs like reeds. It had a body the size of a hen’s, slate-grey and sleek, and a neck folded back on itself in a tight S, and a long dagger of a bill, and at the back of its narrow head two plumes lifted like ears that had remembered they were supposed to be feathers. One foot was drawn up under its breast. It turned its head and looked at the three of them out of eyes that were unmistakably Sorry’s, pale amber.

Nobody had said *heron*. Nobody had said anything at all.

It held the shape for the space of three breaths. Then the plumes softened, and the neck shortened, and the bird folded down into itself like a wet sheet settling over a chair. A small grey cat with ridiculous ears sat on the table beside the alum and began, with great concentration, to wash a paw.

Teo hung the pan back on its hook with enormous care.

“That,” he said, “was a bird.”

“It must have heard someone say it.” Ilse’s voice came out thinner than she liked. “Sometime. It kept it.”

Cass had come down off the bread-board. She stood with one hand at her route-cords, and her face had the look of someone who has walked a familiar road and found the river on the wrong side of it. “Ossels don’t do that,” she said. “They go to what you tell ’em, or they go to mush. They don’t go to something else. Nobody told it bird.”

Sorry finished its paw and started on the other one.

Teo poured the tea, which had stewed black, and they drank it anyway.


* * *


First Bell rang at a quarter past five, and they were standing at the foot of the Wren Stair with the chain when it did, because none of them had been to bed.

It was the first Morning Survey without Hen, and it came at Ilse sideways. Hen had always held the zero end of the chain at the stairfoot and called the counts in her loud Salt Steps voice, *tally one, and tally two, and that’s the tide in*, so that other parties on other stairs had learned to set their pace by her. This morning Teo held the zero end and counted under his breath, and the Wren Stair was so quiet that Ilse could hear the links ticking against the treads.

The stair had turned in the night by about four degrees, sunwise, as it had all week. The Long Gallery had stretched six links at the north end, where the boards always crept. Cass read the grain with her bare palm flat on the floor, and they argued about the north wall, and Cass was right. They closed the traverse at three chains forty-one links. Ilse drafted it on the plane-table in the grey light, in the fair hand Hallam called a draftswoman’s, and she made it true, because the drawing was the one part of this that could still be true.

The Register Antechamber smelled of size and lamp oil and was full of parties queuing with their sheets. Merrin Coyle sat behind his desk with his cuffs dark to the wrist with ink, and he looked up at them with his usual anxious pleasantness, and at the space where Hen should have been, and away.

“Wren Stair,” he said. “Three this morning. I’m sorry. Sign here, and here, and the party line.”

Ilse went first. She took the College pen from its stand, dipped it, and set her wren’s foot in the box a sixteenth of an inch high of the rule and turned a little sunwise, as if the hand had been jogged.

She had never felt the ghost-line when she signed. But this time, as the three strokes and the spur went down, she noticed the absence of what she must have been feeling every morning since Reaping without a name for it: a faint, steady draw through the nib, like the draught that comes under a door when another door is opened somewhere far off in the house. This morning there was no draught. The pen moved through the ink like a pen. Her mark lay on the sheet a little crooked and entirely hers.

Cass signed off the rule without looking, as if she had done it all her life. Teo took the pen and stood over the box, breathing through his nose, and then he began his mark at its end and worked backward to its beginning, and it came out stiff and a hair askew.

Coyle blotted the sheets without a glance and laid them on the pile.


* * *


The day was long. At noon Ilse sat by Hen in the infirmary for a quarter of an hour, and Hen said *hello, Ilse* and *yes* and *I don’t mind*, and turned her grey fingertips over on the blanket as if they belonged to someone else. In the evening she went down to the Register for her copying, and when Coyle had gone to his supper she took the Wren Stair sheet from the morning’s pile, breathed alum-water onto the lens and laid it over the signing boxes.

The violet knot came up fine as a hair on the rule, its thread running off toward the binding. It ran *past* her mark. Her wren’s foot sat a little above it and a little turned, and the line went by beneath it like a road beneath a bridge, touching at one point where the spur crossed it and nowhere else. Cass’s mark crossed it twice. Teo’s, backward and stiff, lay across it like a stick across a stream.

She put the sheet back where it had been, square to the pile, and put out the lamp.

The Wren Stair was dark when she climbed it, and it did not feel right underfoot. It never had, quite, but tonight the treads had a faint give in them, the softness of a step on a ship, and at the turn of the landing the banister moved under her hand by the width of a fingernail and came back. The stair had held. It had held the way a tired man holds a heavy door, with his whole weight against it and his arms shaking.

Teo was sitting on the top step in the dark with his knees drawn up, waiting for her. He didn’t ask. She nodded, and he let his head fall back against the wall.

Below them the Long Gallery creaked, settling, all eleven windows grey with frost.

“Marrow,” Teo whispered, as if the walls might hear him and take offence, “we just lied to the building.”


## Chapter 18: Drift-Fatigue

The infirmary had been built in the days when the College believed in fresh air, and it had never stopped believing in it. The long ward ran the whole north side of the Old Court under a row of tall windows, and at least three of them were always open a hand’s width, so that the curtains round the beds breathed in and out all day like sleepers. Winter light lay along the floor in long pale bars. It smelled of carbolic and barley water and, faintly, of the sea.

There were nine beds with their curtains drawn back, and five of them were full.

Ilse had counted them on the way in, because counting gave her eyes a job. Two second-years she knew by sight from the Mess. A furlong from the Third Range, a tall boy with a ring in one ear, sitting up against his pillows with a book open on his knees that he was not reading. Wil Ashdown in the chair by the far window, as he had been in Gleaning, with his hands in his lap and his face toward the glass, though there was nothing outside it but the grey back of the chapel. And Hen.

Hen was sitting up. Someone had brushed her hair and plaited it, which Hen never did; she wore it loose and in her eyes and blew it out of the way when she was thinking. The plait lay over her shoulder on the blanket like a rope somebody else had coiled there. She looked up when they came down the ward, and she knew them, which was something.

“Ilse,” she said. “Teo.”

“Hello, Hen.”

“Hello.”

Teo had brought a seed-cake. He had baked it on the spirit-stove in two halves, because the stove would only take a small tin, and stuck the halves together with jam, and he had talked about it all the way across the Old Court in a long anxious river of words about caraway and whether Salt Steps people even liked caraway, which he thought they did, because Hen had once eaten four slices of a caraway loaf at the Chain & Anchor and said it tasted of her nan’s kitchen. He set it on the locker beside her bed and took the cloth off it.

Hen looked at the cake.

“It’s cake,” she said.

“It’s seed-cake,” said Teo. “Caraway. It’s—you said once, at the Chain, your nan —”

“Thank you.”

She didn’t touch it. She didn’t look away from it either. She looked at it the way you might look at a stone in a field: correctly, without interest, as a thing that was there.

Ilse sat on the edge of the bed. Hen’s hands were folded on the blanket. The grey had come further since Frost 3. It was past the first joint of every finger on the right hand now, smooth and cool-looking, the colour of a slate roof after rain. The left hand was only faintly dusky at the tips, as if she had been handling pencil leads.

“Did the stair hold?” Hen asked.

“It held.”

“Good.” A pause. “Who is counting?”

“Teo.”

“Teo counts too fast on the landing. He should count slower on the landing.” She said it without any of the old mockery, as a correction to be filed. “You should let me sign. I’m quickest.”

Ilse’s throat closed. “You’re resting,” she said. “Orle says.”

“Yes,” said Hen. She looked at her hands. “I don’t mind.”

Teo sat on the other side of the bed and told her about the Mess. He told her that Absalom Grice had tried to carry four bowls of porridge at once and been defeated by the third. He told her that Mother Nettle had called the Provost a gilded weasel in front of three proctors, and that the crows had agreed with her. He told it well, in his best Lisk storyteller’s manner, leaning in at the good parts. Hen listened to all of it. At the end of each story she said “Oh,” in the tone of someone being told what time it was.

Ilse had once watched Hen laugh so hard at one of Cass’s stories that she had fallen off the end of a bench in the Mess and gone on laughing under the table, with her boots in the air. She looked at the plait on the blanket and thought about that, and then made herself stop, because it was no use to anyone.

When the bell for second lecture rang, Teo cut a slice of the cake and put it on a saucer near Hen’s hand, where she could reach it without deciding to. She thanked him again.


* * *


Dr. Sabeth Orle caught Ilse at the end of the ward with two fingers on her sleeve.

“Marrow. A minute.”

The dispensary was a narrow room behind the ward with shelves to the ceiling and a long deal table scrubbed white. Orle shut the door. She was a square, quick woman with her sleeves rolled to the elbow and her greying hair pinned up anyhow with what looked like a pencil and a knitting needle, and she had the look, always, of someone who had been interrupted halfway through a sum.

“You’re her party. You live with her. You do the Morning Survey with her.” Orle didn’t sit, and didn’t ask Ilse to. “Tell me what she was like a month ago.”

Ilse told her. The laugh. The Salt Steps sayings, *tide’s out on that one*, which Hen used for everything from a bad joke to a dead mouse. How she had begun missing jokes in Fogmonth, and then stopped making them, and then started answering in short flat sentences, like a ledger read aloud. Orle listened with her arms folded and asked short questions. When did she stop. Which came first. Did she sleep. Did she eat. Was she left-handed.

“Right-handed.”

“They all are,” said Orle. “Nearly. Watch.” She went to a shelf and brought down a ledger bound in green cloth, and opened it on the table, and ran her finger down a column without letting Ilse see the rest of the page. “Grey begins on the right hand in every case but one. The one is Benet Lowe in the Third Range, who writes with his left, and his grey began on his left. It always starts in the fingertips of the hand they sign with. Then the palm. Then up the wrist. The other hand follows, slower, weeks behind.” She shut the ledger. “Do you know how Drift-fatigue presents, Marrow?”

“No.”

“Neither does anyone. I’ve been reading about it for two years. It doesn’t exist in a single text before the Provost said it out loud at assembly.” Orle’s voice stayed level, but she put her palm down flat on the green cover as if to stop it opening by itself. “Drift-sickness I know. Vertigo, nausea, lost time. It comes on in the Margin and goes away on held ground. This doesn’t go away. This is burden. It looks like burden, the way it comes on old surveyors who’ve signed too much land. Except these are children, and they haven’t signed anything but their Morning Survey sheets and a paving stone.” She looked at Ilse very straight. “And fatigue doesn’t choose a hand.”

Ilse said nothing. Her own right hand was in her coat pocket. She took it out and laid it on the deal table between them, palm down, because it seemed cowardly not to, and Orle glanced at it, pink and inky and whole, and back at her face.

“I wrote to the Surgeons’ Hall in Tessary,” said Orle. “Twice. In Fogmonth, and again when your friend came in. I asked whether anyone had seen burden presenting in the young without cause. The Provost came to see me on Frost 3.” She smiled without any humour at all. “Very charming. He admired my windows. He said that the College was at a delicate juncture with the Court and the Assembly, and that a letter from the Meridian’s infirmary to Tessary suggesting an unknown malady among our students would be read by people who wished us harm. He said he relied on my discretion. He said it twice, which is how I knew it was an order.”

“Have you stopped?”

“I’ve stopped sending them.” Orle tapped the ledger. “I haven’t stopped writing them.”

The window behind her looked out on the chapel wall, and a cairn-crow was walking along its coping with its grey neck stuck out, like a clerk looking for a lost file. Ilse watched it. She was thinking about the Law of Transfer and the violet line and the copyist’s key in her pocket, and about how much she could say to this woman without saying everything, and she found, as she had found with Teo and Cass, that the lines didn’t come apart neatly. You couldn’t lift one out without the whole drawing coming with it.

“You know something,” said Orle.

“I think,” Ilse said carefully, “that you should watch who signs the most.”

Orle’s eyes narrowed. “Signs what?”

“Anything. Morning Survey. Errata. Extra sheets when a party’s short. The ones who sign the most, the ones who are proud of being quick at it.” She heard her voice and made it steadier. “Hen signs more than the rest of our party put together. She always has.”

Orle looked at her. Ilse had the sensation, familiar from Brask, of being measured by somebody who knew to a hair how wrong her instruments could be.

“You won’t tell me how you know that.”

“Not yet.”

“But you’d tell me if you could.”

“Yes.”

“Hm.” Orle opened the ledger again, turned to a clean page at the back, and wrote a line in a fast, untidy doctor’s hand. Ilse couldn’t read it upside down. “You can visit her any hour you like. Any hour. If the porter argues, send him to me. And Marrow.” She looked up. “If you find out what this is, you bring it to me before you bring it to anyone in a good coat. I’ve five children in that ward, and I’m tired of being admired for my windows.”


* * *


Lisbet Arnaud came for Cass at four o’clock.

They were in the Fieldwork shed, cleaning chains, because it was Ilse’s afternoon to do it for Brask and Cass had come to keep her company and complain. The shed leaked at the north corner, where a bucket stood under a drip that rang every eleven seconds; Teo had timed it. The chains hung from pegs along the walls in their long dull loops, and the two of them were working down the length of one with rags and a pot of tallow, link by link, when the door opened and let in a slice of cold and the senior proctor.

Lisbet Arnaud was a fifth-year with a long, handsome, disapproving face and a voice that had been designed for reading notices. She looked at the chain, and at Cass’s blackened fingers, and at the bucket.

“Miss Tenter. The Provost would like to see you in his lodging. Now, if you please.”

Cass didn’t get up. “What for?”

“I’m sure he’ll tell you.”

“I’m sure he will. Asking you.”

Lisbet’s nostrils thinned. “There are questions,” she said, “concerning your movements in Fogmonth. The week of Brine End.”

The rag stopped in Cass’s hands.

Ilse stood up. “I’ll come.”

“You weren’t asked for.”

“I’m her party. I’ll wait outside.” She wiped her hands on her trousers and looked at Lisbet with the expression she had once used on Jem Rudd, the parish clerk, when he asked how she knew so much about the Tull boundary. “Unless the Provost would prefer that Miss Tenter walk across the Old Court alone with a proctor at her back while the whole First Range watches out of the windows. In which case I’m sure he’ll tell you.”

Lisbet looked at her a moment longer, and then, to Ilse’s surprise, something in the long face gave a little.

“Outside,” she said. “On the bench. And you don’t speak unless he sends for you.”

The Provost’s lodging was at the west end of the Old Court, behind a door with a brass knocker in the shape of a meridian arrow. Inside it was warm and quiet and carpeted so thickly that their boots made no noise. The passage had a long-case clock and a portrait of some long-dead Provost holding a pair of dividers like a man about to carve a joint. Lisbet knocked at a door at the end, and a voice said “Come,” and Cass went in without looking back, with her chin up and the route-cords swinging in her braids. The door closed behind her.

Ilse sat on the bench and listened.

The door was oak and the carpet ate sound, and she couldn’t hear the words, only the shapes of them. Steyne’s voice was low and pleasant and went on and on without pause, like a well-oiled wheel going downhill. Now and then a second man’s voice put in a short phrase, a proctor or a clerk. Then Cass’s voice, short. Then Steyne again, longer, and the word *College* came through the oak twice, and the word *generosity* once. Then Cass’s voice, very much louder, and one phrase in it as clear as if she had been standing in the passage: *“— been on that heath since before your Ninefold had boots —”*

Then nothing. The clock in the passage ticked. The dead Provost carved his joint.

Footsteps came along the passage from the inner stair, light and unhurried, and Ilse looked up and found Master Hallam standing over her with a sheaf of papers under his arm and his spectacles pushed up on his bald head. His gloves were dove-grey kid and fitted like skin.

“Miss Marrow,” he said, with real pleasure, and then, taking in the bench and the door and her face, more gently: “Ah. Waiting for Miss Tenter, I expect.”

“Yes, Master.”

“Don’t fret, my dear.” He shifted the papers to his other arm. “The Provost is a careful man, and careful men ask a great many questions so that they never have to answer one. I’ve told him myself that the Tenting have no more to do with these voids than the gulls do.” He looked at the oak door with an expression of mild, weary fondness, the look of a man regarding a sideboard he has bumped his shin on for thirty years. “He’ll listen eventually. He usually does, once he’s frightened himself sufficiently. Will you be in the Register on Thursday?”

“Yes, Master.”

“Good. I’ve some Salt Steps tithe maps that want a steadier hand than Coyle’s.” He smiled at her over the spectacles, which had slid down again. “Be kind to your friend tonight. It’s a cold thing to be made a stranger in your own house.”

He went on down the passage. Ilse watched him go, and was grateful, and sat with her hands between her knees and waited.

When Cass came out her face was the colour of tallow.

She walked straight past the bench without stopping, and Ilse got up and went after her down the passage and out into the Old Court, where the light was going and the frost was coming up white between the paving stones. Cass didn’t speak until they were halfway across. Then she stopped dead on the meridian line, square on the brass studs where nobody ever stood, and turned round.

“They think we’re unmaking the ground,” she said. “*We.*”

“What did he ask you?”

“Where my wagon winters. Who my nan is. How many in the Tenter wagon and how many in the Wainwright and do we *talk*, the clans.” Her voice was shaking, not with tears. “Whether it’s true the Tenting have songs for loosening ground. Whether I’d been to Brine End. I said I’d been to Corrow to see my nan, and that’s eight miles off, and he said *eight miles is not very far, Miss Tenter, for someone who walks as well as your people do*. Smiling. Like he was paying me a compliment.” She spat on the frost, neatly, beside the brass stud. “And that proctor writing it all down. Every word. And then he says the College has extended great *generosity* to my people by admitting me, and he’s sure I’ll want to repay it, and if I hear anything from my kin, anything at all, I’m to come and tell him. Like a ferret down a hole.”

“What did you say?”

Cass looked at her. The fury in her face was white and set.

“I said we’d been on that heath since before his Ninefold had boots, and I’d bring him word when the gulls did.” She turned and walked on. “Then I said thank you, Provost, like Nan taught me, because she says you thank a man for his bread even if he’s spat in it. Then I came out.”


* * *


There was a skylight above the top landing of the Wren Stair that the party had found in Gleaning, when the landing had drifted half a yard and brought a trapdoor into reach that had not been reachable before. Nobody had reported it. You stood on the banister, pushed up the hatch and pulled yourself through onto the leads, and there you were on the roof of the Second Range with the whole of the Drift Wing laid out round you in the dark.

Ilse came up after Cass with two blankets and Teo’s last bottle of cider. It was bitterly cold. The leads had frosted, and the roofs of the Wing ran away to the west in a long uneven spine of slate and chimney and observatory dome, each one furred white along its ridge. Behind them stood the Old Court, solid and gold-windowed. Before them the roofs simply carried on, out over the heath, getting lower and more crooked as they went, until the Third Range ended in the glass dome of the Observatory of the Tern, and beyond that there was only the Margin, grey under the stars, and then the dark.

Down on the heath a line of wayhorns was moving. Ilse could see them because the frost on their backs caught the starlight: twenty or thirty great pale shapes walking single file along the edge of the Margin, slow as a funeral. Every one of them carried its head turned the same way, toward the Cape, so that the branched antlers made a long row of identical shapes against the heath, like the teeth of a comb. They were pointing at the Old Court. At the held ground. At her.

Cass sat with her back against a chimney stack and drank from the bottle and passed it over.

“Nan’ll laugh,” she said, after a while. “When I tell her. She’ll laugh till she has to sit down. She always said the Big House’d blame us for the weather one day. She just thought it’d be rain.”

“Will you tell her?”

“Course I’ll tell her. I tell her everything. That’s what I’m here for.” Cass turned the bottle in her hands. “*Go and learn what the paper-walls know, then come home and tell us.* That’s what she said. I’ve been sending crows all term. What the food’s like. How you lot measure a room four times and still lose the stair. Hallam’s lectures. She liked the bit about the Laws. She said six was an odd number for laws, and was I sure I’d counted right.”

Ilse laughed, and then wasn’t sure she should have.

They sat. The cider was sharp and warmed nothing. Out on the heath the wayhorns went on walking with their heads turned toward the Cape, and the frost thickened on the blankets.

“At Brine End,” Ilse said, “Brask sang to the hollowing.”

Cass turned her head.

“It was coming across the heath for the ground we’d just attested. I touched the edge of it and lost five minutes. And Brask stood in front of it and sang, low, in a voice I’d never heard her use. And it went away.” She hesitated. “She told me not to say a word of it to the Provost. You’re not the Provost.”

“How did it go?”

Ilse tried. She had no voice to speak of, and she had heard it only once, but she had a good memory for shapes, and she hummed what she could: a slow low line that turned over on itself at the end, like a plough at the headland coming back the other way.

Cass was quiet for a moment. Then she sang it back, properly. Her voice was rough and true, and it went down where Ilse’s couldn’t, and it had words, in a Margin cant Ilse only half followed: something about a road and a ford and the ground being let go of. The second time round she sang it a little differently, a turn added at the end, and the third time differently again.

“That’s a turning-song,” she said. “Nan sings it at them. Every kid on the heath knows it. You don’t fight a hollowing. You sing so the ground’s going somewhere, and they don’t like ground that’s going somewhere.” She frowned at the dark. “Where’d a paper-wall like Brask learn that?”

“She grew up at Thessaly Ford.”

“Village people don’t sing it. Village people put up a fence and pray.” Cass took the bottle back. “Somebody taught her. Somebody Tenting. Somebody who trusted her.”

“You sang it three different ways.”

“It’s a song. You don’t sing a song the same way twice. Ground’s not the same twice.”

“But which is the right one?”

Cass looked at her as though she had asked which of the wayhorns was the right wayhorn.

“All of ’em,” she said. “It’s not *written*, Marrow. It doesn’t sit still and wait to be copied. You learn it by walking it with somebody who knows it, and then you know it, and then it’s yours, and it changes a bit, because you do.” She pulled the blanket tighter round her shoulders. Her breath went up white. “Nan’s got a hundred road-songs in her head, and maybe a thousand turns of them. Every route on the heath from here to Thessaly Ford. Your Register hasn’t got one of them. Your Steyne thinks we’re loosening the ground because he can’t find our paper. Never occurs to him we’ve got none. Never occurs to him that’s the *point*.”

Below them the Wing creaked in the cold, a long complaint passed from roof to roof. Ilse thought of the Register, seven levels of it cut down into the rock, every parish in the Reach pressed flat and filed and held. She thought of Hen’s plait lying on the blanket where someone else had put it.

“You paper-walls,” said Cass, softly, not unkindly, watching the wayhorns go, “think if it isn’t written, it isn’t there.”


## Chapter 19: Corrow

The crow came into the loft at a quarter to seven on Frost 7 with frost on its wings and a length of string tied to its leg.

Ilse was there because Brask had sent her up with a box of chain-tallies for Mother Nettle to sort, and Cass was there because Cass was always there when she could be, sitting on the rafters with her legs dangling among the perches, talking to the birds in the Margin cant they seemed to prefer. The crow came in through the slot at the far end, beat twice, and landed on Cass’s knee as if it had been aiming for it all the way from the heath.

“Bugger me,” said Mother Nettle, from the floor. She was about a hundred and built like a bundle of kindling tied up in a shawl. “That’s not one of mine. That’s a heath bird. Look at the neck on it.” She squinted up. “Bugger the crows. They’re smarter than you lot, and they know where you sleep.”

Cass had gone very quiet. She untied the string from the crow’s leg with quick fingers and held it up to the grey light from the slot. It was a route-cord, like the ones in her braids: plain brown twine knotted along its length in a pattern of single knots and doubles and one strange looped hitch near the end, and a single red thread worked through it at the last knot.

“Who’s it from?” said Ilse.

“Aunt Bet.” Cass ran the cord through her fingers, knot by knot, the way Teo ran a column of figures, and her lips moved. “Corrow. Ground going. Wagons on chocks. Two days.” Her thumb stopped on the red thread. “Come.”


* * *


Brask was in the Fieldwork shed, sitting on an upturned bucket with her pipe cold in her teeth and a field-book open on her knee. She listened to Cass without looking up, and when Cass had finished she went on looking at the field-book for so long that Ilse began to think she hadn’t heard.

Then she shut it.

“Corrow is eight miles south-east,” she said. “Out past the salt-pools and over the low downs. A Margin settlement under active unravelling. It wants surveying by someone who can tell me what’s happening to it.” She took the pipe out of her mouth. “That’s a field exercise. Three students. Tenter, because she knows the ground. Marrow, because she can draw. Anselm, because he can count and because if I leave him here he’ll bake something for the Quiet ward and set fire to the Second Range.”

“Field-Surveyor,” said Cass. “Thank you.”

“Don’t thank me. Report on my desk by noon on the ninth.” Brask got up off the bucket. She went to the shelf at the back and came down with a wicker crow-basket, the small kind that held one bird. “Take one of Nettle’s. If you need the Register, send to me, not to anyone else. And Marrow.” The one eye found her. “You’re going to watch, measure and write it down. You’re not going to sign anything. Not a sheet, not a scrap, not your name in the dirt.”

“Yes, Field-Surveyor.”

Brask held her look a moment longer than was comfortable, the way she did when she was waiting to see whether a measurement would hold. Then she turned to Cass.

“Tell Ottery Tenter,” she said, “that Odile Brask asks after her.”

Cass stared. “You know my nan?”

“Everyone on the heath knows your nan.” Brask put the pipe back in her teeth. “Go on. You’re losing light.”


* * *


They walked it in a little under four hours, with Cass in front reading the grain and Teo at the back with the crow-basket held out from his body as if it might go off.

It was a hard white day with no wind. The salt-pools had skins of ice on them that sang faintly when a stone skittered across, and past them the low downs had the Margin’s habit of not quite agreeing with themselves. Twice Cass put her palm flat on the frozen turf and changed direction for no reason Ilse could see. Ilse paced and kept her own rough traverse from habit, and by the third mile her numbers and Cass’s route disagreed by nearly a furlong, and Cass was the one who was right.

They came over the last down at two in the afternoon and saw Corrow below them, and Teo said, “Oh,” in a small voice, and stopped.

It lay in a broad shallow bowl of turf and gorse with a stream at the bottom of it and a single standing stone on the rise at the far side, taller than a man and leaning a little, black against the white sky. Eleven wagons were drawn up in a loose ring on the bowl’s floor. They were the high-sided Tenting wagons Ilse had seen at the Ossel Fair, every one of them painted along its boards with route-diagrams in red and ochre and blue, roads that wound and forked and came back on themselves, little suns and moons at the forks, and Ilse’s first thought, before anything else, was that she wanted a closer look at the paintwork.

Her second thought was that the wagons were not where they had been.

Each wagon stood on chocks, wedges of timber jammed under its wheels to hold it. Behind each wagon, trailing away uphill toward the west, lay a line of other chocks. They had been set under the wheels and abandoned, and set again and abandoned again, so that every wagon had a little dotted track of wooden wedges strung out behind it across the turf like the footprints of a creature that took a week to cross a field. The longest of the tracks had nine chocks in it. Between the first and the last there was perhaps twenty yards.

The floor of the bowl was sliding downhill toward the stream, and taking the camp with it.

It went at about the pace of a clock’s minute hand, too slow to watch and too quick to ignore. A cooking fire on the near side had walked a yard out of its ring of hearthstones and left a black scar behind it, and its smoke went up crooked. A washing line strung between two wagons had gone so taut that its pegs had popped, and the shirts lay in the turf in a row. A dog lay asleep beside a wheel, and as Ilse watched the ground carried dog and wheel together a hand’s breadth toward the stream, and the dog did not wake.

Her level was making a sound she had not heard it make before. It wasn’t the dropped silence of the Little Wick lane or the shriek of the hollowing at Brine End. It was a low, steady, sliding hum, like a held note with the bow drawn very slowly across it.

“It’s all right,” said Cass. She was looking down at the camp with her jaw set. “It’s slow. Slow’s all right. Slow you can walk off.” She started down the slope. “Come on. Nan’ll want to feed you, and you’ll want to let her. That’s rules.”


* * *


Nan Ottery Tenter was the smallest grown woman Ilse had ever seen, and the loudest.

She came down the steps of the biggest wagon backward, the way old people come down ladders, wrapped in so many shawls that she looked like a heap of washing with a pipe stuck out of the top. Then she turned round, and Ilse saw a face like a walnut, brown and seamed and sharp, with two black eyes in it as bright as a crow’s. She looked at her granddaughter. Then she opened her mouth, and a voice came out of it like a field gate on a dry hinge, rusty and enormous and impossible to ignore.

“Cassia Tenter! Look at the *state* of you! Have they not got soap in the Big House? Have they not got *food*?”

“Hey-ho, Nan.”

“Don’t hey-ho me.” Nan seized Cass by both ears and pulled her face down to her own level, which was a long way, and kissed her hard on the forehead and then on each eye. Then she let go and looked past her at Ilse and Teo and the crow-basket. “And these are the paper-walls.”

“Ilse Marrow. Teo Anselm. My party.” Cass rubbed an ear. “Brask sent us. Field exercise. And Brask says to tell you Odile Brask asks after you.”

Something happened in the walnut face, too quick to read.

“Does she,” said Nan. “Does she now.” She took the pipe out of her mouth and pointed the stem at Ilse. “You. You’re the one that draws.”

“Yes.”

“And him?”

“He counts,” said Ilse, “and cooks.”

Nan looked Teo up and down, slowly, all of him.

“Thank the Road for that,” she said. “At least one of you has sense. Come and eat.”

They ate in the lee of the big wagon round a fire that had to be re-laid twice while they sat at it, because the ground kept carrying it off. Nobody seemed to mind. When the fire had drifted a yard, a boy of ten called Tobin, one of Cass’s cousins, would get up with a shovel and lift the embers back into the hearthstones without being told, as a Fallowmarch child would put a log back on a grate. The stew was salt mutton and barley and something green and bitter that Cass said was heath-kale, and there was a black flat bread baked on a stone, and the argument started before the first bowl was empty.

It was about bread. Teo, who was from Lisk and could not help himself, made a remark about leavening, and Nan took him apart for a quarter of an hour with the whole fire joining in. Lisk bread was air in a coat. Lisk bread was so proud of itself it rose to look at its own reflection. Teo defended his city, going redder and redder, and conceded at last that the black bread was *structurally superior for travel*, which made Aunt Bet laugh so hard she had to put her bowl down.

Ilse ate, and the ground slid gently under her, and she was warm for the first time since Frost began.

When the bowls were empty Nan filled her pipe, and Tobin lit it for her with a coal held in a pair of tongs, and she sat back against the wagon wheel and looked at Ilse through the smoke.

“So,” she said. “The Big House sends a girl who draws to look at our ground going. And what’ll you draw, girl?”

“Where it is,” said Ilse. “Where it’s moving to. How fast.”

“And then?”

“And then Brask reads it.”

“And then she puts it in a drawer.” Nan blew smoke. “Paper. You lot and your paper. You think if you draw a thing flat enough it’ll lie down and stay.” She tapped the pipe stem against her teeth. “We don’t keep paper on the heath. Paper says where a thing is. Song says where a thing *goes*.”

“I don’t understand.”

“No. Course you don’t.” Nan looked at Cass. “What’ve they been teaching her up there?”

“Six laws,” said Cass. “All about sitting still.”

“Ha.” Nan settled her shawls, closed her eyes, and without any warning at all began to sing.

It was not a pretty voice. It was the gate on its dry hinge, rusty and huge, and it should not have been able to carry a tune, but it did, the way an old cart carries a load. The song was slow and went in long lines, and Ilse caught perhaps one word in three of the cant, but she caught enough.

*The road to Corrow goes by the high stone when the moon is new,*
*and the low ford when it’s old.*
*Forty paces from the gorse that leans to sunrise.*
*When the rain’s been three days on the down, go by the sheepfold wall,*
*and count your forty from the wall and not the gorse.*
*When the stream’s loud, keep it on your left hand.*
*When the stream’s quiet, it has moved; go up and find it.*

There was more: verses for snow, and for the dry end of Haysel, and for when the high stone itself had walked, which it apparently did every few years. Ilse sat with her bowl going cold in her hands and listened, and somewhere about the fourth verse the hair began to stand up on her arms.

It had distances in it. Forty paces. Three days. It had bearings: sunrise, the left hand, the leaning gorse. It had conditions, *when* and *if* and *unless*, set out as carefully as the clauses of a charter. It was a survey. It was a survey of a route made by people who had looked at a road that would not stay still and, instead of trying to nail it down, had measured the ways it moved and the reasons for them, and written *those* down, in the only ink that wouldn’t dry.

It was a map. It was a map that moved, and it was *right*. She had just walked four hours across this ground and watched Cass turn for no reason and arrive at the right place, and the reason had been in the song all along.

Nan stopped. The fire had drifted again. Tobin got up with his shovel.

“There,” said Nan, opening her eyes. “That’s where Corrow goes. Now write it down flat and see if it stays.”

Ilse could not answer. Teo was looking at her, and she knew her face was doing something she could not stop.


* * *


It was full dark, and the fire had been carried off and brought back four more times, when Nan told them about the old man.

“Came two nights back,” she said. “Late, past moonrise. On foot from the road, with a lantern, very slow, picking his way. College man, by his coat. Old as me nearly, white beard, nice voice. Polite as a parson. He asked would someone show him the way to the Corrow stone, and Tobin took him up.” She jerked her chin toward the rise, where the standing stone was a black finger against the stars. “Gave the boy a silver mark for it.”

“He did,” said Tobin, from the dark. “A whole one.”

“Stood up there a long while by the stone, Tobin says. Talking to it, quiet, like a man settling a horse. Took his gloves off to touch it.” Nan drew on the pipe. “Grey hands, he had. Grey as that stone. Tobin saw ’em in the lantern. Then he put his gloves back on and thanked the boy, and thanked me on his way down, very kind, and went off to the road. And the next night the ground let go.”

Ilse was very cold. It started in her signing hand and went up the arm into her chest and stayed there.

*A gentle old man with grey hands.* She thought: half the old surveyors in the Reach have grey hands. Brask’s own left hand hung at her side like a dead bird. The College was full of white-bearded men in good coats who had signed too much land, and any one of them might come out to the Margin to look at a standing stone. She thought of the Provost’s passage, two days ago, and a kind voice saying that the Tenting had no more to do with the voids than the gulls did. She thought of gloves the colour of a dove’s breast, and of the words *old Sallow frostbite*, and she put that thought down, very carefully, in a drawer at the back of her mind, and shut the drawer.

She did not count back the days. She was a surveyor. She knew how not to take a measurement.

“Did he say why?” she asked, quite ordinarily.

“Said he was looking at the old benchmark. Seeing it was sound.” Nan’s black eyes were on her, bright as a crow’s. “That stone holds the paper for Corrow. Did Cass tell you we’ve got paper?”

Cass hadn’t. Nan sent Tobin into the big wagon, and he came back with a tin box that had once held Lisk tea, and Ilse felt her throat close, because it was the same kind of tin as the one behind the loose brick in Hobbet. Inside, folded in oilcloth, was a charter. It was a College grant, 1190 H., confirming to the Tenter wagon the winter ground of Corrow under the old Heath Treaty, *the bowl, the stream and the rise unto the standing stone*, with a College seal and a Master’s mark and, at the bottom, a smudged brown thumbprint.

“Mine,” said Nan. “Only paper I ever put my thumb to. My mother wouldn’t, and she was right, but I was young and the winters were hard, and the Big House said this way no lord’s surveyor could ever walk us off it.” She laughed her gate-hinge laugh. “Thirteen years it held.”

Ilse unfolded the charter on her knee and read it by the firelight, every line. It was in order. The measurements were sound, the seal was sound, the mark was sound. It was a perfectly good copy of a perfectly good charter, and the land it described was sliding gently downhill into the stream.

She had seen this at Brine End.

She tore a leaf from her field-book and wrote on it, small, by the fire: *Corrow, Tenter winter ground, College grant 1190 H. Parish copy in order. Please ask the Register for the master. I. M.* She rolled it, and Teo held the basket while she tied it to the leg of Mother Nettle’s crow, and Cass carried the bird to the edge of the firelight and threw it up into the dark. It went north-west without circling, straight as a chalk line, toward the fixed stone of the Cape.

The answer came back before the moon was up. The crow dropped out of the black onto the wagon step and stood there fluffing its neck while Ilse untied the paper. Brask’s hand, square and impatient:

*Not in the Register. Not misfiled. Missing. Keep them moving. B.*

Ilse read it twice and handed it to Cass. Cass read it and handed it to her grandmother, who could not read and did not pretend to. Nan held the paper to the fire for a moment, looking at the shape of the words, and then she gave it back.

“Rent’s coming due,” she said.

Cass turned on the step. Her face in the firelight was hard and young and frightened.

“You always say that, Nan. Since I was little. What does it *mean*?”

Nan put her pipe back in her mouth. She looked at her granddaughter for a long moment, and then past her, up the rise, to where the Corrow stone leaned against the stars.

“What it says,” she said.


* * *


They moved the camp that night, because the moon was old and the song said the low ford.

Ilse had imagined a scramble. It was nothing like that. The Tenting had been packed for two days. The horses were put to in a quarter of an hour, and the chocks were knocked out and thrown into the wagon-beds, and the whole camp simply rolled forward off its sliding ground, eleven wagons in file with a lantern hung at each tail, and Nan sitting up on the box of the first with the reins in her small brown fists.

She sang them out. Aunt Bet sang behind her, and Cass beside the second wagon with her hand on the near horse’s neck, and then the whole line had picked it up, the low long lines going back and forth along the column like a rope passed hand to hand. *The low ford when it’s old.* *Forty paces from the gorse.* *When the stream’s quiet it has moved.*

Teo led the third wagon’s horse, a patient grey mare, and counted under the singing, because he could not help it. Ilse walked beside him with a lantern.

The road moved.

She felt it within the first furlong. The track down the bowl and along the stream was a track one moment and a stretch of gorse the next, and the gorse had a path through it that had not been there, and the stream that had been on their left was suddenly ahead of them, in the wrong place, quiet. *When the stream’s quiet, it has moved; go up and find it.* Nan turned the first wagon uphill without a pause, and the line followed, and a hundred yards on there was the stream again, loud, where the song said it would be, on their left hand. Her level hummed and slid and changed its note, over and over, but it changed in a pattern, rising and falling with the land like a voice with a tune.

In the Little Wick lane, when the ground let go, she had thought the world was ending. She had learned everything she knew in a country where land that moved was land that had been wronged. She waited for the fear to come up through the soles of her boots.

It didn’t come.

She walked a road that would not stay where it was put, in the dark, among people singing it, and with every step it went somewhere, and every time it went somewhere the song had already been there. The lantern swung in her hand. The grey mare’s breath smoked. Teo counted, *twelve hundred and six, twelve hundred and seven*, and the hum in her ears rose and fell, and the stars over the Margin rocked very slightly at the edge of the world, and she was not afraid. She was something she didn’t have a word for yet. She was *listening*.

They came to the low ford a little before dawn. The ground there was firm, as firm as the Margin ever was, and when the wagons had been drawn into their ring and the chocks set and the horses rubbed down, the first grey light came up over the downs and showed the stream running quiet and shallow over its stones where it belonged.

Nan climbed down off the box, backward, and stood with her hands in the small of her back.

“There,” she said, to nobody in particular. “Now it can go where it likes.”

Ilse sat down on the wagon step with the lantern still lit in her hand. In her pocket, she knew without looking, her careful traverse of the night’s route made no sense at all. It ran into the stream twice and through a hill once and ended a furlong from where she was sitting.

She didn’t take it out. She sat in the cold dawn and listened to Cass and Aunt Bet arguing about where to put the fire, and to Teo telling Tobin, very seriously, the exact number of paces from Corrow to the ford, and under all of it, in the turf beneath her boots, the long slow hum of the ground, going on.


## Chapter 20: The Moot

The Moot-stone had been in the Old Court for three hundred and eleven years, and in all that time it had never once been where it wanted to be.

It sat in the south-east corner of the quadrangle, in a sunken square one rod on a side, edged with brass and filled two feet deep with pale grey sand carted up every autumn from the strand below Brine End. It was a block of black basalt two feet long and a foot each way, three hundredweight or near it, its corners worn soft by three centuries of first-years. At each corner of the bed a brass stud stamped with a benchmark arrow was set into the paving. Everything inside the brass edge was Margin ground in a Held Side courtyard, a little patch of give in the middle of all that certainty, and the stone sat on it like a dog on a hearthrug that is not its own.

By eight on the morning of Frost 10 the whole College was in the Old Court to watch it move.

They had put up benches in tiers on three sides of the bed, with braziers between, and the smoke hung flat in the frosty air at the height of the second tier, so that the leagues on the top rows looked down through a faint blue haze like gods on a painted ceiling. The links sat in front with their instruments in their laps; the Masters sat on a dais at the west side.

Magistra Ines Calder stood at the front of the dais with a sandglass in one hand and the Moot urn under her other arm. She waited until the noise had died. Then she waited a good while longer, until it had died again.

“First-years,” she said. “The Concordance Moot.”

“The rules are four, and I shall state them once. First. You will survey the stone and its bed from the four corner studs and from nowhere else. Second. You will have one glass to measure and one glass to draft.” She touched the sandglass. “A glass is a quarter of an hour. It does not care how clever you are. Third. You will chart the stone to any position within its latitude, and declare that position by setting your pin at the place your charter says its south-west corner will lie. Its latitude is about a yard in any direction, on its face. You may not chart it on end. You may not chart it out of the bed. You may not chart it into the Provost’s pocket, however much you may wish to.”

Laughter, in the tiers. The Provost smiled gracefully, as a man smiles at a joke he will remember.

“Fourth. At my word, you will attest together. The stone will go where the better map says. That is not my ruling. It is the ground’s. I merely announce it.” She paused. “Before each bout, the winner of the last will release their charter, so that the stone is free for the next. You will learn to let go of a thing before you learn to win one. That is the most useful lesson the Moot has to offer and very few of you will notice it.

“You will remember from my lectures that the record of greater fidelity prevails, and age prevails only between equals. You may remember the case. *Saffery v. the Parish of Wick*, 1193.” She let the name lie in the cold air for a moment. “A newer survey, better made, superseded a parish map four hundred years old. Fidelity, ladies and gentlemen, not age. Chart well.”

Ilse, on the front bench, did not move. Her instruments lay in her lap.

She knew the case. She had known it since she was thirteen, though not by that name. In the Fallowmarch they called it the year Fask came. Her father’s name was nowhere in the title; he had only been the man standing at the window. She had not expected to hear it said in that clear Tessary voice, in a courtyard five hundred and sixty miles from the Long Field, as an example of the law working properly. She found that she had closed her right hand round the brass tube of her levelglass hard enough to hurt.

Calder drew the names.

She drew them in pairs, with a pause between the first name and the second that the whole College leaned into. *Grice*, and *Anselm*. *Mercer*, and *Tenter*. A clerk chalked each pair on a board by the dais, and the list grew to twenty-eight, and Ilse’s name was not on it.

There were two names left in the urn. Ilse knew whose the other one was before Calder put her hand in, because she could feel him three benches along, sitting very straight with his notebook closed on his knee.

“Reyne,” said Calder.

She took a long time over the pause. The tiers took it with her.

“And Marrow.”

The noise that went up from the benches was the sound a crowd makes when it has been given what it wanted and is a little ashamed of wanting it. Ilse didn’t look at him. The clerk’s chalk squeaked the last pair onto the bottom of the board, where it would wait for her all day.


* * *


The bouts ran from half past eight until the light went, and Ilse watched every one.

Everyone else watched the duelists, because duelists were people and people were interesting. Ilse watched the stone.

The first thing she saw was that most of the winners measured the stone where it lay and charted it there, with their pin against its south-west corner. Calder called it *the standing claim* in a voice that suggested it had bored her every year since she was a girl. A record of what was had no lie in it to strain against, so nine bouts in ten it beat anyone who tried to move the stone at all. Absalom Grice tried to drag it a foot and a half north with a charter that was all ambition and no arithmetic, and Teo stood a standing claim against him and won without the stone so much as quivering. Teo came off the bed scarlet, sat down beside Ilse and put his head between his knees for a while.

The second thing she saw was smaller.

Before every bout the last winner drew the line through their mark that unsigned their charter, and the stone was free. And every time, before the next pair began to measure, the stone *settled*. Nobody else was looking. A sigh in the sand at its south-west corner, a few grains trickling into the gap that opened along its north-east side. It moved about the thickness of a fingernail, and it moved the same way every time.

At the eleventh bout, on her way to the brazier, she pressed a pin into the sand an inch off the stone’s south-west corner where no one would notice it. By the twentieth bout the gap had closed by the width of a straw.

The stone was going somewhere, a fingernail at a time, whenever nobody held it.

Cass’s bout was the twenty-first, against Piety Mercer.

Piety took the standing claim. She measured with the pursed concentration of a girl who has had a drawing-master since the age of six, and her charter was a pretty thing with a ruled border. Cass did not take the standing claim. She walked once round the brass edge of the bed with her palm held flat a few inches over the sand, as she did on the heath, and then knelt at the south-west corner and put her hand right down into it, and the tiers laughed. Then she measured, badly, and drafted, worse, and set her pin five inches south-west of the stone in a slightly crooked line.

“Ground’s leaning,” she said to Calder, when Calder raised an eyebrow at the pin. “Can’t you feel it?”

“I am not required to feel it, Miss Tenter. I am required to announce it.”

They attested. Ilse felt the two records go on the stone like two hands on a rope, and the stone *moved*. It shuddered in its bed, toward Cass’s pin; sand hissed at its corner; it hung there trembling with the tiers gone silent. Then it sank back where it had been, and Piety’s pretty charter held it.

“The record of greater fidelity prevails,” said Calder. “Miss Mercer.”

Cass came off the bed with her face like thunder and threw herself down on the bench between Ilse and Teo.

“Ground wanted to come to me,” she said. “You saw it. It *wanted* to. Her bloody paper held it back.”

“Your paper let it down,” said Ilse.

“Eh?”

“You were right about which way it wanted to go. Your charter couldn’t carry it.” Ilse was looking at the stone. “Your south face was a tenth of a link out. Your corner angles didn’t close. The ground believed you and the map didn’t.”

Cass glared at her. Then she stopped glaring, and looked at the stone, and back at Ilse.

“Road and rain,” she said. “You’ve got that face on.”

Ilse didn’t answer. She was working it through the way Brask had taught her, measurement first and then the ways she might be wrong.

Kasimir would take the standing claim. He was the best draftsman in the year by a distance she could not make up in a quarter of an hour, and his record of the stone would be so exact that the ground would have nothing to argue with. If she took the standing claim against him she would lose on precision. If she dragged the stone anywhere, she would be telling the ground a lie, and he would beat a lie with the truth.

But the stone did not lie where it lay because the ground had put it there. It lay there because Piety Mercer’s charter had put it there, and Orla Devane’s before that, and someone’s before that, all the way back through three hundred and eleven years of first-years setting it down wherever they had happened to find it. The ground had never been asked. Every time it was let go, for the space of a breath, it tried to tell them. A fingernail at a time.

A standing claim was a perfect record of where a piece of paper had put a stone. It was not a record of the ground. And the Law of Fidelity did not ask how true a charter was to the last sheet. It asked how true it was to the land. Somewhere inside the stone’s yard of latitude was the place the ground would choose for itself. Chart that, true to a hair, and there would be nothing in the bed to pull against it.

She thought of Brask’s promise. *No more lies on any College sheet.* She was breaking it every morning in the Antechamber. She asked herself whether a charter of the stone somewhere it had never been was another lie, and turned it over as she would have turned a client’s hedge, and could not make it come out a lie however she held it.

The light began to go a little after four. The clerks lit lanterns round the brass edge of the bed, and the braziers glowed red in the blue dusk. Calder said, “Reyne. Marrow,” and the tiers went quiet all at once.


* * *


Piety Mercer released her charter with an expression of martyred piety, and the stone settled its fingernail’s width, and Ilse was the only one who saw.

Calder turned the glass.

Kasimir went to work at once, and it was beautiful. Ilse watched him out of the edge of her eye, the way you watch a good horse go past. He had a folding rule of black boxwood, brass beam-compasses and a sightvane whose hair-wires caught the lantern light like spider-silk, and he went from stud to stud sighting the stone’s corners and writing the angles in that clear, slanting hand. He measured each face of the block twice. He took the height at all four corners to the hundredth of a link. In the time it took Ilse to kneel at the south-west stud he had sighted two corners from two stations and closed them.

Ilse did not touch the stone.

She laid her levelglass flat on the sand a foot north of it, and watched the bead of quicksilver. It swam, lazily, round and round, as it always did on Margin ground. She moved the glass a foot east. The bead swam. A foot south. It swam. She set it down by the south-west corner, just off the stone, and the bead slowed, and wandered, and came to rest, and trembled there.

Piety’s voice carried from the tiers: “She’s surveying the *sand*.”

Ilse pressed her bare knuckles into the sand along the stone’s south-west side. It was soft there, loose and dry. On the north-east side it was packed hard, a ridge of it, like the bank a boat pushes up against a quay. She lowered her cheek almost to the brass edge and looked along the bed in the lantern light, and saw faint ripple lines in the sand, like a beach when the tide has gone out, all curving round the stone toward its south-west corner. The sand had been creeping under the stone for three hundred years, and every year a first-year had told it to stop.

She set three fine pins in the sand along the stone’s south-west edge, each a hair’s breadth from the basalt, and sat back on her heels for a precious minute of the glass, doing nothing, while the tiers laughed.

The gap at the middle pin closed. By degrees the stone came to it, until pin and basalt touched.

That was the direction. The bead told her where it ended: three inches south-west of where the stone now lay, and turned by perhaps two degrees, sunwise, as the ripple lines turned. There the bead stopped swimming. There the sand would hold the stone without being made to. It was the lowest place in the bed, if *low* meant what the level meant by it: the place where the ground was most at ease.

Half the glass was gone. She got up and measured the stone.

She could not do it as Kasimir did. But she had nine years of measuring hedges by shuttered lantern, and she knew how to measure a thing quickly and know how wrong she was, and by how much. She took the stone’s faces with her folding rule and its corners from each of the four studs with her vane, and wrote each measurement down with its error beside it in small figures, as Halloway had drummed into them: *two feet and three-tenths of an inch, give or take a fiftieth*. The sand ran out in the glass as she wrote down the last angle.

“Draft,” said Calder, and turned it.

She drew the stone where it was going to be: three inches south-west and two degrees sunwise, every face to its measured length, every corner closed, the ripple lines drawn in fine dashes round it to show which way the bed ran. At the foot she wrote the bearing and the rate she had seen, small: *settling south-west by about one-fiftieth of an inch in a quarter-hour when unheld*. She didn’t know whether a charter was supposed to say a thing like that. She wrote it anyway, because it was true, and because a voice at the back of her mind, Nan’s gate-hinge and Halloway’s stammer at once, said it belonged there.

When the glass ran out she walked to the edge of the bed and set her pin. Red ribbon, in the sand, three inches south-west of the stone’s corner.

Kasimir set his on the far side. Blue ribbon, touching the basalt at its very corner.

Somebody at the back called out, “Is that all, Walker?” and Cass told him, loudly and in some detail, where he could put his chain.

Kasimir looked at the red pin.

He looked at it for a long time, and then at Ilse, and she saw him searching it for the trick: the measurement shaved, the angle nudged. He could not find it, because it was not there, and she saw for the first time the faint crease that came between his brows when a sum would not come out. Then he squared his charter on his board.

Calder raised her hand and held it there a long while.

“Attest,” she said.

Ilse set her wren’s foot on the sheet.

It went down true, on its own rule, as it had not gone down on any College paper in a week. And the moment the spur was made she felt the stone take hold of both of them.

It was like having a rope run through her right hand with someone strong hauling on the other end. Kasimir’s record went onto the basalt like iron, laying itself along every face. *Here. Exactly here, to the hundredth of a link. As it is.* Her own record went on beside it, and it didn’t feel like anything she had ever signed.
