In Agnes Tull’s orchard she had leaned her whole attention into the map and argued the hedge west, persuading, insisting, until the ground gave. There was nothing to argue here. The ground was already on her side of the rope. It had been on her side for three hundred years. All she had done was write down what it had been saying.

Her level rose to a high, hard note. The stone groaned.

It was a deep grinding sound, more felt than heard, like a millstone turning over once in its sleep. Sand hissed up in a little bow-wave along its south-west face and spilled over. The tiers made no noise at all. The black block shifted in its bed, slowly, almost thoughtfully, and slid. One inch. Two. It turned as it went, its long face swinging a degree, two degrees, sunwise, and the sand curled round its corner in the lantern light like water round a moored boat’s bow. Three inches. Its south-west corner came to rest against the red pin with a small, final click of basalt on steel.

The high note in Ilse’s ears let go. Her whole right arm ached to the shoulder.

On the far side of the bed, Kasimir’s charter had gone dead in his hands. She could see it from where she stood: the sheet hung in his fingers like any sheet of paper, holding nothing, and he was looking down at it as if it had died while he held it.

Then the tiers went up like a flock of rooks.

Calder lowered her hand into the noise and waited for it, as she had waited for everything, and in the end it came down to her.

“The record of greater fidelity prevails,” she said. “Miss Marrow.” She turned her head toward Kasimir. Her voice was not unkind. “Fidelity to the ground, Mr. Reyne. Not to the page.”

Cass was on her feet on the bench, whistling through her teeth. Teo was laughing into his hands. On the dais the Masters applauded: Halloway with his three watches jingling, Moy with great slow claps like a man splitting logs, the Provost with the gracious economy of someone applauding a result he has not yet decided how to use.

Master Hallam had stood up.

He applauded with his gloved hands, softly, so the grey kid made almost no sound, and he did not stop when the others stopped. He was looking at her over his half-moon spectacles. It was a long look, kind and attentive and interested, the look of a man who has seen a pupil do what he had not known she could do, and wants to understand it. He went on looking for long enough that Ilse, who was smiling back at him, began to feel her smile set on her face like plaster. Then he nodded to her, very warmly, and sat down.

Ilse turned away from the dais and found Brask.

She stood apart at the very edge of the crowd, under the arch toward the Lantern Gate, where the lantern light didn’t quite reach: a tall rawboned shape leaning on the Long Lie. Their eyes met across the roaring court.

Brask took the pipe out of her mouth.

“Hm,” she said, and put it back, and turned, and went away under the arch.


* * *


The tiers emptied into the dark. Ilse stayed by the bed, because the stone was hers now, for a year. She knelt by the brass edge in the last of the light and laid her hand flat on the cold basalt. It sat in its new place as though it had always been there. The sand lay round it smooth and quiet, and when she put the levelglass down beside it the bead did not swim.

She heard him before she saw him. He walked the way he always walked, as if the paving owed him an explanation.

Kasimir stopped on the other side of the bed with his dead charter rolled tight in one hand. In the light of the one lantern the clerks had left he was white to the lips, and very composed.

“How,” he said.

It was not quite a question. She answered it anyway.

“You charted where it was. I charted where it was going.”

“You charted the stone in a position it did not occupy.”

“It does now.”

“It does now because you charted it.” His voice was perfectly level and each word came out complete, like coins being counted onto a table. “You put it somewhere it had not been, and you made the ground agree with you. I have seen you do it before, Miss Marrow. I spent three days on my knees by a hawthorn hedge in Wick Fallow watching the evidence of you doing it.”

“That’s not what this was.”

“Then tell me what it was.”

“The sand moves.” She heard her voice rising and brought it down. “It’s moved all day. It’s moved for three hundred years. Every time somebody let go of the stone it went that way a fingernail, and the next first-year nailed it back. I watched it. I measured the creep. I wrote the rate on the sheet. Every line on that charter is true, Mr. Reyne. Check it. Check it to the hundredth of a link with your beautiful compasses. You won’t find a line I moved.”

“I know.” He said it very quietly. “I have already looked.”

The lantern hissed. In the dark beyond the arch the Lantern Gate’s light burned on its keystone, small and steady.

“That is what I cannot forgive it,” said Kasimir. “It holds. It is better than mine, and I cannot find the line, and everything about it is the thing I came to the Fallowmarch to stop.” He looked down at the stone in its new place, and then at her, and his grey-blue eyes in the lantern light were steady and cold and, under the cold, something she had not expected, which looked a great deal like fear.

“That was not a survey,” he said. “That was a forgery that happened to be true.”


## Chapter 21: The Accusation

The summons came on Frost 12, at the end of Morning Survey, in Lisbet Arnaud’s hand.

Lisbet brought it to the Antechamber herself. She waited until Ilse had set her mark on the Wren Stair sheet, crooked, and then held out a folded paper sealed with the plain black wafer of the Board of Errata, and did not quite look at Ilse while she did it. Ilse had never seen the senior proctor look at anything other than straight on.

*Ilse Marrow, Member, first year. You are required to attend the Board of Errata in the Chapter Room at ten o’clock this morning, to hear a complaint laid against you. I. Calder, for the Board.*

Behind her, Teo said, “Marrow,” and nothing else.

She folded it along its creases and went to breakfast, because there was nothing in the summons that said she couldn’t. She knew everyone in the Mess was watching her eat her porridge. She ate all of it.


* * *


The Chapter Room was above the Library of Record, up a stair so narrow that two people could not pass on it. It was long and low and panelled in dark wood, and its windows looked down on the Moot-stone sitting where Ilse had put it two days ago. Behind a long table, three of five chairs were filled. Magistra Calder sat in the centre. On her right was Provost Steyne, with his fingertips together. On her left was Master Halloway, who kept touching his three watches one after another as if to check that time was still going. A clerk sat at a small desk with a ledger open.

Kasimir Reyne stood at the side of the room, by the window, with his hands behind his back.

On the table in front of Calder lay a charter.

Ilse knew it from the door. She would have known it across a field. It was her own sheet of good Lisk rag, foxed at one corner by the vestry damp, with the Tull orchard strip laid out in her own small exact hand: forty-one stations, the west boundary drawn in its new place two rods west of the old, the four apple trees marked with their little circles and their names. *Pearmain. Russet. Codlin. Old Tom.* At the foot, the wren’s foot, three lines and a spur. Beside the charter lay a stiff Court envelope with a red seal broken, and a lens-glass on a cloth, and a brass bowl of water gone cloudy with alum.

“Miss Marrow,” said Calder. “Stand there, if you please.”

She stood there.

Calder read the complaint in her Tessary courtroom voice, without any of her lecture pauses. *That the Member, before her admission, did make and attest a contrary charter of the orchard strip of Agnes Tull in the parish of Wick Fallow in the Fallowmarch, dated Reaping 14, 1203 H.; that the visible mark upon that charter is the witness-mark registered by the Member at Benchmarking in the Old Wall of this College; and that beneath it, traced in blind ink, lies the registered mark of Abelard Quince, surveyor, deceased in Seedtime 1200 H., by which the burden of the charter was transferred without consent to the mark of a dead man. Complaint laid by Kasimir Reyne, Member. The charter obtained from the parish chest of Wick Fallow by order of the Concordance Court, and examined by the Court’s assessor in Tessary, whose report is attached.*

She set the paper down.

“Mr. Reyne. Do you stand by the complaint?”

“I do, Magistra.”

“State the grounds.”

He stated them in complete sentences, without looking at her. He had examined the hedge at Wick Fallow in Reaping and found that it had moved under a recent charter. He had seen Miss Marrow cut her mark at Benchmarking and recognised it. The parish clerk at Wick had replied that the charter was in order, and so in Fogmonth he had written to the Court asking that the charter itself be sent for examination. It had arrived on Frost 11. He had compared the visible mark against the Old Wall that afternoon. It was the same mark, stroke for stroke.

“And the second mark?”

“Is shown in the assessor’s report, Magistra. I have also seen it myself.”

Calder nodded to the clerk. The clerk rose, dipped a corner of cloth in the brass bowl, filmed the glass with it, breathed on it twice and laid it over the foot of the charter.

Ilse didn’t need to look. She looked anyway. There, under the wren’s foot, faint and violet and perfectly clear, lay the hook through the ring. Quince’s mark, in her own blind ink, made with Quince’s fine steel pen by shuttered lantern in Agnes Tull’s orchard. It was very good. It was in perfect registration. Even now some small cold part of her looked at it with pride.

Some older, quicker part of her was already going through its pockets for a way out. A second hand. A stolen mark. It found nothing, because there was nothing. She had made this thing so well that it would hang her.

“Miss Marrow,” said Calder. “You are not required to answer this complaint today. The Board sits quarterly. Its next sitting is in Thaw, after the equinox, and your tribunal will be heard then. You may answer now, or you may reserve your answer until the tribunal. Which do you choose?”

Ilse looked at the charter. She looked at Kasimir, who was looking at the window.

“I’ll answer at the tribunal,” she said.

“Very well. Silence is recorded too.” The clerk’s pen scratched. “Until the tribunal you remain a Member, with all a Member’s duties. You will attend your classes and your Morning Survey. You will not leave the College grounds without leave of a Master.” Calder turned a page in front of her. “Master Hallam has written to ask that your copying in the Register continue, since there is as yet no finding against you, and since, I quote, he has no other copyist who can draw a straight line. The Board sees no objection.”

“The College,” said the Provost pleasantly, “prejudges nothing. At the same time, Miss Marrow, the eyes of the Court are upon us, and we must be seen to be thorough.”

“Yes, Provost.”

“One further question,” said Calder. “It is a question the Board is obliged to ask. Mr. Reyne. In the course of your inquiry, did you have any reason to believe that any Master or officer of this College knew of the matter before today?”

Kasimir was silent a moment.

“In Fogmonth,” he said, “I asked Field-Surveyor Brask whether she had any concern about the accuracy of Miss Marrow’s surveys. Miss Marrow is her Fieldwork assistant. The Field-Surveyor declined to answer.”

Ilse went cold to the ends of her fingers.

“Then the Board will hear the Field-Surveyor,” said Calder, and closed her ledger. “Tomorrow, at ten. Miss Marrow, you will attend. That is all.”

On the narrow stair Halloway caught her up, out of breath, his watches jingling. “M-Miss Marrow. The—the error is not the sin. It’s the *unrecorded* error. I only—I wanted to say.” He went off down the stair before she could answer.

When she got back to the Wren Stair there was a folded note pushed under the door, on good cream paper, in a careful old-fashioned hand.

*Courage, my dear. The Board is fairer than it looks, and so, I think, are you.—A. H.*

She sat on the edge of Hen’s stripped bed and held it for a long time.


* * *


Brask came before the Board in her field coat, with the Long Lie in her good hand, and did not sit when Calder offered her the chair.

“Field-Surveyor,” said Calder. “You have heard the complaint against Miss Marrow.”

“I’ve read it.”

“Mr. Reyne states that in Fogmonth he asked you whether you had concerns about her surveys, and that you declined to answer him. Is that so?”

“It is.”

“Why did you decline?”

Brask looked down the table at the three Masters, and then sideways at Ilse, who stood with her hands clasped behind her back so that nobody would see them shake.

“Because the answer was yes,” said Brask, “and it was none of his business.”

The clerk’s pen stopped.

“You knew,” said Calder, “that Miss Marrow was a forger.”

“I knew it on the day of the Walk.” Brask said it flatly, the way she gave a bearing. “Her survey had a stream bank on it that closed by fourteen links where nothing on that heath closes. She’d made it close. Surveyors put down what they find. Forgers put down what ought to be there. I’ve been looking at Walkers’ sheets for nineteen years, Magistra. I know the difference with my eye shut.” She paused. “The one I’ve got left.”

“And you said nothing to the Board.”

“I said nothing to anyone. I passed her. I made her my assistant so I could watch her. In Fogmonth I told her I knew, and I made her a bargain: that she put no more lies on any College sheet, and I’d keep her secret.” Brask leaned a little on the Long Lie. “I judged she’d be more use to the College inside it than in a Court cell. I still judge so. You can write that down too.”

The clerk wrote it down.

The Provost had not moved, but something in the way he held his fingertips had changed. “Field-Surveyor,” he said, very gently, “you understand what you are telling us. That a Master of this College knowingly concealed a felony from the Board, and placed a known forger in a position of trust, in a season when the Court is watching our every step.”

“I’m not a Master,” said Brask. “I’m Field-Surveyor Emerita. This College took the Mastership off me nineteen years ago. It’ll be in your files.”

There was a silence round the table that Ilse could have cut with her knife.

They sent the two of them out into the corridor for a quarter of an hour. Brask stood at the window with her back to Ilse, and Ilse stood against the panelling and could not think of a single word that would be any use. Then they went back in, and the Provost gave the sentence himself, because a suspension was the Provost’s to give.

Odile Brask was suspended from teaching, and from all duties of the College, pending the tribunal of Miss Marrow in Thaw. She was barred from the College grounds from sundown that day. Her Fieldwork classes would be taken by a fourth-year proctor until other arrangements could be made.

Brask heard it without moving.

“I’ve been barred from better places,” she said, and turned, and went out, and the Long Lie knocked once on each step of the narrow stair all the way down.


* * *


Ilse found her in the Fieldwork shed an hour later, packing.

Brask had never kept much in the shed that was her own. Into a crate on the bench she was putting field-books in order of date: nineteen years of them, cloth-bound and swollen with damp. The chains hung from their pegs round the walls, cleaned and tallowed. The bucket in the north corner rang under its drip every eleven seconds, and Ilse thought, stupidly, that the fourth-year proctor would not know to empty it.

She didn’t say anything. She took the next field-book from the shelf, checked its date and handed it over. Brask took it without looking and put it in the crate.

They did that for a while.

“This is my fault,” Ilse said at last.

“Your fault’s your own business, Marrow. Mine’s mine.” Brask put in another book. “I chose. I knew what I was choosing on the day of the Walk, and I chose it again in Fogmonth, and I’d choose it today if they gave me the morning back. Don’t you take that off me. It’s about the only thing I’ve done in nineteen years I didn’t have to ask permission for.”

“They’ve barred you from the grounds.”

“I live in Verity Cross. It’s two miles.” She squared the books in the crate. “I’ll sit in my cottage and drink bad tea, and nobody will ask me to be grateful for broth. Somebody empty that bucket, or the proctor will drown.”

Ilse emptied the bucket out of the door into the frozen gorse. When she came back Brask was running the oldest of the chains through her good hand, link by link, its brass fifty-link tally worn smooth as a coin.

Ilse’s mouth was open. Everything was in it, crowding up behind her teeth: the violet lines, the Antechamber, Hen’s hands, the skewed marks she had been setting on every sheet since Frost 3 in breach of the one promise she had ever made this woman. She drew breath.

“Whatever it is,” said Brask, without turning round, “don’t.”

Ilse stopped.

“I’m off these grounds at sundown, and I can see on your face that you’ve something heavy to give me.” Brask hung the chain back on its peg. “I’m not carrying anything out of that gate I can’t put down again. Not today. You hold it. You’re young, and you’ve got two good hands.”

The bucket began to drip into itself again. Eleven seconds. Ilse closed her mouth.

“That stone,” said Brask, after a while. “At the Moot. You wrote the creep on the sheet.”

“Yes.”

“Rate and bearing.”

“Yes. Was that wrong?”

“Did it hold?”

“Yes.”

“Then don’t ask me stupid questions.” Brask lifted the crate off the bench and settled it on her hip, the stilled grey hand hooked under it like a bracket. At the door she stopped and looked back round the shed, at the chains on their pegs and the drip and the bench, the way a person looks round a room for a thing they have forgotten and know they will not find.

“You’ll do the Long Measure without me,” she said. “Good. You’d have leaned.”

She went out. Ilse stood among the hanging chains and listened to the Long Lie going away along the frozen path, its brass shoe ringing on the stones, until she couldn’t hear it any more.


* * *


It snowed on Frost 14.

It began in the dark before First Bell and did not stop. By the time Ilse came out of the Antechamber with the Wren Stair signed, crooked, for the day, the Old Court was white from wall to wall and the snow was still coming down, large and soft and slow, out of a sky the colour of pewter. It fell without any wind at all. It settled on the benchmark arrows cut into the corners of the buildings and filled them in. It lay along the brass studs of the meridian line, so that for a while each one showed as a small dark eye in the white, looking up, and then one by one the eyes closed. In the south-east corner the Moot-stone wore a thick white cap and sat in its bed under the snow, still where she had put it.

Kasimir was crossing the court from the Library of Record, with his collar up and a satchel under his arm, and his footprints went behind him straight as a chain-line.

She hadn’t meant to stop him. She found she had walked out into his path, and that he had seen her and had not changed his course by so much as a link.

He stopped a yard off. Snow had caught in his hair.

“Miss Marrow.”

“You wrote to your mother.”

“I wrote to the Concordance Court,” said Kasimir, “which is the proper authority for the examination of a charter held in a parish chest. My mother is its Chancellor. I did not write to her, and she did not reply.” His breath went up white. “I am aware that you will not believe that.”

“You went through the parish chest at Wick. You had them send my hedge to Tessary in an envelope so some Court assessor could put a lens on it.”

“Yes.”

“Do you know what you’ve done to Brask?”

“I know what the Board has done to Field-Surveyor Brask.” Something moved in his face and was put away. “I did not lie to them. I was asked a question and I answered it. If you wished me to lie for her you should have asked me before Calder did. I would have refused, but you would at least have asked.”

“That’s the thing about you.” Her voice was shaking, and she let it. “That’s the thing I can’t get past. You think the law is a—a fence round the weak. You said so, in the Library. Law protects the weak from the powerful. You believe it. You’d swear on the Atlas to it.”

“I would.”

“Do you know *Saffery v. the Parish of Wick*?”

He went very still.

“Calder taught it,” said Ilse. “At the Moot she held it up for all of us like a bloody saint’s relic. A newer survey, better made, supersedes a parish map four hundred years old. Fidelity, not age. Do you know what it was about?”

“A boundary in the Fallowmarch. Lord Saffery’s surveyor re-charted the parish of Wick Fallow in 1192. The parish appealed on grounds of precedence and lost.” He said it as he would have said it in an examination, and then she saw him hear what he had said. “Wick Fallow.”

“Corwin Fask,” said Ilse. “He came through in the autumn of 1192 with Lord Saffery’s money and a fine leather case and two chainmen, and he walked the whole parish in nine days, and he was very good. He was much better than the old parish map. That was the whole point. He found a place on the boundary of my father’s field where the latitude would bear it, and he charted the line sixty yards in. Sixty yards of frontage. The best soil we had. And the well.” She heard her own voice going on, flat and steady now, as if she were reading out a field-book. “I was twelve. It happened at night. The fence-line came out of the ground with a noise like a tree going down, and the well-head ground sideways on its stones, and my father stood at the window with a lamp in his hand saying *no* over and over, like that would hold it. In the morning our well was on Saffery land. Legally. Attested. Filed, stamped, and better made.”

Kasimir said nothing. The snow came down between them.

“He appealed. The vestry lent the appeal its name and not a penny more, so he spent every mark we had on it. Your Court heard it in Tessary and found for Saffery, and it went into the books as a model of the law working, and Calder teaches it to first-years. Then he went to work as a hired hand on Saffery’s land, which used to be his field. He drew water from his own well for another man’s cattle. He got smaller every year. He died of a fever in 1195 and there wasn’t much of him left for it to take.” Her right hand was aching from the knuckles to the wrist, the deep grinding ache she thought of as the bill. “The money I spent on the coach to come here was the money I’d saved to buy it back. Forty-four marks. Sixteen short.”

“Miss Marrow —”

“You think I don’t know what a forged boundary costs?” She was very close to him now. She didn’t remember moving. “I know exactly what it costs. I’ve watched it cost. Fask’s was *legal*.”

He had gone white again, as white as he had been at the Moot, and she saw that it had gone into him. She saw it go in and land somewhere and stay. For a moment, standing in the snow with his satchel under his arm, he looked as though someone had pulled a chain out of his hands.

And then he held. She watched him do it. She had watched him do it with the dead charter in his fingers at the Moot. Whatever had gone into him, he set his feet on it.

“Then the law was wrong,” he said. His voice was low and not quite steady. “In that case, the law was wrong, and the Court that upheld it was wrong, and I am—I am sorry for it. More than I can say standing here. But you have told me what was done to your father by a man with a better pen than his, and your answer is to be the woman with the better pen.” He took a breath. “You have moved I do not know how many boundaries in the dark, on nobody’s authority but your own. You were right about the widow’s orchard. I do not doubt it. Were you right every time? Who checked you? What happens when the next one with your gift decides that *she* knows where a lord’s hedge ought to be, and the lord pays better than the widow? You put a dead man under your mark so that you would not carry what you did. You do not get to choose who carries. Nobody does. That is what law is *for*.” His hand had come up, without his meaning it to, and he put it down again. “Then the law must be fixed. Not ignored. If everyone with a grievance and a pen —”

“Most people with a grievance,” said Ilse, “don’t have a pen, Mr. Reyne.”

It stopped him. She saw it stop him, the way a chain stops when it comes taut against a stake.

They stood a yard apart in the falling snow. The breakfast bell went, muffled, as if through cloth.

“My father had a pen,” Ilse said. “Fask had a better one. That’s all the law ever was in the Fallowmarch. Whoever could afford the better pen.”

Kasimir looked at her. Snow had settled on his shoulders and he had not brushed it off. She thought he was going to say something else. She thought, for one moment, standing there with her hand aching and her face wet with snow, that he was going to say he would withdraw it.

“I am sorry about your father,” he said at last, very quietly. “I mean that. I will remember it.” He shifted the satchel under his arm. “The complaint stands.”

He stepped round her, carefully, as if she might break or go off, and walked on toward the Ranges.

Ilse didn’t turn to watch him. She stood where she was in the middle of the Old Court and looked down at the line of his footprints coming toward her across the white, straight as a chain-line from the Library door, and stopping where she stood, and going on. The snow was already filling them. In a quarter of an hour they would be gone, and the court would be smooth again from wall to wall, as if nobody had ever crossed it, and nothing in it would be anywhere but where it had been put.


## Chapter 22: Errata Night

On Frost 22 the crow found her on the Wren Stair at four in the afternoon, when the light was already going. It came in through the casement Cass had left open, walked twice round the table as if it owned the lease, and held out its leg with the air of a creditor.

The note was a strip of brown wrapping paper rolled small. Brask’s hand was square and unforgiving, and she had not wasted a word on greeting.

*Confess something useful. Not the obvious thing. They’ve heard the obvious thing.—B.*

Ilse read it twice. The crow got half a biscuit, which it took to the windowsill and dismantled with contempt.

“Well?” said Cass, from the floor, retying the cords in her braids by feel. “What does the old wolf say?”

“To confess something useful.”

“Bold, from a woman who’s not allowed past the Cross.”

Brask had been barred from the College grounds for nine days. Every morning since, Ilse had walked past the padlocked Fieldwork shed and felt the absence like a step missing on a stair she knew in the dark. She put the note in her coat, in the inside pocket where she kept the things she did not want Sorry to eat.

Sorry was asleep on Hen’s bed. Someone, probably Teo, had made it up again over the bare ticking, tight and square the way Hen had always left it, and nobody had slept in it since Frost 2.

“Are you going to?” Teo was at the little mirror by the door, trying to make his choir collar sit flat. It was a band of dark felt with a brass pin, and on him it looked like a bootlace on a haystack. “Confess something. You don’t have to. Most people just drink and shout ‘Recorded’ at their friends, and Master Moy says the choir is excused because we’re singing, though I don’t think that’s actually in the statutes.”

“I don’t know,” said Ilse.

“That means yes,” said Cass.

“It means I don’t know.”

“From you,” said Cass, “that means yes.”


* * *


The Mess on Errata Night was not the Mess. Ilse had eaten in it every day for three months: the long tables scarred with four hundred years of compass points, the star-chart ceiling gone brown with smoke so that the constellations looked like stains on an old map. Tonight someone had taken all that away and left candles in its place.

They stood in their hundreds down every table, fat tallow and thin wax side by side, in saucers and inkpots and the necks of bottles. They crowded the window ledges three deep. They had been fixed with their own drippings along the beams, so that for the first time the ceiling was lit from below, and the painted stars hung there clear and gold. The flames leaned together whenever the doors opened and righted themselves when they closed, the way a field of barley does. Through the tall western windows she could see the Drift Wing going out over the dark heath, its windows lit. In the Old Court the snow fell straight down. Out there, past the Lantern Gate, it came down in slow loops and spirals, each flake on its own private road.

The air smelled of hot cider and cloves and wet wool, and under it, faintly, of Archivist Damask’s vinegar.

There was no seating plan tonight. Masters sat among the links; Ilse saw Mother Nettle wedged between two pale Tessary boys, telling them a story that had made both of them go red to the hairline. Teo went to the choir benches below the dais, where the Sounders’ Choir stood in their felt collars like a row of large, solemn birds. Cass towed Ilse to the end of a table near the windows, with a jug in reach.

“Hen should be here,” said Cass, pouring. She said it to the jug.

“Yes.”

“She’d have confessed something stupid first thing, to get it going. That she counts stairs in her sleep.” Cass pushed a cup across. “She’d have *liked* this.”

Ilse wrapped her hands around the cup. The cider tasted of orange peel and pepper. Her right hand ached in the cold, the old ache of the bill. For three years she had sent it down a dead man’s thread. The Brine End charter and the Moot-stone she carried honestly, herself.

At the high table Hallam sat with his grey-gloved hands folded around a cup he wasn’t drinking from, listening to Master Halloway with his head on one side and an expression of tender patience. Provost Steyne sat at the centre in silver and black, Calder beside him, very upright. At the far end of the dais was the space where, Teo had told her, Brask had stood every Errata Night for nineteen years, refusing a chair, her unlit pipe in her teeth. Nobody had put anything in the space.

Kasimir was three tables over with his party. He had been not looking at her, with great precision, for eight days.


* * *


At the first stroke of the Bell of Hours the doors were shut, and the candles all leaned and settled, and Lisbet Arnaud climbed onto the dais carrying a slate.

It was an ordinary schoolroom slate in a frame of old black wood, with a sponge tied to its corner by a string. Lisbet propped it on an easel, chalked *Frost 22, 1203* at the top, and turned to the hall.

“The Slate is open,” she said. “Anything said tonight goes on the Slate and nowhere else. The Slate is washed at midnight. By the custom of the College, what is washed is not repeated, not entered, not minuted and not held against anyone in any court or any Board, and anyone who tries can take it up with me.” She folded her arms. “Masters included.”

There was a cheer. Ilse had read about the Slate and not believed in it; a forger learns early that no paper forgets. But Lisbet Arnaud did not look like someone who expected to be inconvenienced.

“By custom,” said Lisbet, “the Provost begins.”

Steyne rose beautifully, as if rising were a small kindness he was doing the room. “My dear colleagues. I confess—” He smiled, and let the pause do its work. “I confess that the new chairs in the Refectory were a mistake.”

The hall roared. The chairs had arrived in Gleaning from some Tessary benefactor, so slippery with varnish that you slid off them if you laughed, and everybody had been sliding off them for two months.

“Recorded!” said three hundred people, and Lisbet chalked a stroke on the Slate.

“That’s a paper-wall confession,” said Cass, under the noise. “Hold on. Here’s a real one.”

Halloway was on his feet, holding all three of his watches the way some men hold a hat, pink to the ears.

“I. Ah.” He looked at the watches, as if they might help. “I c-c-confess. In the spring of eleven eighty-nine I surveyed a sheep-fold at Callow Edge, in the Ammonite Hills, for my third-year practical. Dry-stone, irregular heptagon, perimeter four chains and sixty-one links. I closed my traverse with a misclosure of one link in two thousand and attested it, and it was passed.” His stammer had gone entirely. His voice had dropped half an octave and steadied, the way it always did in Mensuration when he reached the numbers. “On the fourth leg I carried a seven instead of a one. My closure was a coincidence. Two errors cancelling. The north wall of that fold has stood eight and a quarter inches out of true for fourteen years, and the sheep do not know, and the farmer does not know, and the Register does not know.” He swallowed. “Every summer I take the coach to Callow Edge and stand by that wall and look at it. I have never told anyone. I would like it to be recorded.”

The hall was quiet for a moment. Then Damask, from somewhere among the second-years, said in his thin delighted whisper, carrying perfectly, “Oh, *Pim*,” and the hall answered, “*Recorded*,” not loud this time but low and warm, three hundred voices at once, like a hand on a shoulder.

Halloway sat down very fast. Calder, beside him, patted his arm without looking at him.

“He takes the coach,” said Cass, wondering.

“Of course he takes the coach,” said Ilse. Her eyes were stinging, and she blamed the smoke. *Error is not sin. Unrecorded error is.* He had said it to first-years every week for fourteen years, with his sheep-fold standing out of true behind him the whole time.


* * *


After that they came quickly, as a dam goes once the first stone shifts.

A league confessed that he had passed Concordance by learning Calder’s face. “Recorded,” said the hall, and Calder said “Also noted,” in a voice like a dropped portcullis. A pale first-year from Lisk confessed that he had been homesick every single day since the Walk and was homesick now, and half the links in the hall shouted “Recorded!” with the fervour of the guilty, and someone passed him a jug.

Orla Devane stood on her bench, which was not done, and held up one hand for quiet, which she got, because Orla could get quiet from a hen-house.

“I confess,” she said, “that I have been reading Piety Mercer’s letters.”

There was a short shriek from Kasimir’s table. Piety Mercer had come half out of her seat, scarlet.

“Since Gleaning,” said Orla. “All of them. Her mother’s, her aunt’s, and the young man from the Lisk canal board, who writes every week.” She put her free hand on her heart. “I confess it as an error. Because they are the dullest letters I have ever read, and I kept reading them, every week, in hope. That is the error. Hope. Recorded?”

“*Recorded!*” Piety said a word that would not have been permitted on any other night, and Lisbet, chalking, said, “Also recorded, Miss Mercer, thank you.” Ilse saw Kasimir put a hand over his eyes. She thought his shoulders were shaking. She looked away before she could be sure.

Mother Nettle confessed, in language that made the Provost study the ceiling, that she fed his message-crow better than he fed his clerks, and would continue. Master Moy, without rising, confessed in a bass voice that came up through the benches that he had excused his choir from confession every year on a statute he had made up. “*Recorded*,” said the choir, with feeling, and Teo, in the middle of the second row, grinned at his feet.

Then the choir sang the old *Meridian Hymn*. The true sounders, out in the Drift, sang lower than hearing; Ilse had felt one through the soles of her boots on the day of the Walk. In a crowded hall on a winter night the choir did something like it: twenty men and women standing quite still, mouths barely open, and a note so low it was less a sound than a change in the room. The cider in her cup shivered into rings. The candle flames trembled in their hundreds, all at once. She felt it in her breastbone and behind her ears where the level lived, and for the length of the hymn the whole building, Held Side and Drift Wing both, seemed to be holding the same note. Someone had propped the far doors open, and she imagined the sound going down the corridor to the infirmary, and Hen lying there with her grey hand on the blanket. She did not know if Hen could feel it. She was gripping the cup hard enough to hurt.

When it ended, nobody said anything for a moment, which on Errata Night was a kind of applause.


* * *


Cass went next. From her face, she had not expected it either. She stood up, the cords in her braids swinging, and Ilse watched three hundred people remember all at once that there was a Tenting girl in the Big House, and arrange their faces.

“Hey-ho,” said Cass.

A few links, Walkers mostly, called it back. “Hey-ho.”

“My nan sent me here to learn what the paper-walls know and bring it home,” said Cass. “That’s what I told everyone, anyhow. Learn it, carry it back, don’t stay. Don’t sign what you can’t carry.” She stopped. Her hands had gone into fists at her sides, and she looked down at them as though they had been put there by someone else. “So I confess. I love the Big House. I love the stupid stairs and the stupid Survey and the bloody bell and you lot, most of you, and I don’t know what I’m going to tell my nan. May the Road forgive me.”

The hall came apart. They roared it, “*RECORDED*,” and stamped so that the candles jumped in their saucers. The Walkers were on their feet. Cass stood there with her chin up and her eyes too bright, then sat down hard beside Ilse and said, very fast, “Don’t.”

“I didn’t say anything.”

“You were going to look at me.”

“I wasn’t.”

“You’re doing it now.”

Ilse filled Cass’s cup. Neither of them said anything else. Under the table, for a moment, Cass’s knee knocked against hers and stayed there.

Then Teo stood up from the choir benches.

He did it the way he did everything that frightened him, all at once, as if launched. In the candlelight, with his collar crooked, he looked like an apologetic oak. He cleared his throat twice.

“I confess,” said Teo, “that I’m afraid of heights.”

There was an instant of perfect silence, and then the whole hall, with one voice and enormous affection, said, “*We know.*”

Teo went crimson. “Yes. All right. That’s fair. The thing with the Observatory ladder was a reasonable response to a ladder. Thank you.” He did not sit down. Ilse saw him look, quickly, toward the open doors and the corridor to the infirmary. “And I confess,” he said, in his small brave voice, the short-sentence voice, “that there’s a girl from the Salt Steps whose laugh is the best sound in this building. Better than the choir. Sorry, Master Moy. I never told her. That was an error. I’m going to correct it when she wakes up.”

Nobody shouted that time. The hall said “Recorded” the way people in Hobbet said the Ninefold’s Grace at a graveside, whether they believed in it or not. Teo sat down. Two of the basses put their arms round his shoulders and he let them.

Cass had her face in her cup. Ilse took Brask’s note out of her pocket, unfolded it, and read it again by the light of the nearest twenty candles. *Not the obvious thing. They’ve heard the obvious thing.*

She thought of the accusation in the Board’s files, in Kasimir’s beautiful handwriting, and the Tull charter with a dead man’s ghost under her wren’s foot. Everyone in this room had heard. Piety Mercer had seen to that within a day. Whatever Ilse said tonight would change nothing. The Slate would be washed at midnight. The tribunal would sit after the equinox. The charter would still have her mark on it in the morning.

That, she thought, was the point. It was the only confession she would ever make that couldn’t buy her anything.

She stood up.

She felt it go along the tables like a crack along ice: heads turning, a cup set down too hard. Then three hundred people being quiet on purpose, which is louder than silence.

On the dais, Steyne’s face had acquired a careful nothing. Hallam had lowered his cup and was watching her over his spectacles, so gently and so worriedly that she nearly sat down again. Three tables away, Kasimir Reyne had finally looked at her. His notebook lay closed on the table, under his hand.

“I’ve lied on paper,” said Ilse. Her voice came out level, which surprised her. “For years.” She considered the hall, the candles, the painted stars. She thought of Agnes Tull’s Pearmain coming home across the dark. She thought of nine years of hedges. “Well.”

Three hundred people worked out what she had said. Someone at the back laughed, a single startled bark, and then a few more, and it went through the room, not mocking but rueful: the laugh you give when someone has said the thing nobody else would.

And then the College answered her.

“*Recorded.*”

It came from every table: the links and the leagues, the choir benches, Mother Nettle, Orla Devane, the homesick boy from Lisk, Halloway with his three watches. It came from the high table, from Calder crisply and from Steyne after a moment, smoothly. It came from Hallam, warmly, his gloved hand lifted a little from the cloth as if in blessing. It was not louder than it had been for the chairs or for Orla’s letters. It was exactly the same, and that was what undid her. The ritual took what she had said and put it with everything else, the sheep-fold and the chairs and the homesick boy, as the Old Court took the snow. It did not make an exception of her. It did not make an example of her. It recorded her.

She sat down. Her knees were not entirely reliable. Lisbet chalked a stroke on the Slate, just one, the same size as all the others.

It was two heartbeats after the hall had finished, when the noise was turning back into talk and the jugs were moving again, that she heard it. It came from three tables over, from a young man sitting very straight with his hand flat on a closed notebook, and it was said not to the hall but, as far as she could tell, to the candle in front of him.

“Recorded,” said Kasimir Reyne.

He did not look at her. Orla, beside him, turned and studied him, then looked across at Ilse and lifted her cup very slightly before she drank.


* * *


At midnight Lisbet Arnaud untied the sponge from the corner of the Slate, dipped it in a basin of water that one of the links held up for her, and washed the Slate clean.

They all stood for it. The sponge went across in long strokes, and the chalk dissolved under it: the date, and the tally beneath, a hundred and nine strokes in rows of five. Grey water ran down the black face into the basin. Ilse watched her own stroke go with the others and could not tell which one it had been. Then the Slate was black and shining, and Lisbet held it up, and the College gave the toast that ended every Errata Night.

“To what is.”

“*And what isn’t, yet.*”


* * *


It was snowing harder when they crossed under the Lantern Gate. The lantern hissed in the flakes, and past it the snow went back to its looping fall, so that walking under the arch was like walking from one weather into another. The Second Range was full of singing, much of it bad.

Teo was quiet all the way up the Wren Stair. Cass went ahead two steps at a time, humming the *Meridian Hymn* in a register no choir would have owned. Inside, Sorry had moved from Hen’s bed to the warm hearth. It opened one amber eye at them and shut it again.

Cass lit the lamp. Teo sat on the end of his bed, took off his choir collar and folded it, and sat with it in his lap.

“Marrow,” he said.

Ilse was hanging her father’s coat on its nail. He said it the way he said it at the bottom of a column that would not add. She turned around.

Teo held out his right hand, palm up, into the lamplight.

For a moment she didn’t see it. His hands were what they always were, enormous and gentle and burned in three places from the stove. Then he tipped the hand toward the lamp, and she saw.

The tips of his first two fingers had gone grey. It was very faint. It started at the edge of each nail and faded to nothing before the first knuckle, like a cloud’s shadow on a hillside. She crossed the room, took his hand and pressed her thumb on the pad of his forefinger. It was cooler than the rest of him, cooler than it ought to have been after a hot hall and a long climb. Under her thumb the flesh was faintly, wrongly firm, like a wax seal that has not quite set.

“When?” she said.

“Four days ago. At Survey. I thought it was the cold.” He was looking at her face, not his hand. “It hasn’t got worse since. I’ve been signing off-register like you showed us, every single morning, I swear I have, a sixteenth high and a bit sunwise, I’ve never once—”

“I know you have.”

“Then why—”

“The old sheets.” She heard her own voice, flat and exact, the voice she used for closing a traverse. It was the only voice she could trust just then. “Everything you signed before Frost 3. Since Reaping. Every morning, every sheet. The trick stops new lines taking. It doesn’t cut the old ones. They’re still filed. They’re still pulling.” She did the sum because it was there to be done. Sixty-odd mornings, four sheets a morning on the busy days. “You’re carrying sixty days of it, and it’s not going anywhere.”

“Oh,” said Teo. He looked down at his hand in hers. “Right. That makes sense, actually. That’s quite elegant, in an awful sort of way, like a pie that’s gone off in a very regular pattern.”

Cass had come to stand behind Ilse. She didn’t say anything. She reached past Ilse’s shoulder and put two fingers on the grey, as Ilse had, and took them away again, and swore once, very quietly, by Road and rain.

“It’s slowed,” said Ilse. “You said yourself. Hen went faster. Hen signed extra, every day, for weeks.” She stopped. “You’re slower. That’s time.”

“How much time?”

She thought of Hen’s face against the white pillow, and Wil Ashdown in his chair by the window, staring at nothing, and she didn’t know, and she would not lie to him about it. Not with the Slate just washed.

“I don’t know,” she said. “So we’re going to be quick.”

Teo nodded. He looked at his fingers for a while in the lamplight. Then he folded them gently into his palm, as if he were putting something away for later, and looked up at her with his round, tired, decent face.

“I meant it, you know,” he said. “About her laugh. I’d like to be there when she does it. Properly there. Not in a chair by a window, looking at nothing.”

“You’ll be there,” said Cass.

“Properly,” said Ilse.

She was still holding his hand. She didn’t let go. Outside the window the snow came down in its slow spirals over the Drift Wing, every flake on its own road, and on the hearth Sorry sighed in its sleep and stretched one grey paw toward the warmth. Down in the Old Court, very faintly, someone was still singing.


## Chapter 23: The Infirmary Ledger

Hen was still in the end bed by the west window, which Dr. Orle said was for the light and which Ilse suspected was so that the nurses could see her from the door without having to walk the length of the ward.

It was the third day of Deepwinter and the light was thin as skimmed milk. It came in across the blanket and lay on Hen’s right hand, which rested on top of the covers where someone had put it. The grey had reached the second knuckle of every finger now. It had a sheen, like pewter that has been handled, and the nails had gone the colour of old candle-ends.

“Teo came,” said Hen. “He brought a pie.”

“Did you eat it?”

“Yes.”

“Was it good?”

Hen considered this. Her eyes were the same blue they had always been, and they were open, and they rested on Ilse’s face with perfect attention, the way a level rests on a table. That was the worst of it. Hen was not vague. She was not asleep. She was simply there, all of her, with nothing moving.

“It was a pie,” said Hen.

Ilse had brought a chain. She had told herself it was for Hen to count, because Hen had always counted things, stairs and links and the seconds between lightning and thunder, and it had seemed a thing Hen’s hands might remember even if Hen didn’t. She laid it on the blanket, twenty links of it, brass tallies and all. Hen looked at it.

“Twenty,” she said. She did not touch it.

“Yes.”

“That’s a fifth.”

“Yes.” Ilse waited. Once, Hen would have said, *A fifth of a chain, a fifth of a cake, a fifth of the way to dinner*, and laughed at herself, the big ridiculous laugh that came out of her like a gull out of a cliff. “Hen. Do you want me to read to you?”

“If you like.”

She read from a book of Salt Steps tide-tables that Cass had found in the library, on the grounds that it was the only thing Hen had ever said she missed. She read the high waters for Saltcombe and Tollhaven for the whole month of Seedtime, in a level voice, and Hen listened to every number, and nothing in her face changed at all. At the end Ilse closed the book and sat with it on her knees, and found that she had been holding her own right hand in her left, under the cover, pressing the thumb into the old chain-scar on her palm until it hurt.

“Marrow.” Dr. Orle stood at the end of the bed in her shirtsleeves, drying her hands on a towel. “When you’ve a minute.”


* * *


Orle’s office was a cupboard with a window, and the window looked onto the coal-yard. There was a desk, two chairs, a skeleton hung on a stand by its skull with a scarf round its neck against the draught, and more paper than Ilse had seen outside the Register. Orle shut the door, went to the desk, unlocked the bottom drawer with a key from a string round her neck, and took out a ledger bound in green cloth.

“Sit,” she said. “Don’t touch Mr. Bones. He comes apart.”

Ilse sat.

“After you came to me in Frost,” said Orle, “I went back further. I’ve the infirmary day-books for forty years in the cellar, and the mice have had some, but not much. I wanted to know when this started.” She opened the ledger on the desk between them and turned it round. “Officially it started this autumn, and it’s seasonal, and it’s fatigue. You’ve seen this. You haven’t seen the front of it.”

It was a doctor’s ledger: columns ruled in red, a neat, fast, slanting hand. Name. Year. Party. Date of first presentation. Signs. Hand.

*Ashdown, W. Link (1st yr). Seedtime 9, 1201. Listless, flat affect, sleeps poorly. Grey to first joint, R. index and second.*

Ilse looked at it.

“He was a first-year,” she said.

“He was a link in his first spring. He’d passed every paper going.” Orle tapped the entry. “They’ve kept him on the rolls as a chain because nobody has had the stomach to strike him off. That’s the College for you. Kind in the cheapest possible way.”

Under Wil Ashdown there were two more for 1201, in Haysel and Frost. There were four for 1202. Then 1203 began to fill the page: Gleaning, Gleaning, Fogmonth, Fogmonth, and *Solberg, H. Link. Frost 2, 1203. Grey past first joint R. hand within the week. Flat. Cooperative. Says she cannot remember why things were funny.*

“And before 1201?” said Ilse.

“Nothing. Not one.” Orle sat back. Her chair creaked. “Oh, there’s Drift-fatigue in the old books, plenty of it. Field parties back from the deep heath, dizzy, sleeping fourteen hours, seeing their grandmothers on the stairs. That’s real. It passes in a week. It doesn’t go grey, and it certainly doesn’t go grey on the signing hand first, every single time.” She put one blunt finger on the column headed *Hand*. Every entry read R, except Benet Lowe of the Third Range, who wrote with his left, and whose entry read L. “Fatigue doesn’t choose a hand, Marrow. I said so to you in Frost and I’ll say it to anyone. Burden does.”

“Have you said so to anyone?”

Orle’s mouth went thin. She reached into the drawer again and took out a single folded sheet of heavy cream paper and slid it across.

*The Provost thanks Dr. Orle for her diligence in this distressing season, and asks that further correspondence on the matter of the seasonal fatigue be routed through his office, so that the College may speak with one voice.*

It was signed with a flourish and sealed with the Provost’s seal, a little meridian line crossed by a quill. It was the politest threat Ilse had ever read, and she had read the letters Lord Saffery’s steward sent to tenants.

“One voice,” said Orle. “His. He came in person in Frost to admire my windows, and when that didn’t take, he wrote.” She took the sheet back and locked it away, and looked at Ilse for a moment with her sharp, tired, decent eyes. “You know something about this. I don’t know what and I’m not going to ask, because I’ve a feeling you’d have to tell me a thing you oughtn’t. You remember what I said about good coats.”

“Before anyone in a good coat.”

“Before anyone.”

“Seedtime 1201,” said Ilse. “You’re sure of the date?”

“I wrote it myself. I was there.”

She walked back through the ward past Hen’s bed. Hen was looking at the window. The chain lay on the blanket where Ilse had left it, twenty links, untouched, and Ilse picked it up and coiled it and put it in her pocket and did not say goodbye, because Hen would only have said *goodbye* back, perfectly, in the right place.


* * *


She had held those bundles to the lamp herself, in Fogmonth, on the night of the damp-lens. The last week of Candlemonth 1200 was on the old Dunmore paper, a wheat-sheaf with a ribbon round its middle, and clean under every mark. The first week of Thaw 1201 was on the new, with the little three-arched bridge of the Bridge Mill at Lisk in the corner, and a violet knot under every signature. Clean until the year turned. She had known since then when the lines began. She had not known, until Orle turned the ledger round, that the Quiet had begun with them.

Thaw 1, 1201: the first morning of the new paper, two hundred students signing it before breakfast. Thirty-nine mornings later Wil Ashdown had walked into the infirmary with two grey fingers. It was not a coincidence of dates. It was a cause and its effect, with a doctor’s neat hand between them.

What she did not know was why the paper had changed.

The next morning, after Survey, she went down to Level One before the copying hour and found the Register stores ledger for 1200 on its shelf behind the counter, a fat calf-bound book of requisitions in Coyle’s hand. She turned to Candlemonth. Lamp oil, sealing wax, tape, gall. Then:

*Candlemonth 12. Survey folios, linen-rag, forty reams, of the Bridge Mill, Lisk, in place of Dunmore & Sons, Tessary. Approved: A. Hallam, Mag. Reg.*

The same approval stood under every other entry on the page, down to the gall and the tape. The Master of the Register signed for the Register’s string.

“Miss Marrow?”

Merrin Coyle stood at the end of the counter with a box of new folios in his arms and an expression of polite alarm. He was a narrow, soft-spoken man with ink-stained cuffs, and he always looked faintly as though he had been caught at something, even when he was the one catching.

“Good morning, Mr. Coyle.” She did not hurry to close the book. Hurrying was the first thing that got you caught. “I was checking a date. Master Hallam wanted the stores entries for the copy-index.”

That was a lie, and a good one, because Hallam had asked her to index the Register’s day-books back to 1195 and simply hadn’t said which first.

“Ah. Yes. Of course.” Coyle set the box on a shelf. His eyes went to the open page under her lamp. “The new stock.”

“You said he was careful with the College’s money.”

“Did I? Yes. Well, he is.” He wiped his hands on his coat, which made the ink on his cuffs worse. “But it wasn’t his idea, really. That was the winter of the Assembly’s audit. The Provost’s office cut everyone’s allowance, every department, by a fifth. Master Hallam was very put out about it. He said cheap paper was a false economy and the Provost would have his economies whether they were sensible or not.” He gave a small, unhappy laugh. “He signed for the new stock under protest. I remember. It was the only time I’ve heard him raise his voice.”

“So it was the Provost’s order.”

“Well. The Provost’s budget. Master Hallam’s signature. These things are—” He made a vague shape in the air. “Why do you ask?”

“The new paper takes ink differently,” said Ilse. “I’ve noticed it when I’m copying. I wondered why.”

“Does it?” said Coyle. Something went across his face then, too quick to read, and he picked up the box of folios again as though he needed it to hold. “I wouldn’t know. I only file them. You’d have to ask the Provost’s office, Miss Marrow, I’m sure I don’t know anything about paper.” He went off into the stacks with the box. At the corner he dropped a pen, and stooped for it, and did not look back.

She put the ledger back as she had found it, its spine flush with its neighbours. Then she went up to breakfast and found she couldn’t eat it.


* * *


The summons came the following afternoon. It was on a card, thick cream board edged in gilt, and the ink was a deep glossy black that smelled faintly of cloves.

*The Provost would be glad of a word with Miss Marrow at five o’clock today, in his study.*

“Glad,” said Cass, reading it over her shoulder. “I bet he’d be glad.”

“He found out in a day,” said Ilse. “I asked Coyle about the paper yesterday morning.”

“Coyle’s a mouse.”

“Mice run somewhere.”

The Provost’s study was on the first floor of his lodging at the west end of the Old Court, where the Held Side was oldest and the level in Ilse’s ears was so steady it was almost a sound. A clerk let her in under the meridian-arrow knocker, past the long-case clock and the dead Provost with his dividers, and up a stair carpeted in red, past more Provosts in oils, each one more serene than the last. The study itself was very warm. There were carpets on the carpets, Lisk work in blues and golds, and her boots, which she had cleaned, felt like an insult to them.

In the middle of the room, on a table of its own, was the Meridian.

It was a model of the whole College, perhaps nine feet from end to end, carved in pale wood and painted. The Old Court was there in honey-coloured lime, every window cut, the Bell of Hours a thimble of real brass in its tower. The Lantern Gate had a chip of amber on its keystone where the lantern should be. Beyond it the Drift Wing ran out across a painted heath along its causeway: the three Ranges, the Observatory of the Tern with a dome of blown glass no bigger than a hen’s egg, the long bent arm of the Second Range where the Wren Stair was. Every piece of the Wing was mounted on brass pins set into slots in the baseboard, and Ilse saw, bending, that the slots were long enough to let each section slide.

“A clerk adjusts it at the end of every month,” said Steyne, behind her. “From the Morning Survey filings. So that the model is always true. I find it soothing.” He had come in by another door, silent on the carpets. “Do sit down, Miss Marrow. Will you take a glass of something? No? Tea, then.”

He poured it himself, from a silver pot, into cups so thin she could see the shadow of the tea through them. He was elegant in the way a well-made instrument is elegant: nothing extra, everything polished. His hair was silver and his hands were pink and perfectly smooth. She noticed his hands because she always noticed hands now. There was not a trace of grey on them, not at the nails, not anywhere. Hands that had not signed much of anything in a long time.

“I’ll be frank with you,” he said, sitting opposite her, “because I think you would prefer it, and because I think we understand one another better than either of us might like. You are in a difficult position.”

“Yes, Provost.”

“Mr. Reyne’s accusation is a serious one, and I am told it is well founded. The Board sits after the equinox. You know what the Board can do. Expulsion, at the least. And once you are not a Member, the Tull matter goes back to the Concordance Court, where, I need hardly tell you, the Chancellor is Mr. Reyne’s mother.” He sipped his tea. “She is not a sentimental woman.”

“No, Provost.”

“And yet.” He set the cup in its saucer without a sound. “The offence predates your membership. There is a respectable argument that the Board has no standing to hear it at all, and that the Court’s own referral was irregularly made. There is a respectable argument that the matter might be deferred, and deferred again, until it is of no interest to anyone. These things,” said the Provost gently, “can be managed.”

Ilse said nothing. She held the thin cup and looked at the model of the Meridian, at the brass pins and the long slots that let the Wing slide.

“Field-Surveyor Brask’s situation,” Steyne went on, “could likewise be revisited. She has served the College for forty years. It would be a great pity for it to end over a misplaced loyalty to a student.”

That one went in under the ribs, and she knew he had meant it to. She kept her face still.

“What would the College want,” she said, “in return?”

He smiled, and it was a real smile, a little rueful, the smile of a man who disliked crudeness and had been forced into it. “So little. Dr. Orle is a fine physician and a very anxious correspondent. She has been upset this winter, and I am told you have been visiting her often. I would ask you not to upset her further. And I would ask you to keep to your copying in the Register and leave the paper supply to the bursary.” He spread his smooth pink hands. “That is all.”

“The paper supply.”

“I understand you have been asking about it. You have a good eye. It does you credit. But you are a first-year with an accusation of forgery over her head, and you are going about the Register asking questions about the College’s accounts, and people will draw conclusions, Miss Marrow, and none of them will be kind to you.” He leaned forward a little. “Do you know what the Court would give for a scandal in the Register? Lady Reyne has wanted the Register under Court authority for fifteen years. Every rumour, every complaint, every anxious letter from an infirmary is a stone in her hand. This College has stood for twelve hundred years because it has kept its own house in order and kept its own counsel. Its survival depends on calm.” He sat back. “The College’s survival depends on calm, Miss Marrow. I am asking you to help me keep it.”

There were students in his infirmary with grey hands. There was a girl from the Salt Steps who could not remember why things were funny. Ilse looked at the Provost and thought, quite coldly, that he knew about them, every one, and had written a letter to Orle about speaking with one voice.

She had lied to magistrates and parish clerks and a Concordance assessor. She knew how to do this.

“I’ll think about it, Provost,” she said, and let her voice catch a little on the last word, as a frightened first-year’s would. “Thank you. For Field-Surveyor Brask. I hadn’t thought there was anything anyone could do.”

“Of course you hadn’t.” He rose, and so did she. He walked her to the door himself, his hand not quite on her shoulder. “Think about it. Don’t take too long.” At the door he paused. “And Miss Marrow? Your Errata Night confession was very affecting. I hope you will not feel obliged to make it again anywhere less forgiving.”

What was washed was not repeated. He had repeated it, pleasantly, on his own doorstep, so that she would know he could.


* * *


“The Provost,” said Cass. “Road and rain.”

She was sitting cross-legged on the table in the Wren Stair with her skinning knife in one hand and a whetstone in the other, and she had been sharpening the same edge for some minutes, in long, unhurried strokes. Sorry sat by her knee and watched the blade go back and forth as though it were a mouse.

“He knew I’d asked about the paper within a day,” said Ilse. “He told Orle to stop writing. He offered to make the accusation go away, and Brask’s suspension, if I stopped. He knows exactly what’s in that infirmary, and he’s keeping it quiet.”

“And the paper changed the year his office cut the money,” said Cass. “Thaw first. First one grey inside six weeks.” She tested the edge on her thumbnail. “Seems tidy.”

“It’s tidy.”

Teo was on his bed with his back against the wall, his knees up, his big hands loose between them. He had pulled the cuff of his right sleeve down over his fingers, which he had taken to doing in company, and which Ilse had pretended not to see. Now he pushed it back and looked at his fingertips by the lamp. The grey was no worse than it had been on Errata Night. Ilse had checked it every morning, and so, she knew, had he.

“I don’t know,” said Teo.

“What don’t you know?” said Cass. “He threatened her. In his nice warm room with his nice silver teapot.”

“Yes. He did. He’s horrible.” Teo frowned at his hand. “It’s only—I’m sorry. It’s that I’ve met men like him. In Lisk. The canal board’s full of them. They’re all ‘we’ and ‘calm’ and ‘the good of the city,’ and if a lock-gate cracks they’ll paint it before they’ll mend it, because mending it means admitting it cracked. They’ll hide anything.” He looked up. “But they don’t *build* things. They haven’t got the patience. Whoever did this cut a plate that puts a ghost-line under every mark on every sheet, every morning, and kept it going for three years. Thousands of them. In perfect registration. Ilse said so herself—perfect. Not one slip. That’s someone who’s very patient and very good with a pen.” He shook his head. “The Provost’s a coward, not a—whatever this is.”

Nobody said anything for a moment. The whetstone had stopped.

“Cowards hire people,” said Cass at last. “Paper-walls do. Pay someone to carry it. Bearing for hire, isn’t it?”

“Maybe,” said Teo. “Maybe that’s it.” He didn’t sound sure.

Ilse turned it over in her mind like a sheet held up to the lamp, looking for the second line under the first. Teo was right that the work was patient. It was also right that Steyne had threatened her the day after she asked about the paper, that his office had cut the money the winter the paper changed, and that he had written to Orle, and that he had offered to buy her silence with the only two things she wanted. A man who had nothing to hide didn’t offer to buy anything. It was the first rule of the trade. Nobody bribes the honest witness.

“It doesn’t have to be his hand,” she said. “It only has to be his order. He wants it quiet. Somebody’s doing it and he wants it quiet. That’s enough to start.”

“Start how?” said Cass.

“Damask’s Palimpsest class comes to raking light this month. If there’s more on those sheets than I can see with a damp lens, he’s the one who can show me how to find it.” She looked at Teo’s hand. “And we keep signing skew. All of us. Every morning.”

“Every morning,” said Teo, and pulled his cuff back down.

Sorry had lost interest in the knife. It had found the Provost’s summons card on the table, where Ilse had dropped it, and was investigating the edge of it with its nose. Before anyone could stop it, it took the corner in its mouth, sat up on its haunches like a squirrel, and began to eat the card from the gilt edge inward, with small decided bites, the glossy black ink first.

“Look at that,” said Cass, with deep satisfaction. “Even the cat’s against him.”

Ilse let it eat. It got as far as *glad of a word* before it stopped, sneezed at the cloves, and went to sleep on the remainder with its ears folded flat, and she sat watching it and thinking about patience, and about pens, and about who could afford to hire both.


## Chapter 24: Raking Light

Archivist Damask began the lesson by putting out every light in the room.

The Palimpsest workroom was under the Library of Record, down a stair so worn in the middle that each step was a shallow bowl. It smelled of vinegar and old glue, and its shutters were lined with felt. When the last lamp was out the dark was total, and twelve first-years sat in it breathing, and somebody’s stomach made a long thoughtful noise, and Orla Devane said “Absalom,” and Absalom said “It wasn’t,” and then a match scraped and there was Damask.

He was very small in the light of one candle, with his brass-armed lenses pushed up on his forehead like a second pair of eyebrows. He laid a single sheet of yellowed paper on the bare oak table.

“What do you see?” he whispered.

“A deed,” said Piety Mercer, who sat nearest. “A conveyance of a mill-leat in the parish of Thorne, dated 841.”

“Yes! Yes. Very good. That is what it *says*.” Damask beamed at her. Then he bent, slowly, the candle in his fist, and brought the flame down until it was nearly touching the table, at the far edge of the sheet, so that its light did not fall on the paper but skimmed across it, flat and low, like the last of the sun across a ploughed field in winter.

The sheet changed.

Ilse had cut paper and soaked it and scraped it all her life. She had not known it had a landscape. Under the raking light every fibre stood up and threw a shadow. The surface of the deed was not flat at all. It was a country of tiny ridges and furrows, and the words of the conveyance lay on it like hedgerows on a hillside. Under those words, and across them, and between them, lay others: shallow trenches where an older pen had pressed, and broad smooth hollows where someone, long ago, had scraped the first writing away with a knife and pumice and written the mill-leat over the top. A whole second document lay there under the first, the way an old field-system lies under a new one, visible only at dusk, when the low sun shows every bank.

“Oh,” said Damask, with enormous tenderness. “Oh! Look. *Look.*”

They crowded round. Even Piety leaned in.

“It was a boundary,” said Damask. “Before it was a mill. A field boundary, between two brothers. One of them died, and the other one scraped him off the deed and sold the water.” He moved the candle a hair, and a name came up out of the shadows, faint as breath: *Edwy*. “There you are, Edwy. There you are, my dear. Nine hundred years under a mill-leat, and here you are.” He looked round at them all, his little lenses catching the flame. “This is what I want you to understand. Nothing written is ever wholly gone. A pen is a plough. It bruises the tooth of the paper, and the bruise remains when the ink has faded or been scraped or never shown at all. Light from above shows you what a sheet wants you to see. Light from the side shows you what it did.”

He gave them each a candle and a reused sheet and set them to it. Kasimir Reyne found three layers on his in ten minutes and began, inevitably, to list them in his notebook. Ilse found a scraped-out sum on hers, a tithe-collector who had got his total wrong and tried again twice. She looked at it and did not see the sum at all.

At the end, while the others were filing out, she lingered by the teaching stack, putting her sheet back.

“Archivist. If a line had been drawn in an ink that doesn’t show—not scraped off, but never visible in the first place—would raking light find it?”

Damask turned to her with an expression of pure delight. “A dry stylus, you mean? Or one of the vanishing inks? Oh, yes, yes. If anything, better. Scraping smooths the tooth. A pen that has never been seen has never been scraped.” He tipped his head like a bird. “Have you something you would like me to look at, Miss Marrow?”

“No,” said Ilse. “I was only wondering.”

“Wonder,” said Damask, “is the whole of my discipline,” and patted her arm, and shuffled off to unshutter the windows.


* * *


She took the sheet that evening from the Frost bundle in the Antechamber stacks, while Coyle was at supper.

It was the Wren Stair and Long Gallery folio for the first day of Frost. She chose it with care. It came before the off-register signing began, it had been superseded by forty-odd mornings since, and it carried four marks, one in each printed box along its foot. It was one of the last sheets Hen had ever signed.

She waited until Teo was asleep and Cass was snoring in the way she always denied, and Sorry had settled in the crook of her knees. Then she slid out of bed, laid the folio flat on the floorboards in the middle of the room, and set a candle on the boards beside it, as low as it would go, with the flame at the paper’s edge.

At first there was only the drawing: the Wren Stair, the turn at the landing, the Long Gallery running away northward, in her own fair hand, with Hen’s round figures down the margin. She moved the candle an inch, and the paper turned into country.

There were the marks, standing up in the light like stones in a field: Teo’s looped pothook T, Cass’s zigzag, her own wren’s foot, Hen’s gull-wing. Under each lay the knot she had seen through her damp-lens, a bruise in the paper where a plate had pressed blind ink into it before any of them had touched a pen, and from each knot the line ran on beneath the printed rule toward the binding. The lens had shown her that much in Fogmonth: lines that went to the margin and stopped, as if they continued on some other sheet. What the lens had been too small to show her was what they did in the last inch.

In the binding margin, where the paper was blank and nobody ever looked, the four lines bent toward one another, the way streams bend on a map. Teo’s ran into Hen’s, and Cass’s into them, and then hers, so that in the last half-inch before the edge four lines had become one. It was a little deeper there, a little more bruised, as if more weight had run down it. At the very edge it ended in a tiny tick: a registration mark, the kind a printer uses to lay one sheet exactly over another.

Ilse sat back on her heels on the cold floor.

She had seen the shape before, on every map of every coast in the Reach. A river comes down to the sea and splits, and splits again, and spreads into a fan of channels, a delta. This was a delta running the wrong way. It did not spread. It gathered. It took many mouths and drew them inland into a single stream, and the stream ran off the edge of the paper toward somewhere else.

A ghost-line was a thread. She had always thought of it that way: a thread from your hand to the land, and, if you were clever, a second thread to a dead man’s hand, so that the weight went to him instead of you. One mark, one thread, one place for the burden to go. She had never in her life drawn a ghost that went anywhere but to a mark.

These did not go to a mark. They gathered, and went to an edge, and to a tick that meant *lay the next sheet here*.

“Somewhere,” she said aloud, very quietly, “there’s a sheet this sits on.”

On the bed, Sorry lifted its head.

She thought of two hundred students signing every morning: two hundred threads on fifty sheets, every set gathering in its margin into one channel, every channel running off its binding edge toward the same place. A root. She thought of a word Quince had used once, drunk and boasting of a chapter-house he had ghosted in his youth so that the dean’s burden ran into a dozen dead canons at once. He had drawn it for her in spilled ale on the tabletop, all the little lines running into one. *You want a conduit, girl, for a big job. Run it all through the one pipe.*

A conduit.

She looked at the thickest of the four lines, the one that ran from Hen’s gull-wing. It was deeper than the others. Of course it was. Hen had signed extra.

She did not sleep much. In the morning she put the folio between the pages of Halloway’s *Elements of Error*, which was the dullest and heaviest book she owned and which nobody, including Halloway, had ever opened for pleasure, and she put the book on the shelf above her bed with the others.


* * *


The note came at noon, by a link who handed it over as if it might go off.

*My dear Miss Marrow—I have been sent a seed-cake from Lisk of quite unreasonable size, and I cannot possibly face it alone. Would you rescue me? My study, four o’clock. Do say yes.—A. H.*

She read it on the Wren Stair and stood holding it for some time.

She could not go to the Provost. She could not go to Brask, barred in her cottage at Verity Cross; and a crow could not carry what she had found. Orle had no power. Damask would be delighted and then tell everyone. She had Teo and Cass and Sorry and a sheet of paper in a book, and Hen had been lying by the window for six weeks being perfectly polite.

And there was Hallam. Master of the Register, which was where the sheets were kept. He had signed for the new paper under protest. He had put her in the Register as a copyist when nobody else in the College would have trusted a Walker with a dust-cloth. He had looked at her across the Mess on Errata Night as if he were afraid for her. He was the one person in this place with authority who had been kind to her before he had any reason to be.

She went.


* * *


Hallam’s study lay behind the Register Antechamber, through a door at the back of the stacks that Ilse had walked past every morning for three months without seeing open. It was oak, old and dark, with an iron latch, and when Hallam opened it to her knock the warmth came out to meet her like a dog.

“There you are,” he said. “Come in, come in. Mind the step, it’s been there since the Ninefold and it has never once moved, and still everyone trips on it.”

It was a small room and it was full: a sea-coal fire in a deep grate, two shabby chairs, books from floor to ceiling and in leaning stacks on the floor with cups on top of them. Maps were pinned over the books: the Salt Steps, the Ammonite Hills, a hand-drawn sheet of the Sallow coast, faded nearly white, with its edges soft as cloth. On the desk, among the papers, sat a paperweight carved from sounder-ivory, a smooth pale oval the size of a hen’s egg, with growth-rings in it like the grain in a shell. There was a smell of woodsmoke and paper and, faintly, of caraway.

The seed-cake was on a plate by the fire. It was, as promised, unreasonable.

“My sister’s girl,” said Hallam, cutting it. “She lives in Lisk and is convinced I don’t eat. She’s quite right. Sit, my dear.”

He poured tea for her, and then took his own cup in both hands and sat back in the other chair with it held against his chest. His grey kid gloves were soft with age at the fingertips. He held the cup the way a man holds a bird he has found in the road.

“These old hands don’t like the winter,” he said, seeing her look. “The Sallow left me a souvenir. Never mind them.” He smiled at her over his spectacles. “How is Miss Solberg?”

“The same.”

“I sat with her last week, for an hour. I don’t know whether she minded. She was very polite to me.” He looked into his tea. “That is the cruelty of it. The politeness. One would almost rather they wept.” He was quiet for a moment. “And Mr. Anselm? I heard him on Errata Night. A brave young man. I hope he tells her.”

“So do I.”

“And you,” said Hallam gently. “Are you sleeping?”

“Not much.”

“No. I shouldn’t think so.” He set his cup down on a stack of books. “I won’t speak of the Board unless you want me to. I’ll only say that when the time comes, I shall tell them what I know of your hand: that it is the best I have seen in a first-year in twenty years, and has never once given me a false line.”

She had not expected that. She drank her tea to give her face something to do.

They talked. It was easy to talk to him.
