He knew the Fallowmarch; he had walked it as a young man re-signing the old parish benchmarks, and he remembered the church at Hobbet with its leaning spire, and laughed when she told him it leaned more now. He told her about the night on the Sallow when Odile Brask had carried a lamp across a standing-rain bog for two hours to find a lost chainman, singing to keep the hollowings off. “The bravest woman I ever knew,” he said. “Still is. Broken, a little, but the break shows you the grain.” He talked of the Reach the way old sailors talked of ships, as *she*.

“People forget she has to be held,” he said, looking at the fire. “They think the ground under Tessary is just there, the way the sky is there. It isn’t. Someone holds her. Every morning, two hundred children walk out onto the Drift Wing with chains in their hands and hold her a little, and they think it’s a chore.” He smiled. “Every map is an act of love, my dear. We hold her still so that the children of Hobbet can find their way home.”

Ilse put her cup down.

“Master Hallam,” she said. “I need to tell you something about the Morning Survey sheets.”

She had planned it on the walk down, the way she planned a traverse: which stations to take, which to leave out. She told him there were lines under the marks. She told him she had seen them under raking light, after Archivist Damask’s class, on an old sheet from the stacks. She told him they lay in perfect registration under each signature and ran to the binding margin and joined there. She did not say *damp-lens* or *blind ink* or *Quince*. She did not say *I know what a ghost-line is because I have drawn dozens of them*. She said, “I think someone is tracing under our marks. I think that’s why the students are going grey. I’ve heard that’s how—forgers do it. To move the weight.”

Hallam did not interrupt her once. He listened with his gloved hands folded in his lap and his head bent, and as she went on his face grew very still and very grave, and when she had finished he took his spectacles off and rubbed his eyes with the back of his glove.

“There is a seventh Law,” he said at last, quietly. “You will have noticed I don’t teach it. We don’t, at the College. For the same reason one doesn’t show children where the knives are kept.” He put the spectacles back on. “Transfer. Yes. It can be done. It is the vilest thing that can be done with a pen, and you are telling me it has been done here, in my Register, under my nose, to my students.” His voice did not rise. It went lower. “Thank you. Thank you for bringing it to me and not to the Mess.”

“The Provost—”

“Aurelian is frightened,” said Hallam. “He has been frightened for three years. Frightened men want things to be calm. Leave Aurelian to me.” He leaned toward her. “Have you shown anyone else? Told anyone?”

She thought of Teo asleep on the Wren Stair with his grey fingertips, and Cass snoring, and Dr. Orle’s green ledger locked in its drawer. They were hers to protect. The habit of nine years closed over them like water.

“No,” she said.

“Not Dr. Orle? Not young Mr. Reyne?”

“No one.”

“Good,” said Hallam. “That was wise. And the sheet itself?”

“I put it back,” said Ilse. “In its bundle.”

“Then that’s where I shall find it.” He sat back, suddenly very old and very tired. “I’m going to look into this myself, my dear, and at once. It’s my Register. Whatever has been done in it is my failure before it is anyone’s crime, and I shall mend it.” He held her eyes. “But I must ask you something hard. Tell no one, not your friends, not the infirmary. Not for my sake; for the students’. If two hundred young people refuse to sign tomorrow morning, the Drift Wing will be out on the heath by noon, and some of them will be in it. You know the story of the Lost Wing. Panic,” said Aurek Hallam gently, “kills more than any illness. Let me find who has done this. Quietly. And then we will make it right.”

“Yes,” said Ilse. “All right.”

“Thank you.” He breathed out slowly and smiled at her, and it was the smile of a man who has been handed something heavy and is glad, all the same, to be the one holding it. “Now. You’ve hardly touched that cake, and I shall be eating it until Thaw if you don’t help. Have the corner piece. It has the most caraway.”

She had the corner piece. He wrapped two more slices for her friends, “whoever they are,” and at the step that had never moved since the Ninefold he put out his gloved hand to steady her elbow, and she did not trip.


* * *


She walked back across the Old Court feeling lighter than she had in six weeks, and noticed it the way you notice a tooth stop aching: carefully, not trusting it. She had put some of the weight down into hands able to carry it. That was what the College was supposed to be for.

“You told a Master,” said Cass, when she got in. She was sitting on the windowsill eating one of the slices of cake. “About the lines. You just told him.”

“I didn’t say how I know, or anything about you. Only that they’re there.”

“Big House looks after Big House, Marrow. You know that. That’s all it does.”

“Not him,” said Ilse.

Teo, who had eaten his slice in two bites, said, “I think it’s good someone grown-up knows. I’m sorry. I just do.” He hesitated. “It’s very good cake.”

Cass looked at the cake in her hand, and at Ilse, and shrugged, and finished it.

Before she went to bed, Ilse took Halloway’s *Elements of Error* down from the shelf, opened it at page two hundred and nine, and looked at the folio lying flat between the pages. Then she closed the book, put it back in its place, square to the shelf-edge, and blew out the candle.


* * *


She woke in the dark before First Bell because something was walking on her face.

It was Sorry, treading round and round on her pillow, trembling under her palm, its fur standing out in a bristling halo. It made a small noise she had never heard it make, a thin creak like a door. She lit the candle.

Sorry leapt from the pillow to the top of the wardrobe in one bound and crouched there, staring down at the room, its outline not quite steady, as if the candlelight could not decide where it ended. Its amber eyes were enormous.

The wardrobe had moved.

That was not new. Every morning it stood a foot or so out from the wall, and one of them pushed it back. But this morning it stood well into the room, turned at an angle, its doors facing the window, and the floorboards showed four long pale scrapes in the dust.

Ilse got out of bed. She went to the shelf above her bed and took down Halloway’s *Elements of Error*. It was where she had put it, square to the shelf-edge. She opened it at page two hundred and nine.

There was nothing between the pages.

She went through the book page by page, then shook it over the bed, then did the same with every other book on the shelf, quietly and very thoroughly, because the first rule of losing a thing is to be certain it is lost. The door was shut. The latch was down. Teo was asleep on his back with one arm over his face, and Cass was curled toward the wall, and Hen’s bed was square and empty, and nothing else was out of place at all.

Coyle counted his bundles. She had seen him do it, every week, with his lips moving. Coyle had found her with the stores ledger open, and the Provost had known about it within a day.

She took her folding rule from her coat on the nail and measured from the wall to the back corner of the wardrobe. Two feet, eleven inches and a half: four links and a little more, nearly three times its usual drift. She measured the angle against the line of the floorboards with her thumb and a scrap of paper. Then she took her field-book out of the coat and wrote it down, the date, the distance, the angle, *sheet gone from book, door latched, no other disturbance*, in her smallest, most careful hand, because an error unrecorded was a sin and this, whatever else it was, was an error in her measurements of the world. When she had finished she put the book away, and lifted Sorry down from the wardrobe and held it against her chest until it stopped shaking, and then she went and woke the others.


# Part Four: The Long Measure


## Chapter 25: Kasimir’s Ledger

A week after the sheet went missing, Hallam had sent her a note.

*My dear — Inquiries proceed. They are delicate and they are slow, and I would ask you for patience, which I know is a great deal to ask of anyone your age. Rest assured that you are not forgotten. Burn this. — A. H.*

She had burned it, and thought it a strange thing to be grateful for being told to wait. She had not told him the sheet was gone. She had told him it was in its bundle, and a lie, once laid down, is a station you have to keep measuring from.

It was Candlemonth now, the twentieth day, the snow gone to grey slush in the Old Court, and nothing had changed. Hen lay in her bed by the window. Teo’s fingertips were no worse and no better. Every morning the three of them signed the Survey skew, a sixteenth high of the rule and a little sunwise, and every morning the Wren Stair held, a little wobbly, like a table with a folded letter under one leg. Coyle would not meet her eye in the stacks. The Provost had not summoned her again. He did not need to.

She climbed to the crow-loft at sunset with a letter for Brask in her pocket, as she did every week.

The loft was at the top of the old tower above the Library of Record, up a stair and two ladders, a long low room under the roof slates. It was open to the west through a row of arches, and the whole of the Drift was framed in them like a painting. Tonight the sun was going down into the sea behind the Tern Stack, and its light came in flat and red along the floor. Out over the heath, three miles off, a single hill stood under its own private rain, a grey column of it falling and falling out of a clear sky, lit pink from the side, the way it had stood since Fogmonth.

The crows were on their perches in their dozens, grey-necked and black-backed, muttering. Along the sill of every arch they had built their little cairns: stacks of pebbles three and four high, no bigger than a fist, each one set on the very point where the hold was strongest. Mother Nettle never let anyone move them. *They know where the world is*, she said, *which is more than you lot do.*

“Brask again?” said Mother Nettle, from her stool. She was all knuckles and beak under a crow-feather cap, and she was mending a jess with her teeth.

“Brask again.”

She took the letter, rolled it with two twists of her crooked fingers, and whistled a crow down onto her wrist. “Go on, Tansy. Down to the Cross, to the cross old woman.” The crow dropped away into the red light. Mother Nettle wiped her fingers on her shawls. “And here’s the other one.”

Ilse turned. The trapdoor had lifted, and a fair head was coming up through it, and then a grey-blue coat with the snow still wet on its shoulders.

Kasimir Reyne climbed into the loft, closed the trap behind him, stood up, and hit his head on a beam.

Mother Nettle cackled, a long rattling noise like a stick dragged along railings, and it set the crows off, so that the whole loft was full of wings, and Kasimir stood with his hand pressed to his skull and his eyes shut.

“Bugger the crows,” said Mother Nettle happily. “They’re smarter than you lot. *They* duck.”

“Good evening, Mother Nettle,” said Kasimir, with dignity. He opened his eyes. “Good evening, Miss Marrow.”

“Mr. Reyne.”

“I would like ten minutes of your time.” He lowered his hand and examined it for blood, and found none, and seemed faintly disappointed. “I would like them somewhere that neither my mother’s Court nor your Provost’s office can overhear. I followed you up here because it is the only place in the College that I am reasonably confident has neither.”

“Crows don’t tell,” said Mother Nettle. “And I’m deaf when I want.” She went back to her jess.

Ilse looked at him. They had not spoken since the Old Court in the snow, when she had told him about her father and the Long Field and he had said *then the law must be fixed* and walked away, and left his accusation in the Board’s files where it still lay.

“You filed an accusation of forgery against me,” she said. “The Board hears it in five weeks.”

“Yes.”

“And now you want ten minutes.”

“Yes. The accusation is true. This is also true.” He took out the small black notebook with its corners worn round that she had seen in his hand since the night at Widow Tull’s hedge. “I am capable of holding two true things at once, Miss Marrow. I had hoped you might be.”


* * *


He sat on an upturned crate under the arches with the notebook open on his knee, and she sat on another, and the red light lay across the pages between them.

“I was in the Fallowmarch last summer on a Concordance inspection of the voids,” he said. “What you don’t know is that I was there at my mother’s request, and her interest is not in hedges. Since the spring of last year there have been unravellings in the Held Reach. The College calls them Drift incursions, or Margin mischief, and says they are nothing to concern the Court. My mother believes the College is losing its grip on the Reach and is concealing it. She asked me to find out whether she was right.” His mouth tightened. “I would like to be clear that I know what she will do with the answer.”

“Take the Register.”

“If she can.” He looked down at the page. “I have been keeping a list.”

The front of the notebook was errors observed, as she had always known it was: small entries in his perfect hand, dated, each with its correction. *Fogmonth 4: Grice’s chain read 99 links at a tally; recalibrated. Fogmonth 11: my own levelglass reading transposed (4.2 for 2.4); corrected.* Then, toward the back, the entries stopped and a table began. It was ruled in fine ink across two pages, and the columns were headed *Place*, *Date of void*, *Nature*, and then, in a separate block on the facing page, *Register warrant*, *Signed*, *Dates of travel*, *Route*.

He had seven places.

*Hythe Corner, Salt Steps. Blossom 1202. Harbour wall unknit, 9 rods.*
*Little Sallow. Fogmonth 1202. Well displaced 22 yds.*
*Marlow, Fallowmarch. Seedtime 1203. Drainage ditch wandering; about 3 chains.*
*Fennock, Fallowmarch. Greening 1203. Gatepost and 2 rods of fence migrating.*
*Little Wick lane, Wick Fallow. Reaping 15, 1203. Lane unravelled, ford dispersed, milestone sunk.*
*Brine End. Fogmonth 9, 1203. Well displaced 40 yds; two cottages slid.*
*Corrow. Frost 7, 1203. Camp ground let go entire.*

“Where do warrants come from?” said Ilse. Her voice sounded quite ordinary.

“When a Master travels on College business, the Register issues a warrant. It pays for post-horses and coach seats on the Great Meridian Road and the coast roads, and the posting houses enter it in their ledgers so the College can be billed at the quarter.” He touched the facing page. “Assessors inspect posting-house ledgers for road tax, and I hold an assessor’s commission. It took me since Reaping, nine posting houses, and one very long evening in which Orla Devane charmed the bursar’s clerk while I read his daybook upside down.” He did not smile, but something in his face nearly did. “She would like it known that she does not regret it.”

Ilse read down the right-hand page.

Every void had a warrant beside it. Every warrant was a Register tour of inspection, and every one was signed in the same hand, which Kasimir had copied so faithfully that she could see the slope of the capitals and the little hook on the final letter: *A. Hallam, Mag. Reg.* And every tour had brought him within a morning’s ride of the place, a day or three before the ground let go.

*Reaping 8–21, 1203. The Cape to the Fallowmarch and return. Stages: Verity Cross, Tessary, Hobbet (two nights).*

“Brine End,” said Ilse. “He was in the Salt Steps that week.”

“His warrant says the Salt Steps, which are north. The posting house at Verity Cross says that on the first morning he hired a gig, drove it south, and brought it back at dusk muddy to the hubs.”

“I was in Hobbet that week,” said Kasimir. “I was there for the voids. I did not see him. I saw you.”

Out over the heath the hill stood under its rain. The light on it was going from pink to grey.

“No,” said Ilse.

“Miss Marrow—”

“No. Listen.” She put her hand flat on the page. “Dates are the easiest thing in the world to make agree. I’ve done it. You take a hundred tours and a hundred voids and pick the seven that line up. Hallam travels all the time. It’s his office. Of course he’s near when something happens.” She heard herself talking faster and could not stop. “And it’s the Provost who’s been covering this. The Provost wrote to Dr. Orle to stop her writing to Tessary. The Provost had me in his study six weeks ago and offered to make your accusation disappear if I stopped asking about the paper supply. The paper changed in 1201 when the Provost’s office cut the money. The Provost—”

“The Provost,” said Kasimir, “has left Cape Verity three times in two years, all three to sit in the Assembly at Tessary, on dates that are printed in the Assembly calendar and that match none of these. And the Provost does not sign travel warrants. These are in Hallam’s hand.” His voice was very level. “There were eleven Register tours in that period. Seven of them were followed by voids. I did not choose seven out of a hundred. I chose seven out of eleven. The other four were in the Ammonite Hills and Lisk, where the ground is oldest and most deeply held, and where, I would suggest, a void is very much harder to make.”

She said nothing.

“I may be wrong,” said Kasimir. “I have written down every way I can think of in which I might be wrong. It is on the next page. It is a long page.” He closed the notebook on his finger. “But I came to you because you are the only person in this College who would understand the evidence that would prove me right, if it exists. In Fogmonth I found you in the Antechamber at midnight, with something in your hand you did not want me to see, and you were more frightened than I have ever seen anyone. You are not a person who frightens easily. I have spent three months trying to work out what a forger would be afraid of in a room full of honest paper.” He looked at her. “What did you find?”

Mother Nettle had stopped mending. Ilse looked at the columns in his beautiful hand and thought of the old milestone, HOBBET II, standing up to its shoulders in the lane.

“Not here,” she said. “It needs the dark. Can you get yesterday’s Survey sheet for your party out of the Antechamber without anyone seeing?”

“I file my party’s sheets myself,” said Kasimir. “I always have. Nobody else does it properly.”

“Of course you do,” said Ilse.


* * *


The Fieldwork shed had been padlocked by order of the Board since Frost, but the order said nothing about the back door, which had never had a lock, only a wedge, and Ilse had been Brask’s assistant long enough to know where the wedge was kept.

Inside it was cold and smelled of damp canvas and linseed oil and, very faintly, of Brask’s pipe. The chains hung on their pegs where Ilse had coiled them before the shed was shut. The Long Lie’s peg by the door stood empty, and Ilse found she could not look at it.

She shuttered the one window and lit a single candle and set it on the bench, and Kasimir laid the sheet down beside it: the Survey folio for the Observatory stair and the Tern Passage, dated Candlemonth 19, signed in four places at the foot. Absalom’s laborious cross. Piety’s spiky little steeple. Orla’s flourish like a cat’s tail. And his own, a beautifully drawn R with a level line through it, the most exact mark on the sheet.

“Watch the foot of the sheet,” said Ilse, and brought the candle down to the bench until the flame was level with the paper, and let the light rake across it.

She heard him stop breathing.

It was all there, as it had been on the Wren Stair folio five weeks before: the paper standing up into country, the knot bruised under each mark, and from each knot the hair-fine line running beneath the rule toward the binding. Then, in the blank margin, four streams bending toward each other, Absalom’s into Orla’s, Piety’s into them, and then his, until one channel ran out to the edge and its little registration tick.

“Each line is in blind ink,” said Ilse quietly. “Gall and alum and a little quicksilver. It dries to nothing. It’s laid on the sheet before anyone signs it, a knot shaped to sit under your mark, because you’re all taught to sign in the same box, and every one of you does it the same way every morning. When you set your mark on it with intent, you take the hold, and you take the burden that goes with it. And then the ghost takes the burden from somewhere else and runs it down that line into you.” She moved the candle a little, and the channel in the margin deepened. “On an ordinary forgery the ghost runs to one mark. A dead man’s, usually. This doesn’t run to a mark. It runs off the sheet. There’s another sheet somewhere that all these lines sit on. A root. A conduit. Every sheet in the Antechamber since Thaw 1201 is feeding into it, and it’s feeding back.”

Kasimir did not say anything for a long time.

Then he bent until his face was a hand’s breadth from the paper and looked at his own mark with the ghost under it, running away to the margin. He reached out one finger toward the knot under his R and stopped short. His face had gone the colour of the paper.

“Every morning,” he said.

“Yes.”

“Since Reaping. I have signed every morning since Reaping. I have never missed one. I have signed extra when Absalom was ill.” He straightened and put both hands flat on the bench and looked at them, then turned them over, the long clean fingers, the nails. There was no grey on them. She watched him look for it anyway, at every fingertip in turn, the way a man pats his pockets for a key he knows he has dropped.

“There’s nothing,” she said. “You’d see it at the nail first. Your capacity must be high.”

“That is what they said about my father.”

She waited.

“Lord Matthias Reyne,” said Kasimir, to his hands, “bore for the Court for thirty years, and everyone said his capacity was boundless. Then he could not change his mind about which coat to wear, and then he could not climb the stairs. At fifty he went to stand in the chapel at Reyne House, and he is standing there still.” He was quite steady. “They keep him dusted.”

The candle hissed.

“I write perfect charters,” said Kasimir, “because a perfect charter does not strain. If the record is true to the hair, the land does not pull against it, and I do not carry. That is why I check every link. That is why I keep this.” He touched the notebook in his coat. “Every error is a place where the land might one day pull against me. I have spent my whole life making sure there is nothing in the world that can pull.” He looked at the folio. “And every morning for five months, someone has been running a line under my hand and feeding me weight. Quietly. Without asking. The one thing I have spent my life—” He stopped. “Forgive me. I am not usually like this.”

“I know,” said Ilse. “I’ve watched you.”

He gave a short laugh with no breath in it.

“You can stop it taking any more,” she said. “Not what’s already there. That stays filed. But new lines. Sign off-register. Set your mark a sixteenth of an inch high of the rule and turn it a little sunwise, so it doesn’t sit on the knot. That’s enough. We’ve been doing it since Frost. The stretch wobbles a little, but it holds.”

Kasimir stared at her. “Deliberately sign out of true.”

“Yes.”

“On a College sheet.”

“Yes.”

“That is—” He searched for the word, and she watched him find it and decide, with visible pain, to say it. “That is a lie.”

“It’s a small one,” said Ilse. “On a College sheet. Welcome.”

He looked at her across the candle, and then, unexpectedly, he put his hand over his eyes the way he had on Errata Night, and his shoulders shook once, and when he took the hand away he was nearly smiling.

“Recorded,” he said.


* * *


Afterward they sat on the bench with the candle between them, and for a while neither said anything. The wind had got up off the sea and was worrying at the roof, and in the rafters a loose slate ticked.

“The Provost could have given the order,” said Ilse at last. She knew as she said it that she was saying it to hear how it sounded. “He could have told someone in the Register what to do.”

“Who?” said Kasimir, quite gently.

She did not answer.

Who orders the paper for the Survey folios, and signs for it, under a protest nobody heard but his own clerk. Who insists, kindly, always kindly, that every sheet be signed in the Antechamber and nowhere else. Who knows the seventh Law and does not teach it, *for the same reason one doesn’t show children where the knives are kept*, and made the whole Antechamber laugh when Kasimir asked about contrary records. Who travels. Who wears grey kid gloves at all times, for frostbite. A College gentleman, out of the Meridian, all the way from the Cape; ever so polite. Asked the way to the old milestone. A gentle old man with grey hands, Nan Ottery Tenter had said in her smoky wagon at Corrow, polite as a parson. Asked the way to the Corrow stone.

The pieces went into place one after another, each one with a small exact sound, like links going into a canvas bag. She had always loved that sound.

“He gave me tea,” she said.

Kasimir turned his head.

“Seed-cake. From his sister’s girl in Lisk. He gave me the corner piece because it had the most caraway.” She could hear her voice and it sounded, still, quite ordinary, which was odd, because nothing else was. “He said he’d speak for my hand at the Board. He said Brask was the bravest woman he ever knew. He said every map is an act of love.” She swallowed. “He sat with Hen. He told me that. He said she was very polite to him.”

“Miss Marrow.”

“I told him.” She looked at the sheet under the candle. “In Deepwinter. I found the root lines on one of our old folios and I didn’t know who else to take it to, so I took it to him. I told him there were lines under the marks. I told him they ran to the margin and joined. He asked me if I’d told anyone else and I said no. He asked me where the sheet was and I said I’d put it back.” She laughed, or something did. “I lied to him to protect my friends from *him*, and I didn’t even know that was what I was doing. And that night someone came into our room while we were asleep and took it out of my Mensuration book and shut the door behind them and put the latch down.”

The slate ticked in the rafters. The wind leaned on the shed.

She thought, without wanting to, of how kind he had been. That was the part she could not make go anywhere. It had not been a performance. She had spent her whole life watching people perform kindness for her, magistrates and stewards and the Provost with his silver pot, and she knew the shape of it, and that afternoon by the fire had not had that shape. He had been glad of her company. He had been sorry for Hen. He had meant every word about the Reach and the children of Hobbet, and he had walked up the Little Wick lane in his kid gloves and let it go, with Tam Brewin coming down it singing.

The kindness had been real. That was the worst of it. The man was this, and the kindness had been real, both at once.

Two true things.

Kasimir was watching her. He had not moved closer and he did not touch her, and she was grateful for both. After a while he took his notebook out of his coat and opened it at the page with the long list of every way he might be wrong, and looked at it for a moment, and then turned to a clean page and uncapped his pen.

“What are you writing?” she said.

“The date,” said Kasimir, “and what you have just told me.” He wrote it in his small perfect hand, and blotted it, and looked up at her across the candle. “He asked whether you had told anyone else. You said no. He took the sheet. He has written you a note since, I imagine, telling you to be patient.”

“Burn this,” said Ilse. “It said burn this.”

“Then he has stopped asking himself whether you understand what you found.” He closed the notebook. “He will not ask again.”

Ilse looked at the empty peg by the door where the Long Lie had stood for nineteen years, and at the sheet under the candle with its four small rivers running the wrong way into the dark, and at her own right hand, the signing hand, resting on her knee.

“No,” she said. “He knows I know.”


## Chapter 26: What the Sallow Gave Back

The cart road from the College to Verity Cross was two miles of frozen ruts, and Kasimir Reyne walked every one of them as though he had been asked to assess it.

He placed his boots on the crowns of the ruts rather than in the troughs, where the ice was thinner. It was the walk of a man who had been taught as a boy that the ground owed him an explanation. Ilse had walked these ruts all autumn behind Brask with an armful of chains, and she let her feet find their own way, and twice in the first mile she had to wait for him.

“You might have brought a lantern,” he said, the second time.

“You might have brought one. You’re the one with the coat that costs more than my parish.”

“Lanterns on the cart road after Evening Bell are logged at the porter’s lodge.” He came level with her. Frost had got into his hair where it escaped his hat. “I did not think either of us wanted the porter to write down that the Chancellor’s son and the accused forger had gone out together to call upon the suspended Field-Surveyor.”

“Put like that it sounds like a song.”

“It sounds like a conspiracy, Miss Marrow, because it is one.”

She had no answer to that. The road went over the shoulder of the down, and Verity Cross lay below them, a scatter of roofs and three lit windows, with the sea a sound to the north. Two days since the crow-loft, since Kasimir had opened his notebook of errors on his knee in the red light and shown her seven dates and seven travel warrants and one name. She had done her Morning Survey since and signed her sheets off-register, and sat in the second row while Aurek Hallam lectured on the Law of Witness with his gloved hands folded on the lectern. He had smiled at her once, kindly, over his spectacles, and she had smiled back. It turned out that a forger’s face was the last thing to go.

Brask’s cottage was halfway along Netmakers’ Row, near the old harbour, with its back to the weather and a yard full of lobster pots that had never seen a lobster. A light showed under the blue door. Ilse knocked twice, and the door opened before her hand had come down from the second knock.

Brask filled the frame. She had the Long Lie in her good hand, held crosswise like a bar, and her white hair stood up as if she had been running her fingers through it. Her eye went to Ilse, and then past Ilse to Kasimir, and stayed there.

“Marrow,” she said. “And the Chancellor’s boy. Is this an arrest?”

“No, Field-Surveyor,” said Kasimir.

“Pity.” She stood aside. “Wipe your feet. The floor’s the only thing in here I’ve attested.”

That was a lie, and a joke, and Ilse could not remember ever hearing Brask make both at once. She wiped her feet.


* * *


The cottage was one room and a loft, with books on every flat surface and several that weren’t flat: books propping a table leg, books stacked into a stool, a book face-down on the cold hearth with a cairn-crow feather in it for a marker. Brask had a spirit-lamp going on the old plane-table board and a chipped brown teapot beside it, and she poured three cups of something that might once have been introduced to tea but had not stayed long in its company.

“Drink it,” said Brask. “It’s warm. That’s its only virtue.”

Kasimir drank without a flicker. Ilse thought better of him for that.

Then he laid his notebook open between the teapot and the lamp, and in complete, careful sentences he told Brask what he had told Ilse in the crow-loft. A list of voids, kept since his summer in the Fallowmarch. The posting-house ledgers along the Meridian Road, which an assessor might inspect for road tax, and the bursar’s daybook of travel warrants, read upside down. Brine End, Fogmonth 9. Corrow, Frost 7. The Little Wick lane at Wick Fallow, Reaping 15. A ditch at Marlow, a gatepost at Fennock, two more. Before each one, by a day or by three, a warrant for an inspection tour, and each warrant bore the same signature.

He did not say the name aloud. He turned the notebook round and let Brask read it.

Brask read the whole page, and then she put her good forefinger on the first line and went down the dates again the way she went down a field-book, looking for the place where the numbers would stop agreeing. They didn’t stop. At the bottom she laid her hand flat on the table, next to the stilled grey one, and left it there.

“Marrow,” she said. “You’ve something to show me, or you wouldn’t be here. You’d have sent him on his own and let me throw him in the sea.”

Ilse had brought that morning’s Morning Survey sheet in the lining of her father’s coat. She unfolded it on the table, lit a candle-end at the lamp and set it down on the boards so low that its flame stood level with the paper, and slid it across.

On the sheet the Wren Stair and the Long Gallery lay in her own fair hand: three chains forty-one links, the angle of the landing, the bearing of the turned stair. At the foot was her wren’s foot, set a hair off its proper place. Under the grazing flame the paper rose into hills and valleys too faint to see by daylight, and among the valleys ran a fine pale violet line. It rose from a faint bruised knot where her mark ought to have been, missed the mark by the width of her deliberate skew, and ran away under the rule toward the binding. In the margin it met the others and *gathered*, as every such line on every sheet she had looked at gathered, toward a single place.

“That is blind ink,” said Brask.

“Gall and alum and quicksilver. It’s printed on the sheets before we ever see them. Every sheet since 1201.”

“A ghost.”

“A ghost-line, under every mark in the College, running to one root.” Ilse moved the candle back. The violet line sank into the paper and was gone. “I don’t know where the root is. I know where it isn’t. It isn’t in the Antechamber. It isn’t anywhere a student can go.”

“And the Quiet.”

“Wil Ashdown went grey in Seedtime 1201. Dr. Orle has it in her ledger. Two more that year, four the next. Hen. Teo’s fingertips.” She made herself say the last part. “I told him, Field-Surveyor. In Deepwinter I sat in his study and told him there were ghost-lines on the Morning Survey sheets. He gave me tea and thanked me and asked me to keep it quiet for the students’ sake. The next morning the sheet I’d filched was gone from my room.”

Brask didn’t move. Ilse watched the good hand on the table, because it was the only part of Brask telling her anything, and saw the fingers draw in against the wood until the nails were white.

“Gloves,” said Brask.

Neither of them answered. She wasn’t asking.

“He started with the gloves three winters back,” said Brask. “Old Sallow frostbite.” She made a sound that was not a laugh. “We came home off the Sallow in Highsun. There was no frost. I lay in the infirmary six weeks with my eye in a basin and he sat by me every day with his hands as pink as a baby’s. I thought, twenty years is a long time for frostbite to wait. And then I thought, he carried you twenty leagues, Odile. Let the man have his gloves.”

“You couldn’t have known,” said Kasimir.

Brask turned her eye on him. It was not a kind look. It was the look she gave a student who had said something soft where something measured was wanted.

“I can always have known, boy. That’s the job.” She looked down at her hand, and she opened it, deliberately, finger by finger, and laid it flat again. “I knew the wrong thing. That’s worse. I knew he came back certain.”

“Certain of what?” said Ilse.

“Of everything.” Brask reached past the teapot for her clay pipe and held it without filling it. “You never knew him before. Hallam at thirty was the best doubter I ever worked with. Give him a closed traverse and he’d find you three ways it might be wrong and a fourth you hadn’t thought of, and he’d be *happy* about it. He used to call the First Atlas the finest guess anyone ever made. He was my second on the Sallow because he argued with me. I wanted someone who’d argue.”

She turned the pipe in her fingers.

“Four didn’t come back. I lost the eye. Somewhere in the last of it, when he had me on his back and we were walking out on a bearing he’d taken off a cairn-crow, something happened to Aurek that I didn’t see, because I was mostly dead at the time. When I woke in the infirmary he was sitting there with his pink hands, and he was certain. The Sallow had given him back to me, and I was so grateful to have anyone given back that I didn’t count what was missing.” She set down the pipe. “He sat in front of the Board and he didn’t say I was mad. He didn’t say I was right either. He said the Drift was a danger to everything we loved and the College’s duty was to hold. He was never wrong about anything again. They made him Master of the Register six years later.”

Ilse thought about Hallam by his study fire, saying *every map is an act of love, my dear,* and about how she had believed him, and still did. She could not find the line he had moved, because she didn’t think there was one. He believed every word.

“Certainty’s a symptom,” she said. “Of burden. That’s what Orle says. The mind goes rigid before the hand does.”

“Sometimes,” said Brask. “And sometimes a man watches four friends go into the ground like stones dropped down a well and decides he’ll never let anything move again, and he doesn’t need burden to do it. He does it with his whole heart. That’s the kind I’d be frightened of.” Her eye was dry and very bright. “I am, as it happens.”

Kasimir said, carefully, “So am I.”

“Good.” Brask pushed the teapot back to the middle of the table. “Then we’re all sensible. Drink your tea.”


* * *


The tea had gone cold, which did not make it worse. Brask made more, and while it stewed she walked the one room with the Long Lie, three paces and turn, the brass shoe ringing on the flags, while Ilse and Kasimir laid out what they had.

What they had was a notebook of dates, some sheets with violet under them, a doctor’s ledger and the word of a dead man’s apprentice.

“And no root,” said Kasimir. “We cannot point to the place the lines run. We can say only that they run.”

“Your mother would make something of it, boy,” said Brask. “You’ve thought of taking it to her.”

Kasimir did not deny it. He sat very straight with his cold hands round the cup.

“I have thought of it,” he said. “My mother has wanted the Register under the Court since before I was born. If I give her a Master of the Register who has been ghosting students, she will have it within the year. That would be convenient, which is not the same thing as just, and the Board would see only the convenience. A suspended Field-Surveyor. An accused forger. The Chancellor’s son.” He set the cup down. “We are the three least credible witnesses in the College. If I sat on the Board I would not believe us.”

“You wouldn’t believe us anyway,” said Ilse. “You don’t believe anything you haven’t measured.”

“That is why I would not believe us.”

Brask snorted. She stopped walking.

“Forty years,” she said. “He’s been the kindest man in that building for forty years. He held my hand in the infirmary, and he’s given his own money to every Walker who came through the Gate with holes in their boots. There are masters who’ll hear the word ghost and look at Marrow, because she knows how, and then at me, because I hate them, and then at you, because of your mother, and they’ll go to bed and sleep like lambs. You won’t undo forty years with a candle held sideways.” She thumped the Long Lie on the floor. “So. What does he want?”

“To hold the Reach,” said Ilse.

“That’s what he says. What does he *need?*”

“Signatures,” said Ilse. “Marks on sheets with his lines under them. Two hundred students a morning, a sliver each. That’s been enough for three years and it isn’t enough any more, or he wouldn’t be pruning parishes. The voids are getting closer together. Brine End and Corrow were three weeks apart.”

“So he needs more,” said Kasimir.

“He needs a great deal more, all at once.” Ilse looked at Brask. “Where does the whole College sign one sheet?”

Brask had already turned to the window, toward the Cape, as though she could see the Lantern Gate from here with its one light on the keystone.

“The Equinox Charter,” she said. “Thaw 22. Every member of the College under the arch at dawn. Three hundred marks on one folio, and the Master of the Register prepares the folio. He’s done it every year since he took the Register. Nobody watches him do it, because nobody ever has.” Her voice was quite level. “If I wanted to bind a whole College at once, that’s where I’d do it.”

Ilse felt it in her signing hand first, a cold ache across the knuckles. Hen had gone Quiet on a sliver a morning. She tried to think what a full measure would do, poured into three hundred people in a single dawn, and her mind wouldn’t draft it.

“The Long Measure comes first,” said Kasimir. He had a pencil out. “Thaw 21. The winning party’s route charter is presented at the Gate and becomes the basis of the Equinox Charter. That is the custom. He prepares the folio, but he prepares it from the winning charter.”

“He’ll have his own folio ready whatever happens,” said Brask. “But if the winning charter is good enough, he has to be seen to use it, or explain why not, in front of three hundred people and the Provost.”

“Four weeks,” said Ilse.

“Twenty-nine days,” said Kasimir, without looking up.

“Thank you, Mr. Reyne.”

“You are welcome, Miss Marrow.”

Brask looked from one of them to the other, and then she sat down heavily and put her face in her good hand for a moment, and when she took it away she was only tired.

“You’ll do it without me,” she said. “I’m not allowed past the porter’s lodge, thanks to you, boy.”

“Yes,” said Kasimir. “I am sorry for it.”

“Are you.”

He considered. “I am sorry that it was necessary. I am not sorry that I told the truth.”

“Hm.” Brask poured him more tea, which was perhaps a punishment. “That’s the first sensible thing anyone named Reyne has said in my hearing. Drink it, and go home, both of you, before the porter wonders. And Marrow.”

Ilse stopped with her coat half on.

“Be civil to him,” said Brask. “If he’s had your sheet out of your room, he’s watching to see what you do. Do nothing. Give him a student. Can you do that?”

“I’ve been doing it for two days.”

“I know. I can see it on you.” She looked at Ilse, and her voice dropped, which Ilse had never heard it do. “It’s a hard thing to look at the kindest man you know and see what he’s become. I’ve had half an hour to do it in. You’ve had two days. Neither of us should have had to. Go on.”


* * *


They didn’t go straight to the Ranges. Neither of them suggested it. When they came in by the postern, Kasimir turned not toward the Lantern Gate but toward the bell-tower.

“There is a ladder from the bell-chamber to the leads above the Library of Record,” he said. “My cousin Lisbet showed me. She is a proctor and believes that rules are for other people’s cousins.”

“Is it logged at the porter’s lodge?”

“Nothing on a roof is logged, Miss Marrow. That is the principal charm of roofs.”

The leads were a flat valley between two slopes of slate, with a parapet that looked west. Ilse climbed out onto them and stood up, and the whole sky came down on her.

The air had been scrubbed with frost. Fallowmarch skies had always had a hedge at the bottom. Here there was nothing at the bottom but the Margin and then the Drift, and over the Drift the stars were wrong. They were the right stars in the right places, but at the rim of the world, out past the Wing, the low ones wavered. They didn’t twinkle. They slid gently back and forth, a finger’s width, the way a reflection moves in a pond when a fish turns under it. The horizon out there was not sitting still, and the stars rocked on it like lamps on a boat.

“I come up here,” said Kasimir, beside her, “to look at that.”

“It’s horrible.”

“Yes.” He leaned his forearms on the parapet. “I find it calming. I cannot say why.”

They stood there for a while. Below them the Drift Wing breathed out over the heath, and one window in the Third Range slid a little and stopped.

“My father,” said Kasimir, “was fifty when he stilled.”

He had told her this in Brask’s shed, in four sentences, like an entry read out. She waited to see if there would be a fifth.

“He bore for the Court. For duty, which is a kind of hire in which the wages are thanked rather than paid. Every boundary in the Lisk reclamations carries his mark. When the Ambry rose in ’93 and the levees strained, he felt it in his knees. I was eleven. He could not climb the stairs to say goodnight, and so I came down. Then he could not turn his head to see me in the doorway. Then he stood in the chapel at Reyne House, where he had asked to stand.” His voice was perfectly level. “I visited him every day for a year and then I stopped, because I found that I was talking to him as though he could hear me, and I could not bear to find out whether he could.”

The stars at the horizon rocked on their pond.

“That’s why you’re so good,” said Ilse. “Your charters. That’s why they’re flawless.”

“If a record is exactly true, the land does not pull against it. No strain. No burden.” He turned his hands over on the parapet and looked at them. “I have attested ninety-four records since I came to the College and I feel almost none of them. I have made myself very good, Miss Marrow, so that nothing will ever have a reason to hold on to me.” He laughed a little, without much in it. “And then I found my mark on a sheet with a violet line under it, and it was not my fault, and I could not have prevented it, and it was in me anyway. My hands are quite pink. I look at them every morning. I am terrified of being still.”

It was the plainest thing she had ever heard him say. She looked at his hands, long and clean and steady, and at her own, small and ink-stained, and found that she wanted to put one of hers on one of his, which was ridiculous. She kept them on the cold stone.

“I’m frightened of being found out,” she said. “That’s the obvious one. You found me out.”

“I did.”

“That’s not what I mean, though.” It was hard to measure a thing you had never let anyone look at. “Quince used to say a good lie is ninety-nine parts truth. A good lie’s just a true thing with one line moved. And I’ve started to think—” Out on the heath a light that might have been a wayhorn’s eye, or a lantern, or nothing, came and went. “I might be made the same way. Everything true about me except one line, and the line’s the bit that holds the rest up. If you took it out I don’t know there’d be anyone there. I’m frightened that I’m hollow. That it’s forgery all the way down.”

He didn’t say *you’re not*, which was what anyone else would have said. He was quiet for long enough that she thought he wasn’t going to answer, and she was obscurely grateful.

Then he said, “Ilse.”

She turned her head. He was looking at her, not at the horizon, and in the starlight his face had lost all its careful assessment and was only young and tired and very close. She could see that he did not know what he was going to say next. She had never seen that in him before, and it undid her more than anything he could have said.

Neither of them moved. A gull went over, very high, crying once. Out past the Wing the stars swayed and settled.

“It was a good forgery,” he said at last, quite low. “The Tull hedge. I measured it for three days. I could not find the line you had moved. I had to find the dead man’s mark instead.” He looked down at his hands. “You were not hollow when you drew it. Nobody hollow could have drawn it so well.”

It was not what either of them had been about to say. Ilse knew it, and knew that he knew it, and found that it would do, for now. She was smiling in the dark like a fool.

“That,” she said, “is the most Reyne compliment anyone has ever paid me.”

“I have been told I give very few.”

“You should be careful. It’ll go to my head.”

“I do not think,” said Kasimir Reyne, “that anything has ever gone to your head, Miss Marrow, without first being made to produce its papers.”

She laughed out loud, and the sound went off the leads and away over the Margin, and below them in the Second Range a window opened and someone shouted at them to shut up, some of us have Morning Survey. They ducked below the parapet like children and stayed there, backs against the cold slates and shoulders not quite touching, until the window shut again, and then a while longer, while overhead the stars stood fixed and true, and away at the edge of the world they rocked.


## Chapter 27: Registration Guides

Mother Nettle had agreed to the plan with an enthusiasm that worried everyone except Cass.

“A crow from Tessary,” she said, rubbing her knuckly hands together. “For the Registrar-Adjunct personal, and won’t give it up to nobody else, and pecks. Oh, he’ll come. That one’d climb a burning ladder for a sealed letter.” She cackled. “Bugger me, I’ve not had this much fun since the Provost’s hat.”

“What happened to the Provost’s hat?” said Teo.

“Crows,” said Mother Nettle, with satisfaction.

It was Thaw 12, 1204 by the College clocks and still Candlemonth by the weather. The crow-loft ran the length of the roof-space at the top of the old tower, perch-boards in tiers and a hundred-odd cairn-crows muttering on them like a congregation waiting for a dull sermon. Along the sills and under the perches the crows had built their small stacks of pebbles, as they did wherever they slept. Ilse had counted them once: a hundred and twelve tiny cairns, each true to its shelf, the most honestly held objects in the College.

She sat at the far end of the long table with a lantern turned low and Sorry along her forearm. Teo stood by the hatch, not looking down through it. Cass sat on a crate beside it, flicking a sling-stone up and catching it.

At a quarter to nine the ladder creaked.

Merrin Coyle came up through the hatch head first, his ink-stained cuffs turned back and his anxious face lit from below by his lamp. He said, “Mother Nettle, I was told—” and then he saw Teo, and Cass, and Ilse, and he stopped with his shoulders out of the hatch like a man in a bath.

Cass stood up, let the hatch-cover fall shut behind him with a bang that sent forty crows into the air, and sat on it.

“Hey-ho, Master Coyle,” she said. “Mind the step.”


* * *


He didn’t try to run. He looked at Cass sitting on the hatch, and at Teo, who was very large and looked sorry about it, and then he came the rest of the way up and set his lamp down among the message-tubes. His hands were shaking. He folded them to stop it.

“Is this to do with Miss Marrow’s inquiry?” he said. “Because I only keep the folios—”

“It’s to do with the folios,” said Ilse. “Sit down, Master Coyle. Nobody’s going to hurt you. Teo, does he look hurt?”

“He looks cold,” said Teo. “I’ve got tea. It’s in a jar, which isn’t ideal, but jars are better than you’d think—”

Coyle sat and took the tea. Mother Nettle had retreated down the loft and was pretending to mend a nesting box with her head on one side like one of her birds.

Ilse unrolled a stolen Morning Survey sheet and drew a lit candle-end across it, low, so that the grazing light made the paper into a country of tiny hills. The violet line came up beneath her skewed wren’s foot the way a fish comes up under ice.

Coyle’s face told her everything before he spoke. It wasn’t surprise. It was the face of a man meeting an old acquaintance somewhere he had hoped never to.

“The guides,” he said.

“What guides?”

“The registration guides.” He swallowed. “I print them. Every sheet. There’s a second plate. Master Hallam gave it to me in 1201, when we changed to the Lisk paper, and the ink with it. It’s a guide-print. It keeps the students’ drafts square to the Register sheets, so the filing will be true. He said—” Coyle stopped. He looked at the candle. “I asked why blind ink. He said, so as not to distract the students, my dear Merrin. They have enough to think about at First Bell.”

“Did you believe him?” said Ilse.

“Yes.”

She waited.

“For a year,” said Coyle. “Perhaps more. They use guide-prints in the Lisk counting-houses for ledgers; there’s nothing strange in it. But these don’t sit where a guide would sit. They sit under the marks. Always under the marks, and nowhere else. I told myself it was for the filing.” He looked up. “You know what it is. That’s why you’ve been looking at them.”

“I know what it is.”

“Then tell me.” His voice cracked. “Please. I’ve been afraid to know for two years, and I’m so tired of being afraid of it.”

So she told him what blind ink was for, in hedge country, and what a ghost-line did when it lay in perfect registration beneath another person’s mark. She did not tell him how she knew. He didn’t ask. Halfway through he put his face in his hands.

When she had finished, Teo sat down on the bench beside him, which made the bench complain, and laid his own hands on the table in the lamplight. The tips of his fingers were the colour of a slate roof after rain, fading to pink at the second knuckle.

“I’m not angry with you,” Teo said. “I want to be clear about that, because if I were you I’d be expecting somebody to be. I’ve just been signing my stretch every morning, very neatly. I’m quite proud of my mark.” He looked at the grey. “Hen signed more than I did. She can’t remember why things are funny.”

Coyle made a noise like something tearing.

“The Equinox folio,” he said. “Oh, Ninefold. The Equinox folio.”

“Tell us,” said Ilse.

“Other years I prepare it. I size the sheet and rule the margins, and he draws the frame on the morning, from the Long Measure charter.” Coyle was speaking fast now, before his courage closed. “This year he took it blank, on Candlemonth the third, and locked himself in his study with it three nights running. I saw his hands once, last Frost, when he took a glove off to wipe his spectacles. Grey to the wrist. Not like Master Anselm’s. Like stone. When he came out in Candlemonth he was grey in the face as well. He sat down on the Antechamber steps and I brought him water, and he patted my hand and said I was a good man.”

“The frame,” said Ilse. “The under-drawing. Did he do it himself?”

“He won’t let me near it. He won’t let me near the other one either.”

“What other one?”

“The master sheet. In his study, in the press by the window, under glass. I’ve only seen it from the door. He calls it the Conduit.” Coyle looked at the lamp. “He’s never been short with me in eleven years except once, the day I moved the sounder-ivory paperweight on his desk to dust under it. He took it out of my hand and put it back where it had been and said, *Please don’t,* in a voice I didn’t know he had.”

Ilse filed that, not knowing yet where it went.

“Why?” said Cass. She hadn’t moved from the hatch. “That’s what I want to know. The paper-walls do a lot of things for money and a lot for spite. Why’s a kind old man drain the Big House dry?”

Coyle was quiet so long that Ilse thought he would refuse. Then he said, very low, “I carry the lamp for him down to Seven, sometimes. He doesn’t let me past the grille. He goes in to the Zero Sheets with his own instruments and measures them, and he talks while he does it. I don’t think he knows he does.” Coyle wet his lips. “He says, a hair a season. He says, two hairs, this winter. He says she’s coming apart at the centre and the Provost won’t let him say so.” He looked round at the three of them. “The Zero Sheets are softening. He’s not stealing anything. He’s trying to save Tessary.”

At the far end of the loft Mother Nettle had stopped pretending to mend the nesting box.

Ilse understood that she had been wrong about the size of the thing. She had thought she was looking at a crime, a matter for a Board. It was a crime laid on top of the world. Underneath Hallam’s violet lines there was the Zero Stone, and a million people who had never heard of the Morning Survey, and the centre of the map going soft.

Sorry got up off her arm and walked across the table and sat down in front of Coyle and looked up at him with its amber eyes, and after a moment the Registrar-Adjunct put out one ink-stained finger and stroked it between its too-big ears.

“I’ll help,” he said, to the ossel. “I expect I’ll be very bad at it.”


* * *


The Errata Society met in the Mess kitchen on Thaw 14 after the last scullion had gone up to bed, under the copper pans hanging like bells, with Teo at the board making onion tart because, he said, he could not think about the end of the world without something in the oven. Kasimir sat at the end of the scrubbed table in his good coat as though he were sitting a Patent examination. He had come in by the scullery door, which no one had told him about.

“Brask’s answered,” said Cass. One of Nettle’s grey-necks had come in at the window and walked the length of the table eating crumbs. Cass shook a curl of paper out of its message-tube, read it, snorted, and passed it to Ilse.

It was four lines in Brask’s cramped, sloping hand.

*Coyle’s right. I’d bet my other eye on it. Aurek would never do this for less than the world.*

*Win. Draw it so it can’t be ghosted. If you don’t know how, you have a week. Find out.*

*Don’t lean on each other so hard you fall over.*

*Burn this. B.*

It went round the table and into the range.

“A week,” said Teo. “A week and a day. I’ve counted. I know you knew that, I just like to say it.”

“Before we come to how,” said Kasimir, “I think we must speak of whether.”

They all looked at him. He sat very straight.

“I do not like where my thinking goes,” he said, “and I would rather say it here than under the Gate. If Coyle is right, the First Atlas is failing. Tessary holds a million people. Master Hallam has found a way to hold them, at a cost. The cost is unjust. It is also smaller than the alternative.” He laid his hands flat on the table. “I am not saying that we should let him. I am saying that if we stop him, we must be able to say what happens to Tessary afterward, and I cannot.”

The range ticked. Teo had stopped rolling pastry.

“It’s about three hundred,” Teo said. “Us. The whole College. Against a million. If you just do the sum—” He stopped. He looked at his fingertips on the pastry board, slate-grey against the flour. “I hate that I can do the sum. I want you to know I hate it. My mam in Lisk would be in the million, near enough. My little brothers.”

“Do it for Corrow, then,” said Cass.

She hadn’t raised her voice. Her face was quite still.

“Do it for Brine End. Do it for the lane at your place, Ilse, with the carter in the ditch. He’s been doing your sum on us for three years, Teo. Edge for the middle. The middle’s worth more, so the edge goes. My nan’s wagon had a charter. Paper-wall charter, treaty of 1190, signed and filed. He walked up to the Corrow stone polite as a parson and asked the way, and next night the ground let go under the children. And there’s always a middle. That’s the thing about middles. You hold Tessary ten years by eating three hundred of us, and what do you eat in year eleven?”

Nobody answered.

“It don’t stop,” said Cass. “It only gets nearer.”

Kasimir inclined his head to her, gravely, as he did to Calder when she cited a precedent he had not known.

“Yes,” he said. “I think that is the answer to the argument. I wanted to hear someone make it who was not me.”

Ilse had been silent through all of it. She thought of Hallam on the Antechamber steps, grey in the face, patting Coyle’s hand. She thought of him at forty-four with pink hands, sitting by Brask’s bed every day for six weeks. She thought of Hen.

“He’s right that she needs holding,” she said. “Tessary. I believe Coyle. I believe Brask. The centre’s coming soft, and if nobody does anything it’ll come apart, and that’s a million people waking up to find their street’s wandered off.” She looked down the table at each of them. “Then we find another way to hold her. We don’t pay for it with Hen.”

“What other way?” said Kasimir, not unkindly.

“I don’t know.”

“That,” said Kasimir, “is a very honest answer and a very poor plan.”

“People have done more in a week and a day,” said Teo. “Not many. But some.”

So they made a plan, with the smell of onions and butter coming up out of the range, and Kasimir wrote it in his notebook of errors because he said that was where it belonged. It had three parts.

One. They had to win the Long Measure, so that their route charter was the one presented under the Gate and the one the Equinox Charter must be drawn from, in front of everyone.

Two. Their charter had to be drawn so that a ghost couldn’t take beneath it. Ilse knew a dozen ways to put a ghost under a mark and none to keep one out, except the off-register skew, and a skew on a whole charter would be a lie the size of the Drift Wing. The Wing would wander on it like the Lost Wing of 902. A ghost was patient. It only needed a line to lie under.

Three. On the morning, while the College stood under the Gate, Cass and Kasimir would go into Hallam’s study with Coyle and take the Conduit out of its press: Cass because she could move unseen, Kasimir because he could say *I am the Chancellor’s son* in a voice that would make a proctor think twice.

“Then there’s the other thing,” said Kasimir. He turned back a page. “The articles of the Long Measure. A party is four. It says so in the fourth article. Miss Solberg is in the infirmary.”

“So we’re three,” said Cass. “So what?”

“A party of three may compete under the short-party penalty. One hour added to its time.” Kasimir looked up. “I would join you if I could. If I leave Orla and Piety and Absalom, I will have bought your hour with theirs. And Absalom cannot read a sightvane without help.”

“You’re a terrible snob,” said Cass.

“I am an accurate snob.”

“An hour,” said Ilse. She did the sum without wanting to, and it was not a good sum. Last year’s winning party had come home in a little under twenty hours, and Kasimir’s party would expect to beat that, because Kasimir was Kasimir. “We’d have to beat everyone else by an hour and then some.”

“Then we beat everyone by an hour and then some,” said Teo, and shut the oven door.


* * *


On Thaw 18 she took Hen a twist of salt caramels, because Hen had once eaten nine in an evening and claimed it was medicinal. Hen sat up in the third bed by the infirmary window with her grey hands on the coverlet, her hair brushed too flat by someone who was not Hen. She said, “Thank you,” and put the caramels on the side table unopened.

Ilse told her they would run the Long Measure as three, because the articles said four and Hen was a lazy cow lying about in bed. Two months ago Hen would have thrown a pillow at her. Now she looked at the window for a while.

“Three is a bad number for a chain,” she said. “Someone has to hold both ends.”

“I know.”

“Teo will want to hold both ends. Don’t let him. He’ll pull too hard.” Hen’s flat voice didn’t change at all. “Cass should lead. Cass can feel which way it goes.”

Ilse took Hen’s grey hand, which was cool as a stone in shade, and put a caramel in it and folded the fingers over. At the door she looked back. Hen was looking down at her own closed fist, as though she had found a thing in it she couldn’t remember asking for.


* * *


The Lantern Count began at Evening Bell on Thaw 20, the eve of the Long Measure, with the whole of the College standing in the Old Court in the cold holding unlit lanterns.

Three hundred people stood on the honey-coloured flags in coats and scarves: masters in gowns, proctors with staves, Halloway with all three watches out at once, Mother Nettle with a crow on each shoulder. The brass studs of the meridian line ran through the crowd, and people stood carefully on either side of it, so that there was a narrow empty lane down the court where nobody stood at all.

A proctor on a ladder lit a taper from the keystone lamp and handed it down to the Provost, and Steyne lit the first lantern and passed the flame to the masters. Hallam lit his own and turned and lit the next, which belonged to a frightened first-year from the Ammonite Hills, and said a word that made her laugh. Then the light went out through the crowd hand to hand until the Old Court was full of small flames. Ilse lit hers from Teo’s, and Teo lit his from Cass’s, and Cass had lit hers from Orla Devane, who had said “Don’t set your hair on fire, Tenter,” and been told “Don’t set your mouth on fire, Devane,” and both had looked pleased.

Across the court Hallam’s eyes found her over his spectacles. He lifted his gloved hand. Ilse lifted hers and smiled at him with her forger’s face, and he smiled back with his kind one, and then the line began to move and he was lost behind lanterns.

They went out under the Gate two by two, the Provost first, and onto the causeway into the First Range. As each pair passed under the keystone they began to count.

Teo had explained it at supper with gravy on his chin. The Wing from the Gate to the far end of the Third Range was one thousand three hundred and six paces at the founders’ stride, and everyone counted their own aloud, so that everyone was on a different number and all the numbers went down the Wing together.

“One,” said Ilse, under the keystone. “Two. Three.”

Behind her Teo said, “One,” and Cass, “One,” and ahead of her the Provost was already at forty, and the numbers rose around her like water in a lock.

The First Range was nearly stable, and the counting there was crisp. Then the causeway kinked into the Second Range, and the floor took on its faint give, like a plank laid over a ditch. Three hundred voices were counting now, each on its own step. It wasn’t a chant, because nobody was together. It was more like rain: every drop falling in its own place, and all of them together one steady sound that filled the corridors.

“Two hundred and twelve,” said Ilse. “Two hundred and thirteen.”

The level was steadier than she had ever felt it in the Wing.

She noticed it the way you notice that a pain has stopped. Every morning at First Bell the Long Gallery shifted under their chain and the bead in the levelglass swam, and they wrestled it back onto the sheet and signed. Tonight there wasn’t a drop of ink on the whole procession, and the Wing was quiet around them. It wasn’t still. It moved under the counting like a sleeping dog under a hand, settling, as though it liked being walked.

At the turn of the Wren Stair Ilse looked back from the window and saw the procession behind her: a line of small lights strung along the whole length of the Wing, back over the kink of the causeway to the Gate, where the keystone lamp burned over the last of the leagues still filing through. The line bent where the Wing bent. It swayed a little where the Wing swayed. It was like looking down from a hill on a road at night, a road made of nothing but people walking it.

“Five hundred and eight,” said Cass, behind her, and then, very softly, under the count, so that only Ilse could hear it, she began to hum.

It was the Corrow song, from the night the whole camp had sung their wagons to the firm place. *The road to Corrow goes by the high stone when the moon is new.* Cass was only humming the tune between her numbers, probably without knowing she did it.

“Five hundred and nine,” said Ilse, and missed a step, and had to catch it up.

Something was nagging at her, the forger’s itch at the back of the neck that told her when a sheet was wrong. But nothing here was wrong. That was the trouble. She looked at the lanterns going ahead of her into the Third Range, swaying with the floor, held by nothing but people counting their way along. She thought of Nan Ottery in the firelight with her pipe. *Paper says where a thing is. Song says where a thing goes.*

She thought of the Ladder, in the dark, with the room turning under them and Halloway’s delight. She couldn’t think why she had thought of the Ladder.

“Six hundred,” said Teo, at her shoulder, in his soft voice, and added, “I’m sorry, I know you know, I just like the round ones.”

The thought went, as names go when you reach for them too fast. Ilse let it. She counted. They went on under the glass dome of the Observatory, where the lanterns filled the curve of the dome with small gold reflections. A thousand. A thousand and one. At the far end of the Wing, where the last window looked out over the black Margin, the line turned back on itself, and now the counts going out and the counts coming home passed each other in the corridors, numbers rising and numbers falling, voices brushing past in the lamplight.

On the way back, somewhere in the Second Range, Kasimir passed her going the other way, counting in his clear exact voice, “Nine hundred and forty, nine hundred and forty-one.” His eyes met hers over his lantern. He didn’t break his count, and neither did she. But he lifted the lantern an inch as he went by, the way a man on a road at night lifts his light to someone coming the other way, to say that he has seen them, and that the road is clear.


## Chapter 28: The Long Measure

At a quarter to six on the morning of Thaw 21, forty-seven students stood under the Lantern Gate with chains on their shoulders, and Magistra Ines Calder read them the articles of the Long Measure as though each one had personally offended her.

“The course,” she said, “is the Tern Stack. Three leagues west by south-west. You will survey your route out and your route back by instrument, closing each leg. You will set and attest a station-pin at each closing, so that the route holds behind you for your return.” She paused, long enough for a gull to cross the arch. “At the Stack, one member of each party will re-attest the beacon cairn. The cairn has been re-attested every year since 211. I do not intend it to fail in mine.”

“Accuracy first, time second. Your charters will be judged at this Gate. The winning charter will be the basis of tomorrow’s Equinox Charter.” Calder lowered the paper and looked along the parties over the top of it. “Twelve parties. One under the short-party penalty, which adds an hour to its time, by the fourth article, as in *Penwarden’s Party v. the Proctors*, 1131. Miss Marrow.”

“Magistra.”

“You understand the penalty.”

“Yes, Magistra.”

“And you are content to run as three.”

“No, Magistra,” said Ilse. “But we’re running.”

Calder wrote something down, and Ilse would have given a good deal to know what.

The keystone lamp burned overhead and the sky behind the Drift Wing had gone the colour of a bruise healing. Ilse had the plane-table board strapped to her back and the levelglass on a cord round her neck, and inside her coat Sorry was a small warm weight that purred whenever she breathed in. It had refused to stay in the room. It had sat in the doorway and made a sound like a kettle until she gave in. Cass had the sightvane and the route-cords. Teo had the chain over his shoulder and a face the colour of porridge.

“I’m fine,” he said, before she could ask. “I had two breakfasts. I’m mainly fine.”

Kasimir’s party stood three places down: Orla yawning, Absalom Grice bouncing on his toes, Piety Mercer with her lips moving in prayer or arithmetic, and Kasimir, still and combed, counting the links of his chain by touch. He didn’t look at her. She hadn’t expected him to.

Then the masters came down the line to wish the parties well, and Hallam was among them.

He went from party to party with his gloved hands clasped behind him and a word for each. He told Absalom Grice to look before he leapt, and Absalom laughed far too loudly. He came to Ilse’s party last and looked at the three of them over his spectacles with real tenderness, and Ilse made her face into the face of a student being told a kind thing by a kind master.

“Three,” said Hallam. “Three brave Walkers and a penalty. Miss Solberg would be proud of you.” He laid a gloved hand on Teo’s arm, briefly, and then on Cass’s, and Cass stood it with only a slight tightening round her eyes. Then he turned to Ilse, and his voice dropped, so that it was for her alone.

“Come home safe, my dear.”

“I mean to, Master Hallam.”

He patted her shoulder and went back to stand with the Provost. Ilse had spent nine years learning to hear the second line under a sentence, and she listened now with everything she had, and could not tell whether there was one. He might have been wishing her well. He might have been telling her he knew. He might have been doing both at once, with his whole heart, and that was the possibility that frightened her most.

The Bell of Hours struck six. Calder said, “Go,” and forty-seven students went out under the lantern into the Margin.

As they came off the causeway onto the heath Ilse looked back once. On the low rise beyond the College wall, outside the grounds, where nobody could say she had trespassed, a tall figure stood against the grey sky with one hand on a staff. Brask didn’t wave. She lifted the Long Lie a foot off the ground and set it down again, once, the way she did in Fieldwork when someone had finally closed a traverse.


* * *


They did the Margin fast, because the Margin was theirs.

Cass led, because Hen had said so, and Teo had said “Of course Cass leads, Cass can feel it, I mostly feel my breakfast.” Cass went ahead with the sightvane and the forward end of the chain, reading the grain off the gorse and picking ground that would stay put long enough to measure. Teo held the back end and called the links. Ilse drafted at every station, and they ran each leg in the quick sequence they had learned on the Walk: chain, bearing, level, draft, close, attest the pin, go. A station-pin was an iron spike with a charter the size of a playing card wired to it, and when Ilse set her wren’s foot to each one the ground round the spike went firm for a yard and she felt a small, distinct tug in her signing hand. Twelve pins by the edge of the Margin. Twelve small tugs.

By nine they were into the near Drift, and the land began to have opinions.

The first was the standing rain. It stood on a long low hill like a grey curtain hung from nothing, falling on the hill and only on the hill, out of an otherwise clean sky, its edge as sharp as a wall. Cass put her hand into it up to the wrist and it came out dripping while her elbow stayed dry.

“It’s been raining there since Candlemonth,” she said. “Uncle Pell says. There’s a frog in there the size of a dog.”

“There is not,” said Teo.

“Size of a small dog.”

They chained through it, because going round would have cost a mile. Inside, the light was green and the noise was everything, and Ilse drafted under her coat with Sorry complaining against her ribs. She closed the leg at fourteen chains and sixty links with an error of two links, wrote the error down, and was absurdly proud of it. They came out into sunshine with water running out of their boots, and behind them the rain went on falling on its hill as if they had never been there.

The second was the skein.

They came over a rise and saw it. A stream came down off the downs in an ordinary bed of stones, and at the bottom of the slope, instead of going on, it rose. It went up off the ground in a smooth glassy arch, twenty feet high at the crown, and came down again forty yards farther on, and ran away across the heath in its bed as though nothing had happened. Under the arch the grass was dry. Through the water, as through a window, the sky showed rippled and green.

In the arch there were eels. They were long and silver and thin as a boot-lace, and they hung in the moving water with their heads into the current, and every now and then one of them slipped out of the top of the arch into the open air and swam a yard of nothing, flicking, before it fell back in.

Nobody said anything for a while. Ilse found that her pencil was shaking very slightly above the board, and not with fear.

“Skein-eels,” said Cass softly. “Nan says if one touches you, you’ll never drown.”

“Is that true?” said Teo.

“No idea. Nobody I know ever got touched.”

They measured the skein, because Ilse could not bear to leave it off the sheet. She took the height of the arch from two stations and noted beside it, *the stream rises here, 20 ft at crown, and falls again at 2 chains; this is the bed of a stream, not its course.* She looked at that for a moment afterward. She didn’t know why it pleased her so much.

The third was the fold.

They walked into it in the early afternoon without seeing it. One step the air was the thin cold of the Thaw; the next it was warm as a bread-oven, so suddenly that Teo yelped. The fold lay in a hollow of heath a hundred yards across, a pocket of summer that had got lost, and in it the gorse was in flower and the air was full of moths, small and pale and powdery, turning in slow drifts over the yellow gorse the way snow turns in a draught. When Ilse breathed out, the nearest moths tumbled away on her breath and came back.

“Fold-moths,” said Cass. “They don’t leave. They’re born in it and they die in it. Whole lives in one warm room.”

“That’s very sad,” said Teo.

“Is it?” said Cass, and looked round at the flowers and the moths and the gold light, and didn’t seem to think so.


* * *


The hollowings found them at the glasswrack.

The glasswrack fields lay in a long shallow valley a league short of the sea, where the wind came straight in off the water. The glasswrack grew in spirals out of pale sand, crystal stems twisted like barley-sugar, and in the wind they rang like a hundred wet fingers drawn round the rims of a hundred glasses, so that the whole valley hummed a chord that never resolved. It was very beautiful and very hard to think in.

She was setting the fortieth pin when the level dropped out of her ears.

It went the way it had gone at Brine End: not fading but cut, a held note stopped mid-breath. She looked up. Across the valley, beyond the ringing spirals, the air had gone soft in a patch the size of a cottage. It wouldn’t come into focus. The glasswrack under it had stopped ringing. The patch slid toward them, as unhurried as a cloud shadow, and behind it the sand slumped in a long shallow furrow.

Beyond it there was another, and another, farther back along the routes the other parties had taken. Ilse could see their station-pins from here, iron points catching the low sun, and around every pin the air was blurring.

“Road and rain,” said Cass. “Look at them all.”

“The pins,” said Ilse. “Hundreds of fresh pins all down the course. All new attestations. That’s what draws them.”

“I’ve never seen so many,” said Teo. His voice had gone small. “Nobody’s ever seen so many. Have they? Marrow. Have they?”

Away to the north someone was shouting. One of the Ammonite parties was running back the way it had come, and an empty board lay on the sand behind them with a blur sliding over it.

The nearest hollowing was a hundred yards off and closing.

“Cass,” said Ilse.

“I see it.” Cass had her head up like a dog scenting. “Ground there,” she said, and pointed with her chin. To their left the floor of the valley was moving, a long tongue of loose pale sand sliding gently seaward with the glasswrack riding on it, ringing as it went. “Get on the moving bit. They don’t like it. Nan says.”

“Nan says a lot of things,” said Teo.
