“And she’s alive at seventy-nine, ain’t she? Move.”

They picked up the chain and the board and went, half-running, onto the sliding sand. It was like stepping onto a barge. The ground went out from under their feet and came back, carrying them, and Ilse staggered and caught Teo’s arm and felt the sand flow seaward under her boots at about the pace of a slow walk. The glasswrack spirals went by them ringing.

Behind them the hollowing reached the edge of the moving ground and stopped.

It drifted a little along the edge of the slide and back, like a moth against a lit window, unwilling. The sand under it was still. The sand under her was moving. It would not cross. After a while it turned and slid back toward the line of pins, where the ground stood fixed and freshly signed.

Ilse watched it go with her heart going hard. A thought came and went too fast to catch, like the one in the Lantern Count. It had to do with the skein. *This is the bed of a stream, not its course.* She reached for it and it went.

“Marrow,” said Teo. “Marrow, we’re going quite fast toward the sea.”

They got off the slide at the far end of the valley, and Ilse took a bearing back to the fortieth pin and closed the leg across the moving ground with a note that said *bed moving seaward, about 1 chain in 3 minutes*, and they went on.


* * *


They came to the Tern Stack at nightfall, first of the twelve.

It stood in the shallows a furlong off the last of the land, where the Drift ran out into the sea. They went out along a spit of shingle in the last of the light with the tide hissing on both sides. Close to, the Stack was a black tower of rock a hundred and forty feet high, streaked white by a thousand years of birds and cut with ledges where the terns slept. On its flat top, against the last green of the western sky, Ilse could just make out the beacon cairn: a stack of dressed stone with an iron fire-basket on it, unlit.

Teo stood at the foot of it and looked up and didn’t say anything at all.

A notice was nailed to a post at the foot of the climb under oiled cloth. Ilse read it by lantern. *The cap of the Stack was fractured in the Frost storms. Part of the summit is hollow. Climb with care. Do not stand on the seaward side of the cairn.*

“Hollow,” said Cass. “Lovely. Which part?”

“Doesn’t say.”

“’Course it doesn’t.” Cass looked up at the Stack and flexed her hands. “Right. I’ll go. I’m lightest after you, and you’re the one with the board, and I can read the rock—”

“You can’t sound it,” said Teo.

They both looked at him.

His face in the lantern light was grey and wet and absolutely set. He had put down the chain. His hands were shaking, and he put them in his armpits to stop them.

“The cap’s cracked,” he said. “Somebody has to know where it’s hollow before they put their weight on it. You can read which way the rock leans, Cass, but you can’t hear what’s inside it. I can. That’s my whole—that’s the only thing I’m actually for.” He swallowed. “It’s a hundred and forty feet. I worked it out on the way. Call it a hundred and eighty handholds, if they’re a good foot apart, which they won’t be, because nothing ever is. I’m going to count them. Don’t talk to me unless it’s about rock.”

“Teo,” said Ilse.

“I know.” He looked at her. “I know, Ilse. I’m going anyway.”

He had never called her Ilse when he was frightened. He called her Marrow when he was frightened. She understood that he had gone past frightened into somewhere else, and she didn’t argue.

There was a horn of rock at the foot of the Stack, worn smooth by a century of ropes, and Ilse took two turns of their line round it and braced the line across her back, so that if Teo fell the rock would take his weight and not her. Cass tied the other end round Teo’s chest in a Tenting knot with a twist in it. Teo put the levelglass and a folded folio in his coat and a hooded lantern on his belt, and Sorry put its head out of Ilse’s coat and made a small sound at him.

“Thank you,” said Teo to the ossel, very seriously, and put his hands on the rock.

“One,” he said.

He went up slowly. That was the first thing. He didn’t rush, and he didn’t freeze; he went up one hold at a time, and at every hold he stopped and knocked on the rock above him with his knuckles, the way he had knocked on the walls of the Ladder in the dark, and listened. Then he said the number and moved.

“Eleven. Twelve.”

“Lean left,” Cass called up. She was standing back on the shingle with her head tipped right back, reading the face of the Stack the way she read a heath. “Rock’s sound there. The grain goes in, not out. Left, Teo. That’s it.”

“Thirteen.”

Ilse paid out the line. It went up through her hands rough and wet and alive, and she felt every one of his movements down it, the pauses while he knocked and listened. The light went. The lantern on his belt became a single point of gold moving up the black rock, and the stars came out above it, and the tide came in round the spit with a long hush.

“Forty-four. Forty-five.”

“Big ledge above you. Terns on it. Don’t mind them.”

“I mind them a great deal.” A burst of furious wings and screaming above, white shapes boiling out into the dark. Teo’s light stopped. The line went rigid in Ilse’s hands, and her heart stopped with it. Then: “Forty-six.”

It went on longer than Ilse would have believed a thing could go on. Her arms burned and went numb and burned again, and her hands bled where the line ran. She noticed only the gold point of the lantern and the numbers coming down out of the dark. Ninety. A hundred and two. Teo’s voice had gone hoarse and very steady, the voice of a man reciting a lesson he knew by heart. Once he stopped for so long that Cass cupped her hands and shouted “Teo!” and the answer came back, perfectly calm, “I’m sounding. Shut up,” and Cass laughed out loud with tears on her face.

“A hundred and sixty. A hundred and sixty-one.”

“You’re nearly at the cap. Go right of the crack. Right, Teo, the grain goes back on itself left of it—”

“I can hear it. It’s hollow on the left. Like a pie with no filling.” A pause. “Sorry. That wasn’t rock.”

“A hundred and seventy-eight,” said Teo, from very far above them. “A hundred and seventy-nine.”

Then the gold point went over the edge and was gone, and the line went slack in Ilse’s hands, and from the top of the Tern Stack, a hundred and forty feet up in the dark, came a sound that it took her a moment to recognise. It was Teo, laughing.

They waited. Up there he would be sounding the cap, measuring the cairn by lantern, drafting with his big careful hands, setting the stones of the beacon in their true places on the page. Then he would sign.

She felt it when he did. Everyone on the spit must have felt it, because Cass made a small noise. It was like a bell struck once, far off: the level rang behind her ears, clear and certain, and the black Stack above them steadied as though it had taken a breath and decided to keep it.

Then the beacon lit.

It went up all at once, a bloom of orange in the iron basket, and the whole Tern Stack leapt out of the dark: the white streaks on its sides, the ledges, the terns wheeling round it in the firelight like sparks. Far out on the black water a road of orange light ran away toward the west. Ilse stood with the line in her bleeding hands and looked at it, and it was the most beautiful thing she had ever seen, and her friend had made it.

“He did it,” Cass was saying, over and over, “he bloody did it, the great soft lump, he did it,” and Sorry had climbed out of Ilse’s coat entirely and was sitting on her shoulder with its ears straight up, staring at the fire with its amber eyes.

Coming down took him an hour. He counted that too.

When his boots touched the shingle he stood there for a while with his forehead against the rock. Then he turned round, and he was grinning in a way Ilse had never seen him grin, the whole of his round face gone loose with it, and he held out the folded charter to her with both hands, the way you hold out something hot.

“Two hundred and three,” he said. “Up. I lied about the hundred and eighty. Two hundred and three handholds, and I sounded every one, and the cap’s hollow on the left like I said, and I signed it on the right. My hands are shaking. I don’t mind. I don’t mind at all.” He looked up at the fire. “I don’t like heights. I want to be clear. I still don’t like them.”

Cass flung her arms round his neck and nearly pulled him over. Ilse took the charter, and checked it by lantern, because she was a surveyor, and it was perfect. Then she put her bleeding hand on his arm and left it there, and he put his big hand over it, and neither of them said anything.


* * *


Kasimir’s party came down the spit forty minutes later, at a run, with their lanterns swinging.

They had seen the beacon. Absalom was red and blown, Orla wry, Piety white and furious. Kasimir stopped at the foot of the Stack and looked up at the fire, and then at the three of them standing under it.

He looked at Ilse. Then he inclined his head to her, very slightly, the way one surveyor acknowledges another across a boundary they will both sign.

Ilse nodded back.

“Hey-ho, Reyne,” said Cass. “Mind the left side of the cap. It’s hollow. Teo heard it.”

Something moved in Kasimir’s face. “Thank you,” he said, to Teo, not to Cass, and meant it, and turned to rig his own line.

They couldn’t wait; they had an hour to make up. Teo, who had climbed the Tern Stack and could apparently now do anything, picked up the pack without being asked, and they went back along the shingle with the beacon throwing their three long shadows out ahead of them over the glasswrack.

At the landward end of the spit Ilse put her eye to the sightvane to take the back-bearing on their last pin, and it wasn’t there. The bearing ran out to empty sand. She swung the vane and found the pin, still upright, catching the beacon light on its iron head, eleven yards south of where she had set it, and beyond it the long ridge they had crossed that afternoon was sliding, the whole black length of it, as patient as a tide. Cass had already seen. She hitched the route-cords at her belt, said, “Ground’s going home without us,” and began to walk faster.


## Chapter 29: The Open Map

By midnight they had re-chained the same leg three times, and it had moved three times, and Ilse had stopped being angry with it and started taking notes.

The ridge they had crossed that afternoon was going somewhere: south and a little west, toward the sea, at a pace she could see if she watched a single tuft of heather against a single star. Their pins went with it. Each one she reached she found carried off its mark and straining in the sand, the spike leaning, the card fluttering, the ground round it held hard while everything else flowed past. Each one pulled at her signing hand like a child pulling at a sleeve.

And the hollowings were everywhere.

They hung over the ridge in the dark, twenty or more, patches of blur that put out the stars behind them, clustered round the strained pins the way wasps cluster on a split plum. The level in Ilse’s ears kept going out and coming back like a lamp guttering.

“Leave the pins,” said Cass. “They’re eating ’em. We set ’em, and they come.”

“If we leave them we can’t close the route.”

“If we stay with ’em we can’t close nothing ever again.”

Ilse looked at her board, where the return leg wandered across the sheet in three attempts like a drunk man’s signature, and thought of the penalty hour, and of Hallam under the Gate with his own folio ready.

Then Teo said, “Listen.”

From beyond the ridge to the north came a girl’s voice, screaming, and under it a man’s shout cut short. Lanterns showed there, low down in a hollow between this ridge and the next. They weren’t moving forward. They were swinging, the way lanterns swing when the people holding them are trying to keep their feet.

“That’s Reyne’s lot,” said Cass. “They passed us an hour back on the straight line. I saw his light.”

Ilse could see the whole of it laid out like a sheet. If they went north, the penalty hour became three, and their charter came in late and broken, and Hallam drew his folio from someone else’s map. If they went on, perhaps they won.

Teo had already picked up the chain.

“Road and rain,” said Cass. “’Course we’re going. What are you stood there for?”


* * *


The fold was a long trough between two ridges, and both ridges were moving, and not the same way.

They came down into it sliding. The near ridge went south and the far one north, and the ground between them twisted like a wrung cloth, heaving one way and then back. In the middle of it, on a patch of turf coming apart at the edges, Kasimir’s party were holding on.

Orla Devane had a lantern in each hand and her feet braced wide. Absalom Grice lay on his back beside her with his face the colour of tallow and his left leg wrong below the knee, bent in a way legs don’t bend. Piety Mercer was on her knees beside him, and it was Piety who had been screaming, and she was screaming still.

“—*your* fault! Your Margin tricks, your filthy wagon songs, you’ve sung the ground loose under us, you—”

“Shut up, Piety,” said Orla, without heat. “Hello, Marrow. We seem to be in a spot.”

Kasimir knelt at the centre of the turf with his board across his knees and a lantern on the ground beside him. He was drafting. As Ilse slid down beside him he finished a sheet, set his mark to it, and pressed it flat on the turf with his palm, and she felt it take. The ground under them stiffened, held, for the space of two breaths.

Then it tore. She heard it, a sound like a wet sheet ripping, and the turf lurched, and Kasimir made a small sound through his teeth and doubled over his right hand.

“Again,” he said. His voice was shredded. “It is the latitude. It is enormous here. I chart it to its present position and it is not there by the time I have signed. I have done it nine times.”

“Stop,” said Ilse.

“If I stop, the ground goes out from under Absalom.” He was already ruling a fresh sheet. His right hand shook so badly that the rule skidded. In the lantern light the skin across his knuckles had gone a strange dull colour, like pewter under dust. “It is very simple, Miss Marrow. I must be faster than it.”

Above the fold the hollowings were gathering.

They came over the lip of the near ridge, one and then three and then more, sliding down toward the turf where Kasimir’s nine torn charters lay. Teo said, “Oh, no,” very quietly. Cass stood and opened her mouth to sing, the low Tenting song Brask had sung at Brine End, and her voice came out cracked and stopped. She tried again. Nothing came but breath. Ilse had seldom seen Cass afraid.

The nearest hollowing slid down the slope toward Absalom’s broken leg.

Cass grabbed Ilse’s coat. She tore it open, and Sorry tumbled out into the lantern light, small and grey, ears flat. And Cass Tenter, who believed it was wrong to describe a living thing into whatever you wanted, pointed at it and roared.

“That’s a *dog!* That’s the biggest, ugliest, fiercest dog that ever walked the Margin! That’s a dog with teeth like a gin-trap and a bark like the Bell of Hours, and it eats hollowings, and it *hates* them!”

Sorry flickered, and swelled, and stood up.

It was the size of a wolfhound and it seemed much bigger. Its coat stood out in a grey ruff like a thornbush and its jaws were wide and wet and full of teeth, and in its great shaggy head its eyes were still Sorry’s, pale amber and appalled. It stood over Absalom on legs like fenceposts. Then it lowered its head, and it barked, a sound that went off the walls of the fold like a shot, and it charged.

It hurled itself across the shifting sand at the hollowing, barking and barking, and the hollowing pulled back from the moving, roaring thing as the other had from the sliding sand. It slid away up the slope, and the dog went after it to the lip of the ridge and stood there bellowing at the dark.

Then it came back down to Ilse and stopped in front of her, and it shrank like a bellows emptying. In three heartbeats it was a small grey shape of no particular kind, all ears and eyes, curled in the sand. Ilse picked it up. It was asleep before her hands closed round it, breathing fast and shallow, and it was shivering.

“Sorry,” said Cass, hoarse. “Sorry, Sorry, I know. I know you hate it.”

Ilse buttoned the ossel back inside her coat. On the ridge there were more hollowings than before.


* * *


Kasimir set his mark to the tenth charter, and it held for two breaths, and it tore.

This time Ilse watched the ground rather than the sheet. She saw the turf hold, rigid, while the trough heaved around it, the sand piling against the held patch like water against a sluice gate. She saw the tear begin where the strain was greatest, and the ground break free and swing on, south, as it had been going to all along. And she saw the hollowings on the ridge lean toward the tear, all at once, like flowers toward a window.

His charter said the turf lay *here*. It was a beautiful charter. It was exact to a hair.

And it was true for perhaps a second. A second after he signed, the turf was a foot south of where the charter said, and then two feet, and then swinging back north past the place and beyond it, and for all that time the charter was wrong. Not much wrong. Wrong the way a stopped clock is wrong: a flawless record of an instant, held up against a thing that was never still.

*A record’s hold is proportional to its accuracy and completeness.* Hallam had taught them that in the Antechamber with his gloved hands on the lectern. The Law of Fidelity. Kasimir’s charters were accurate, but they weren’t complete. They left out the one thing about this ground that was most true about it. They left out that it moved.

The land pulled against them because they were wrong about it. The burden ran up Kasimir’s hand because they were wrong. The hollowings came to the fighting the way flies come to a wound.

Ilse knelt in the heaving sand and felt the whole of it come together in her head, piece by piece, like the pieces of a traverse closing.

The Ladder. The room turning under them in the dark, the moment she had stopped fighting the drift and measured its rate instead, and Halloway’s joy over the rate annotation. They had recorded the drift instead of correcting it, and the room had held.

The Moot. The stone grinding three inches toward her mark, because she had charted it where the sand wanted it to lie. *Fidelity to the ground, Mr. Reyne, not to the page.*

Nan Ottery in the firelight at Corrow, her pipe going. *Paper says where a thing is. Song says where a thing goes.* And Cass humming the Corrow song in the Lantern Count: *the road goes by the high stone when the moon is new, and the low ford when it’s old.* That wasn’t superstition. That was a survey of a road that moved, true at the new moon and true at the old, and the Tenting had walked by it for generations and never been lost.

Brask at her cold hearth. *The map is a promise, not a cage. Know which one you’re making.*

And her own pencil that afternoon, beside the skein: *this is the bed of a stream, not its course.* Beside the slide in the glasswrack: *bed moving seaward, about one chain in three minutes.* She had drawn two open things already today without knowing, and they were the two best lines on her sheet.

On the Walk, half a year ago, she had come to a stream bank that wouldn’t close by fourteen links, and she had corrected it, because she was a forger and forgers made things neat. Brask had laid a fingernail on that bank. *Forgers correct. Surveyors record.*

What was the truest thing you could say about this ground? That it lay here? It didn’t. That it lay nowhere? It did; it lay *somewhere*, and the somewhere had edges. It swung, and it swung between limits. Its latitude was enormous, but it wasn’t infinite. Latitude was only that: the range of states the land could be in.

So you didn’t chart a state. You charted the range.

You said: *this ground lies between here and here, and moves thus.* And every word of it would be true, at every instant, for as long as the ground kept doing what it was doing. A record like that would have nothing in it to be wrong. It would be more faithful than any point charter ever drawn, Kasimir’s included, because it was complete. By the Law of Fidelity it would hold. By the Law of Precedence it would beat any narrower record laid against it. And by the Law of Burden it would cost almost nothing to carry, because the land would have nothing to pull against. You wouldn’t be holding it still. You would only be telling the truth about how it moved.

It wasn’t a new law. It wasn’t a trick. It was a law she had been taught in her first month, followed all the way to the end.

“Kasimir,” she said.

He looked up from an eleventh sheet, half ruled.

“Stop charting where it is,” said Ilse. “Chart where it goes.”


* * *


She was not good at explaining. She was good at drawing, and she drew it for him on the back of his ruined tenth charter: two lines for the limits of the swing, an arrow between them, a number beside the arrow. She watched him understand it, and hate it, and go on looking anyway.

“That is not a charter,” he said. “A charter says where a thing is.”

“It does say where a thing is. It says all of where.”

“It is a—” He stopped. She could see him searching for the legal word and not finding one. “It has no fixed line in it.”

“There’s no fixed line in the ground.” She put her finger on the swinging limits. “I can feel the give. I can feel how far it’ll go before it can’t, the same way I felt how far a hedge would go. I can’t draw it fine enough. Not here, not to a hair. You can.” She looked at his hand, at the pewter dulling his knuckles. “You said it on the roof. If a record is exactly true, the land doesn’t pull against it. So make it exactly true.”

He looked at her in the swinging light. Then he wiped his rule on his sleeve and turned to a clean sheet.

They did it together, all seven of them, in the bottom of the moving fold, with the hollowings hanging on the ridge above.

Teo lay flat on the sand with his ear pressed to it, sounding, and counted the swing aloud, the way he had counted the Tern Stack. “Forty-one,” he said, “from the north limit to the south. Forty-one, and back in forty-three, it’s not even, it’s slower going north, it’s pulling uphill.” Cass knelt with her palms flat and read the grain, which way the swing leaned and where it bent. “Not straight,” she said. “It curves. Like a gate on a hinge. Hinge is east.” Ilse shut her eyes and felt for the give, the way she had felt for it in Widow Tull’s orchard with her palm on a wet sheet, and found the place at each end of the swing where the ground would go no farther, where it came up against its own latitude like a gate against its post. She put Orla there, at the north limit, with a lantern held high, and after a moment’s hesitation she put Piety at the south.

“Me?” said Piety. Her face was blotched with crying.

“You’ve got a lantern and two legs,” said Ilse. “Stand where I tell you and don’t move unless the ground does.”

Piety stood.

Kasimir took the limits. He sighted on Orla’s lantern and on Piety’s at every swing, again and again until the numbers stopped changing, and he drew the way she had always envied, without a wasted line. He put down the north bound and the south bound and the hinge, and the curve of the swing between them, and in the margin, in his beautiful exact hand, the rate: *forty-one seconds north to south, forty-three south to north, about a hinge bearing east by north.* It wasn’t a picture of a place. It was a picture of a movement, the first she had ever seen drawn true.

He held out the pencil to her.

They signed it one after the other on the open sheet, her wren’s foot and his mark beside it, and pressed it down on the turf between them, and Ilse felt it take.

It wasn’t like any attestation she had ever made. There was no tug in her signing hand. There was no pull. It was like setting down something heavy on a table built to hold it. The level in her ears, which had been guttering all night, came back clear and low and steady.

And the ground stopped fighting.

It didn’t stop moving. It went on swinging, north and south, on its long hinge. But the heave went out of it. The wrenching twist that had been tearing the fold apart eased, and the turf under Absalom stopped lurching and began, instead, to sway. Slow and gentle, forty-one seconds and forty-three, like a boat riding at anchor on a calm tide. Ilse felt it under her knees, and it didn’t frighten her at all.

On the ridge, the hollowings drifted.

They hung where they were for a while, as though puzzled. Then one by one they slid away over the lip of the ridge and out into the dark. There was nothing for them here any more, nothing fixed and fighting. Only ground going where it went.

The fold was very quiet. Absalom Grice said faintly, “Is it over? Is that it?”

And Cass began to laugh. She sat down in the sand and laughed with her head back and her braids swinging, laughed until she wept, and after a moment Teo was laughing too, and Orla, and even Kasimir, holding his ruined right hand in his left, put his head down on his knees and shook.


* * *


Afterward, while Teo splinted Absalom’s leg with Kasimir’s spare rule and two straps, Ilse sat with the open charter on her board and looked at it by lantern.

She looked at it the way she would have in hedge country: as a forger.

She had ghosted Quince’s mark under her own thirty-odd times, and she knew in her fingers what a ghost needed. Registration. Line for line, a ghost had to lie in perfect register with what it ran beneath, or it slid off and took nothing. That was why her off-register skew had spared Teo every sliver since Frost. And Hallam’s guides had to keep register at both ends: under the drawing on the sheet, and through the Conduit with the Zero Sheets, the most fixed lines in the world, twelve hundred years unmoved. A ghost carried weight from one *here* to another *here*.

She looked at the charter. The north bound. The south bound. The hinge, the curve, the rate.

There was no *here* on it. Every line was a limit or a motion: the bounds were not where the ground lay but where it could not go, and the hold lived in the swing between them. A trace laid beneath could match the ink to a hair and still be in register with nothing, because the record’s truth was in the range, and no Zero line in the world described a range. You couldn’t trace a movement in blind ink.

“A ghost needs the lines to sit still,” she said aloud.

Kasimir was sitting beside her with his hand wrapped in his scarf. He turned his head.

“Say that again.”

She said it again, and watched him take it apart and check every joint of it the way he checked a traverse, and watched it hold.

“Then if the Equinox Charter were drawn open,” he said. “If the Wing were drawn open—”

“Then there’s nothing for him to put under it.”

He looked at her. In the lamplight his face was grey with exhaustion and pain, and there was a look in it she had never seen there before, and it took her a moment to name it. He was amazed.

“We will have to draw the Wing,” he said. “On the way in. The causeway, all of it, open.”

“Yes.”

“Before dawn.”

“Yes.”

Kasimir looked at the east, where there was no sign yet of the sun.

“Then we had better be going,” he said.


* * *


They came under the Lantern Gate at twenty minutes past five on the morning of Thaw 22, seven of them, with Absalom Grice on a litter made from two plane-table boards and a chain.

They had drawn the whole return open: the ridge and the fold and the glasswrack slide, the Margin heath, and last of all the Drift Wing itself, chained in along the causeway in the dark before First Bell, Teo counting, Cass reading the floor, Ilse feeling for the give of every corridor, Kasimir drawing the bounds. The Wren Stair turned, as it always had, and now the sheet said so, and how far, and how fast. Seven marks stood at the foot of the route, Absalom’s shaky and Piety’s small and very neat. The Wing sheet they left unsigned, as every party did: by the articles the Wing was the College’s to attest, under the Gate, at the Equinox. And at the Gate end of the causeway Ilse had asked Kasimir to draw the Wing twice. He had looked at her, and drawn it twice, and not asked why.

Theirs was the only party to finish the course. The Ammonite girls had come in at sunset. Five parties had come back in the night without their routes, and four were still out, with proctors gone after them.

The judges stood under the arch: Calder, Halloway, the Provost in a dressing gown under his gown, and Hallam.

Calder read the charter by the keystone lamp, sheet by sheet, and gave it to Halloway, and Halloway made a small strangled noise.

“Oh,” he said. “Oh, it’s—they’ve—Magistra, it’s the rate annotation. It’s the *whole* thing. The b-bounds and the rate, and it closes, it closes on every leg, I don’t—the error’s *recorded*, it’s all—” He looked up at Ilse with his three watches swinging and his eyes wet. “Marrow, it’s *beautiful*.”

“It is irregular,” said Provost Steyne smoothly. “Seven is not four. And this is not, so far as I understand it, a charter. A charter fixes a place.”

“A charter,” said Calder, “records what is.”

The arch was very quiet. Above them, carved in the stone, the words ran from one side to the other. WE RECORD WHAT IS.

Calder let the pause sit.

“I have examined this record,” she said. “I find it true. I have felt it held, and it holds. I find it holds more lightly and more firmly than any route charter I have seen in eleven years of this course. The fourth article governs parties, Provost. It does not govern truth. By the Law of Fidelity, which is older than any article, this charter stands.” She rolled the sheets. “It will be the basis of the Equinox Charter. Master Hallam will prepare the folio from it. Thank you, Miss Marrow. Mr. Reyne. Have someone set that young man’s leg.”

She held the roll out. Hallam took it.

He unrolled it under the lamp with his gloved hands and looked at it a long time. Ilse watched him read the bounds of the fold, and the hinge, and the rate. She watched him turn to the last sheet, the Drift Wing drawn open from the Gate to the far end of the Third Range, and read the Wren Stair, turning, and how far, and how fast. His spectacles caught the light. His gloved forefinger moved along the line of a bound, the way a man feels along a wall in the dark for a door he knows is there.

There was no door. She saw him find that out.

He looked up, then, over his spectacles, and found her in the lantern light, and for a moment he was not kind or anything else, only an old man who had been the best doubter Brask had ever known, looking at a thing he had not thought of.

Then he smiled, gently, and rolled the charter up again with great care.

“Remarkable,” said Aurek Hallam. “Truly remarkable, my dear. Get some sleep. We have a long morning ahead of us.”


## Chapter 30: Under the Lantern Gate

The second sheet of the Drift Wing had been inked on the causeway in the dark, and at half past five, in the Hall of Instruments, Kasimir still would not let her roll it until the ink had stopped shining.

“It’s dry,” she said.

“It is dry on the surface. If you roll it now the Second Range will print itself onto the back of the Observatory and we shall have charted a building inside out.” He held the lamp low and sighted along the sheet with his cheek nearly on the table. “There. Now it is dry.”

It was the ugliest thing either of them had ever made. A charter of the old kind was all single lines, ruled and certain. This one had none. Every wall of the Drift Wing was drawn twice, its near limit and its far, with the space between hatched in Kasimir’s fine diagonal, so that the whole Wing looked like a building seen through rain. Every length carried two figures. *Long Gallery, 1 ch. 56 l. to 1 ch. 65 l.* *Wren Stair, turning on its foot through 4 degrees either hand.* *Third Range, swinging on the causeway through 2 ch. 6 l., westward on the ebb, home on the flood.* They had chained the Wing going out and again coming home, and between the two lay the truth: not where the Wing was, but how far it went. The limits were his, written to a hair. The sense of where to set them was hers. The first sheet was in Hallam’s gloved hands. This one was theirs.

She could have signed it then, in the empty Hall, and the Wing would have swung open on its causeway before anyone was out of bed. Kasimir had said so on the walk in, and she had said no, and he had thought about it and agreed. An open Wing at half past five would save nobody. The Morning Survey sheets in the presses would still carry their knots, and the Conduit would still sit in Hallam’s study, and Hen would still lie in the end bed looking at nothing. The Conduit only left that study when its master stood under the arch. And three hundred people who had been told about Drift-fatigue for three years needed to *see*.

“You’ve made the Observatory look drunk,” she said.

“The Observatory has been drunk since 1140. We have simply never been honest about it.” There was a scratch down his jaw from the fold. “I have to go. Coyle will lose his nerve if he waits alone, and Cass will lose her temper if she waits with him.” He pulled his coat on one-handed, the right hand still wrapped in his scarf, the brass buttons dull with Drift-mud. “I will be where we said.”

“Kasimir.”

He stopped with his hand on the door. She had meant to say a word about the lock, or the stairs, or Hallam. What came out was, “Your cuffs are filthy.”

“I am aware. It is the first time in my life.” The corner of his mouth went up. “I find I do not mind as much as I was raised to.”

Then it was just her and Teo, asleep on the bench with his hands folded on his chest like a knight on a tomb. The grey on his fingertips had not gone back a hair. She rolled the charter round its rod, slid it into its case, and woke him.


* * *


The College gathered under the Lantern Gate at a quarter to six, with the fog still coming in off the sea.

There were three hundred of them, and they filled the Old Court from wall to wall, gowns grey over their coats, breath smoking. The brass studs of the meridian ran down the middle, and nobody stood on them. Above the fog the arch went up into a sky the colour of a pigeon’s breast, and on the keystone the lantern burned in its iron cage, small and gold, the one light in the College nobody was ever allowed to put out. Under it, cut so long ago that the letters had gone soft: WE RECORD WHAT IS.

Beyond the arch the Drift Wing was a long darkness with its windows lit, the Observatory dome faint as a reflection in a puddle. It had held all night on yesterday’s Survey and the last of last year’s Equinox Charter. Ilse could feel it at the back of her head, a low note going thin.

A black oak lectern stood under the arch. On it lay the Equinox folio, its corners weighted with brass, the Wing drawn in single, certain, beautiful lines. She had copied for that hand two evenings a week since Gleaning.

“Breathe,” said Teo.

“I am breathing.”

“You’re breathing like a bellows with a hole in it, which I know because I am also breathing like that.” He had insisted on carrying the charter case. “There are forty-four proctors. I don’t know why I needed to know that. It’s helping.”

Sorry stirred against her ribs. It had slept like a dropped stone since the fold, and by every rule Cass knew it should have slept till nightfall. Now it lifted its head. Its ears came up, then flattened, and its whole small body turned under her hand like a compass needle until it pointed past the Masters’ bench toward the low door of the Register.

Cass had been leaning by that door five minutes ago, braids tucked into her collar, looking bored. Now there was only wall. In the clerks’ row on the steps, where Merrin Coyle should have been sitting with his inkhorn, there was a gap.

Hallam came out at ten to six.

He walked slowly, a little stooped, his white beard combed and his spectacles catching the lantern. The grey kid gloves. The plain black gown of the Register, without any of the velvet Steyne wore. People quietened for Aurek Hallam the way they quietened in a chapel.

He took his place at the lectern. Provost Steyne stood at his right hand, silver and splendid. Magistra Calder sat at the end of the Masters’ bench with a face like a shut book.

“My dear friends,” Hallam said. He did not need to be loud. The arch took his voice and laid it gently over the court. “Last night a party of seven came home to us out of the dark. We sent twelve parties to the Tern Stack, and the Drift was crueller this spring than I have known it in forty years, and only one came home with the course run. And it was not one party. It was two, who had been rivals, and who chose to be one.” He found Ilse in the crowd and smiled at her with such plain warmth that her stomach turned over. “The Ninefold wrote, *the Reach is held by many hands or by none*. I have never seen it truer.”

“They have brought us something new. An open charter, which attests not a place but a range. Magistra Calder has ruled, rightly, that it is true, and that by custom it stands as the basis of this morning’s charter. It is remarkable, and that is exactly why I must ask you to be patient with it.” He laid one gloved hand on his folio. “We do not know what an open charter does in a year, or in a storm. We know what *this* does. I propose that we set the open charter aside, with honour, pending study, and sign the folio we know. For safety, my dears. The Wing must hold. There are students who sleep in the Third Range every night of the year, and they will not thank us for an experiment.”

It was the most reasonable thing she had ever heard. She watched the heads begin to nod. Steyne nodded. Of course Steyne nodded.

“I will have my objection recorded, Master Hallam,” said Calder, without rising.

“Recorded, Magistra, with my respect.”

And Ilse, who had spent nine years making sure nobody looked at her for longer than it took to sell a basket of eggs, stepped out of the front row onto the brass of the meridian, where nobody stood, and said, “No.”


* * *


She had decided, coming in over the heath at three in the morning with one pole of Absalom’s litter on her shoulder, that she would not make it pretty. Making things pretty was the one thing she was good at, and look where that had got everyone.

“Under every Morning Survey sheet filed in this College since 1201,” she said, “there’s a second drawing. Blind ink. Gall and alum and quicksilver, so it dries to nothing. Every time you sign a Morning Survey sheet in the Antechamber, you sign over a ghost-line, and a sliver of the burden of something very old runs down it into your hand.”

Her voice was smaller than his, and the arch did not lay it gently over anyone.

“That’s the Quiet. It isn’t Drift-fatigue. It starts in the signing hand because the signing hand is what signed. Hen Solberg signed more than anyone in our party, because she was quickest, and because she was kind.” Her voice caught, and she waited for it to steady. “And the voids. Brine End. Corrow. The Little Wick lane in the Fallowmarch, where I was standing when it came apart. Every one followed an inspection tour from this College.”

“Miss Marrow,” said Steyne. “This is neither the time—”

“There are seven Laws,” Ilse said. “Master Hallam teaches six. The seventh is Transfer. It says you can trace a mark under another mark, line for line, and the burden will run to the one underneath. They don’t teach it here. Every hedge-forger in the Fallowmarch knows it.” She made herself look at the Masters, then at the crowd, then, last, at Hallam. “I know it because I am one. I’ve moved hedges for money since I was fourteen, and for three years under a dead man’s mark, so he’d carry the cost instead of me. That’s how I know what I’m looking at. That’s the only reason anyone in this court can see it at all.”

The silence was not empty. It was full of three hundred people adjusting what they knew of her, and she could feel them doing it, like a field of hedges all straining at once.

Hallam took off his spectacles, polished them on a fold of his gown, and put them back. When he looked at her over them his face held nothing but sorrow. It had always been a kind face. It still was.

“My dear friends,” he said gently, “you have heard our accused forger. She is a gifted young woman, and on the eve of her tribunal she would have you doubt the Register. I do not ask you to think ill of her. I ask only that you think which of us has been honest with you for forty years.”

It went through the crowd like wind through barley, and she watched the barley bend.

Then, from her left, a voice she knew said, “She pulled us out of the fold.”

It was Piety Mercer, who had screamed at them in the fold, with a bandage round her wrist and her pious little mouth set hard. Orla Devane, beside her, folded her arms. “She did. Rather rudely. But she did.”

It was not enough. But Steyne’s hand, which had been lifting toward the proctors, went back to his side.

Hallam saw it. He only sighed, as though the morning had grown more tiring than he had hoped, and dipped his pen. “The Wing will not wait for us, my dears, and nor will the sun.” He signed the Equinox folio, a neat looped sigil like a coiled rope, and the level in Ilse’s ears changed pitch.

She felt the folio take. It was beautiful work. The Wing, thin at the end of its year, slid into those single lines and lay still. And under the lines, where no one could see, the ghost woke: a long cold pull that had nothing to do with the Wing, going away under the Old Court, down through seven levels of stone, toward the oldest thing in the College, which wanted holding.

It was waiting for the next pen.

“Proctor Arnaud,” said Hallam. “If you would lead the proctors.”

Lisbet Arnaud came forward, tall and correct, and took the pen from his gloved hand.

There was no time to think, so Ilse measured. Precedence asked two questions, in order. Which record was truer? And only if they were equal, which was older? She did not need to be first. She needed to be truer, and she needed to be before Lisbet Arnaud’s pen, because she did not know how much a ghost could pour into a hand in the time it took to sign. The Wing was the College’s to attest. She would do it alone.

“Teo.”

He had the case open before she finished the word. His grey fingers dropped the cap and did not stop for it. The charter came out unrolling into the fog, and he caught the far end, and they held it open between them on the steps of the Lantern Gate like a banner nobody had asked for.

She uncorked the common black with rope-burned fingers. She did not lay her palm on the sheet and agree with it, the way Quince had taught her. She did not have to. There was no lie in it to agree with. It said the Long Gallery lay between a chain and fifty-six links and a chain and sixty-five, and so it did. It said only what was so.

At the foot of the sheet, where nobody had yet signed, she set her own mark. Three lines and a spur.


* * *


The Drift Wing breathed.

Nobody, afterward, found a better word. It began at the First Range: the square stone face of it leaned out a hand’s breadth into the dawn and settled back, slow as a sleeper’s chest. Then the Second Range, a long sigh of lit windows sliding a foot along the dark and returning. Out at the end of the causeway the Observatory dome swung like a lamp on a chain, caught the first of the sun on its glass, and threw it back across the heath in a long gold blade.

It did not come apart. It swayed, all of it, within its limits, and the sound that came across the Margin was old timber easing, the creak of a house settling for the night. The note in Ilse’s ears, which had been thin and straining for a year, opened into a sound she had never felt from any building. It rose and fell. It was the level of a thing that was allowed to move.

Someone said, “Oh,” on a high note, and then a great many people said it.

Hallam had put his gloved palm flat on his own folio, the way you feel a charter to see if it holds. She watched his face change. The Wing had slipped out of his lines as easily as a hand out of a glove and gone to the record that told the truth about it. His folio was only a drawing now, and a drawing bound nothing. A ghost rode an attestation; the pull beneath his lines, which had been waiting for three hundred pens, had no hold left to run along, and let go.

The fog had been settling on the folio all morning. Lisbet Arnaud bent to it, pen in hand, and frowned. “Master. There’s something under the lines.”

On the damp linen, faint violet lines had come up through the sheet beneath the drawing, the way the second line had come up under Ilse’s mark when Sorry spilled Coyle’s tea. Pale as a vein under skin. Perfectly registered. The word went back through the crowd faster than anything Hallam had said.

“Sign it, Lisbet,” said Hallam quietly. “It will do you no harm now. It holds nothing any longer. That is rather the trouble.”

Across the court, the Register door banged open.

Kasimir came through it at a dead run, his hair all over his face, a sheet of linen-rag clutched flat to his chest like a page saved from a fire. Cass was a pace behind him with her sling in one fist, and behind her came Merrin Coyle, stumbling, his inkhorn bouncing on its cord, looking as though he had been sick and might be again. At the foot of the steps Kasimir stopped so short that Cass ran into his back. He could not speak. He held the sheet out to Ilse.

Sorry went rigid in her coat and pressed itself as far from the sheet as it could, its fur standing in a ridge.

She took the Conduit.

To the eye it was nearly blank: a double folio of good rag, a few registration crosses, and at the bottom, in black ink, the coiled rope of his mark. But the fog found it at once. The damp came up through the linen and the violet came with it, hundreds of lines fine as hairs, each beginning at a little knot of strokes that was a student’s mark, each running inward, branching and joining and thickening, a river delta drawn backward, all of it flowing to the root of the sheet and into the coiled rope. In that tangle was a wren’s foot. In it, too, was Hen. And it hummed, not like the honest Ammonite charter he had once let her hold, but lower and sicker, a note that went down into the ground under her and kept going.

Hallam came down from the lectern, one step and then the next, a gloved hand on the stone of the arch. He stopped two steps above her, and when he spoke it was not to the crowd.

“You’ll kill her.”

“Master Hallam—” said Steyne.

“You will *kill* her.” His voice cracked straight across, like old ice. “The centre is failing, Ilse. Not the Margin. The Zero Sheets. The First Atlas. I went down to Level Seven in twelve hundred with a lens and a rule, as I had every year since I took the Register, and they were softening. A hair a season. Then two hairs. I measured it for a year, because I could not believe my own hand, and then I laid the figures on the Provost’s desk.” He looked past her, over her head, at Steyne. “And I was told to keep it quiet.”

Steyne looked away. Three hundred people watched him do it. He looked down at the brass studs of the meridian as if he had never noticed them before.

“Do you know what it is,” Hallam said, “to *measure* the end of the world, to the hair, and be told to keep it to yourself?” Behind the lenses his eyes were wet, and tired, and absolutely certain. “I tied my own mark into the Zero Sheets with my own hand, and look.” He held up his gloved hands, and they did not shake, because they could not. “So I asked for help. A sliver from each of you, so small most of you never felt it. And I pruned the edges, Ninefold forgive me, because a tree that will not be pruned will fall. A few hundred of us, my dear, against a million of her children. The children of Hobbet are among them. Your mother is among them.”

She saw it then, and the seeing was worse than anything she had imagined in Brask’s shed with Kasimir. There was no cruelty in him. There never had been. There was only a man who had loved a thing so long that it had turned him to stone from the inside, and stone could not change its mind. *He came back certain*, Brask had said. The grey was not only in his hands.

“You could have told us what we were signing,” she said. “Some of us would have signed it anyway.”

“Some.” He smiled at her, and it was terrible, because it was fond. “Never enough, my dear. That is the whole history of the Reach in two words.”

She knew what tearing it would do. A ghost lived by registration, and a torn sheet lay in register with nothing. A ghost-trace was a channel, and if you broke a channel the water did not vanish. It went back the way it had come, every sliver from every hand, down the delta to the root. The weight had been his before it was theirs. He had tied his own mark into the Zero Sheets, and the Conduit had only lent it out. Quince had carried her weight for three years and never felt it, because Quince was in the Hobbet churchyard. The root of this sheet was standing two steps above her, alive, in grey kid gloves.

Her hands would not move.

Hallam looked at them and understood. Some last stiff thing went out of his face, and what was left looked very old.

“Do it, then,” he said quietly. “Someone has to carry her.”

She tore the Conduit in half.

There was no flash. There was only the sound of good linen parting, small and dry, like a sheet torn for bandages. Then she tore the halves across, because she was thorough, and let the pieces fall on the step.

Everything went still. It was not the stillness of held ground. It was a held breath let go, everywhere at once. The cold pull under the Old Court stopped. She felt a thread in her own right hand go, a thread she had never noticed until it was cut, and her fingers opened on their own. Beside her Teo made a small sound. He was staring at his hand on the edge of the open charter, where the grey was going out of his fingertips like a tide, pink coming back into the skin from the knuckle down.

Up in the infirmary, whose tall windows looked onto the Old Court, somebody laughed.

It was not a polite laugh. It was a big, startled, unlovely bark, the laugh of someone who has just remembered the end of a joke told them a whole winter ago, and it rang across the silent court from an open window. Ilse would have known it at the bottom of the sea. Teo’s face crumpled, and he did not let go of the charter to wipe it.

“That’s Hen,” he said. “That’s Hen.”

On the step above them, Hallam’s gloves split.

They went along the seams first, at the backs of the fingers, the fine kid opening like the skin of a ripe plum, then across the palms. What was underneath was the same colour as the gloves. It had always been. The grey went up past his wrists while she watched, under the black sleeves, and came out again at his collar, a slow slate tide climbing his throat into the white of his beard. He did not cry out. He looked only mildly surprised, like a man handed a bill a little larger than he had expected, who has decided on reflection that it is fair.

He sat down on the steps of the Lantern Gate, carefully, the way an old man sits on a bench in the sun. He turned his face east, toward the sun coming up over the Reach, and Tessary far beyond it.

Then he was still. His eyes stayed open. The light came up into them, and they did not blink.

Nobody moved. Calder was on her feet with a hand pressed flat to her mouth. The fog was lifting off the sea. Behind the arch the Drift Wing went on breathing, in and out, as though it had waited a very long time to be allowed to.

Above them, on the keystone, the lantern burned in its iron cage, small and gold, over words that nobody had ever thought to doubt.

Ilse looked down at her hands. They were shaking so hard she could not have held a pen, and she could not make them stop.


## Chapter 31: The Board of Errata

Hen ate four boiled eggs, a heel of bread, a dish of stewed prunes she had always claimed to hate, and most of Teo’s breakfast, and then she cried for a quarter of an hour without stopping, and then she laughed at herself for crying, and then she asked whether there was any more bread.

“You’ll be sick,” said Dr. Orle, from the next bed.

“I don’t care. I’ve been not caring about bread for four months.” Hen tore the crust off the new slice and looked at it as though it were a letter from home. Her hair was lank and her freckles stood out on her skin like flecks of rust on a white plate, and her right hand, when she reached for the butter, was pink to the fingertips. “Tide’s in,” she said, and laughed again, the big unlovely bark that had come out of the window on the morning of the twenty-second, and two beds down a first-year who had been Quiet since Deepwinter laughed too, without knowing why.

The infirmary had been like this for a day and a half. Nine beds, nine students sitting up, all of them hungry and most of them weeping at odd moments, and Orle going from one to the next with her sleeves rolled and her mouth very straight. Ilse had been sitting on the end of Hen’s bed since First Bell.

“I remember all of it,” Hen said, more quietly, when Orle had moved on. “That’s the strange part. I remember you sitting there in Frost, and Teo bringing me those awful little cakes, and Cass shouting. I remember knowing you were all being kind. I just couldn’t find where the kind was supposed to go.” She turned the crust over. “It was like the tide going out and never turning. You stand on the flats and you know there should be water.”

“I knew about the lines,” Ilse said. “For two weeks before you went Quiet. I knew, and I didn’t tell anyone, because telling would have meant telling what I was.”

Hen looked at her for a while. The look was flat for a moment, from habit, and then it was not flat at all.

“Teo said you’d say that,” she said. “He said you’d been saving it up. He said I was to tell you that you’re an idiot and then give you a prune.” She held one out. “It’s a horrible prune. I’ve picked it specially.”

Ilse ate the prune.

Teo came in at nine with his hands held out in front of him like a man carrying a full tray. He had been doing this to everyone for a day and a half. He held them out to Hen, who kissed the fingertips one at a time in front of the whole ward, which made him go so red that the first-year two beds down laughed again.

At the far end of the ward, by the window, a young man in a grey infirmary shirt was sitting up very straight with his hands on the blanket. Ilse had first seen him in the corridor outside, in Gleaning, on a bench, staring at nothing, with slate-coloured fingers. Wil Ashdown. He had not said a word that anyone on the Wren Stair had ever heard. Now he was watching the orderly set a tray down on the table by the door, and he cleared his throat, and everyone in the ward went still to listen.

“Is that marmalade?” said Wil Ashdown.

The orderly said that it was.

“I used to like marmalade,” said Wil, slowly, as if he were reading it off a sheet. He looked down at his own hands, which were pink, and then out of the window at the Old Court, and then back at the tray. “I think I still do. Could I have some?”

Orle, who had written to Tessary eleven times and been told by the Provost to stop, turned her back to the ward and stood looking at a shelf of brown bottles for a long moment. When she turned round again she was brisk.


* * *


Cass told her about the lock that afternoon, on the roof of the Second Range, with her boots hooked over the gutter and the Wing swaying gently under them like a hammock.

“Annotated. Wouldn’t take a crowbar. Wouldn’t take Coyle crying on it, and he tried.” She spat over the edge, thoughtfully. “Your Reyne goes white and says, the key, man, where does he keep the key, and Coyle says *I don’t know*, and then Coyle says, *oh*. Picks up that great ivory paperweight off the desk. Hollow. Unscrews at the base like a pepper-pot. Key inside, wrapped in a bit of chamois.” She shook her head. “Knew all along, the eel. Just didn’t know he knew.”

“Is he all right?”

“Coyle? Wept on the Antechamber floor for an hour, then burned every unprinted folio in the press-room.” Below them, out on the causeway, the Observatory dome rocked a little westward with the ebb. “They carried the old man down to the Crypt last night. Orle’s orderlies. Eight of them. Said he was heavy as a church door.” She picked at a route-cord. “I didn’t want to be glad, Marrow. I keep thinking of Nan’s camp sliding down the hill in the dark. And I’m not glad.”

“No,” said Ilse.

At noon on the day of the equinox, after the Wing had been breathing for six hours and nobody had died of it, Magistra Calder had laid the open charter on the black oak lectern under the arch and the whole College had signed it, one by one, three hundred marks under her wren’s foot. Coyle had raked the sheet first, at Calder’s order, and found it clean; Ilse knew it would not have mattered if he had not. Nothing in blind ink could keep register with a range. She had watched from the steps. Not one of them had felt anything go down into the ground. She had felt the College take its own weight again, every hand light, the way it ought to have been.

On the twenty-fourth, Provost Steyne addressed the Mess.

He stood at the high table in his velvet and spoke for nine minutes, and he was very good. He spoke of a grievous discovery, and of grave misconduct by a single Master, a man who had served the College for forty years and whose mind, the infirmary believed, had been disordered by the burden he had chosen to carry in secret. He spoke of the Quiet, now cured. He thanked the students of the Long Measure, without naming any. He spoke of calm, and of the confidence the Assembly might continue to place in the Register.

He did not say the words *Zero Sheets*. He did not say *Level Seven*, or *softening*, or *the centre is failing*. Three hundred people had heard Hallam say those words under the Lantern Gate two days before, and the Provost of the Meridian walked all the way round them like a man walking round a hole in a floor.

Ilse noticed. She saw Kasimir, at the Patent end of the Mess, notice too. He took out his notebook of errors observed and made an entry.

When Steyne sat, there was a silence. Then, from the Walkers’ table, somebody said, not loudly, “Broth.” And somebody else said it, and then a dozen people, low and flat and steady, like the Sounders’ Choir at the bottom of a hymn. *Broth. Broth. Broth.* Steyne did not look up from his plate. The proctors did not stop it. Lisbet Arnaud, at the head of the proctors’ table, examined her spoon.


* * *


The Board of Errata sat on the twenty-eighth of Thaw in the Hall of Instruments, because no other room in the College had a table long enough.

They had pushed the great theodolites back against the walls under their dust-sheets, so that the hall was lined with shrouded shapes like a congregation of patient ghosts, and down the middle they had set the long table with five chairs behind it. Magistra Calder sat in the centre, in Court black. On her right sat Halloway, with his three watches laid out in front of him in a row, and Master Moy, broad and quiet as a quarry wall. On her left, Dr. Orle, and old Damask, who had brought a lens on a brass arm and kept turning it in his fingers.

In a chair by the tall east window, a little apart, sat the Chancellor of the Concordance Court.

Lady Imogen Reyne was smaller than Ilse had expected, and older, and more beautiful. She had her son’s grey-blue eyes and none of his hesitations. She wore a dove-grey travelling coat and gloves of black kid, and she sat with her hands folded on the head of an ebony cane and watched the room the way Ilse watched a sheet she suspected of being forged: without hurry, without warmth, waiting for the place where it would give. When Ilse came in, she looked at her once, from boots to hair, and then looked away as if the inventory were complete.

On the table in front of the Board stood a box. It was a Court evidence box of black-japanned tin, the kind that went by mail coach under seal, and its lid was open.

Ilse knew what was in it before she reached the chair set for her. She would have known it from across a field.

“The charge,” said Calder, and paused, as was her habit, long enough for a person to count three, “is forgery. Contrary charting without licence, upon parish charters of the Held Reach, within the Fallowmarch, over a period of years. The particulars have been laid by the Concordance Court upon the information of Mr. Kasimir Reyne, a Member of this College. The Board has the sheets.” She laid a hand on the lid of the tin box. “Twenty-nine of them, the sheets themselves and not copies, drawn by Court writ over the winter from the chests of eleven parishes. Each bears, as its visible mark, a wren’s foot. Each bears beneath it, in blind ink, the mark of Abelard Quince, surveyor, of Hobbet, deceased.”

She did not look at Kasimir, who sat at the side of the hall with his hands on his knees. Ilse did. He had done this all winter. While she sat by Hen’s bed, while they stood on the roof of the Old Court under the cold stars, he had been writing to parish clerks across the Fallowmarch and waiting for the coach. He met her eyes and did not look away and did not apologize. She found, to her surprise, that she did not want him to.

“The Board calls Field-Surveyor Brask.”

Brask came in from the Old Court with the Long Lie in her right hand and her grey left hand hanging at her side, and the patch black over her eye. She had been reinstated at eight o’clock that morning, for the purpose of this testimony, by a minute of the Board.

She gave her evidence standing. She told them about the Walk survey, and the stream bank that did not close by fourteen links, and the way Ilse had drawn it closed. She told them about the conversation in her cottage in Fogmonth and the promise she had extracted, and that she had told no one.

“You knew the candidate to be a forger,” said Calder, “and you did not report it to this Board.”

“I knew her to be a surveyor who had forged. There’s a difference. I’ve spent nineteen years in a leaking shed trying to teach it.” Brask leaned on the Long Lie. “She’s the best pair of hands I’ve had through that shed since it was built, and the worst liar, which is something in a forger. She lied to me twice this year and both times I knew before she’d finished her sentence. Put that in the minutes.”

“It is in the minutes, Field-Surveyor.”

“Good.” Brask looked at Lady Reyne, briefly, with her one eye, and then back at the Board. “Is that all?”

It was all. She went to stand at the back of the hall by the shrouded theodolites, and Ilse felt her there the whole rest of the morning, the way you feel a benchmark at your back.

“The Board calls Mr. Reyne.”

Kasimir stood where he was, at the side of the hall, and when he spoke he spoke in complete sentences, without contractions, the way he did when he was on his dignity, and his voice did not shake at all.

“Miss Marrow forged,” he said. “I have no doubt of it, and I have never had any. I saw the root-tear under the Tull hedge in Reaping and I knew what it was. Every sheet in that box is a forgery, and I gathered them, and I would gather them again.” He paused. “I must also tell the Board something it may not wish to hear. The law under which Miss Marrow would be expelled this morning is the same law under which Master Hallam would still be Master of the Register. His crime was invisible to every honest instrument in this College. It was invisible to me. I have the best hand of my year, and I signed over his ghost-lines every morning for five months and saw nothing. It was visible to exactly one person, and she could see it only because she had done, on a small scale and for poor people, the thing he was doing to us all.” He turned his head, then, and looked across the hall at the chair by the east window. “I do not ask the Board to say that forgery is right. I ask the Board to notice what our honesty failed to see, and who saw it for us.”

Lady Reyne did not move. Her gloved hands stayed folded on the head of her cane. But Ilse, who had not stopped watching her, saw the knuckles go white through the black kid, and then go back.


* * *


“Miss Marrow,” said Calder. “The Board will hear you.”

She had written this out four times on the backs of old Mensuration problems and burned all four, because they were too good. She stood up without anything in her hands.

“It’s all true,” she said. “All twenty-nine. There are thirty-one, actually. Two the Court didn’t find. A ditch at Fennock, in the chest at Saint Ivo’s, and a field gate on the Marlow road. I’ll write out where they are.” Halloway, without being asked, made a note. “I started at fourteen with Abelard Quince, and when he died I went on alone. I used his mark under mine so that the burden would go to him, because he was dead and couldn’t feel it, and I thought that was clever. I moved things back where I believed they belonged. Sometimes I was right. Mostly I was right. That isn’t a defence. It’s just what happened.”

She took a breath.

“I’d like to release them,” she said. “All of them. Now. With my own hand, if the Board permits it. They’re my sheets. They should be unsigned by the hand that signed them. The original and the hand, Magistra. That’s all release asks. It doesn’t ask me to be standing in the ditch.”

There was a stir along the table. Damask stopped turning his lens. Calder looked at her for a long moment, the full count of three and then some.

“You understand what release means for the land.”

“Yes, Magistra.”

“The Tull charter is among them.”

“I know.”

Calder looked down the table. Halloway nodded, jerkily. Moy nodded. Orle nodded. Damask said, “Oh — yes, yes, of course,” and then, more quietly, “oh, my dear.” Calder took the first sheet out of the tin box and laid it on the table in front of the empty chair, and turned the chair to face it.

Ilse sat. She took her pen out of her coat, the same pen, and the common black, and drew the first sheet toward her.

It was a ditch at Lower Hendle, from the spring of 1201. She remembered the client, a cowman with a stammer and three daughters, and the ditch, which a neighbour’s new survey had walked eleven yards into the cowman’s grazing. She looked at her wren’s foot in the corner, three lines and a spur, a little stiffer than she made it now. She put the nib to it and drew one straight line through it, from the spur to the topmost toe, and meant it.

The level in her ears gave a small faint drop, like hearing a door close in another house. Five hundred and sixty miles east, a ditch would be sliding eleven yards back into a neighbour’s field, and a cowman with three daughters would wake to find it there. Under her line, in blind ink no one in the room could see, Quince’s mark let go of its thread at the same moment. She felt that too. It was very slight. It was like unshouldering a pack you have carried so long your back has forgotten it is there.

*Sorry, old man*, she thought. *You can put it down now.*

She drew the second sheet toward her.

It took most of an hour. Nobody spoke. The Board watched, and Brask watched from the back of the hall, and Kasimir watched from the side, and Lady Reyne watched from the window with her face like a winter field. A hedge at Marlow, twenty-two yards. A gatepost at Bell Cross. A well-head at Ashby that Ilse had moved four feet one wet night in 1202 so that a widower could reach it without crossing his brother-in-law’s yard. Some of the drops were so faint she barely felt them. Some went through her like a missed stair. One, a strip of water-meadow at Coldharbour, she had been especially proud of; she had walked it eleven times. She drew the line through the wren’s foot and felt the meadow go, and her hand did not shake, and she was glad of that, and she did not know why.

The Tull charter was last. She had known it would be, because Calder had laid them out by date.

It was the best work she had ever done. Forty-one stations, two hundred and twelve measured lengths, every angle checked twice by sightvane and once more by spite. She knew the hedge on it the way she knew the lines of her own palm. She thought of the slow green slide of it through the dark, the sparrow’s nest riding along on its fork. She thought of Agnes Tull coming down the path in her nightcap with the candle held up, saying *there’s my old Pearmain back*.

She would write to Agnes tonight. She did not yet know what she would say. *I’m sorry* was true but not enough, and Agnes deserved better from her than a thing that was true but not enough.

She drew the line.

Far away in the Fallowmarch, forty yards of hawthorn would be walking back east tonight, two rods, thirty-three feet, taking four apple trees with it onto Lord Saffery’s land. It would not want to. But Fask’s charter was still there, and nothing now said otherwise. The drop in her ears was the deepest of the morning. She sat with her pen in her hand and let it go through her.

Then she capped the ink and put the pen back in her coat.


* * *


The Board withdrew for twenty minutes. When it came back, Calder did not sit.

“The Board finds,” she said, “that Ilse Marrow is guilty of forgery.” The pause. “The offences were committed before her membership of this College, and upon charters of the Held Reach. They are, properly, matters for the Concordance Court. Except that she is now a Member, and the privilege of the College is not the Board’s to waive, nor the Court’s to take.” She did not look at the east window. She did not need to. “Under the precedent of *Errata v. Pell*, 1061, a Member found guilty of an offence of charting is to be sentenced to restitution and to the use that the College most needs. The candidate has made restitution this morning, in the Board’s sight, and beyond what this Board would have dared ask of her.” Another pause, longer. “As to use.”

“This College has not admitted a student to the discipline of Contrary Charting since 1097. The discipline remains in our statutes. It is restricted. It is dangerous. It is the only discipline we possess whose purpose is to understand, from the inside, how a record may be made to lie. The Board is of the view, after the events of the twenty-second, that the College has been without it for a hundred years longer than was wise.” Calder laid both hands flat on the table. “Ilse Marrow will remain a Member of this College, on probation, as a student of Contrary Charting. Her instruction will be in the charge of Field-Surveyor Odile Brask, whose suspension is lifted from this hour. Any further contrary record made by the candidate without the knowledge of her Master will end her membership and deliver her to the Court. The Board is risen.”

There was a sound from the back of the hall that might have been Brask clearing her throat.

Lady Imogen Reyne rose from her chair by the window. She took up her ebony cane and settled her coat, and walked the length of the Hall of Instruments between the shrouded theodolites, unhurried. She passed within an arm’s length of her son. He stood very straight. She did not turn her head, and she did not slow, and she did not speak, and then she was out of the door into the Old Court and the sound of her cane on the flags went away across it, tap, tap, and was gone.

Kasimir stood where she had left him for a while. Then he sat down, and took out his notebook, and did not write anything in it.

Ilse went out by the other door.

The Old Court was empty at that hour. The sun had come out, thin and white, a spring sun with no heat in it yet, and it lay along the honey stone and caught the brass studs of the meridian one after another down the length of the court, so that they made a line of small fires. Beyond the Lantern Gate the Drift Wing swayed in the light. The lantern on the keystone was almost invisible in the sun, but it was burning. It was always burning.

She stood on the step where Hallam had sat down. She was a Member of the College of the Fixed Meridian, legitimately, on honest terms, with a discipline and a Master and her name in the minutes of the Board, and she had given up everything she had ever made to get there. She had nothing to be proud of. She had looked for pride, standing there, out of habit, the way you feel in a pocket for a coin that you know isn’t there.

It wasn’t there. Something else was. It was lighter than pride, and it did not need anybody to see it, and she found that it would hold her weight.


## Chapter 32: Until the Term Is Spent

The Crypt of Benchmarks was colder than the sea.

Ilse went down on the third of Seedtime, in the hour after supper when the chapel was empty, with a lamp and one silver mark in her pocket. The stair under the chapel turned twice and opened into a long low vault of the same honey stone as the Old Court, gone the colour of weak tea down here where no sun had touched it in a thousand years. Lamps burned in niches at intervals along the walls, tended by Orle’s orderlies. Between the lamps, in two long rows facing each other across the flagged aisle, stood the benchmarks.

There were forty-one of them. She had counted once, before the Ladder, when the party had come down with pennies for luck. A woman in a surveyor’s coat of a cut two hundred years out of date, one hand raised a little as if to stop someone at a door. A boy, barely older than a link, who had stilled in 1088 holding a sea-wall against a spring tide, his grey face turned up and his mouth a little open. They were alive, in a sense nobody fully understood. They did not breathe that she could see. They did not blink. Orle’s people dusted them once a week with soft brushes, and spoke to them while they worked, because it seemed rude not to.

In every grey hand there were coins. Centuries of them. Pennies and half-marks and foreign silver and buttons from students who had nothing else, stacked in the curled palms and wedged between the fingers, so that when the lamplight moved the whole Crypt glittered faintly from waist height, like a field of small moons.

Hallam was at the far end, by himself.

They had not been able to stand him up. He had stilled sitting, and seated he remained, on a plain oak bench that the orderlies had carried down and set at the end of the eastern row. Someone had straightened his gown and combed his beard. The half-moon spectacles were still on his nose. His hands rested on his knees, palms half-turned upward, and they were bare, and they were grey: not the soft grey of kid, but slate, the cool dense grey of a roof after rain, from the fingertips up into the sleeves. Orle’s people had set the bench so that he faced east. He was looking at the Crypt wall. Beyond it, three hundred miles off through the rock, was the Zero Stone.

There were already two pennies in his left hand. She had not expected that. She stood looking at them for a while.

“Well,” she said at last. Her voice went a short way into the vault and stopped. “I came to tell you some things. You can’t stop me, so.”

She sat down on the end of the bench beside him.

“Hen’s back in the party. Orle let her out on the first. She laughed so hard at Teo falling over the chain in the Long Gallery that she had to sit on the stair. Wil Ashdown asked for marmalade. He’s eaten nothing else for a week. Orle says she’ll let him, because she wants to see what happens.” She looked at her hands. “The Wing’s holding. Under the open charter. It sways a hand’s breadth at the First Range and two chains at the far end, and it goes out on the ebb and comes home on the flood, and nobody has been fined for a lost stair in eleven days. Halloway has written a paper. It has forty-one footnotes.”

“You let me hold the Ammonite charter,” she said. “In Gleaning. In the Antechamber. You put it in my hands and said four hundred years of stone, my dear, feel it, and I felt it, and you were watching my face while I did. You said I had a draftswoman’s hand.” She stopped. “I’ve been trying to work out whether you meant it. Whether all of that was just how you picked which of us would sign the most sheets. And I think you meant it. I think that’s the worst part. I think you were kind to me, and you’d have let me go Quiet, and both were true, and you didn’t see any difference.”

He looked at the wall.

“You were right about the centre,” she said. “Brask thinks so. Halloway went down to Level Seven with a lens. You were right, and you told the wrong man, and then you did the wrong thing about it, and I tore it up in front of everyone, and I’d do it again.” Her throat had closed a little. She waited. “But I’m sorry it was you at the bottom. I am sorry about that.”

She took the silver mark out of her pocket. It was one of the six left over from the Long Field money, after the coach to the Cape. It was the last thing she owned that had been meant for the field. She held it a moment, and then she leaned over and put it into his grey right hand, which was empty, and closed his fingers over it as far as they would go, which was not far. They were as cold as the sea.

Then she took her lamp and went back up into the chapel, where someone had left a window open, and the evening air was full of the smell of gorse.


* * *


Brask walked her out into the Margin at dawn on the fifth, as she had on the third of Gleaning, half a year ago, before any of it.

The gorse was in full flower now, from the College wall to the edge of the heath, a yellow so loud it seemed to make a noise, and the whole Margin smelled of it, sweet and nutty, like warm biscuits left on a sill. Larks went up out of it and hung singing. The ground breathed under their boots, and Ilse found she no longer braced against it. Brask walked ahead with the Long Lie, setting it down at every pace, reading the grain through the brass shoe. She had been Field-Surveyor again for a week, and in charge of Contrary Charting for the same week, a charge the College had not given anyone since 1097 and which, as far as Ilse could tell, came with no salary, no room and no instructions.

“First lesson,” Brask had said on the first morning, in the leaking shed. “Everything you know is a lie. Your job, for the next four years, is to work out which bits, and how they were made, and who made them. Then you write it down. Then you sign it.” She had sniffed. “I’ll be checking your spelling.”

Now she stopped at the top of a low rise where a cairn-crow had built a cairn of seven white pebbles on a flat stone. The College lay behind them on its headland, the Held Side honey-gold in the early sun and the Drift Wing trailing out over the heath, moving slightly on its tide.

“Halloway went down to Seven again last night,” Brask said. “Calder went with him. They took the lens and those three watches of his.” She knocked the Long Lie on the stone. “It’s faster. Two hairs a season when Hallam measured it. Now it’s nearer two a month.”

Ilse had known it would be. She had known it since the twenty-second, in the place behind her ears where she knew things before she would admit them. The old man had been holding the Zero Sheets up with three hundred borrowed hands and his own, and she had taken the hands away.

“Because of me.”
